Limited-life foundation (e.g., Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation — spend down within 30 years of death): investment horizon SHORTENS over time → must progressively:
1. Reduce/stop new PE commitments
2. Sell private portfolios in secondary markets
3. Shift to liquid assets
4. Accept lower returns as risk tolerance decreases
→ This is essentially an institutional version of the lifecycle/glide path concept from individual retirement planning
Links to: Section 10.5 (estate planning), Section 20 (retirement planning)

**Mission-Related Investing (Impact Investing):**
Growing trend: directing assets beyond 5% annual grants toward mission-aligned investments (e.g., Ford Foundation allocated $1B over 10 years to inequality-related investments)
→ Challenge: mission investments may sacrifice return → lower portfolio returns → faster asset depletion → reduced long-term grant-making capacity
→ Decision rule: only pursue mission investing if expected return is competitive on risk-adjusted basis, or if the mission impact is SO significant that the return sacrifice is justified by the foundation's primary purpose
Links to: Section 16 (ethics), Section 13.5 (shareholder engagement)

### 17.6 Banks and Insurance Companies

**Banks — Balance Sheet Framework:**
Assets: Loans (~50%), Debt securities (~25%), Central bank deposits/cash/receivables (~25%)
Liabilities: Deposits (~50%+), Short-term funding (commercial paper), Long-term debt (10-15%), Trading/repo payables (10-20%)
Capital: Common equity, preferred, subordinated debt → subject to Basel III/CRD IV requirements

**Bank Investment Portfolio Objectives:**
1. Maximize net present value of shareholders' capital
2. Manage interest rate risk (asset/liability gap management)
3. Maintain regulatory capital adequacy (LCR, NSFR)
4. Provide liquidity buffer for deposit outflows and loan commitments

**Bank Liquidity Regime (Post-GFC):**
Pre-2008: banks used investment portfolios for yield enhancement → lower quality, less liquid securities
Post-2008: regulatory focus on capital adequacy → portfolios now higher quality, more liquid → mandated LCR (highly liquid assets must exceed 100% of 30-day expected outflows) and NSFR (stable funding sources relative to assets)
→ Inference chain: GFC → bank failures → regulatory response (Basel III, Dodd-Frank) → higher capital requirements → less risk-taking → banks shift to higher quality portfolios → lower investment returns → banks seek fee income to compensate → systemic risk reduced but profitability compressed

**Insurance Companies — Two Distinct Models:**

**Life Insurers:**
- Liability duration: 20-40 years (long)
- Liability nature: actuarially predictable (mortality tables), but subject to longevity risk
- Investment approach: LDI-dominated, duration-matched FI portfolio + limited growth allocation
- General account: company bears investment risk (whole life, fixed annuities)
- Separate account: policyholder bears investment risk (variable life, variable annuities)
→ Key risk: interest rate risk → if long rates fall, the present value of long-duration liabilities INCREASES faster than assets → ALM mismatch → capital adequacy threat

**P&C Insurers:**
- Liability duration: shorter (1-5 years for most claims)
- Liability nature: less predictable (catastrophe risk, litigation uncertainty)
- Investment approach: more conservative, shorter duration FI, higher liquidity reserves
- Combined ratio = (Claims + Expenses) / Premiums → if >100%, underwriting is unprofitable → investment returns must compensate
→ P&C insurers face CORRELATED risk: natural disasters cause claims AND market disruption simultaneously → need highly liquid, defensive investment portfolio

**Three Accounting Systems for Financial Institutions:**
1. Financial reporting (GAAP/IFRS): communicates to shareholders → smoothest income
2. Statutory accounting (SAP/Solvency II): for regulators → most conservative, strips intangibles → determines capital adequacy
3. Economic/mark-to-market: true market values → most volatile but most accurate
→ PM must understand ALL THREE because investment decisions that look optimal under one system may be suboptimal under another
→ Insurance example: buying a high-yield bond may improve economic returns but worsen statutory capital ratios → regulatory constraint binds even when economic logic supports the trade
Links to: Section 12 (FI dynamics), Appendix E (credit analysis), Section 5 (risk management)

### 17.7 Institutional Investor Comparison Matrix

**Decision Framework: Matching Investor Type to Optimal Portfolio**

| Investor Type | Time Horizon | Liquidity Needs | Risk Metric | Tax Status | Typical Return Target |
|--------------|-------------|----------------|------------|------------|---------------------|
| DB Pension (corporate) | Long, shortening as matures | Low-medium | Surplus volatility, funded ratio | Tax-exempt | Discount rate (3-6%) |
| DB Pension (public) | Long | Low-medium | Funded ratio, contribution volatility | Tax-exempt | Actuarial assumed return (7-8%) |
| DC Pension | Individual participant-dependent | Medium (portability) | Account balance volatility | Tax-deferred | Participant-selected |
| SWF (stabilization) | Short-medium | HIGH | Probability of negative return | Tax-exempt | CPI + low spread |
| SWF (savings) | Very long | LOW | Probability of real value loss | Tax-exempt | Real return 3-5% |
| SWF (pension reserve) | Long → shortening | LOW → increasing | Probability of meeting obligations | Tax-exempt | CPI + 4-5% |
| University Endowment | Perpetual | LOW (2-4% net spending) | Total return volatility (10-15% SD) | Tax-exempt | HEPI + 5% real |
| Private Foundation | Perpetual or limited-life | MEDIUM (mandatory 5%+) | Total return volatility | 2% excise tax (US) | CPI + 5% + expenses |
| Life Insurance | 20-40 years | Medium-high | Surplus volatility, capital adequacy | Taxable | Match liability discount rate |
| P&C Insurance | 1-5 years | HIGH | VaR, CVaR, stress tests | Taxable | Investment return to offset combined ratio |
| Banks | Perpetual (but short instruments) | VERY HIGH (LCR, NSFR) | VaR, stress tests, capital ratios | Taxable | NIM optimization, capital preservation |

**Cross-Institutional Inference Chains:**

Low interest rates → DB pension liabilities inflate → funded status deteriorates → plans increase risk-taking OR adopt LDI → demand for long-duration bonds surges → further compresses long rates → creates feedback loop where the "cure" (LDI) exacerbates the "disease" (low rates) for non-hedged plans
→ Simultaneously: low rates → life insurers face ALM mismatch → guarantee risk on fixed annuities increases → insurers must either raise premiums, reduce guarantees, or take more investment risk → systemic vulnerability
→ Meanwhile: endowments facing low expected returns → shift to alternatives → PE/VC fundraising surges → increased competition for deals → return expectations should be revised DOWNWARD → but many endowments maintain high return assumptions → potential for disappointment
Links to: Section 15 (economics), Section 12 (FI dynamics), Appendix D (risk premiums)

Limitation: Institutional investor behavior is heavily influenced by regulatory regimes that differ by jurisdiction and change over time. The frameworks above provide analytical structure but specific regulatory requirements must be verified for each jurisdiction and time period. Additionally, reported asset allocations for pension funds and SWFs may lag actual allocations due to reporting delays and the difficulty of marking illiquid assets to market.
