## Section 18: Performance Evaluation, Attribution & Manager Selection

### 18.1 Three Components of Performance Evaluation

**Performance Evaluation = Measurement + Attribution + Appraisal**

1. **Performance Measurement:** WHAT was the portfolio's return?
→ Calculates absolute return and excess return (portfolio return minus benchmark return)
→ Must include risk measurement (ex post: volatility, max drawdown; ex ante: VaR)

2. **Performance Attribution:** HOW was the return achieved?
→ Decomposes excess return into allocation, selection, and interaction effects
→ Must account for ALL of the portfolio's return or risk exposure
→ Must reflect the actual investment decision-making process

3. **Performance Appraisal:** Was performance due to SKILL or luck?
→ Uses risk-adjusted ratios (Sharpe, Sortino, Information Ratio, Appraisal Ratio)
→ Requires statistical significance testing — paradox of skill means luck explains an increasing proportion of performance differentials as aggregate skill improves

**Critical Principle: Attribution must mirror the investment process**
Bottom-up stock picker → measure stock selection, NOT sector allocation (sector effects are byproducts)
Top-down macro manager → measure allocation decisions across countries/sectors
Factor-based manager → measure factor exposures vs. benchmark
→ PM heuristic: if attribution analysis doesn't reflect how the manager actually makes decisions, the results are meaningless — redesign the attribution framework
Links to: Section 13 (equity PM), Section 12 (FI dynamics)

### 18.2 Return Attribution — Brinson Models

**Brinson-Hood-Beebower (BHB) Model — Three Effects:**
- Allocation effect: A_i = (w_i − W_i) × B_i → value added/lost by over/underweighting sectors relative to benchmark
- Selection effect: S_i = W_i × (R_i − B_i) → value added/lost by picking different securities within sectors
- Interaction effect: I_i = (w_i − W_i) × (R_i − B_i) → combined effect of allocation AND selection

Total excess return = Allocation + Selection + Interaction
→ PM heuristic: if allocation is negative but selection is strongly positive, the manager is a good stock picker but poor sector allocator → consider mandating benchmark sector weights

**BHB Flaw:** Overweighting a sector with positive returns always shows positive allocation, even if the sector underperformed the overall benchmark. This creates misleading attribution.

**Brinson-Fachler (BF) Model — Fix:**
A_i = (w_i − W_i) × (B_i − B) → compares sector return to OVERALL benchmark return, not zero
→ At the portfolio level, total allocation effect is identical between BHB and BF
→ At the sector level, BF provides more economically meaningful results
→ PM heuristic: always use BF model for client reporting — it answers the right question: did overweighting this sector add value relative to the overall market?

**Arithmetic vs. Geometric Attribution:**
Arithmetic: effects ADD to total excess return in a single period → intuitive for non-practitioners → BUT sub-period effects don't sum to multi-period excess return → requires smoothing adjustments
Geometric: effects MULTIPLY to total geometric excess return → no smoothing needed across periods → BUT less intuitive
→ Decision rule: use arithmetic for client-facing reports (easier to explain), geometric for internal analytics (more accurate multi-period)

### 18.3 Attribution Approaches Comparison

**Three Attribution Data Approaches:**

| Approach | Input Data | Accuracy | Complexity | Best For |
|----------|-----------|----------|-----------|---------|
| Returns-based | Portfolio and benchmark returns only | Lowest | Lowest | HFs, opaque strategies, no holdings data |
| Holdings-based | Beginning-of-period holdings + returns | Medium | Medium | Low-turnover strategies, passive |
| Transactions-based | Holdings + all transactions + costs | Highest | Highest | High-turnover, active strategies |

→ Decision rule: use the most granular approach available. If holdings data is available, NEVER use returns-based alone.
→ Returns-based attribution is most vulnerable to data manipulation and timing effects

**Macro vs. Micro Attribution:**
Macro attribution: evaluates the FUND SPONSOR's decisions — strategic asset allocation and manager selection
→ Did the sponsor add value by overweighting certain asset classes? Did they select good managers?

Micro attribution: evaluates the PORTFOLIO MANAGER's decisions — allocation within their mandate and security selection
→ Did the manager add value through sector tilts within their mandate? Did they pick good stocks?

→ Multi-level attribution chain: Fund sponsor SAA → TAA deviations → Manager selection → Within-manager allocation → Security selection → Transaction timing
→ Each level can be evaluated independently — the total fund-level excess return is the sum of all levels

### 18.4 Fixed Income Return Attribution

**FI Attribution Decomposition (5 sources):**
1. **Income return:** coupon payments accrued during period
2. **Roll return:** price change from "rolling down" the yield curve as bond ages (assuming unchanged curve)
3. **Shift return:** price change from parallel yield curve movement (duration × ΔY)
4. **Twist/butterfly return:** price change from non-parallel curve movements (key rate durations)
5. **Spread return:** price change from credit spread changes (DTS framework)
6. **Residual:** unexplained portion (transaction costs, timing, model error)

→ For each component, compare portfolio contribution vs. benchmark contribution → identifies WHERE active value was added
→ PM heuristic: if a FI manager's excess return is primarily from spread return, they are taking credit risk (not skill in rates) — be wary of confusing beta with alpha in credit
Links to: Section 12.3 (FI return decomposition)

### 18.5 Risk Attribution

**Risk Attribution vs. Return Attribution:**
Return attribution: explains RETURNS from active decisions
Risk attribution: explains RISK from active decisions
→ Must be used together — a positive return attribution from allocation could have come with disproportionate risk

**Risk Attribution Approach Selection:**

| Investment Process | Relative (vs. Benchmark) | Absolute |
|---|---|---|
| Bottom-up | Position's marginal contribution to tracking risk | Position's marginal contribution to total risk |
| Top-down | Attribute tracking risk to allocation and selection decisions | N/A |
| Factor-based | Factor's marginal contribution to tracking risk and active specific risk | Factor's marginal contribution to total risk and specific risk |

→ For benchmark-relative: tracking risk (tracking error) is the key metric
→ For absolute return: total portfolio volatility or VaR is the key metric
→ PM heuristic: a manager who consistently adds return with LOW marginal contribution to risk is genuinely skilled. A manager who adds return with HIGH marginal risk contribution may just be taking uncompensated bets.

### 18.6 Performance Appraisal Measures

**Six Key Risk-Adjusted Performance Ratios:**

| Ratio | Formula | Measures | Risk Metric | Best For |
|-------|---------|----------|------------|---------|
| Sharpe | (R_p − R_f) / σ_p | Excess return per unit of total risk | Standard deviation | Standalone portfolios, total risk context |
| Treynor | (R_p − R_f) / β_p | Excess return per unit of systematic risk | Beta | Portfolios within a diversified total portfolio |
| Information Ratio | (R_p − R_B) / σ(R_p − R_B) | Active return per unit of active risk | Tracking error | Benchmark-relative mandates |
| Appraisal Ratio | α / σ_ε | Jensen's alpha per unit of non-systematic risk | Residual std dev | Active managers with factor exposure |
| Sortino | (R_p − r_T) / σ_D | Return above target per unit of downside risk | Target semi-std dev | Capital preservation, asymmetric returns |
| Capture Ratio | UC / DC | Asymmetry of participation in up/down markets | N/A (ratio of ratios) | Manager suitability assessment |

**Capture Ratio Decision Framework:**
Upside capture > 100%: manager outperforms in up markets
Downside capture < 100%: manager loses less in down markets
Capture ratio (UC/DC) > 1: convex return profile (DESIRABLE) → participates more in upside than downside
Capture ratio < 1: concave return profile → participates more in downside than upside
→ PM heuristic: for UHNW clients focused on capital preservation, prioritize managers with capture ratio > 1.2 AND downside capture < 80% — this combination indicates genuine skill in risk management

**Drawdown Analysis:**
Maximum drawdown: largest cumulative peak-to-trough loss during any period
Drawdown duration: total time from peak to recovery (drawdown phase + recovery phase)
→ Asymmetry reality: a 50% loss requires 100% gain to recover. A 20% loss requires 25% gain.
→ COVID-19 example: S&P 500 max drawdown of ~19.6% (Jan-Mar 2020), drawdown duration ~7 months (Jan-Jul 2020)
→ PM heuristic: when evaluating managers, drawdown duration matters MORE than drawdown magnitude for client suitability — a UHNW client in decumulation cannot wait 5 years for recovery
Links to: Section 10 (private wealth), Section 4 (behavioral biases)

### 18.7 Benchmark Selection

**Seven Types of Asset-Based Benchmarks:**
1. Absolute return benchmarks (e.g., T-bills + 300bps)
2. Broad market indexes (e.g., S&P 500, Bloomberg Agg)
3. Style indexes (e.g., Russell 1000 Value)
4. Factor-model-based benchmarks (custom factor loadings)
5. Returns-based (Sharpe style analysis) benchmarks
6. Manager peer groups (manager universe median)
7. Custom security-based (strategy) benchmarks

**Seven Properties of a Valid Benchmark (Bailey, Richards, Tierney):**
1. **Unambiguous:** securities and weights clearly identifiable
2. **Investable:** can be replicated (at least gross of expenses)
3. **Measurable:** return calculable on reasonably frequent basis
4. **Appropriate:** consistent with manager's style and expertise
5. **Reflective of current investment opinions:** manager familiar with constituents
6. **Specified in advance:** not created after the fact
7. **Accountable:** manager accepts ownership

→ PM heuristic: peer group benchmarks FAIL multiple properties — not investable, not specified in advance, subject to survivorship bias, incentivize herding. Use peer groups only as secondary reference, NEVER as primary benchmark.

**Benchmark Misspecification Chain:**
Wrong benchmark selected → attribution analysis measures wrong things → allocation effect captures style differences (not skill) → appraisal ratios are meaningless → manager evaluation is flawed → sponsor makes wrong hiring/firing decisions → suboptimal outcomes for beneficiaries
→ Example: value manager benchmarked against broad market → style return (B − M) gets lumped into measured alpha → manager appears to add value when style is in favor, appears to destroy value when style is out of favor → this is STYLE BIAS, not manager skill

**Normal Portfolio Decomposition:**
P = M + S + A
P = portfolio return, M = market return, S = style return (B − M), A = active management return (P − B)
→ True active return = P − Normal Portfolio → only this measures genuine skill
→ Misfit active return = (P − Investor Benchmark) − True Active Return → measures style mismatch
→ When misfit is large, the investor's benchmark is WRONG

**Alternatives Benchmarking Challenges:**
HFs: no single appropriate benchmark → use risk-free + spread, factor models, or strategy-specific peer universes → all have significant limitations
PE: use IRR (not TWR) → compare vs. PME (public market equivalent) → Long-Nickels PME, Kaplan-Schoar PME → choice of PME methodology matters
RE: appraisal-based indexes → smoothed, lagged → unsmooth before comparing to liquid assets
Commodities: futures-based indexes → weighting schemes vary dramatically across providers
→ Caveat: all alternatives benchmarks have material limitations. Stale pricing, survivorship bias, and lack of investability make precise performance evaluation extremely difficult.
Links to: Section 14 (alternatives), Section 13.6 (equity benchmark selection)

### 18.8 Manager Selection Framework

**Type I and Type II Errors in Manager Selection:**
Type I error (false positive): hire a manager without skill → COSTLY — fees paid for no alpha, underperformance
Type II error (false negative): fail to hire a skilled manager → OPPORTUNITY COST — miss alpha
→ PM heuristic: Type I errors are more damaging because they involve actual fee payments and potential underperformance. When in doubt, DON'T hire — the opportunity cost of Type II errors is lower than the direct cost of Type I errors.

**Manager Due Diligence Framework:**
1. Investment philosophy: is it coherent, sustainable, and differentiated?
2. Investment personnel: experience, stability, depth of team, succession planning
3. Investment process: signal creation → signal capture → portfolio construction → monitoring
4. Operational due diligence: AUM, firm structure, compliance, risk management, technology
5. Terms: fees (AUM-based vs. performance), lockups, redemption terms, alignment of interests
6. Behavioral biases in investment teams: overconfidence, anchoring, groupthink, status quo bias

**Fee Structures:**
AUM-based: aligns with asset growth, not performance → creates incentive to gather assets, not generate alpha
Performance-based: aligns with returns → but creates incentive to take excessive risk, especially with high-water marks
→ PM heuristic: the best fee structure is AUM-based with performance fee that has: (1) hurdle rate tied to benchmark, (2) high-water mark, (3) clawback provision, (4) fee cap → this maximizes alignment while limiting perverse incentives
Links to: Section 16 (ethics), Section 4 (behavioral biases)

Limitation: Performance evaluation is inherently backward-looking. Past attribution results do not guarantee future skill. The paradox of skill means that as average skill increases, luck explains a greater proportion of observed performance differences. Statistical significance of manager alpha typically requires 10-20+ years of data — far longer than most evaluation periods or manager tenures.

---

## SECTION 19: DERIVATIVES & OVERLAY STRATEGIES
*Source: CFA L3 V4 Modules 1-2, pp 1-140. Exhaustive extraction V5.*

### 19.1 Option Strategy Foundations — Position Equivalencies & Synthetics

**Put-Call Parity and Synthetic Positions (pp 7-14):**
Put-call parity: c + PV(X) = p + S → rearranging creates synthetic instruments
Synthetic long forward = long call + short put (same strike, same expiry) → equivalent to being long the underlying at forward price
Synthetic short forward = short call + long put → equivalent to being short underlying at forward price
Synthetic long call = long stock + long put → protective put replicates call payoff
Synthetic long put = short stock + long call → replicates put payoff

Key inference chain: Put-call parity violation detected → arbitrage opportunity → buy cheap side, sell expensive side → but transaction costs and early exercise (American options) may eliminate profit → PM heuristic: apparent arbitrage in options markets is usually explained by dividends, early exercise premium, or transaction costs
Links to: Appendix C (quantitative anchors), Section 12 (fixed income option-adjusted spreads)

**The Greeks — Decision Framework for PMs (pp 12-16):**
Delta (Δ): sensitivity of option price to change in underlying price. Call delta: 0 to +1. Put delta: -1 to 0.
→ PM heuristic: delta approximation breaks down for large moves; use gamma to estimate delta change for big moves
Gamma (Γ): rate of change of delta. Highest for ATM options near expiry → gamma risk is GREATEST when you are short ATM options close to expiry
→ Inference chain: short gamma position + large move in underlying → delta changes rapidly → losses accelerate → requires frequent rebalancing → transaction costs erode returns → this is WHY selling ATM options near expiry is dangerous
Vega (ν): sensitivity to implied volatility. Long options = long vega. Short options = short vega.
→ PM heuristic: vega is highest for ATM options and increases with time to expiry; for a 1% change in implied vol, a vega of 0.114 means the straddle value changes by $0.114
Theta (θ): time decay per day. Long options have negative theta (lose value daily). Short options have positive theta.
→ Critical tradeoff: long gamma = negative theta (you pay for convexity through time decay). Short gamma = positive theta (you earn time decay but face convexity risk)
→ Inference chain: theta as "rent" for gamma → long straddle buyer pays ~0.075/day in theta → needs realized moves > implied moves to profit → if realized vol < implied vol, theta decay > gamma gains → strategy loses money

Quantitative anchors from Exhibit 22 (p 42): ATM straddle at S=50, X=50: call delta 0.534, put delta -0.465, straddle delta ≈ 0.069 (near zero), straddle gamma 0.139, straddle vega 0.114, straddle theta -0.075/day

### 19.2 Covered Calls & Protective Puts — Income & Insurance

**Covered Call (pp 17-25):**
Structure: long stock + short call
Maximum profit: (X - S₀) + c₀ (strike minus purchase price plus premium received)
Maximum loss: S₀ - c₀ (stock goes to zero, cushioned by premium)
Breakeven: S₀ - c₀
→ PM decision rule: write covered calls when (1) mildly bullish or neutral, (2) need income generation, (3) willing to sell at strike price, (4) want to reduce portfolio delta
→ UHNW application (p 55): Parker case — client needs $30,000 cash, PM writes 50 MNZA 170 calls generating $32,250, reduces delta from +5,000 to +2,480, accepts risk of shares being called away at 170
→ Inference chain: covered call writing for cash needs → reduces portfolio delta → if bearish on stock, this is GOOD (lower delta = less downside exposure) → but if stock rallies past strike, client misses upside → TAX CONSEQUENCE: if shares called away, triggers capital gain → for UHNW clients with low cost basis, this can be devastating
Links to: Section 10 (private wealth, concentrated positions), Section 4 (behavioral biases — endowment effect may prevent selling)

Caveat: Covered call return distribution is NOT equivalent to a bond. The right tail is capped but the left tail extends to near-total loss. The strategy transfers the right tail of the return distribution to the call buyer in exchange for premium income.

**Protective Put (pp 25-30):**
Structure: long stock + long put
Maximum profit: unlimited (minus put premium)
Maximum loss: S₀ - X + p₀ (stock price minus strike plus premium paid)
Breakeven: S₀ + p₀
→ PM decision rule: buy protective puts when (1) long-term bullish but short-term bearish, (2) want to maintain upside exposure, (3) need defined maximum loss, (4) event risk hedging (earnings, elections, regulatory decisions)
→ Key insight (p 67-68): McLaire case — hedge fund manager buys 100,000 puts at €42.50 strike for €1.72 each (€172,000 total) to protect against earnings miss. After positive earnings surprise (+5% stock move), puts lose 62% of value but stock gains €210,000 → sell puts to recover €66,000 → net cost of insurance: €106,000
→ PM heuristic for strike selection: compare profit-per-unit-of-premium across strikes. ATM puts are expensive but offer dollar-for-dollar protection. OTM puts are cheaper but only protect below strike.
Quantitative anchor: Put strike 42.5 offers profit/premium ratio of 1.73 vs 45.0 strike ratio of 1.08 → the slightly OTM put is more capital-efficient for the expected drawdown scenario

Limitation: Continuous protective put buying is VERY expensive and will significantly drag portfolio returns. The occasional purchase for event-driven hedging is more practical than systematic insurance programs.

### 19.3 Spread Strategies — Directional Bets with Defined Risk

**Bull Call Spread (pp 32-40):**
Structure: long call at X_L + short call at X_H (same underlying, same expiry)
Net cost: c_L - c_H (debit spread — costs money to establish)
Maximum profit: (X_H - X_L) - (c_L - c_H) = spread width minus net premium paid
Maximum loss: c_L - c_H = net premium paid
Breakeven: X_L + (c_L - c_H)
→ PM decision rule: use when moderately bullish, want defined max loss, and want to reduce cost vs. outright call purchase
→ Alternative strike selection tradeoff (pp 33-34): PBR 15/17 bull call spread (max profit 0.87, max loss 1.13, BE 16.13) vs PBR 16/17 (max profit 0.54, max loss 0.46, BE 16.46). Wider spread = higher max profit but higher max loss and lower breakeven. Narrower spread = lower risk, lower reward, higher breakeven.
→ Greek analysis of spreads: spread delta = long call delta - short call delta. PBR 15/17 spread delta = 0.306, PBR 16/17 spread delta = 0.165 → wider spread is more sensitive to price changes

**Bear Put Spread (pp 35-36):**
Structure: long put at X_H + short put at X_L (debit spread)
Maximum profit: (X_H - X_L) - (p_H - p_L) = spread width minus net premium
Maximum loss: p_H - p_L = net premium paid
Breakeven: X_H - (p_H - p_L)
→ PM decision rule: use when moderately bearish. Vol skew makes this cheaper: OTM puts (which you're selling) have higher implied vol → you sell expensive vol, buy cheaper vol

**Spread Refinement — Dynamic Management (pp 36-39):**
→ Advanced tactic: Aguila case — bought long call at 40 for 1.50, stock rises to 48, writes 50-strike call for 1.91 → converts to bull spread with minimum profit of 0.41 (locked in) and maximum profit of 10.41
→ Key insight: you can ADD a short leg to an existing profitable long position to lock in gains → converts unlimited upside to defined range, but guarantees minimum profit
→ Delta management: adjust spread dynamically by closing short leg when price drops (increasing delta) and re-establishing when price rises (decreasing delta) → Caveat: this dynamic management is "exceedingly difficult to achieve" in practice (p 39)

### 19.4 Straddles & Collars — Volatility Plays & Risk Reduction

**Long Straddle (pp 40-42):**
Structure: long call + long put (same strike, same expiry)
Maximum profit: unlimited (either direction)
Maximum loss: c₀ + p₀ (total premium paid — occurs if stock = strike at expiry)
Breakevens: X - (c₀ + p₀) [lower] and X + (c₀ + p₀) [upper]
→ This is a PURE VOLATILITY BET: you are long gamma and long vega, paying theta
→ Quantitative anchor: ATM straddle at S=50 costs 4.57 → needs 9.14% move (50 ± 4.57) to break even → PM must believe realized vol will exceed the ~9% breakeven threshold
→ Hamlet case (pp 59-60): planned straddle at 14.14 cost, but news leak increases IV from 30% to 45% → straddle cost jumps to 21.09 → breakevens widen from ±8.4% to ±12.4% → Hamlet abandons trade
→ Inference chain: anticipated vol event → IV rises BEFORE the event (market prices it in) → straddle becomes more expensive → you need even larger moves to profit → the window to buy straddles is BEFORE the market prices in the volatility event → once IV rises, the straddle buyer is paying for vol that's already priced in
Links to: Section 15 (regime changes), Appendix D (volatility risk premium)

**Short Straddle:** inverts all of the above — you earn premium, short gamma, short vega, positive theta. Maximum gain = premium collected. Unlimited potential loss. Used when you expect low realized volatility and stable markets.

**Collar (pp 43-46):**
Structure: long stock + long put (at X₁) + short call (at X₂), where X₁ < S₀ < X₂
Zero-cost collar: put premium paid ≈ call premium received
Maximum profit: X₂ - S₀ + c₀ - p₀ (capped at call strike)
Maximum loss: S₀ - X₁ + p₀ - c₀ (floored at put strike)
→ Economically, the collar position is INTERMEDIATE between pure equity and fixed income. As strikes converge → approaches risk-free rate. As strikes widen → approaches equity-like return.
→ Portfolio delta approaches zero as stock price moves outside the strike range in either direction (delta of long stock +1, offset by put delta approaching -1 or call delta approaching +1)
→ UHNW application (pp 61, 111-112): Steinbacher case — client holds 100,000 Tundra shares at €14, low tax basis, doesn't want to sell → OTC collar (buy put, sell call) protects downside without triggering taxable sale → ALTERNATIVE: equity swap for same economics without options
→ Inference chain: concentrated stock position → low cost basis → selling triggers massive capital gain → collar preserves exposure range without sale → BUT collar has limited upside → trade-off between tax deferral and return cap → for UHNW clients, the tax savings from deferral often exceed the opportunity cost of capped upside
Links to: Section 10 (concentrated positions, monetization strategies)

### 19.5 Calendar Spreads & Volatility Surface

**Calendar Spread (pp 46-48):**
Long calendar spread: buy longer-dated option + sell shorter-dated option (same strike, same type)
→ Net theta positive (short-term option decays faster), net vega positive (longer option more sensitive to IV changes)
→ PM decision rule: use when (1) near-term neutral but longer-term directional, (2) expect IV to increase, (3) stock expected to remain near strike until short option expires
→ Maximum profit: when short option expires worthless at the strike, and long option retains maximum time value
→ Key risk: stock moves too far from strike → both options lose time value rapidly → position loses money
→ Short calendar spread: opposite — buy near-term, sell longer-dated. Profits from big moves or declining IV.
→ Quantitative anchor (pp 46-47): XYZ at 45, buy JAN 45 call at 3.81, sell SEP 45 call at 1.55 → cost 2.26. SEP expires worthless, JAN call = 3.48 → profit = 1.22

**Implied Volatility Surface (pp 48-52):**
Implied volatility varies by: (1) moneyness (strike relative to spot), (2) time to expiry → forms a 3D surface
Volatility skew: OTM puts have higher IV than ATM or OTM calls → persistent across asset classes since 1987 crash
→ Cause: demand for portfolio insurance (OTM puts) exceeds supply → market makers raise put IV
Volatility smile: both OTM puts AND OTM calls trade at premium IV to ATM → U-shaped curve (less common in equities, more in FX)
Volatility term structure: normally in contango (longer-dated options have higher IV). In stress: inverts (near-term IV spikes above long-term)
→ PM signal: sharp increase in skew + surge in absolute IV level = market turning bearish → Exhibit 30 (p 51): S&P 500 90%-110% skew ranged 3-19 vol points from 2005-2017, with spike to 19 during financial crisis
→ Risk reversal strategy: sell OTM put + buy OTM call (or vice versa), delta-hedged → profits from changes in RELATIVE implied volatility between puts and calls, not from absolute IV level
Quantitative anchors (Exhibit 29, p 50): Nikkei 225 skew 6.5 vol pts, S&P 500 skew 8.3, Euro Stoxx 50 skew 8.5, DAX skew 9.0

### 19.6 Strategy Selection Matrix — The Master Decision Framework

**Direction × Volatility Matrix (Exhibit 32, p 53):**

| | Bearish | Neutral/Range | Bullish |
|---|---|---|---|
| IV ↓ expected | Write calls | Write straddle | Write puts |
| IV unchanged | Write calls + buy puts | Calendar spread | Buy calls + write puts |
| IV ↑ expected | Buy puts | Buy straddle | Buy calls |

→ PM decision rule: ALWAYS specify BOTH your directional view AND your volatility view before selecting a strategy
→ Common PM errors: (1) buying calls when IV is elevated (paying too much for vol), (2) selling straddles before known events (selling vol cheap), (3) ignoring theta cost of long positions
→ Inference chain: bullish + expect IV decrease → write puts (collect expensive premium that will decay as IV falls AND stock rises, making puts worthless) → this is BETTER than buying calls in declining IV environment because long calls lose vega value even if direction is correct

### 19.7 Swaps for Risk Management — Interest Rate, Currency, Equity

**Interest Rate Swaps (pp 84-88):**
Pay-fixed, receive-floating swap: negative duration (benefits from rising rates)
Receive-fixed, pay-floating swap: positive duration (benefits from falling rates)
→ Swap duration: MDUR_S = duration of floating-rate bond - duration of fixed-rate bond
→ For pay-fixed: MDUR_S is negative (floating duration ≈ 0, minus positive fixed duration)
→ For receive-fixed: MDUR_S is positive

Target duration formula: N_S = [(MDUR_T - MDUR_P) / MDUR_S] × MV_P
→ Quantitative anchor (Example 2, pp 87-88): €50M portfolio, MDUR_P = 5.5, target MDUR_T = 4.5, swap MDUR_S = -2.00 → N_S = [(4.5-5.5)/(-2.00)] × €50M = €25M notional swap
→ PM heuristic: to REDUCE duration → enter pay-fixed swap (negative duration offsets portfolio's positive duration). To INCREASE duration → enter receive-fixed swap.

Basis risk warning: hedging instrument and portfolio are imperfect substitutes → residual spread risk remains even after interest rate hedging. Corporate bond portfolio hedged with interest rate swap still has credit spread exposure.
Links to: Section 12 (fixed income dynamics, duration management)

**Fixed-Income Futures Hedging (pp 90-95):**
Bond futures duration reflects cheapest-to-deliver (CTD) bond characteristics
CTD determination: low coupon + long maturity + above-notional-yield → most likely CTD
BPV of portfolio: BPV_P = MDUR_P × 0.01% × MV_P
BPV of futures: BPV_F = BPV_CTD / CF (conversion factor)
Basis point value hedge ratio: BPVHR = [(BPV_T - BPV_P) / BPV_CTD] × CF

→ Quantitative anchor (Example 5, pp 93-94): €50M portfolio, MDUR 9.50, MV €49.531M → BPV_P = €47,054.45. CTD bond MDUR 8.623, price 98.14, CF 0.619489 → BPV_CTD = €84.63. Full hedge (BPV_T = 0): BPVHR = -€47,054.45/€84.63 × 0.619489 = -344 contracts
→ Partial hedge to MDUR 8.50: BPV_T = €42,101.35 → BPVHR = (€42,101.35 - €47,054.50)/€84.63 × 0.619489 = -36 contracts

Hedging imperfections: (1) CTD bond can change, (2) convexity not captured by duration alone, (3) non-parallel yield curve shifts, (4) basis risk between futures and portfolio

**Currency Exposure Management (pp 95-99):**
Cross-currency basis swap: exchange notional principals + periodic interest payments in two currencies
→ Basis is quoted on non-USD leg: negative basis = strong USD demand → borrowing USD costs premium
→ PM application: earn synthetic domestic yield by investing in foreign bonds and hedging with currency swap → if basis is favorable, synthetic yield > domestic yield
Currency forwards/futures: used to lock in exchange rates for known future cash flows
→ Quantitative anchor: CAD50M exposure hedged with 500 CAD futures contracts (CAD100,000 each) at USD/CAD 0.7838

**Equity Swaps & Futures (pp 99-105):**
Three types: (1) receive-equity, pay-fixed; (2) receive-equity, pay-floating; (3) receive-equity, pay-another-equity
Total return swap: includes dividends → provides synthetic equity exposure without physical ownership
→ UHNW application (p 102): concentrated stock holder enters total return swap → pays total return on concentrated stock → receives floating rate → economically "sells" position without triggering tax event → maintains voting rights → this is the SWAP ALTERNATIVE to a collar
→ Quantitative anchor (Example 9, pp 100-101): $100M portfolio, 30% hedge via equity swap. Bull scenario (+5%): portfolio +$5M, swap -$1.2M = net +$3.8M. Bear scenario (-5%): portfolio -$5M, swap +$1.8M = net -$3.2M → effectively creates 70% equity / 30% cash portfolio

Equity futures for beta adjustment: N_f = [(β_T - β_S) / β_f] × (S/F)
→ β_T > β_S → buy futures (positive N_f). β_T < β_S → sell futures (negative N_f). β_T = 0 → full hedge: N_f = -(β_S/β_f)(S/F)
→ Quantitative anchor (Example 10, p 104): £40M portfolio, β_S = 0.85, target β_T = 1.10, FTSE 100 futures at £73,000, β_f = 1.00 → N_f = (1.10-0.85)/1.00 × £40M/£73,000 = 137 contracts. Verification: market +2% → portfolio return 2.2% → effective beta = 2.2%/2.0% = 1.10 ✓

**Cash Equitization (p 105):**
Problem: cash drag in equity portfolio reduces tracking to benchmark
Solution: buy equity index futures on cash holdings → N_f = (β_T/β_f)(S/F) where β_S = 0 (cash has zero beta)
→ Quantitative anchor (Example 11): JPY140M cash, Nikkei 225 futures at JPY23M per contract → 140/23 ≈ 6 contracts
→ PM heuristic: ANY portfolio with unintended cash holdings should consider cash equitization to minimize tracking error
Links to: Section 11 (portfolio construction, tracking error)

### 19.8 Volatility Derivatives — VIX & Variance Swaps

**VIX Futures (pp 105-108):**
VIX: CBOE Volatility Index, measures 30-day expected S&P 500 implied volatility
→ VIX futures term structure shapes: contango (normal — upward sloping, longer-dated > spot), flat (vol expected stable), backwardation (stress — near-term > long-term, fear of imminent sell-off)
→ Quantitative anchor (Exhibit 2-3, p 107): Day 1 (flat): Apr 16.68, May-Sep 17.00-17.80. Day 60 (backwardation): Apr 33.46, declining to Sep 18.90. Day 120 (contango): Apr 9.77, rising to Sep 16.10
→ Roll cost: in contango, long VIX futures lose value as they converge to lower spot → negative carry. In backwardation, long futures gain from convergence → positive carry.
→ Critical insight: VIX ETPs suffer from PERSISTENT contango roll cost → long-term VIX ETP holdings systematically destroy value → only suitable for SHORT-TERM tactical hedging
→ VIX options: European style, priced off VIX futures (not spot VIX). Buy VIX calls to hedge equity sell-offs. Buy VIX puts to profit from stable markets.
→ PM heuristic for tail risk hedging (pp 64-66): Wu case — buy 15.60-strike VIX call (2.00) + sell 14.75-strike VIX put (1.55) → net cost 0.45 per contract → creates asymmetric exposure: profits from vol spike above 16.05, loses from vol decline below 14.75 → this is CHEAPER than buying VIX calls outright

**Variance Swaps (pp 108-111):**
Settlement = Variance notional × (Realized variance - Variance strike)
Realized variance = 252 × [Σ(R_i²)/(N-1)], where R_i = ln(P_{i+1}/P_i)
Vega notional vs variance notional: Variance notional = Vega notional / (2 × Strike)
→ CRITICAL FEATURE: variance swap payoff is CONVEX in volatility → long variance position gains MORE from vol increase than it loses from equivalent vol decrease → this convexity makes variance swaps superior tail-risk hedges compared to at-the-money options
→ Variance strike typically ≈ implied vol of 90% moneyness put → approximately the implied vol at the point of the vol skew
→ Mark-to-market formula (Eq 16, p 109): VarSwap_t = Variance notional × PV_t(T) × {(t/T)[RealizedVol(0,t)]² + (T-t)/T × [ImpliedVol(t,T)]² - Strike²}
→ Key insight: sensitivity to implied vol DIMINISHES over time (as t approaches T, the realized portion dominates)

Quantitative anchor (Example 12, pp 110-111): Olivia Santos sells $50,000 vega notional, strike 20% → Variance notional = $50,000/(2×20) = 1,250. After 6 months: realized vol 16%, new 6-month fair strike 19%, rate 2.5%. MTM = 1,250 × 0.987654 × {(6/12)×256 + (6/12)×361 - 400} = -$112,963 → positive for Santos (she's short). Settlement if realized vol = 18%: 1,250 × (324-400) = -$95,000 → Santos receives $95,000

### 19.9 Derivatives in Asset Allocation & Rebalancing

**Tactical Asset Allocation with Futures (pp 113-117):**
→ Use equity index futures and bond futures simultaneously to shift allocation without physical trading
→ Rossi case (pp 114-116): €500M portfolio, shift from 70/30 stocks/bonds to 60/40
  - Sell 952 FTSE MIB futures (reduce Italian stocks €100M, β target = 0)
  - Buy 138 DAX futures (add German stocks €50M, β target = 0.9)
  - Buy 297 Euro-BTP futures (add Italian bonds €50M, target BPV = €32,250)
→ Advantages over physical rebalancing: (1) lower transaction costs, (2) faster execution, (3) no market impact on underlying, (4) easily reversible, (5) no settlement delays
→ Caveat: futures positions require margin management, roll costs, and imperfect tracking

**Rebalancing with Futures (pp 116-117):**
→ Grant case: portfolio drifts from 40/60 target to 43.4/56.6 after market appreciation → sell 124 EURO STOXX 50 futures + buy 25 Euro-Bund futures → restores target without selling stocks or buying bonds

**Asset Allocation with Swaps (pp 117-119):**
→ TMM case: shift from 75% stocks / 25% bonds to 90% stocks / 10% bonds using equity + FI swaps
→ Equity swap 1: receive S&P 500 total return, pay MRR - spread, $27M notional (adds large-cap exposure)
→ Equity swap 2: receive SPSC total return, pay MRR - spread, $3M notional (adds small-cap exposure)
→ FI swap 1: pay BBT total return, receive MRR - spread, $25M notional (reduces gov bond exposure)
→ FI swap 2: pay BAMLC total return, receive MRR - spread, $5M notional (reduces corp bond exposure)
→ Caveat: cash flow mismatch risk — if equity receipts < FI payments, portfolio faces liquidity shortfall

### 19.10 Inferring Market Expectations from Derivatives

**Fed Funds Futures (pp 119-122):**
Fed funds futures price = 100 - Expected FFE rate
Probability of rate hike = (Implied FFE rate - Current rate) / (New rate - Current rate)
→ Quantitative anchor (p 121): futures at 97.90 → implied FFE = 2.10%. Current target midpoint 1.875%, new midpoint 2.125%. P(hike) = (2.10-1.875)/(2.125-1.875) = 90%
→ Caveat: end-of-month dips in FFE rate create bias → data providers smooth using forward rate structures
→ For longer horizons, implied probabilities have WEAK predictive power → market-implied path often diverges significantly from actual outcomes

**Inflation expectations:** CPI (inflation) swaps → fixed rate exchanged for realized CPI → the fixed rate = market's inflation expectation
**Volatility expectations:** VIX futures curve → term structure shape signals near vs. long-term vol expectations

Links to: Section 15 (global economics, central bank policy), Section 12 (yield curve analysis)

### 19.11 Cross-Domain Inference Chains — Derivatives

1. Client needs cash but loves stock → covered call generates income + reduces delta → if called away, triggers capital gain → UHNW tax cost may exceed income benefit → alternative: total return equity swap (pays stock return, receives cash flow) avoids sale → swap has credit risk to counterparty → collar provides similar economics but is options-based → choose based on tax situation, counterparty exposure, and desired payoff asymmetry
Links to: Section 10 (concentrated positions), Section 16 (ethics of suitability)

2. PM expects market correction + vol spike → buy VIX calls + sell VIX puts (risk reversal on VIX) → cost = 0.45/contract → BUT VIX call pricing depends on VIX futures (not spot) → contango means calls may not respond 1:1 to spot VIX spike → ALTERNATIVE: long variance swap (convex payoff, direct exposure to realized vol) → but variance swaps are OTC (counterparty risk, illiquid) → choice depends on: desired convexity, counterparty tolerance, liquidity needs, roll cost tolerance
Links to: Section 14 (hedge fund strategies), Appendix D (volatility risk premium)

3. Portfolio drifts from target allocation → physical rebalancing triggers transaction costs + taxes → futures overlay: sell equity index futures + buy bond futures → maintains target beta and duration without touching underlying → need to manage: margin requirements, roll dates, basis risk, cash equitization of excess cash from futures margin
Links to: Section 11 (portfolio construction), Section 17 (institutional PM)

4. Bearish view + expect IV to decline → Exhibit 32 says "write calls" → but writing naked calls has unlimited risk → PM adds protective long call at higher strike (creating bear call spread) → defined risk, defined reward → spread delta < naked call delta → more conservative expression of same view → if wrong about direction, max loss = net premium received (credit spread) → this LAYERED approach is how PMs transform textbook recommendations into risk-appropriate trades

5. Interest rate risk management chain: Rising rate fear → enter pay-fixed IRS (negative duration added) → OR sell bond futures (BPVHR calculation determines quantity) → swaps: customizable, OTC, counterparty risk, no margin calls. Futures: standardized, exchange-traded, margined daily, basis risk from CTD bond. PM choice depends on: hedge horizon (swaps for longer), portfolio composition (futures for treasury-like portfolios), operational preferences
Links to: Section 12 (fixed income dynamics)

Limitation: All derivative strategies assume continuous market access, adequate counterparty credit, and reasonable bid-ask spreads. In stress scenarios (2008, 2020), options spreads widen dramatically, counterparty risk increases, and margin calls can force premature liquidation. The formulas in this section assume no frictions, parallel yield curve shifts, and constant Greeks — all assumptions that break down during the exact scenarios where hedging is most needed.

### 19.12 Practice Problem Decision Rules & Common Errors

**BPVHR Calculation — Three Common Errors (pp 132-133):**
Correct formula: BPVHR = (BPV_T - BPV_P) / BPV_CTD × CF
Error 1: Using CTD bond PRICE instead of BPV_CTD in denominator → understates contracts needed (650 vs correct 743)
Error 2: Omitting the conversion factor (CF) → overstates contracts needed (1,026 vs correct 743)
Error 3: Using modified duration of CTD bond instead of portfolio's target modified duration when computing BPV_T → wrong target BPV
→ PM heuristic: ALWAYS verify three components: (1) BPV_T uses TARGET duration × portfolio MV, (2) denominator uses BPV_CTD (not price), (3) multiply by CF. Missing any one produces dramatically wrong hedge ratios.

**Cash Equitization Formula & Common Errors (pp 133):**
Correct: N_f = (β_T / β_f) × (Cash / F_contract_value) where F_contract_value = futures price × multiplier
Error 1: Using total portfolio value instead of excess cash as S → massively overstates contracts (121 vs correct 6)
Error 2: Using quoted futures price instead of contract value (price × multiplier) → massively overstates contracts (1,455 vs correct 6)
→ PM heuristic: cash equitization uses ONLY the cash amount being equitized, NOT the total portfolio value. And ALWAYS compute contract value = price × multiplier.

**Swap Duration Blending Formula (pp 132):**
To increase portfolio modified duration from MDUR_P to MDUR_T using receive-fixed swap:
MV_P × MDUR_P + N_S × MDUR_S = MV_P × MDUR_T
Solving: MDUR_S = [(MDUR_T - MDUR_P) × MV_P] / N_S
Quantitative anchor: €30M bonds at MDUR 3, add €20M receive-fixed swap → MDUR_S required = [(5-3)×30M]/20M = 3.0

**Swap Notional for Duration Targeting (pp 135-136):**
N_S = [(MDUR_T - MDUR_P) / MDUR_S] × MV_P
Quantitative anchor: Target MDUR 5.0, current MDUR 7.8, swap MDUR -2.4848 for pay-fixed → N_S = [(5.0-7.8)/-2.4848] × €90.1M = €101.5M notional
→ PM heuristic: pay-fixed swap has NEGATIVE modified duration → adding it REDUCES portfolio duration. The notional required often EXCEEDS the portfolio market value — this is normal and expected.

**Variance Swap Mark-to-Market (pp 134):**
VarSwap_t = Variance Notional × PV_t(T) × {(t/T) × [RealizedVol(0,t)]² + [(T-t)/T] × [ImpliedVol(t,T)]² - Strike²}
Where: Variance Notional = Vega Notional / (2 × Strike)
Common errors: (1) forgetting to PV the result, (2) switching realized and implied vol² terms
Quantitative anchor: $1M vega notional, strike 15 (var strike 225), realized vol 20%, implied vol 18% at t=5, T=12 → Var Notional = $33,333.33, gain = $4,317,775

**Bond Basis Arbitrage Rules (pp 135):**
Positive basis (bond price > futures-implied price) → SELL the basis: sell bond, buy futures
Negative basis (bond price < futures-implied price) → BUY the basis: buy bond, sell futures
→ PM trap: "selling the basis" means selling the BOND (not selling futures). Students confuse the direction.

**Hedging Instrument Selection by Maturity (pp 135):**
10-year Treasury bond exposure → use longer-dated bond futures (NOT Eurodollar futures, NOT receive-fixed swap)
Short-term exposure → interest rate futures or Eurodollar strips work
→ Key insight: receive-fixed IRS LOSES value when rates rise → it moves WITH the bond, not AGAINST it → cannot hedge a long bond position. Must use pay-fixed IRS or sell bond futures.

**Interest Rate Futures Hedging for Future Borrowing (pp 135):**
Lock in borrowing rate: sell IR futures contracts at 98.05 (implied rate 1.95%)
At borrowing time: loan at 2.70%, unwind futures at 97.30, gain = 98.05 - 97.30 = 75 bps
Effective rate = 2.70% - 0.75% = 1.95%
→ PM heuristic: the effective locked-in rate equals the implied rate at hedge inception, regardless of where rates actually move (assuming perfect hedge)

**Rebalancing with Simultaneous Equity + Bond Futures (pp 136):**
When reducing equity allocation: sell equity index futures (β_T = 0, creating "synthetic cash")
Simultaneously: buy bond futures to add duration exposure to the freed-up notional
The cash from equity futures reduction becomes the BPV_P = 0 starting point for bond futures calculation
→ This two-step approach is the standard institutional method for tactical allocation shifts without touching underlying holdings

**VIX Carry Roll-Down Trade (pp 138):**
When VIX term structure is in contango (front < second month):
Buy VIX front-month futures + sell VIX second-month futures
As front-month approaches expiry, it converges to spot VIX (which is lower than futures)
Second-month rolls down to become new front-month → spread compresses → profit
→ Cannot invest in spot VIX directly — must use futures or options
→ PM caveat: this trade is a bet on STABLE or DECLINING vol. If vol spikes, contango inverts to backwardation and the trade loses significantly.

**Concentrated Stock Monetization via Equity Swap (pp 138):**
CEO owns 100M shares at €30 → wants to diversify 10% → equity swap on 10M shares (€300M notional)
CEO pays equity total return on 10M shares, receives floating rate (MRR)
Effect: economically converts 10% of position to floating-rate exposure WITHOUT selling shares
→ UHNW advantage: no capital gains trigger, retains voting rights, maintains appearance of ownership
→ Risk: counterparty credit exposure, potential margin calls if stock rallies (CEO owes equity upside)
Links to: Section 10 (concentrated positions), Section 16 (disclosure obligations under CFA Standards)

**Cross-Currency Basis Swap Key Rules (pp 137):**
Principals exchanged at BOTH inception AND maturity (unlike plain IRS)
If demand for borrowing in currency A is strong → positive basis for lending currency A → the lender of currency A earns extra spread
Quantitative anchor: US investor in Italian bonds, strong USD demand → positive basis → investor's USD interest receipts increase via basis spread

**Multi-Asset Allocation Shift via Swaps (pp 138-139):**
To shift from 1/3 stocks + 2/3 bonds to 50/50 with sub-allocation changes:
(1) Receive domestic equity return on $2M notional (adds domestic stock)
(2) Receive foreign equity return on $3M notional (adds foreign stock)
(3) Pay corporate bond return on $5M notional (removes bonds)
MRR flows net to zero across all swaps → clean synthetic rebalancing
→ PM heuristic: the KEY to multi-swap allocation is ensuring all floating legs reference the same rate and all payment dates align → MRR inflows and outflows cancel

Links to: Section 11 (portfolio construction), Section 17 (institutional PM), Appendix C (quantitative anchors)

---

## SECTION 20: CURRENCY MANAGEMENT & FX OVERLAY
*Source: CFA L3 V4 Module 3, pp 141-237. Exhaustive extraction V6.*

### 20.1 Currency Risk Decomposition & Return Formula

**Domestic-Currency Return Formula (p 149):**
R_DC = (1 + R_FC)(1 + R_FX) - 1
Where: R_DC = domestic-currency return, R_FC = foreign-currency asset return, R_FX = percentage change of foreign currency against domestic currency
→ The relationship is MULTIPLICATIVE, not additive. The cross-product term (R_FC × R_FX) is usually small but NOT zero.
→ PM trap: students often use R_DC ≈ R_FC + R_FX (the approximation). This works for small returns but creates material errors for large moves.

**Multi-Currency Portfolio Return (p 152):**
R_DC = Σ ωᵢ[(1 + R_FC,i)(1 + R_FX,i)] - 1
Can be decomposed: R_DC = Σ ωᵢ[R_FC,i + R_FX,i + (R_FC,i × R_FX,i)]
= weighted asset return + weighted currency return + weighted cross-product
Quantitative anchor: Bhatt portfolio — 50% US (10% asset, 0% FX) + 25% EUR (5% asset, 2% FX) + 25% JPN (-3% asset, 4% FX) → weighted asset return = 5.5%, weighted currency return = 1.5%, cross-product = -0.005% → total R_DC = 7.0%, currency contribution = 1.5%

**Domestic-Currency Risk (p 153):**
σ²(R_DC) ≈ σ²(R_FC) + σ²(R_FX) + 2σ(R_FC)σ(R_FX)ρ(R_FC, R_FX)
→ Key insight: correlation between asset returns and currency returns MATTERS. Higher ρ(R_FC, R_FX) → higher σ(R_DC) → MORE reason to hedge
→ PM decision rule: if ρ(R_FC, R_FX) is high and positive, currency risk AMPLIFIES asset risk → hedge aggressively. If ρ is low or negative, currency provides natural diversification → less urgent to hedge.
→ Caveat: an increase in correlation from 0.50 to 0.80 increases the VARIANCE (risk) of R_DC but does NOT change the LEVEL (expected return) of R_DC. Students confuse these.

### 20.2 Strategic Currency Management Decisions

**Factors Favoring NOT Hedging (pp 155-158):**
1. Long time horizon → currency effects tend to mean-revert over decades
2. Low hedging costs expectation → but hedging is NEVER free
3. Belief that currencies provide diversification benefit (low ρ)
4. Cost of carry trade works in your favor (selling the high-yield currency costs negative roll yield)
→ Key research finding (Campbell 2010): optimal hedge ratio for international BOND portfolios is almost always close to 100%. For single-country foreign EQUITY portfolios, optimal hedge ratios vary widely (50-100%+) depending on both investor's domestic currency and currency of foreign investment.

**Factors Favoring Hedging (pp 156-158):**
1. Short time horizon → currency noise dominates short-term returns
2. High risk aversion → DB pension plan vs. endowment (different hedge ratios)
3. Significant FX exposure relative to portfolio → the larger the foreign allocation, the more currency risk matters
4. High correlation between asset returns and FX → amplifying effect
5. Regulatory or IPS constraints requiring specific hedge ratios
6. Fixed-income heavy portfolio → bonds have lower vol than equities, so currency vol dominates more → hedge ratios for FI portfolios should be HIGHER than for equity portfolios

**Currency Management Spectrum (pp 158-162):**
Passive hedging → Discretionary hedging → Active currency management → Currency overlay
- Passive: rules-based, 100% hedge, no manager discretion → lowest cost, eliminates currency risk but also eliminates any currency return
- Discretionary: manager can deviate ±X% from neutral hedge ratio (e.g., 97-103%) → primary goal = risk management, secondary goal = modest alpha
- Active currency management: FX as profit center, significant deviation allowed → primary goal = alpha generation
- Currency overlay: separate specialist manager handles FX → can be independent "FX as asset class" program
→ PM decision framework: IPS band width signals which approach. ±3% band = discretionary. ±25% band = active. Unlimited = overlay as asset class.

### 20.3 Active Currency Strategies — Four Approaches

**1. Economic Fundamentals (pp 164-165):**
Estimate "fair value" of currency based on PPP, balance of payments, monetary policy differentials → trade toward equilibrium
→ Assumes spot rates converge to fundamental value over time
→ Limitation: works better over LONG horizons; can be very wrong short-term

**2. Technical Analysis (pp 165-166):**
Historical price patterns repeat → support/resistance levels, moving averages, trend-following
→ Key principle: technicians believe current prices already reflect all fundamental information, so analyzing fundamentals is unnecessary
→ Limitation: pattern recognition is subjective, prone to data-mining bias

**3. Carry Trade (pp 166-169):**
Borrow in low-yield currency, invest in high-yield currency
Equivalent to: trading the forward rate bias (buying forward discount currencies, selling forward premium currencies)
Equivalent to: earning positive roll yield
→ Forward rate bias: the forward rate is NOT the center of the distribution of future spot rates → currencies trading at forward discounts tend to appreciate less than implied by the forward rate (or depreciate less than expected)
→ Carry trade = positive expected return most of the time, BUT subject to occasional catastrophic losses → negatively skewed return distribution
→ PM heuristic: carry trade Sharpe ratios look attractive during calm periods but collapse during crises (2008, 2015 CHF). The strategy is essentially selling tail risk insurance.
→ Higher volatility in spot rate is WORSE for carry trades — it increases the probability of the sudden unwind that generates large losses

**4. Volatility Trading (pp 169-171):**
Trade views on the DISTRIBUTION of future FX rates, not their LEVEL
Long straddle/strangle: expects vol increase → profit from large moves in either direction
Short straddle/strangle: expects vol decrease → profit from time decay if FX stays range-bound
→ Speculative vol traders want to be NET SHORT volatility (earn premium, profit if options expire OTM)
→ Hedgers of vol risk want to be NET LONG volatility (buy protection against unexpected moves)
→ Delta hedging: continuously adjust spot/forward positions to neutralize delta, isolating pure vol exposure

### 20.4 Forward Contracts, FX Swaps & Hedge Mechanics

**Why Forwards Dominate FX Hedging (pp 176-178):**
1. Available for virtually every currency pair, settlement date, and amount
2. No margin requirements (unlike futures which require initial + variation margin)
3. OTC forward volume DWARFS exchange-traded futures volume
4. Forwards are more liquid for institutional-size trades
→ Futures advantages: daily settlement (lower credit risk), standardized (no counterparty negotiation), available to retail via smaller contract sizes

**Static vs. Dynamic Hedging (pp 178-179):**
Static hedge: set and forget → hedge ratio drifts as asset values change → accumulates unwanted currency exposure → this is PURE currency risk
Dynamic hedge: rebalance periodically → keeps hedge ratio close to target → but HIGHER transaction costs
→ PM decision rule: higher risk aversion → more frequent rebalancing → more dynamic. Lower risk aversion → less frequent → more static.
→ Higher tolerance for active trading + conviction in market view → allow hedge ratio to deviate more from neutral

**FX Swap Mechanics (pp 179-180):**
FX swap = simultaneous spot + forward transaction (one leg buys, other sells the base currency)
Used to "roll" maturing forward contracts forward
Matched swap: no change in hedge size, no market view → use MID-MARKET spot rate
Mismatched swap: hedge size changes OR manager has directional view → use BID or OFFER side
→ Kwun Tong case: Hedge #1 (matched, no view) → mid-market rate. Hedge #2 (mismatched, view that HKD/EUR spot will decrease) → uses bid side to INCREASE hedge size and profit from expected EUR depreciation

**Roll Yield (pp 180-183):**
Roll yield = |(F_P/B - S_P/B) / S_P/B| — the implicit cost or benefit of rolling a hedge forward
Positive roll yield: buying base currency at forward DISCOUNT or selling at forward PREMIUM → "buy low, sell high"
Negative roll yield: buying at premium or selling at discount → cost of hedging
→ Roll yield ≡ carry trade ≡ forward rate bias — these three concepts are MATHEMATICALLY EQUIVALENT
→ Implementing carry trade = earning positive roll yield = trading the forward rate bias
→ CRITICAL: if the hedge requires selling the low-yield currency and buying the high-yield currency → NEGATIVE carry → negative roll yield → hedge is EXPENSIVE. This creates a cost-benefit tension.

**Hedging Decision Framework (pp 182-184):**
Compare: (1) expected roll yield (cost of hedge) vs. (2) expected currency depreciation (benefit of hedge)
If expected depreciation > expected cost → HEDGE (even risk-neutral manager agrees)
If expected cost > expected depreciation → risk-neutral manager says DON'T hedge, but risk-averse manager may STILL hedge because forecast uncertainty means actual depreciation could exceed expectation
→ Kwun Tong case: HKD/GBP — roll yield cost = -0.099%, expected GBP depreciation = 3.77% → HEDGE (benefit far exceeds cost)
→ Kwun Tong case: HKD/ZAR — roll yield cost = -2.5%, expected ZAR depreciation = 2.11% → cost > benefit → hedge depends on risk aversion and conviction

### 20.5 Currency Option Strategies for Hedging

**Protective Put on Currency (pp 184-185):**
Long base currency in P/B quote + long put on base currency → floor on selling price
If P/B rate falls (base currency depreciates) → exercise put, sell at strike
If P/B rate rises (base currency appreciates) → let put expire, sell at higher market rate → capture upside
→ ATM put = most expensive but provides dollar-for-dollar protection immediately
→ OTM put = cheaper but leaves a gap between spot and strike where portfolio is unhedged

**Risk Reversal (Collar) (pp 189):**
Long OTM put + short OTM call on base currency
→ Buys downside protection (put), finances it by selling upside potential (call)
→ In FX markets: long risk reversal = long call + short put. Short risk reversal = long put + short call.
→ Net cost can be ZERO if strikes chosen so premiums match → zero-cost collar
→ PM trap: "risk reversal" in FX means long call + short put (NOT the equity collar convention). Direction depends on whether hedging a LONG or SHORT base currency position.

**Put Spread (pp 189-190):**
Long OTM put at higher strike + short deeper-OTM put at lower strike
→ Reduces cost vs. outright put, but protection only between the two strikes
→ Below lower strike: portfolio is UNHEDGED again (the short put neutralizes the long put)
→ 1 × 2 put spread: sell 2x notional of deep-OTM put vs. 1x of near-OTM put → can be zero-cost but creates LEVERAGE risk below lower strike

**Seagull Spread (pp 190-191):**
Three-option structure: long ATM put + short OTM put + short OTM call (short seagull)
OR: short ATM option + long OTM put + long OTM call (long seagull)
→ Short seagull: full protection from ATM strike down to short put strike + gives away upside above call strike + downside risk re-emerges below short put strike → cheaper than straight put
→ Long seagull: cheap downside protection (kicks in at OTM put strike) + unlimited upside beyond OTM call strike + financed by selling ATM option
→ PM heuristic: seagulls trade MORE protection/upside features against lower cost by writing TWO options instead of one

**Exotic Options (pp 191-192):**
Knock-in options: vanilla option created only when spot touches a barrier level
Knock-out options: vanilla option ceases to exist when spot touches barrier → CHEAPER than vanilla (more restrictive)
Digital (binary) options: pay fixed amount if ITM at expiry (like a lottery ticket) → used for speculative FX views, rarely for hedging
→ Exotics are used primarily by currency overlay managers and sophisticated speculative accounts, rarely by institutional hedgers or corporate treasuries

### 20.6 Building Block Decision Framework for Currency Hedging

**Step-by-Step Process (p 192):**
(a) Identify base currency in P/B quote
(b) Determine if base currency must be BOUGHT or SOLD to implement hedge
(c) If BUYING base currency → core hedge = long call or long forward → reduce cost by buying OTM call or writing options
(d) If SELLING base currency → core hedge = long put or short forward → reduce cost by buying OTM put or writing options
(e) More discretion + stronger view → more directional tilt superimposed on core hedge
(f) Adjust strikes and notional amounts to move toward zero-cost structure

→ PM master rule: EVERY cost-reduction measure involves trading away EITHER downside protection OR upside potential. There is no free lunch. Lower cost = less protection and/or less upside. The PM must understand and accept these residual risks.

### 20.7 Cross-Hedges, Macro-Hedges & Minimum-Variance Hedge Ratios

**Cross-Hedging Multi-Currency Portfolios (pp 195-196):**
When portfolio has MULTIPLE foreign currencies, residual currency exposures may be CORRELATED → natural hedge exists
→ Example: US investor long AUD and NZD → USD/AUD and USD/NZD highly correlated (ρ = 0.85) → long AUD + short NZD naturally hedges much of currency risk → σ(R_DC) of long-short portfolio (5.6%) < either individual currency risk (8.3% or 10.6%)
→ PM heuristic: before hedging each currency individually, check correlations. Correlated foreign currencies partially hedge each other.

**Proxy Hedge vs. Cross Hedge (pp 195-196):**
Proxy hedge: hedge foreign currency back to domestic currency using a DIFFERENT but correlated instrument (e.g., hedge AUD exposure with NZD/USD forward because AUD and NZD are highly correlated)
Cross hedge: shift currency risk from one FOREIGN currency to another foreign currency (e.g., convert IDR exposure to THB exposure using cross-rate derivatives)
→ Both introduce BASIS RISK — the correlation between the hedged exposure and the hedging instrument may change unexpectedly

**Macro Hedge (pp 197-198):**
Hedge the ENTIRE portfolio against broad risk scenarios rather than individual currency pairs
Examples: gold allocation (haven during crisis), VIX overlay (vol spikes during FX stress), currency basket derivatives
→ More efficient for multi-currency portfolios where hedging each pair individually is expensive
→ Basis risk is significant — macro hedge correlations can break down precisely during the crises they're meant to protect against

**Minimum-Variance Hedge Ratio (pp 198-201):**
For direct hedges (same currency forward): MVHR ≈ 100% (spot and forward move nearly 1:1)
For cross-hedges or joint optimization: MVHR = ρ(R_DC, R_FX) × [σ(R_DC) / σ(R_FX)]
This is the OLS regression β coefficient from: R_DC = α + β(%ΔS_hedge) + ε
→ Quantitative anchor (Kilmarnock case): σ(%ΔS_GBP/USD) = 2.7%, σ(R_DC) = 4.4%, ρ = 0.2 → MVHR = 0.2 × (4.4/2.7) = 0.33 → optimal hedge = 33% of $10M = $3.3M in USD/GBP forward
→ CRITICAL: For single-country EQUITY portfolios, MVHR can be VERY different from 100%
→ McYelland case: β̂ = 1.35 → MVHR = 135%! The hedge notional EXCEEDS the portfolio value because CHF and SMI are highly correlated (ρ = 0.6 over 10 years)
→ Caveat: MVHR is estimated from HISTORICAL data and is only approximate. The correlation and volatility estimates are BACKWARD-LOOKING and may not persist. Must monitor and rebalance.

**Basis Risk Warning (pp 199):**
Any cross-hedge or macro-hedge introduces basis risk
Basis risk = imperfect correlation between hedged exposure and hedging instrument
→ Correlations can shift suddenly and unexpectedly → what was a minimum-variance hedge can become a position that AMPLIFIES risk
→ Unlike forward-based direct hedges (which converge at maturity via arbitrage), cross-hedges have NO convergence guarantee

### 20.8 Emerging Market Currency Challenges

**EM-Specific Risks (pp 205-208):**
1. Higher transaction costs: wider bid-offer spreads, fewer derivative products, "specialty" pricing by dealers
2. Cross-rate illiquidity: CLP/THB doesn't trade directly → must go through USD as intermediary → TWO bid-offer spreads
3. Crowded carry trades: EM currencies attract carry-seekers → slow build-up → sudden panicked unwind → severe losses
4. Fat-tailed, negatively skewed distributions: EM currency returns are NOT normally distributed → VaR and Sharpe ratio UNDERSTATE true risk during calm periods
5. Government intervention: capital controls, pegged exchange rates, forced devaluations → creates false sense of stability followed by sudden regime changes
6. Contagion: during crises, EM currencies become highly correlated ("risk-on/risk-off") → diversification benefits disappear → "haven currencies" (USD, JPY, CHF) vs. "commodity currencies" (AUD, NZD, ZAR)

**Non-Deliverable Forwards (NDFs) (pp 207-208):**
Used when capital controls prevent physical delivery of the controlled currency
Cash-settled in the non-controlled currency (usually USD)
Settlement = difference between NDF rate and spot rate at maturity, converted to USD
→ NDF credit risk is LOWER than outright forward (only the settlement difference is at risk, not full principal)
→ NDF pricing may DIVERGE from covered interest rate parity because capital controls prevent the arbitrage that enforces CIP
→ Common NDF currencies: CNY, KRW, RUB, INR, BRL
→ Key risk: government policy changes can cause sudden, large moves in both spot and NDF rates, erasing gains earned during periods of apparent stability

### 20.9 Currency Overlay Program Design

**Separating Currency Beta from Currency Alpha (pp 226-227):**
Currency beta: the hedging function → can be done internally, rules-based
Currency alpha: active management for profit → requires specialized FX expertise
→ Solution when internal team lacks FX expertise: hire currency overlay manager as sub-adviser
→ Overlay manager handles alpha generation; internal team handles beta (hedging)
→ This separation allows the fund to benefit from active currency management without building internal FX trading capability

**Client Matching to Overlay Approach (pp 215-216, 229-231):**
Low risk tolerance + close-to-benchmark objective (Kastner) → fully passive hedge, NO overlay needed
Moderate risk tolerance + limited discretion ±5% (Braunt Pensionskasse) → directional view overlay with tight bands
High risk tolerance + alpha mandate (Franz Trading GmbH) → "FX as asset class" overlay → benefits MOST from adding overlay manager
→ PM decision rule: the client MOST LIKELY to benefit from an additional overlay manager is the one with the highest risk tolerance and widest discretion bands

**Overlay Program Alpha Correlation (p 231):**
The more UNCORRELATED the currency overlay alpha is with other portfolio programs → the more likely it is to be allowed in terms of strategic portfolio positioning
→ This is the portfolio-level diversification argument: if currency alpha has low correlation with equity alpha and FI alpha, it improves the overall efficient frontier

### 20.10 Practice Problem Decision Rules & Common Errors

**Domestic-Currency Return Decomposition Common Errors:**
Error 1: Confusing DOMESTIC currency with PRICE currency in the P/B quote → must ensure R_FX uses domestic currency as price currency
Error 2: Adding R_FC + R_FX instead of using multiplicative formula → (1+R_FC)(1+R_FX)-1
Error 3: If R_DC = 0% and R_FX = +5%, then R_FC must be NEGATIVE (portfolio lost value in local terms)
Solving: R_FC = (1+R_DC)/(1+R_FX) - 1 = (1+0%)/(1+5%) - 1 = -4.76%

**Hedging Instrument Selection (pp 235-236):**
Dynamic forward hedge is preferred when: (1) client is risk-averse, (2) IPS requires periodic rebalancing, (3) portfolio is large enough for OTC forwards
Forward preferred over futures: (1) more flexible (any currency pair, settlement, amount), (2) no margin requirements, (3) more liquid for large institutional trades, (4) simpler administratively
→ Futures preferred when: small portfolio, retail investor, standardized currency pair, need daily settlement credit protection

**Hedge Rebalancing Cash Flow Calculation (pp 236):**
Step 1: Settle maturing forward at the OFFER side (selling base currency forward → buying it back to settle means using offer)
Step 2: Buy the asset currency at SPOT using the BID side
Step 3: Net cash flow = Step 1 inflow - Step 2 outflow → can be positive (gain) or negative (loss)
Quantitative anchor: Delgado case — sell USD2,500,000 at all-in forward rate 0.8944 → receive EUR2,795,170. Buy USD2,500,000 at spot bid 0.8875 → pay EUR2,816,901. Net outflow = EUR21,731.

**Positive Roll Yield Strategies (pp 237):**
Strategy 1: Implement the carry trade → buy high-yield currency, sell low-yield currency
Strategy 2: Trade the forward rate bias → buy forward discount currency, sell forward premium currency
→ These are EQUIVALENT strategies expressed differently. If base currency trades at forward premium → selling it forward earns positive roll yield (sell high, buy back low at maturity).

**Surprise Rate Hike → Currency Play (pp 204):**
Expected NZD rate hike → NZD will appreciate → buy 5-delta put on NZD/AUD
Why 5-delta (deep OTM)? → percentage change in premium for a given spot move is HIGHER for deep-OTM options → it's a leveraged "lottery ticket" on the event. A 25-delta option would appreciate less in percentage terms.

**EM Currency Return Distributions (pp 237):**
EM currency returns have FATTER TAILS and NEGATIVE SKEW compared to DM normal distributions
→ VaR based on normal distribution UNDERSTATES true risk
→ Sharpe ratio looks attractive during calm periods but is MISLEADING → apparent outperformance can disappear into deep losses faster than investors can react
→ CCM should assume fat-tailed, negatively skewed distribution for EM currency portfolio → NOT normal distribution

### 20.11 Cross-Domain Inference Chains — Currency Management

1. UHNW client with international real estate holdings → property values denominated in foreign currencies → currency risk is LAYERED on top of illiquid asset risk → hedging with forwards creates liquidity mismatch (forward settlement requires cash, but RE is illiquid) → options provide better hedge structure (max loss = premium paid, no margin calls) → but options are expensive for long-dated hedges → SOLUTION: rolling 3-month OTM puts as cheap catastrophe insurance, accept day-to-day FX fluctuation → UHNW tax consideration: currency gains/losses on hedging instruments may have different tax treatment than gains/losses on underlying RE
Links to: Section 10 (private wealth), Section 14 (real estate alternatives)

2. Pension fund with EUR liabilities investing in USD bonds → must hedge USD/EUR exposure → FI portfolios should be hedged at ~100% (Campbell research) → but hedge creates negative roll yield if EUR rates < USD rates (selling USD at forward discount) → negative roll yield = drag on returns → fund faces choice: (a) accept roll yield cost for certainty, (b) reduce hedge ratio to capture some USD carry → IPS and fiduciary duty usually push toward (a) → UNLESS investment committee grants discretionary authority within narrow bands
Links to: Section 12 (fixed income), Section 17 (institutional PM — DB pensions)

3. SWF with EM equity allocation + active currency mandate → carry trade on EM currencies generates positive expected return → BUT EM currencies have negative skew and fat tails → VaR-based risk monitoring understates true exposure → SWF experiences "long periods of calm profits followed by sudden large losses" → solution: combine carry trade with volatility overlay (long OTM puts on EM currencies) → put premiums reduce carry trade return but protect against tail events → this is the OPTIMAL structure for capturing carry premium while managing tail risk
Links to: Section 17 (SWFs), Section 14 (hedge fund strategies), Appendix D (risk premiums)

4. Currency overlay manager selection chain: identify client risk tolerance and IPS constraints → determine position on currency risk spectrum → if passive: no overlay needed, use rules-based forward hedging → if discretionary: overlay manager with tight bands, fundamental-based approach → if active/alpha-seeking: overlay manager with wide bands, multiple strategy approaches (carry + technical + fundamental + volatility) → evaluate overlay manager by: (a) alpha generation, (b) correlation with existing portfolio strategies, (c) operational due diligence on FX trading infrastructure → the BEST overlay programs have alpha that is UNCORRELATED with other portfolio managers' alpha
Links to: Section 18 (manager selection), Section 16 (fiduciary duty)

5. Hedging decision integration: foreign equity allocation → first check ρ(R_FC, R_FX) — if high and positive, currency AMPLIFIES equity risk → then check forward points — is roll yield positive or negative? → then check IPS constraints — what hedge ratio range is permitted? → then select instrument — forward (most common), options (if want to preserve upside), cross-hedge (if direct hedge unavailable) → then determine hedge ratio: MVHR if cross-hedging, 100% if passive, IPS-constrained deviation if discretionary → MONITOR: static hedge drifts, correlations shift, forward points change → rebalance dynamically based on risk aversion and conviction
Links to: Section 11 (portfolio construction), Section 19 (derivatives overlay)

Limitation: Currency management is inherently probabilistic. No approach — fundamental, technical, carry, or volatility — has been shown to generate CONSISTENT alpha after transaction costs over long periods. The forward rate bias (carry trade) is the most empirically robust source of FX returns, but it is compensation for bearing tail risk, not true alpha. All hedging strategies involve trade-offs between cost, protection, and upside potential. The "best" approach depends entirely on the client's IPS, risk tolerance, investment horizon, and the manager's convictions — there is genuinely no single correct answer.
