## Section 18.9: Performance Evaluation — Practice Problem Decision Rules & Deepened Heuristics (V7)

**Capture Ratio Profiles & Asymmetry (Exhibit 22-25, pp 61-63):**

Key quantitative anchors from four stylized profiles (Jan 2000–Dec 2013, S&P 500 + T-bills):
- Long only (UC 100%, DC 100%, CR 1.0): cumulative 64.0%, annualized 3.60%, Sharpe 0.10, max drawdown −50.9%
- Low beta (UC 50%, DC 50%, CR 1.0): cumulative 54.2%, annualized 3.14%, Sharpe 0.14, max drawdown −28.3%
- Positive asymmetry (UC 75%, DC 25%, CR 3.0): cumulative 228.1%, annualized 8.86%, Sharpe 0.71, max drawdown −26.9%
- Negative asymmetry (UC 25%, DC 75%, CR 0.3): cumulative −24.4%, annualized −1.98%, Sharpe −0.40, max drawdown −48.9%

Critical insight: CR = 1.0 does NOT mean identical performance. Low beta (CR 1.0) had HIGHER Sharpe (0.14 vs 0.10) and MUCH lower drawdown (−28.3% vs −50.9%) than long only (CR 1.0). The CR tells you about asymmetry, NOT about risk-adjusted performance.

**Gain/Loss Asymmetry Rule:** Losses require proportionally greater gains to recover because the gain is calculated on a LOWER base. Lose 10% of $100 → $90 → need $10/$90 = 11.1% to recover. This is NOT because losing is "easier" — it's pure math of the denominator changing. At extreme losses: 50% loss needs 100% gain; 90% loss needs 900% gain.

**Capture Ratio Calculation Method (from practice problem, pp 64-65):**
Step 1: Separate periods into upside (benchmark > 0) and downside (benchmark < 0)
Step 2: For each side, calculate GEOMETRIC AVERAGE of manager returns and benchmark returns separately
Step 3: Upside capture = geometric avg manager up / geometric avg benchmark up
Step 4: Downside capture = geometric avg manager down / geometric avg benchmark down
Step 5: CR = upside capture / downside capture
Common error: Using arithmetic averages instead of geometric → will give different answer

**Evaluating Positive Asymmetry — Is It Skill or Strategy? (p 63):**
Decision rule: When a manager shows positive asymmetry (CR > 1), determine the SOURCE before attributing skill:
- Rolling OTM put strategy → inherently convex payoff → positive asymmetry is the STRATEGY, not skill
- Manager who times market exposure (reduces equity in down markets, increases in up) → asymmetry reflects SKILL if repeatable
- Consistency check: compare stated investment process with reported performance → inconsistency = red flag for implementation or reporting problems

**Drawdown as Stress Test (p 63):**
Drawdowns reveal whether the investment process is robust. Key questions after large drawdown:
1. Did manager implement stated process consistently? If deviation caused the drawdown → process concern
2. How did manager respond? → evidence of robustness of portfolio construction and risk management
3. Was the drawdown firm-specific or market-wide? → firm-specific drawdowns are MORE concerning (operational/process risk)
4. Did principal-agent conflict emerge? → during large drawdowns, manager may prioritize business survival over client interest

**Path Dependence and Investor Behavior (p 63):**
Even if a manager remains suitable, the investor's PERCEPTION of risk may change after a drawdown → risk tolerance is path-dependent
Decision rule: If IPS hasn't changed AND manager evaluation is unchanged → resist temptation to exit at the worst time
Exception: Investors closer to retirement with less recovery time → MORE weight on absolute drawdown measures → may rationally prefer managers with shallower, shorter drawdowns

**Manager Skill Evaluation Framework (Exhibit 26-27, pp 65-68):**

Manager A case study (MSCI Pacific, 5-year period):
- Manager A return 9.42% vs benchmark 9.25% → only 17 bps outperformance
- Attribution: allocation effect −43 bps (POOR country allocation) + selection effect +59 bps (GOOD stock picking) = net +17 bps
- Interpretation: Manager is a good STOCK PICKER but poor ALLOCATOR → these are different skills
- Overweighted Australia (30% vs 25.4%) which underperformed → lost 24 bps from allocation
- Underweighted Japan (51% vs 60.5%) which outperformed → lost 21 bps from allocation
- But stock selection in Japan +47 bps, Australia +31 bps → strong stock selection

Appraisal measure comparison across three managers:
- Manager A: Sharpe 0.68 (below benchmark 0.73), Treynor 0.35 (HIGHEST), IR 0.43, Sortino 0.82
- Manager B: Sharpe 0.76 (HIGHEST), Treynor 0.32, IR 0.41, Sortino 0.51
- Manager C: Sharpe 0.66, Treynor 0.19, IR 0.30, Sortino 1.03 (HIGHEST)

Key decision rule: Different appraisal measures can give CONTRADICTORY rankings. Manager A ranks best on Treynor and IR but worst on Sharpe. This is because:
- Sharpe measures return per unit of TOTAL risk → penalizes Manager A's higher volatility
- Treynor measures return per unit of SYSTEMATIC risk → rewards Manager A's lower beta exposure
- IR measures active return per unit of active risk → rewards consistency of outperformance
- The "right" measure depends on the portfolio role: standalone allocation → Sharpe; within diversified portfolio → IR or Treynor

**Practice Problem Decision Rules (Q1-14 Solutions, pp 77-80):**

1. Attribution must account for ALL of portfolio's return/risk, reflect the investment decision-making process, quantify active decisions, and provide complete understanding. Drawing conclusions about quality (Response 1) is the PURPOSE, not the attribution itself.

2. For top-down FI manager with benchmark → relative-risk attribution is most appropriate → attribute TRACKING RISK to allocation and selection decisions. NOT factor decomposition (that's for bottom-up).

3. Target semideviation = (Average return − MAR) / Sortino ratio. Given 8.20% return, 5.00% MAR, 0.87 Sortino → target semideviation = (8.20% − 5.00%) / 0.87 = 3.68%. Common error: confusing Sortino numerator with Sharpe numerator (Sharpe uses risk-free rate, Sortino uses MAR).

4. Capture ratio > 1 indicates POSITIVE asymmetry (convex return profile). UC/DC = 0.66/0.50 = 1.32.

5. Maximum drawdown of −24% with 4-month drawdown duration over 10 years → quick recovery → portfolio rebounded efficiently. Drawdown duration = start of decline to full recovery, NOT just to trough.

6. Return-based attribution is LEAST accurate of three approaches (return-based, holdings-based, transaction-based). Uses only total portfolio returns → cannot identify specific active investment decisions.

7. Carhart factor model: negative HML sensitivity (−0.17 vs benchmark +0.04) → manager has GROWTH tilt (negative loading on value factor). NOT momentum tilt (WML was negative too).

8. Factor contribution to active return: SMB contributed LEAST (0.29% absolute, 7.33% proportion). Compare absolute contributions, not just factor returns.

9. WML (momentum) return was +3.38% but manager was underweight (−0.05 vs benchmark 0.07) → missed opportunity. Weighting MORE toward momentum would have ADDED value. HML return was −9.60% so more value tilt would have DETRACTED.

10. Brinson allocation effect formula: (w_i − W_i)(B_i − B). South America: (20.38% − 18.82%)(35.26% − 22.67%) = 0.20%. Common error: using portfolio return instead of benchmark return for the segment.

11. Positive allocation effect = overweight in a segment whose benchmark return EXCEEDS total benchmark return, OR underweight in a segment whose benchmark return is BELOW total benchmark. Developed Asia: underweighted (29.86% vs 31.16%) a segment that underperformed (12.85% vs 22.67%) → POSITIVE allocation effect.

12. South America security selection + interaction = −3.11% (−311 bps) → primary source of overall underperformance (−441 bps total from selection/interaction). South America's portfolio return (20.00%) dramatically underperformed its benchmark return (35.26%).

13. Bond benchmark validity: "measurable" (daily reported performance) = YES. "Investable" = NO (cap-weighted bond index rebalanced frequently, difficult to replicate). "Appropriate" = NO (includes foreign bonds, fund invests only domestic).

14. DB pension plan → assets required to pay SPECIFIC FUTURE LIABILITY → LIABILITY-BASED benchmark is most appropriate. Key trigger: any time the plan has defined benefit obligations, think liability-driven.

---

## Section 21: Investment Manager Selection & Due Diligence (V7)

### 21.1 Manager Selection Process Overview

The manager selection process has THREE broad components, always in this sequence:
1. **Define the Universe** — filter the feasible set of managers by suitability, style, and active vs. passive
2. **Quantitative Analysis** — attribution/appraisal, capture ratios, drawdown analysis
3. **Qualitative Analysis** — investment due diligence (philosophy, process, people, portfolio) + operational due diligence (procedures, firm, vehicle, terms, monitoring)

Critical assumption underlying all due diligence: a consistent, robust investment process will generate a SIMILAR RETURN DISTRIBUTION relative to risk factors through time, assuming market dynamics haven't dramatically changed. Due diligence tests whether this assumption holds.

### 21.2 Type I and Type II Errors — The Hidden Cost of Manager Decisions

**Null hypothesis:** The manager is NOT skillful (no skill)

**Type I Error (False Positive):** Hiring or retaining a manager who subsequently UNDERPERFORMS expectations. Rejecting the null when it is correct. You thought the manager had skill, but they didn't.

**Type II Error (False Negative):** NOT hiring or firing a manager who subsequently OUTPERFORMS or meets expectations. Failing to reject the null when it is incorrect. You missed a skilled manager.

**Why decision-makers over-focus on Type I errors:**
- Regret avoidance: Type I errors are EXPLICIT COSTS (visible underperformance) → psychologically painful
- Measurability: Type I errors directly linked to compensation → performance is monitored regularly
- Transparency: clients SEE the underperforming manager → must explain the decision
- Type II errors are OPPORTUNITY COSTS → invisible → what is the performance impact of NOT hiring a particular manager? Hard to measure

**Performance implications — the distribution overlap matters:**
- In EFFICIENT markets → skilled and unskilled manager distributions OVERLAP heavily → smaller cost difference between Type I and II errors → the cost of retaining an unskilled manager is low, and the opportunity cost of not hiring a skilled one is also low
- In INEFFICIENT markets → distributions are more distinct → higher cost differential → more important to get the selection right
- If performance is MEAN-REVERTING → firing poor performers (Type I correction) leads to losing the subsequent reversion → you sell low → and hiring strong performers (Type I risk) means buying high

Inference chain: Mean-reverting markets + behavioral tendency to chase performance = systematic destruction of value through manager selection timing → study found institutional plan sponsors lost >$170 billion from poor timing of allocation decisions → products receiving contributions subsequently underperformed products experiencing withdrawals
Links to: Section 4 (behavioral biases), Section 17 (institutional PM)

**Monitoring framework to detect Type II errors:**
Track not only current managers but ALSO managers evaluated and not hired, and managers that were fired
Ask: Are there identifiable factors that differentiate hired vs. not-hired managers? Are those factors consistent with the decision-maker's philosophy?

### 21.3 Style Analysis — RBSA vs. HBSA

**Returns-Based Style Analysis (RBSA):**
Top-down approach: regresses portfolio returns against security market index returns to estimate factor sensitivities

Advantages: straightforward, doesn't require position-level data, comparable across managers and through time, objective (not subject to window dressing), can run immediately after data available, identifies important drivers of return/risk even for complex strategies

Disadvantages: IMPRECISE — attributes performance to an UNCHANGING average portfolio during the period → cannot identify impact of dynamic investment decisions → may distort decomposition of value added. Stale prices in illiquid portfolios (PE, VC) understate risk exposure. Longer periods give better estimates but may not reflect current exposures. Not suitable for high-turnover strategies where exposures change rapidly.

**Holdings-Based Style Analysis (HBSA):**
Bottom-up approach: estimates risk exposures from ACTUAL securities held at a point in time

Advantages: estimation of CURRENT risk factors, comparable across managers and through time, provides accurate view of manager's risk exposures, can be estimated immediately after data available

Disadvantages: computational effort increases with strategy complexity, depends on transparency from manager (problematic for HF, PE, VC), snapshot in time may not reflect portfolio going forward (particularly for high-turnover), stale pricing of illiquid securities understates risk, subject to WINDOW DRESSING (managers change holdings at reporting dates to look good)

**Decision rule for choosing RBSA vs. HBSA:**
- If manager holds publicly traded liquid securities with available holdings → HBSA preferred (more accurate, current)
- If manager is opaque (HF, PE) and holdings data unavailable → RBSA by necessity
- If want to check for window dressing → compare RBSA (uses actual returns, not subject to dressing) with HBSA (uses holdings, subject to dressing) → INCONSISTENCY between RBSA and HBSA signals potential window dressing or style drift
- Both should be tracked OVER TIME to detect style drift

**Style analysis must be:** Meaningful (represent important sources of return/risk), Accurate (reflect actual exposures), Consistent (comparable over time and across managers), Timely (available when needed for decisions)

### 21.4 Active Share & Closet Indexing

Active Share = ½ × Σ|Strategy Weight_i − Benchmark Weight_i|

Active Share ranges from 0 (exact replication) to 1 (no overlap with benchmark)

**Active Share × Tracking Risk Matrix:**
- High Active Share + High Tracking Risk → Concentrated stock pickers
- High Active Share + Low Tracking Risk → Diversified stock pickers
- Low Active Share + High Tracking Risk → Sector rotators
- Low Active Share + Low Tracking Risk → CLOSET INDEXERS (charging active fees for passive-like performance)

Warning: A manager using sampling techniques to build a portfolio may APPEAR as a closet indexer based on low active share, but could actually be a diversified stock picker depending on the universe. Active share is relative to the benchmark and category definitions used.

Decision rule for UHNW/institutional: If paying active management fees → verify active share is meaningfully above zero → closet indexing is a wealth-destroying agency problem → the manager captures AUM-based fees while delivering near-index returns
Links to: Section 10 (UHNW fee sensitivity), Section 17 (institutional governance)

### 21.5 Investment Philosophy Due Diligence

The investment philosophy is the FOUNDATION of the investment process. Every strategy is built on assumptions about factors driving performance and the manager's ability to exploit them.

**Two categories of market inefficiency:**
1. **Behavioral inefficiencies** — mispricing from participant biases (trend-following, loss aversion) → TEMPORARY, lasting long enough to exploit before price converges to intrinsic value
2. **Structural inefficiencies** — mispricing from external/internal rules and regulations → can be LONG-LIVED, assume continuation of the rules rather than convergence

Decision rule: Structural inefficiencies are MORE likely to be repeatable and sustainable than behavioral ones → prefer managers exploiting structural inefficiencies when seeking long-term consistent alpha

**Philosophy evaluation questions (what separates genuine alpha generators from pretenders):**
1. Can the manager clearly and consistently articulate their philosophy? → inability to explain = red flag
2. Are the assumptions credible and consistent? → a manager who claims active skill in a market they also describe as efficient = internal contradiction
3. How has the philosophy evolved? → ideally UNCHANGED over time (repeatable) → ad hoc changes in response to performance or flows = not robust
4. Are return sources linked to credible inefficiencies?

**Capacity assessment:**
- Does the inefficiency provide sufficient frequency of opportunity to cover transaction costs and fees?
- Is it repeatable (can be captured by a systematic process)?
- Is it sustainable (at what AUM level would returns be diluted to unacceptable levels)?
- All else equal, the more well-known an inefficiency → the more likely it is to be arbitraged away → capacity shrinks

**Signs of credible alpha generators (from Minahan excerpt):**
1. Managers who measure the success of each STEP of the process, not just ultimate outcome
2. Managers who recognize every strategy can be arbitraged away and plan for strategy evolution
3. Managers who can identify the SPECIFIC inefficiency they exploit with every position
4. Managers who know their companies so well they interpret change faster than the market (even without an explicit alpha thesis)

### 21.6 Investment Decision-Making Process — Four Elements

**1. Signal Creation (Idea Generation):**
Key question: Is the information unique, timely, or interpreted differently?
- Unique information → informational edge (increasingly hard to maintain in regulated markets that reduce informational asymmetry)
- Timely information → speed advantage (declining with technology)
- Interpreted differently → analytical edge (most sustainable form of alpha)

**2. Signal Capture (Idea Implementation):**
Is the translation from idea to position repeatable and consistent? Who ultimately approves investment positions?

**3. Portfolio Construction:**
- Allocation process should be consistent with philosophy and signal frequency
- Position sizing should reflect conviction AND correlations
- Hard vs. soft stop-losses → hard stops can increase turnover and trading costs; soft stops preserve manager discretion
- Dollar-neutral ≠ beta-neutral → for true market-neutral, need BETA neutrality (aggregate beta of longs = aggregate beta of shorts)
- Liquidity assessment: What % can be liquidated in 5 business days? What % requires >10 days?
- AUM growth check: have portfolio characteristics changed with asset growth? → if same positions held but AUM doubled → dilution of returns

**4. Portfolio Monitoring:**
Ongoing feedback loop — external (market dynamics, economic environment) and internal (performance, risk profile, style drift)
Performance attribution and style analysis = continuous monitoring tools, not one-time evaluation

### 21.7 Investment Personnel & Behavioral Biases in Teams

**Key person risk:** Strategy overly dependent on one individual → departure negatively affects performance
Mitigants: non-compete agreements, ownership stakes, bonus structures, team depth

**Three behavioral biases in investment teams:**

**Groupthink:** Team minimizes conflict and dissent → pursuit of harmony overrides independent judgment → closed-mindedness and confirmation bias → ignoring dissenting information
Example: Team convinced recession is imminent from inverted yield curve + falling commodities → positions defensively → but ignores contradictory leading indicators → groupthink

**Authority bias:** Deferring to subject matter expert or most senior team member → undermines the purpose of team decision-making (synthesis of diverse perspectives)
Example: Former tech analyst on PM team vetoes semiconductor stock → other PMs defer to "expertise" even though their own analysis disagrees

**Aversion to complexity (Parkinson's Law of Triviality / "bike-shedding"):** Disproportionate attention to trivial issues at expense of important-but-complex ones → investment committees spend time on easy topics, neglect difficult ones like asset allocation and manager selection

**Mitigation strategies:**
- Optimal team size: 3-5 members (research finding) → larger teams = more coordination problems + groupthink risk
- Secret ballot voting → reduces groupthink and authority bias
- Pre-set meeting agendas → most important topics first with longest time → reduces complexity aversion
- Team diversity (social and cognitive) → reduces groupthink, analogous to how uncorrelated assets reduce portfolio volatility

### 21.8 Operational Due Diligence

Operational DD evaluates the firm AS A BUSINESS, not just the investment process. Key principle: investment managers are businesses with business risk → a great investment process inside a failing business is worthless.

**Back office & process checks:** Trading policy, soft dollar commissions, protection against unauthorized trading, fee calculation methodology, security allocation fairness across accounts, IT backup/cybersecurity

**Third-party service providers:** Prime broker, administrator, auditor, legal counsel → independent verification of performance and reporting. Red flag: FREQUENT AUDITOR CHANGES (Madoff used a tiny 3-person audit firm → red flag that should have been caught)

**Risk management integration:** Should be INTEGRAL to the firm, not peripheral → independent risk officer, risk manual, hard/soft investment guidelines, monitoring procedures, breach remediation process

**Firm sustainability assessment:**
- Ownership structure (independent vs. subsidiary) → trade-offs: independence = more autonomy but higher cost structure; subsidiary = financial support but potential business risk conflicts
- Breakeven AUM → asset base must generate enough fee revenue to cover total firm expenses
- Outside ownership conflict: owner wants to grow AUM for fee revenue → may prevent holding illiquid small-cap positions that can't absorb more capital
- Employee co-investment → alignment of interests (positive), but too much → personal/business risk for manager that may override client interest

**Madoff lessons for operational DD:**
- Returns too consistent for stated strategy → red flag
- Strategy described as too complex for outsiders to understand → red flag
- Broker/dealer also operated asset management → conflict of interest
- Small unknown auditor → inadequate independent verification
- Markopolos couldn't reconcile track record with investment process → the FIRST red flag was process inconsistency

### 21.9 Investment Vehicles — SMA vs. Pooled

**Separately Managed Accounts (SMA) advantages:**
- Direct ownership of securities → clear legal ownership, no co-mingling risk, protected in manager bankruptcy
- Customization → can express individual constraints, ESG preferences, tax-loss harvesting
- Tax efficiency → investor pays taxes only on REALIZED capital gains → can implement tax-lot management
- Transparency → real-time position-level detail

**SMA disadvantages:**
- Higher cost (no economies of scale, each account is operationally separate)
- Tracking risk from customization (performance reflects client constraints, not just manager skill)
- Investor behavior risk — transparency + control = temptation to micromanage → performance chasing, familiarity bias, loss aversion, disaggregation tendency

**Pooled vehicles (funds) advantages:**
- Lower cost (operational costs spread across investors)
- Access to strategies requiring critical mass
- Less susceptible to individual investor micromanagement

**Pooled vehicle disadvantages:**
- No customization
- Liquidity varies dramatically by vehicle type
- Subject to OTHER INVESTORS' redemption decisions → forced selling in down markets

**Liquidity hierarchy (most to least liquid):**
1. Closed-end funds and ETFs (intra-day trading)
2. Open-end funds (daily NAV, end-of-day price certainty)
3. Hedge funds (redemption frequency + notification period + lockup + gates)
4. Private equity and venture capital funds (10-year fund life, capital calls and distributions only)

Decision rule for UHNW: If tax efficiency is a priority AND account size justifies the cost → SMA. If tax-exempt (foundation, endowment) → SMA's tax advantage is irrelevant → pooled may be preferred for cost savings.
Links to: Section 10 (UHNW tax management), Section 17 (institutional vehicle selection)

### 21.10 Management Fee Structures & Incentive Analysis

**Fee trend data (2000-2021):**
- Active equity mutual fund expense ratio: 1.06% (2000) → 0.68% (2021), decline of 36%
- Active bond mutual fund expense ratio: 0.77% (2000) → 0.46% (2021), decline of 40%
- Index equity fund expense ratio: 27 bps (2000) → 6 bps (2021)
- Index bond fund expense ratio: 21 bps (2000) → 6 bps (2021)

**Three performance-based fee structures:**
1. **Symmetrical (full sharing):** Fee = Base + Sharing × performance (positive AND negative) → manager fully exposed to both upside and downside → BEST alignment of interests → but unpopular because managers face bankruptcy risk
2. **Bonus (upside only):** Fee = Higher of [Base] or [Base + Sharing × positive performance] → manager protected on downside, participates in upside → MOST COMMON in practice → creates option-like payoff for manager
3. **Capped bonus:** Fee = Higher of [Base] or [Base + Sharing × positive performance, up to maximum] → limits manager's upside → reduces incentive to take excessive risk near the cap

**Performance fee as call option (critical insight):**
A bonus-style fee = base fee + long call option on active return (strike = base fee) − short call option (strike = maximum fee)
Per option pricing: higher active return VOLATILITY → higher option value → incentivizes managers to take MORE risk
Implication: Bonus-style fees can lead to misestimates of portfolio risk → standard deviation of NET returns is LOWER than standard deviation of GROSS returns because the performance fee reduces variability on the upside but not the downside → ASYMMETRIC net return distribution

**Performance fee effects on reported volatility:**
Management fee (fixed %) → reduces return level but does NOT change standard deviation
Performance fee (% of gains) → reduces BOTH return level AND standard deviation of net returns → in up months, fee is accrued (reduces net return); in down months, fee accrual is reversed (partially offsets loss) → measured monthly net returns are compressed toward zero

**High-water mark and clawback provisions:**
High-water mark → performance fees earned only on returns ABOVE the previous peak NAV → protects investors from paying fees for recovering prior losses
PE base fees applied to COMMITTED (not just invested) capital → investors pay fees on capital not yet deployed
Hedge fund return incentive: when HWM is far above current NAV → performance fee option is deep OTM → manager may return capital rather than attempt to earn their way back → rational for manager but detrimental to investor who misses subsequent recovery

**Fund of funds fee problem:** Double layer of fees (FoF fee + underlying fund fees) + "asymmetry penalty" → investors share profits from winning funds but absorb FULL losses from losing funds → net result can be significantly negative
Solution emerging: portfolio-level fee structures (fees on total FoF value rather than individual fund level)

### 21.11 Manager Selection Practice Problem Decision Rules (V7)

**From Module 2 Solutions (Q1-43):**

1. Investment process (not transparency or operations) is the MOST important qualitative consideration for repeatable performance.

2. Universe screening should AVOID excluding based on historical risk-adjusted returns → focus should be on risk profile and identifying candidates to fill the desired portfolio role → avoid performance-chasing bias in initial screening.

3. Fund selection for individual investor: evaluate key person risk (team depth), staff turnover (institutional knowledge loss), compensation alignment (incentive structure). Theta fund preferred over Zeta (single founder = key person risk, high turnover) and Eta (no performance-based compensation = weaker alignment).

4. Type I/II in mean-reverting context: avoiding underperformer (Eta) = Type II risk (missing reversion to outperformance). Chasing outperformer (Theta) = Type I risk (catching mean reversion back to underperformance). Goal: avoid making decisions based on SHORT-TERM performance.

5. Style analysis most useful when: strategies hold publicly traded securities with frequent pricing → less meaningful for VC/PE (illiquid, infrequently priced).

7. Pre-manager selection checklist: (a) decide outside support is needed, (b) complete IPS, (c) determine asset allocation → WITHOUT these, cannot identify suitable managers or evaluate fit.

9. Simultaneous Type I AND Type II errors: Hiring unskilled Manager 3 for large-cap (Type I) AND not hiring skilled Manager 2 for small-cap (Type II) → answer B.

10. Highly distinct return distributions of skilled vs. unskilled → HIGH opportunity cost of not hiring skilled manager → manager selection is MOST valuable in inefficient markets.

11. Boinic example: Type I = hiring Firm D (which underperformed). Type II = not hiring Firm B (which outperformed). Type I is more psychologically troubling due to regret, visibility, and compensation linkage.

12. Style analysis inconsistent with stated philosophy → process may NOT be repeatable → if results track over time, suggests style drift rather than process failure.

14. Style drift detection: manager with large-cap stock + investment-grade bond mandate → reducing investment-grade bond allocation = normal (within style) → but allocating to small-cap stocks or cash = OUTSIDE mandate = style drift.

17. Capture ratio < 1 → downside capture exceeds upside capture → manager's relative performance is WORSE in down markets.

18. UC = 75% and DC = 125% → manager underperforms benchmark in BOTH up AND down markets → benchmark goes up 10%, manager goes up 7.5% (underperforms) → benchmark goes down 10%, manager goes down 12.5% (underperforms). This applies regardless of whether benchmark is positive or negative.

19. Yang case (age 59, retiring in 3 years, loss-averse): select fund with SMALLEST drawdown and SHORTEST drawdown duration → Gamma (28% drawdown, 12 months). NOT Alpha (57% drawdown despite high capture ratio).

20. Aspen's philosophy is INCONSISTENT: claims passive strategy but seeks to exploit inefficiencies (active behavior). Philosophy has evolved in response to market performance (ad hoc, not systematic). Two red flags → do NOT hire.

22. Patnode (widow seeking predictable income): Laurbær preferred → recognizes strategies can be arbitraged away, monitors contribution of each theme → evidence of adaptability and process discipline. Alcanfor and Mylesten make generic claims without evidence of process-specific success metrics.

24. Parade endowment (low turnover preference): Manager B (portfolio liquidatable in 5 business days) is most appropriate → liquid portfolio means lower trading costs. Manager A's hard stop-losses increase turnover. Manager C's turnover exceeds signal frequency (inconsistent process).

25. SMA vs. pooled vehicle: Transparency → SMA. Investor behavior risk → Pooled (less micromanagement). Cost → Pooled (direct ownership safety in SMA but higher cost). Liquidity → SMA (not subject to other investors' redemptions). Tracking risk → Pooled (lower than customized SMA).

32. Circue Foundation (moderate fee sensitivity, heightened closet indexing sensitivity, some liquidity tolerance): Open-end fund with incentive fee → daily liquidity acceptable, incentive fee combats closet indexing by motivating active management, moderate fee sensitivity accommodated.

36. Incentive fees DECREASE volatility of net returns (compress returns toward zero). Management fees only decrease the level, not the volatility.

37. Symmetrical fee structure → fees affected by both positive AND negative performance → Yellow Wood Fund.

43. Carpenter Management's bonus-style fee with maximum cap = closest to a call option on active return (base fee is strike, maximum fee creates a short call at higher strike → long call spread payoff).

### 21.12 Cross-Domain Inference Chains — Manager Selection

1. UHNW client with $50M portfolio seeking hedge fund allocation → IPS specifies tax efficiency and customization needs → SMA preferred for equity sleeves but HF strategy REQUIRES pooled vehicle → evaluate: lockup period acceptable given client liquidity needs? → gate provisions = risk during market stress → soft lock preferable to hard lock for UHNW clients who may need emergency liquidity → if lockup terms are acceptable, evaluate operational DD with extra scrutiny on: auditor quality, administrator independence, compliance culture → Madoff-type fraud risk is HIGHER in hedge fund space due to less regulatory oversight → after all screening, layer on performance fee analysis: bonus-style fee incentivizes risk-taking → verify risk management framework is robust and INDEPENDENT of portfolio management
Links to: Section 10 (UHNW liquidity), Section 14 (hedge fund alternatives), Section 16 (fiduciary duty)

2. Pension fund with liability-driven mandate evaluating FI managers → benchmark must be LIABILITY-BASED (not asset-based market index) → style analysis: use RBSA first (FI holdings may be less transparent), then validate with HBSA when available → evaluate FI-specific attribution (interest rate contribution, credit selection, yield curve positioning) → check for style drift: is manager maintaining duration match with liabilities? → if manager has high active share relative to liability benchmark, attribution should explain where the tracking risk budget is being spent → excess tracking risk without corresponding IR improvement = poor risk budgeting → consider replacing with lower active share manager or passive LDI approach
Links to: Section 12 (FI dynamics), Section 17 (DB pensions), Section 18 (FI attribution)

3. Endowment investment committee evaluating manager team → committee itself subject to behavioral biases → Parkinson's Law: spending meeting time on easy topics (manager presentation quality, office visit impressions) instead of hard ones (statistical significance of track record, attribution decomposition, capacity analysis) → mitigation: pre-set agenda with most complex analytical topics FIRST, use secret ballot for hire/fire votes → committee should track its own Type II errors (monitor fired managers and candidates not hired) → if pattern of Type II errors emerges (regularly firing managers who subsequently outperform) → evidence of trend-following behavior in the committee's own decision-making
Links to: Section 4 (behavioral biases), Section 17 (endowments)

4. Family office evaluating quant equity long-short manager after August 2007 quant crisis → manager experienced 13.4% drawdown in 3 days → key questions: (a) did manager follow stated process or panic sell? (b) did stop-loss triggers force selling at worst time (locking in losses while market rebounded to −5.7% for the month)? (c) was the drawdown market-wide (systematic) or firm-specific (idiosyncratic)? → August 2007 was SYSTEMATIC (many quant strategies simultaneously deleveraged) → managers who held through recovered → managers who sold locked in losses and many closed → decision: drawdown from disciplined risk management (acceptable) vs. drawdown from panic (unacceptable) → HOW the manager responded matters more than the drawdown magnitude itself
Links to: Section 19 (derivatives risk management), Section 14 (HF strategies)

## Section 22: Global Investment Performance Standards (GIPS) — Compliance Framework (V7)

### 22.1 Purpose, Scope & Key Definitions

**GIPS Mission:** To promote ethics and integrity through globally accepted standards for calculating and presenting investment performance to prospective clients and investors.

**Five objectives:** (1) Promote investor interests and instill confidence, (2) Ensure accurate and consistent data, (3) Obtain worldwide acceptance of a single standard, (4) Promote fair global competition among investment firms, (5) Promote industry self-regulation globally

**Who can claim compliance:**
- Only investment management FIRMS and asset owners that manage assets on a discretionary basis and compete for business
- Compliance is FIRM-WIDE — cannot be claimed for only some products, composites, or portfolios
- Consultants, software houses, custodians, performance measurement providers CANNOT claim GIPS compliance

**Key terms:**
- **Firm:** An investment firm, subsidiary, or division held out to the public as a DISTINCT BUSINESS ENTITY — organizationally and functionally segregated from other units, with autonomy over the investment decision-making process
- **Composite:** An aggregation of one or more portfolios managed according to a similar investment mandate, objective, or strategy. The composite return = asset-weighted average of all portfolio returns in that composite
- **Segregated account (SMA):** A portfolio owned by a single client
- **Pooled fund:** Portfolio with multiple investor ownership interests. Subdivided into: broad distribution (mutual funds, UCITs — offered to general public) and limited distribution (PE, HF — not publicly offered)
- **Prospective client:** Person/entity that has expressed interest and qualifies to invest in one of the firm's strategies
- **Total firm assets:** Aggregate fair value of ALL assets (discretionary AND non-discretionary, fee-paying AND non-fee-paying) for which the firm has investment management responsibility. INCLUDES sub-advised assets. EXCLUDES advisory-only assets and uncalled committed capital
- **Fair value:** The amount at which an investment could be sold in an orderly, arm's-length transaction between willing parties

**Core principle:** Fair representation and full disclosure of investment performance is the KEY ethical principle. GIPS standards are VOLUNTARY (ethical, not legal), but: fair representation likely requires adherence to BOTH the minimum requirements AND the recommendations.

### 22.2 Fundamentals of Compliance

**Compliance is all-or-nothing:** Firms must meet ALL applicable requirements. No exceptions. No partial compliance.

**Firm definition is critical:** It determines the scope of compliance activities and establishes boundaries for total firm assets. The definition has lasting practical consequences because GIPS applies firm-wide.

Decision rule for defining the firm: How is the investment management organization held out to the public? If a unit of a larger company is marketed as a specialist (e.g., HNW/family office specialist) with its own investment team, traders, and admin → it may qualify as a separate "firm" for GIPS purposes. If two divisions share the same investment process, approved security list, and style models → neither alone can claim compliance independently.

**Historical performance is NOT to be altered** — apart from correcting errors. Corporate restructurings, mergers, rebranding → cannot change historical reported returns.

**Definition of Discretion:**
- All actual, fee-paying, DISCRETIONARY segregated accounts MUST be in at least one composite
- All actual, fee-paying, DISCRETIONARY pooled funds MUST be in at least one composite if they meet the definition
- NON-DISCRETIONARY accounts and portfolios MUST NOT be included in composites
- A portfolio is discretionary if the manager is able to implement the intended investment strategy

**When client restrictions make a portfolio non-discretionary:**
- Client mandates legacy holdings that cannot be sold → may skew performance → evaluate materiality
- Client requires advance approval of all trades by investment committee → likely non-discretionary (cannot freely implement strategy)
- Client prohibits derivatives but strategy doesn't use them → restriction is IMMATERIAL → portfolio remains discretionary
- Client prohibits derivatives and strategy relies on them → restriction is MATERIAL → portfolio is non-discretionary
- Client makes frequent large withdrawals requiring high liquidity → may prevent full strategy implementation → potentially non-discretionary
- Firm may set a materiality threshold (e.g., if <10% of portfolio is non-discretionary assets, still include)

**Simulated/model/backtested/hypothetical performance:**
- MUST NOT be included in any composite
- MUST NOT be linked to actual composite performance
- MAY be shown as SUPPLEMENTAL INFORMATION only (clearly labeled)
- Seed money portfolios (firm's own assets) → CAN be included in composites if they are actual portfolios managed to the strategy

### 22.3 Return Calculation Methodology

**Time-Weighted Return (TWR) — REQUIRED for most portfolios:**
TWR reflects the change in value and negates the effects of external cash flows → isolates the MANAGER'S investment decisions from client-directed timing of cash flows.

Formula (no cash flows): r_t = (V_1 − V_0) / V_0

Formula (with cash flows — true TWR): r_twr = (1 + r_t,1) × (1 + r_t,2) × ... × (1 + r_t,n) − 1
Sub-period returns are geometrically linked. Periodic and sub-period returns MUST be geometrically linked.

**Valuation requirements:**
- Portfolios must be valued at least MONTHLY (at calendar month end or last business day)
- If not calculated daily AND a large cash flow occurs intra-month → portfolio must be valued and a sub-period return calculated at the date of the large cash flow
- "Large cash flow" is defined by the FIRM for each composite (no universal threshold) — may be absolute dollar amount or % of portfolio value
- Private market investment portfolios (RE, PE, infra) → must be valued at least QUARTERLY

**Modified Dietz method (approximation when portfolio not valued at cash flow date):**
r_ModDietz = (V_1 − V_0 − CF) / (V_0 + Σ(CF_i × w_i))
where w_i = (CD − D_i) / CD is the time-weighting factor for each cash flow

Modified IRR method: Find r such that Ending Value = Σ[CF_i × (1+r)^w_i] + V_0(1+r)

Key insight: In flat/steadily rising markets, Modified Dietz approximation is close to true TWR. In VOLATILE markets, large external cash flows can cause significant estimation error → true TWR (with intra-period valuation) is much more accurate.

Example: True TWR = 0.41%, Modified Dietz = 0.51% (10 bps error from single $10,000 inflow in flat market). But in volatile market: true TWR = 8.00%, Modified Dietz = 9.52% (152 bps error!).

**Money-Weighted Return (MWR) — permitted ONLY when firm controls external cash flows AND:**
(1) Portfolios are closed-end, OR (2) fixed life, OR (3) fixed commitment, OR (4) illiquid investments are a significant part of the strategy
Annualized since-inception MWRs must be calculated at least annually. Daily external cash flows must be used (for periods beginning with 2020 GIPS Standards effective date).
MWR portfolios must be valued at least annually and at period end for which performance is calculated.

**Returns for < 1 year MUST NOT be annualized** → prevents firms from inflating partial-period results

**Cash and cash equivalents:** MUST be included in ALL total return calculations, even if managed by a separate firm (manager-of-manager arrangements). The cash allocation is a discretionary decision affecting total return.

### 22.4 Treatment of Expenses, Fees & Valuation

**Transaction costs:** MUST be deducted from returns. Include brokerage commissions, exchange fees, taxes, spreads from internal/external brokers. Do NOT include: custody fees charged per transaction (these are custody fees, not transaction costs).

**Bundled fees:** If transaction costs cannot be identified and separated from a bundle → composite gross-of-fees return must be reduced by the ENTIRE bundled fee OR the portion that includes transaction costs.

**Gross-of-fees return:** Return after deducting transaction costs but BEFORE management fees and custodial fees.
**Net-of-fees return:** Return after deducting transaction costs AND investment management fees.

**Fair value valuation hierarchy (recommended, in declining preference):**
1. Objective, observable, unadjusted quoted market price for identical investments in active markets (BEST)
2. Quoted prices for SIMILAR investments in active markets
3. Quoted prices for identical or similar investments in markets that are NOT active
4. Market-based inputs (other than quoted prices) observable for the investment
5. Subjective, unobservable inputs (LEAST preferred — used for illiquid/complex instruments like PE, RE, derivatives on company-specific securities)

### 22.5 Composite Construction & Maintenance

**Anti-cherry-picking rule:** ALL actual, fee-paying, discretionary segregated accounts MUST be in at least one composite. ALL actual, fee-paying, discretionary pooled funds meeting a composite definition MUST be included. This prevents firms from showing only their best-performing portfolios.

**Non-fee-paying discretionary accounts MAY be included** (with disclosure). Non-discretionary accounts and simulated/model portfolios MUST NOT be included.

**Composite definition hierarchy:**
Investment Mandate → Asset Class → Style/Strategy → Benchmark → Risk/Return Characteristics

**Composite TWR calculation methods (three approaches):**
1. Beginning assets weighting: r_C = Σ(r_pi × V_0,pi / ΣV_0,pi)
2. Beginning assets + weighted cash flows: r_C = Σ(r_pi × V_pi / ΣV_pi) where V_pi includes weighted external cash flows
3. Aggregate Return method: treat entire composite as single portfolio, apply Modified Dietz to aggregated assets and cash flows

Composite TWR must be calculated at least monthly (except private market composites).

### 22.6 Composite Timing Rules — Adding, Removing, Switching Portfolios

**New portfolios:** Include on a TIMELY and CONSISTENT basis after the portfolio comes under management. Typically at the beginning of the next full measurement period after funding. If funded in kind or in illiquid markets → firm has discretion on timing but must apply the policy CONSISTENTLY per composite.

**Terminated portfolios:** Include through the LAST FULL measurement period in which the firm had discretion. If terminated effective May 31 and notified to stop trading → last full month = April 30 (include through April only). Historical performance of terminated portfolios must REMAIN in the composite.

**Switching between composites:** Only permitted when: (1) documented changes to client's investment mandate/objective/strategy make another composite more appropriate, OR (2) the original composite is redefined. Historical performance stays with ORIGINAL composite — does NOT move to the new composite.

**Significant cash flows:** A sufficiently large client-directed cash flow may temporarily prevent strategy implementation → portfolio may be temporarily removed from composite. Firm must define "significant" ex ante, on a composite-specific basis. Alternative: use TEMPORARY NEW ACCOUNTS to isolate incoming/outgoing cash until invested/distributed.

**Minimum asset levels:** Firm may set minimum portfolio size for composite inclusion (below which strategy cannot be fully implemented). Portfolios falling below minimum → treated as non-discretionary for that composite. Changes to minimums MUST NOT be applied retroactively.

### 22.7 Presentation & Reporting Requirements

**GIPS Composite Report (TWR) required elements:**
- Composite AND benchmark annual returns for ALL years
- Number of portfolios (if ≥6) at each period end
- Composite assets at each period end
- Total firm assets at each period end
- Internal dispersion (if ≥6 portfolios for the full year)
- Three-year annualized ex post standard deviation of monthly composite AND benchmark returns (if monthly returns available)

**Minimum years of performance:** At least 5 years (or since inception if composite < 5 years). Must build to at least 10 years by adding one year annually.

**Internal dispersion measures:** High/low, range, equal-weighted standard deviation, or asset-weighted standard deviation of annual returns of portfolios in the composite for the FULL year.
- Equal-weighted SD: S_c = √(Σ(r_i − r̄_c)² / n) — treats all portfolios equally
- Asset-weighted SD: uses portfolio weights (w_i = V_0,i / V_0,total) — gives more weight to larger portfolios
- From example: German equity composite with 14 portfolios → equal-weighted mean 2.32%, SD 22 bps; asset-weighted SD 21 bps

**Three-year annualized ex post standard deviation:** Required for BOTH composite and benchmark → provides risk context → allows comparison across GIPS-compliant firms.

### 22.8 Portability of Performance

**Four conditions for linking past firm/affiliation performance (composite-specific):**
1. Substantially ALL investment decision makers are employed by the new/acquiring firm
2. Decision-making process remains substantially INTACT and INDEPENDENT within new firm
3. New firm has RECORDS that document and support the reported performance
4. NO BREAK in the track record between old and new firm

If conditions 1-3 met BUT there IS a break in the track record → past performance may REPRESENT the historical record of new firm but the two records MAY NOT be LINKED.

Grace period: If GIPS-compliant firm acquires non-compliant firm → ONE-YEAR grace period to bring non-compliant assets into compliance for future reporting.

### 22.9 Verification

**Purpose:** Independent third-party assessment of firm's policies and procedures for constructing composites and calculating/presenting performance in compliance with GIPS.

**Scope:**
- Verification is FIRM-WIDE — cannot be performed on a single composite or pooled fund only
- Verification does NOT provide assurance on the accuracy of any SPECIFIC composite or pooled fund presentation
- A verified firm may additionally have a PERFORMANCE EXAMINATION on specific composites/pooled funds → can state that a performance examination report has been issued

**What verification provides:** Greater confidence in the firm's claim of compliance. It is RECOMMENDED (not required) by GIPS standards. Practical reality: in many markets, non-verification is a competitive impediment → RFPs routinely ask about verification status.

**Verification process:**
- Verifier must understand firm's corporate structure, policies, and procedures
- Sampling methodology allowed for testing
- Must test: composite construction/maintenance, portfolio assignment to composites, return calculations, GIPS Report accuracy, marketing materials for GIPS references
- Must verify that input data (holdings, income, cash flows) is supported by independent third-party documentation (custodial/brokerage statements)

### 22.10 GIPS Practice Problem Decision Rules (V7)

1. Firm definition: Parent company with both institutional and retail → ENCOURAGE broadest definition → but if two autonomous subsidiaries with separate teams → each can be separate firm.

2. US entity of global firm with its own investment process = distinct business entity → qualifies as separate firm.

3. Non-discretionary segregated accounts MUST NOT be included in composites. Non-fee-paying discretionary accounts MAY be included (with disclosure).

4. TWR calculation with intra-period cash flows: value portfolio at date of each large cash flow → compute sub-period returns → geometrically link. Example: Q1 return = [(1+R_Jan) × (1+R_Feb) × (1+R_Mar)] − 1.

5. GIPS does NOT require firms to adhere to principles of financial accounting → GIPS has its own fair value definition and valuation hierarchy.

6. Cash/cash equivalents MUST be included in ALL return calculations, even if managed by another firm.

7. Custody fees charged per transaction = custody fees, NOT transaction costs (even though charged per transaction).

8-10. Modified Dietz composite return calculations: weight each portfolio by beginning assets (method 1) or beginning assets + weighted cash flows (method 2) or aggregate return method (method 3). All three give slightly different results due to cash flow timing.

11. A single portfolio CAN be included in MORE than one composite (e.g., large-cap composite AND large-cap growth composite) if it meets both definitions.

12. Backtested/simulated returns CANNOT be linked to actual composite performance → violation of GIPS.

13. SMERF case: client requires advance trade approval by investment committee + large regular withdrawals → likely NON-discretionary → exclude from all composites.

14. "Securities held at a gain must not be sold" → most likely non-discretionary (materially constrains manager). "Average credit quality = investment grade" and "securities issued in developed markets" → typical fixed-income constraints, do NOT make portfolio non-discretionary.

15. Portfolio funded in kind needing time to invest in bank loans → include on timely and consistent basis per firm's documented policy (e.g., when substantially invested in target strategy).

16. Terminated 15 April, instructed to stop trading → last FULL measurement period with discretion = March → include through 31 March only.

17. Client changes mandate from core-plus to core → switch to new composite → historical performance STAYS with original (core-plus) composite.

18. Same as 17 — Bida endowment switches from core-plus to core → history stays in core-plus.

19. Portfolio falls below minimum → exclude for months below minimum → when restored, re-add. During the exclusion period, Bamako must not include the portfolio.

20. Client requires advance approval of all transactions by controller → loss of discretion → exclude from ALL composites (not just the specific composite, and not from total firm assets).

22. Garcia wants to show only 5 years (2015-2019) to hide poor pre-2015 performance → VIOLATION → firm has been GIPS-compliant since 2012 and has built 13-year track record → must present MINIMUM 10 years (or since inception of compliance).

23. Acella cannot link O'Connor's JEMStone performance unless substantially all decision makers, intact process, records available, and no break in track record. Hiring one PM alone does NOT meet the portability test.

24. Verification makes the compliance claim MORE CREDIBLE → does NOT certify internal controls → does NOT ensure accuracy of specific composites.

25. Verification must be FIRM-WIDE → cannot verify only equity and balanced composites while excluding fixed income → must include ALL composites.

### 22.11 Cross-Domain Inference Chains — GIPS

1. UHNW family office evaluating two competing managers → one claims GIPS compliance with verification, other does not → GIPS-compliant firm provides: standardized return calculations (TWR), composite construction preventing cherry-picking, internal dispersion showing consistency across portfolios, 3-year rolling risk measures → the family office can make MEANINGFUL comparisons → without GIPS, Manager B could present only best-performing accounts → GIPS reduces information asymmetry in manager selection → directly supports Section 21 (manager selection due diligence)
Links to: Section 21 (manager selection), Section 10 (UHNW)

2. Pension fund with liability-driven mandate → manager presents GIPS Composite Report → pension trustees should examine: (a) is the benchmark liability-based or asset-based? → GIPS requires benchmark appropriate to strategy, (b) internal dispersion → high dispersion suggests customized portfolios within composite may not truly share the same mandate, (c) 3-year standard deviation of composite vs. benchmark → if very different, composite risk profile may not match liability characteristics → potential mismatch between what's reported and what the fund needs
Links to: Section 17 (DB pensions), Section 18 (benchmarks)

3. Endowment investment committee reviewing GIPS report after manager acquisition → portability analysis: did substantially all investment decision-makers move? → is the process independent within the new firm? → are historical records intact? → is there a break in the track record? → if all four conditions met → performance can be linked → if there's a break → can represent but NOT link → committee must evaluate whether the reported track record genuinely reflects the team and process they are hiring
Links to: Section 17 (endowments), Section 21 (operational DD)

4. Manager with PE allocation presents MWR instead of TWR → this is PERMISSIBLE under GIPS because PE portfolios meet the criteria (closed-end, fixed life, illiquid investments) → BUT MWR reflects cash flow timing (which the PE manager controls) → when comparing PE managers, MWR is more appropriate than TWR → TWR would distort returns by ignoring the timing of capital calls and distributions → UHNW client evaluating PE allocation should understand that MWR and TWR give different signals and are NOT directly comparable
Links to: Section 14 (PE alternatives), Section 18 (return measures)

Limitation: GIPS standards, while essential for fair presentation, have genuine loopholes and areas of judgment: (1) the definition of "discretion" is subjective — firms can classify borderline portfolios to improve or worsen composite results; (2) the definition of "large cash flow" varies by firm — no universal threshold, creating inconsistency; (3) composite definitions involve judgment — too broad = wide dispersion hiding true strategy, too narrow = near-useless small composites; (4) verification does NOT guarantee accuracy of any specific composite — it tests policies and procedures, not individual calculations; (5) survivorship bias in composites — while terminated portfolios' historical returns remain, the composite gradually loses poor-performing managers, potentially overstating long-term track records; (6) fair value of illiquid assets relies on subjective inputs (Level 4 in hierarchy) — particularly problematic for PE, RE, and structured products where valuations may be smoothed or stale.

---

## Section 23: Ethics & Professional Standards — Standards I-VII Deep Dive

*Source: CFA Program 2026 L3 V5 — Ethical and Professional Standards (pp 21-210+)*
*Deepens and replaces Section 16 with exhaustive standard-by-standard guidance, decision rules from 150+ examples, and cross-domain inference chains for UHNW/institutional practice*

### 23.1 Standard I: Professionalism — Complete Framework

**Standard I(A): Knowledge of the Law**

Core Rule: Members must understand and comply with ALL applicable laws, rules, and regulations (including the Code and Standards). When laws conflict → follow the MORE STRICT requirement. Must not knowingly participate in or assist violations. Must dissociate from violations.

Decision Framework — Global Application Matrix (CRITICAL for exam):
- Member in country with NO securities laws (NS), does business in country with LESS strict laws (LS) → follow Code and Standards (C&S is strictest)
- Member in LS country, does business in MORE strict country (MS) → follow MS country law (local law is strictest)
- Member in MS country, does business in LS country → follow MS country law (home law is strictest)
- Key principle: ALWAYS identify which law applies, then determine which is MORE STRICT → follow that one
- "Applicable law" = law of the locality where business is conducted, which may differ from where the member resides
- When MS law says law of client's home country governs AND client's home country law is stricter than C&S → follow that stricter law

Dissociation Escalation Ladder (practical decision model):
Step 1: Attempt to stop the behavior directly (discuss with perpetrator)
Step 2: Report to supervisor or compliance department
Step 3: If unsuccessful → dissociate from the activity (remove name from reports, refuse assignments, refuse to accept client)
Step 4: If firm continues violation → may need to resign employment
Step 5: Consider reporting to regulator (encouraged but not required by C&S; may be required by local law)
WARNING: Inaction combined with continuing association = potential participation in the violation

Key Decision Rules from Examples:
1. Legal counsel says violation is hard to prove → does NOT absolve you → still must report to supervisor, seek independent opinion, determine if regulator should be notified
2. Firm advertises composite omitting departed accounts → you discover it → must dissociate → refuse to use inflated materials → escalate to compliance → if firm refuses to correct, consider leaving
3. Working in developing country with no insider trading laws → C&S is stricter → must follow C&S including Standard II(A) on MNPI
4. Registered adviser in foreign jurisdiction → must follow THAT jurisdiction's laws even if they are stricter than your home country's laws → Karramba IPO example: local law prohibits adviser from personal IPO participation → MUST comply even though US law might allow it
5. Religious/cultural laws (e.g., Islamic law) → when distributing globally, proactively disclose where fund may not be suitable for investors with specific legal/religious requirements
6. Social media communications → must comply with CURRENT regulatory requirements → failure to stay current on evolving regulations = violation
7. Potential unethical broker payments → follow firm's reporting procedures → advocate for disclosure → document concerns

**Standard I(B): Independence and Objectivity**

Core Rule: Must use reasonable care to maintain independence and objectivity. Must not offer, solicit, or accept any gift/benefit/compensation that could reasonably compromise independence or objectivity.

Threat Categories to Independence (decision model for identifying violations):
1. Buy-side pressure: Portfolio managers pressuring sell-side analysts to maintain favorable ratings to protect positions → analyst must base opinion solely on fundamentals
2. Investment banking pressure: IB colleagues pushing favorable research on current/prospective IB clients → firm must maintain firewalls; analyst compensation must NOT be linked to IB assignments
3. Issuer pressure: Company management threatening to cut off access if negative report issued → analyst must maintain objectivity regardless
4. Intrafirm pressure: Sales department pushing analyst to recommend bonds firm has in inventory → must refuse unless market price already reflects known issues
5. Performance measurement pressure: Internal request to remove underperforming accounts from composite → must refuse (links to GIPS Section 22)
6. Manager selection/procurement influence: Pay-to-play schemes, gifts to pension fund decision-makers → BOTH sides violate I(B)

Gift/Entertainment Decision Framework:
- From NON-CLIENT (brokers, vendors, issuers): Must be modest, token items only → lavish gifts (World Cup tickets, resort trips) = VIOLATION even if disclosed → best practice is strict firm value limit
- From CLIENT: May accept bonuses/gifts IF disclosed to employer → client gifts are deemed less likely to impair objectivity because compensation relationship already exists → BUT supervisor must monitor for favoritism patterns
- Travel funding: Best practice = commercial transportation at own/firm's expense → accept modest travel ONLY when commercial unavailable AND for legitimate information-gathering (e.g., property tour)
- Manager selection: Must not solicit gifts/donations from those seeking mandates → political contributions to pension fund decision-makers = pay-to-play violation

Issuer-Paid Research Rules:
- Independent analysts must STRICTLY limit compensation type → accept ONLY flat fee NOT linked to conclusions or recommendations
- Must fully disclose nature of compensation and potential conflicts
- Must conduct thorough, independent, unbiased analysis
- Must distinguish between fact and opinion
- Compensation through stock warrants or equity instruments that appreciate with positive coverage = creates incentive to bias → VIOLATION

Recommended Procedures for Firms:
- Protect integrity of opinions (policy that research reflects unbiased analyst opinion)
- Create restricted list when firm unwilling to permit dissemination of adverse opinions
- Restrict special cost arrangements (pay for own transportation to issuer meetings)
- Limit gifts (strict value limits based on local customs)
- Restrict employee IPO participation
- Independence policy with separate reporting structures for research vs. IB
- Appoint senior compliance officer

**Standard I(C): Misrepresentation**

Core Rule: Must not KNOWINGLY make misrepresentations relating to investment analysis, recommendations, actions, or other professional activities. "Knowingly" = knew or should have known.

Scope of Misrepresentation (broader than most candidates realize):
- Guaranteeing returns on volatile investments → ALWAYS a violation (cannot guarantee specific return on equities, cannot guarantee no loss)
- EXCEPTION: May describe guarantees built into product structure (e.g., principal-protected notes) or institutional guarantees
- Omitting material information from models/reports → misrepresentation by omission
- Cherry-picking accounts for composites → misrepresents firm's ability (links to GIPS Section 22)
- Presenting out-of-date fact sheets → misrepresents current fund characteristics
- Using only one outperforming fund to represent firm's overall results → overemphasis = potential misrepresentation
- Shopping for higher valuations of illiquid securities → misrepresents value, leads to overcharging advisory fees

Performance Reporting Traps (links to Sections 18, 22):
- Benchmark must be comparable to strategy → using inappropriate benchmark = misrepresentation
- Hedge fund using "cash plus" benchmark → must disclose reasoning
- Non-traded securities valued from multiple sources → switching providers to get higher value = misrepresentation

Plagiarism — 5 Forms (all violations):
1. Taking report/study and releasing as own work (most flagrant)
2. Using excerpts verbatim without acknowledgment
3. Citing "leading analysts" without naming specific sources
4. Presenting statistical forecasts without identifying sources or caveats
5. Copying proprietary models/algorithms without authorization

Work Completed for Employer Exception: Firm owns research created during employment → firm may continue using after analyst leaves WITHOUT attribution → BUT member cannot reissue previously released report solely under their own name at a new firm

Social Media: Same standards apply online as offline → perceived anonymity does not excuse misrepresentation → must ensure all social media communications comply

**Standard I(D): Misconduct**

Core Rule: Must not engage in professional conduct involving dishonesty, fraud, or deceit, or commit any act that reflects adversely on professional reputation, integrity, or competence.

Scope: Covers ALL conduct reflecting poorly on professional integrity — broader than I(A) which focuses on law compliance. I(D) includes behavior that may be legal but damages professional reputation.

Key Distinctions:
- Personal bankruptcy from fraudulent/deceitful business conduct → violation
- Personal bankruptcy from circumstances beyond control → NOT a violation per se
- Alcohol/substance abuse affecting job performance → violation (adversely reflects on competence)
- Civil disobedience for personal beliefs (environmental protests) → generally NOT a violation if does not reflect poorly on professional reputation
- Insurance fraud (altering reimbursement forms) → violation even though not directly investment-related
- Suspecting hedge fund is not properly marking to market → must pursue through firm's reporting procedures → if all internal channels fail → consider reporting to regulator

**Standard I(E): Competence (NEW in 2023 Revision)**

Core Rule: Must act with and maintain the competence necessary to fulfill professional responsibilities. Not just about achieving competence but MAINTAINING it as responsibilities evolve.

Competence = Knowledge + Skills + Abilities:
- Knowledge: Body of information applied to function
- Skills: Capabilities for role-specific tasks
- Abilities: Capabilities and attitudes supporting outcomes

Critical Decision Rules:
- Passing licensing exam alone does NOT demonstrate competency if you didn't actually learn the material → must have genuine understanding
- Promotion to supervisory role → must develop NEW competencies (management, compliance oversight) beyond prior technical skills
- Client requests investment in unfamiliar asset class (crypto) → must FIRST develop competence before investing → hastily investing in trending products without understanding = violation
- Failure to uncover fraud that misled the entire industry → does NOT indicate incompetence IF research was thorough and appropriate
- ESG integration into research → must develop actual ESG competence (hire specialists, attend seminars, obtain certifications) before incorporating into reports

### 23.2 Standard II: Integrity of Capital Markets — Complete Framework

**Standard II(A): Material Nonpublic Information (MNPI)**

Core Rule: Must not act on or cause others to act on material nonpublic information that could affect the value of an investment.

Materiality Test (2 prongs, BOTH must be met for MNPI):
1. MATERIAL: Would disclosure probably impact security price? Would reasonable investor want to know before making investment decision? Information that would significantly alter the total mix of information currently available.
2. NONPUBLIC: Has information been disseminated to the marketplace in general (not just a select group)?

Materiality Factors:
- Specificity of information (more specific → more likely material)
- Extent of difference from public information
- Nature of information (earnings, M&A, management changes)
- Reliability of source (corporate insider = highly reliable → more likely material; competitor rumor = less reliable → less likely material)
- Ambiguity of price impact (ambiguous → less material)
- Passage of time (information can become immaterial over time)

Material Information Includes: Earnings, M&A, asset changes, new products/discoveries, licensing/patents, customer/supplier developments, management changes, auditor changes, securities events (defaults, dividends, stock splits), bankruptcies, legal disputes, government economic data (pre-release), large pending trades, rating changes.

"Nonpublic" Determination:
- Posted on internet via press release/filing → PUBLIC
- Disclosed to room full of analysts in private briefing → still NONPUBLIC (not available to general marketplace)
- Selective disclosure to top shareholders → NONPUBLIC until broadly disseminated
- Social media platforms requiring membership → verify info is also accessible through public sources before treating as public

Mosaic Theory (CRITICAL — most tested concept):
- Analysts may use significant conclusions derived from public AND nonmaterial nonpublic information
- Even if those conclusions WOULD have been material inside information had they been communicated directly by the company
- Analyst skill in piecing together information = the value they provide → must NOT be penalized for reaching correct conclusions through legitimate research
- KEY: Must save and document ALL research supporting mosaic theory conclusions → evidence strengthens assertion that conclusions were reached through appropriate methods
- Example: Analyst visits furniture stores, talks to designers, checks public financial reports → concludes company will miss earnings → this is MOSAIC, not MNPI, even though conclusion matches what insider would know

Expert Networks — Decision Rules:
- Paying experts for industry insights → PERMITTED
- Expert provides actual confidential product test results → MNPI → cannot trade even if expert signed disclosure agreement → member must independently assess whether information received is material and nonpublic
- Analyst's own recommendation, if from influential analyst → the recommendation itself may be material → but analyst reached conclusions through legitimate research → not required to make report public → clients who paid for research get it first

Recommended Procedures — Firewalls:
- Substantial control of interdepartmental communications (clearance area in compliance/legal)
- Watch, restricted, and rumor lists for employee trading monitoring
- Physical separation of departments handling sensitive information
- Prevention of personnel overlap between IB and research
- Reporting system with designated compliance officer for "looking over the wall"
- Restrict proprietary trading when firm has MNPI (but total prohibition on market-making is inappropriate → market makers remain passive, take only contra side of unsolicited customer trades)

**Standard II(B): Market Manipulation**

Core Rule: Must not engage in practices that distort prices or artificially inflate trading volume to mislead market participants.

Two Types:
1. Information-Based Manipulation: Spreading false rumors, "pumping" stock with misleading positive information, issuing speculative research timed to exploit quiet periods
2. Transaction-Based Manipulation: Wash trading between own accounts to create artificial volume, trading between related funds to manipulate price, securing dominant position to exploit derivatives pricing

Key Distinction: Intent matters → legitimate trading strategies based on perceived market inefficiencies ≠ manipulation even if they impact prices → manipulation requires intent to deceive or mislead

Decision Rules from Examples:
- Pump-and-dump via online chat rooms → violation even if disclosures made IF intent is to mislead
- Analyst exaggerates earnings estimate and selectively previews to momentum clients to force company response → violation of BOTH II(B) and III(B) Fair Dealing
- Trading between own funds to increase volume/price of thinly traded stock → violation even if "beneficial for fund participants"
- Exchange arranging minimum volume commitments to demonstrate liquidity → violation UNLESS fully disclosed (disclosure transforms from manipulation to legitimate marketing)
- Manipulating model inputs to achieve desired structured product rating → violation with systemic consequences (2008 crisis parallel)
- Creating false online profiles to spread acquisition rumors → violation even if no personal profit and intent was to harm portfolio manager

### 23.3 Standard III: Duties to Clients — Complete Framework

**Standard III(A): Loyalty, Prudence, and Care**

Core Rule: Duty of loyalty to clients; must act with reasonable care and exercise prudent judgment; must place client interests before employer's or own interests.

Fiduciary Duty Hierarchy (decision model):
1. Determine: Am I in a fiduciary relationship? (managing assets, advisory relationship, custody of client funds)
2. If YES → fiduciary duty applies IN ADDITION to Standard III(A) → follow the more strict requirement
3. If NO → Standard III(A) still requires loyalty, prudence, and care as minimum benchmark
4. KEY: Standard III(A) does NOT render all members fiduciaries → but DOES require working in client's best interest regardless

Identifying the Client (often tested, frequently misidentified):
- Individual portfolio management → client = the individual
- Pension plan/trust management → client = the BENEFICIARIES (not the person/entity who hired you)
- Fund managed to index/mandate → duty of loyalty, prudence, care to invest consistent with stated mandate → client loyalty and care for individual fund investors is responsibility of advisers with advisory relationships
- Performance analyst for firm → client may be the investing public as a whole
- Company executive relationship → public shareholders may be the relevant client

Soft Commission Rules:
- Using client brokerage to purchase research services without corresponding client benefit → VIOLATION of loyalty
- Directed brokerage (client directs specific broker) → not a loyalty violation per se → BUT must seek "best price" and "best execution" → must disclose that directed brokerage may not achieve best execution
- "Best execution" = trading process that maximizes portfolio value within stated objectives and constraints

Proxy Voting:
- Duty of loyalty includes voting proxies in informed, responsible manner
- Proxies have economic value → must not blindly vote with management
- Cost-benefit analysis may show some proxies not worth voting → must disclose proxy voting policies to clients

Recommended Procedures for Firms (comprehensive checklist):
1. Follow all applicable rules and laws
2. Establish client investment objectives (reasonable inquiry into experience, risk/return, financial constraints)
3. Consider all information when taking actions (client needs, investment characteristics, total portfolio)
4. Diversify investments to reduce risk of loss
5. Carry out regular reviews
6. Deal fairly with all clients
7. Disclose conflicts of interest
8. Disclose compensation arrangements
9. Vote proxies in clients' best interests
10. Maintain confidentiality
11. Seek best execution
12. Place client interests first

**Standard III(B): Fair Dealing**

Core Rule: Must deal FAIRLY and objectively with all clients when providing investment analysis, recommendations, taking investment action, or engaging in other professional activities. Note: "fairly" ≠ "equally" — cannot reach all clients simultaneously, but must not discriminate.

Two Categories:
1. Investment Recommendations: Dissemination of new/changed recommendations must give all clients fair opportunity to act
2. Investment Action: Portfolio management actions (IPO allocations, block trades) must treat all clients fairly

Key Decision Rules:
- Sharing recommendation with select clients at luncheon before general distribution → VIOLATION
- After broad email distribution, calling three largest institutional clients to discuss in detail → NOT a violation (already widely disseminated; premium service disclosed and available to all)
- Giving commingled fund priority over pension fund accounts for same strategy → VIOLATION (both are clients)
- IPO oversubscribed → must prorate among all suitable clients; forgo own/family allocation; use round-lot basis to avoid odd-lot distributions
- Allocating profitable MBS trades to favored client after the fact (delayed trade tickets) → VIOLATION of III(B) and potential III(A)
- Social media post of recommendation before distribution to all clients → VIOLATION (see also IV(C) supervisory duty)
- Minimum lot allocations when issue is oversubscribed → NOT a violation if each client gets at least minimum lot and allocation is proportionate

Recommended Procedures:
- Limit people who know about pending recommendation changes
- Shorten time between decision and dissemination
- Publish guidelines for pre-dissemination behavior
- Simultaneous dissemination (all branch offices, all client types)
- Maintain client/holdings lists for notification of changes
- Written, documented trade allocation procedures (FIFO, pro rata, block trade with same price)
- Systematic account review to detect favoritism
- Disclose different service levels (must be available to everyone, not selectively offered)

**Standard III(C): Suitability**

Core Rule: Two contexts — (1) advisory relationship: must gather client info and ensure investment is suitable given IPS; (2) mandate management: must invest consistent with stated objectives/constraints.

IPS Required Elements (CRITICAL — links to Section 10 Private Wealth):
- Client identification (type, nature, beneficiaries, portion of total assets managed)
- Return objectives (income, growth, maintenance of purchasing power)
- Risk tolerance (ability AND willingness — the LOWER of the two governs)
- Investment constraints: liquidity, time horizon, tax considerations, legal/regulatory, unique circumstances
- Performance benchmarks

Key Decision Rules:
- Same recommendation for 60-year-old aggressive investor AND 40-year-old conservative investor → VIOLATION for the conservative client (must tailor to each IPS)
