- Covered calls on risk-averse client's equity portfolio → NOT a violation IF properly explained in context of entire portfolio characteristics
- IPS says only liquid investments → PM invests 4% in PE seed fund → VIOLATION of BOTH III(A) and III(C) even though equity exposure stays within limit
- PM shifts to high-beta stocks driven by new compensation system, not IPS changes → VIOLATION
- Unsuitable client request → must discuss concerns → educate on how request deviates from IPS → at minimum, client must acknowledge and accept conditions → if material impact, update IPS → if client refuses to update and insists, consider terminating advisory relationship
- Managing fund to index/mandate → NOT responsible for suitability of fund for individual investors → only responsible for investing consistent with mandate
- Selecting submanager based solely on lowest fee → potential VIOLATION (must evaluate risk profile, not just cost)

**Standard III(D): Performance Presentation**

Core Rule: Must make reasonable efforts to ensure performance information is fair, accurate, and complete. Must not misstate performance or mislead about what clients can expect.

Links to GIPS (Section 22): Compliance with GIPS = best method to meet III(D) obligations.

Key Decision Rules:
- Claiming "25% annual compound growth" when it occurred in only one year → VIOLATION
- Presenting prior firm's track record without disclosing it was earned elsewhere → VIOLATION
- Using simulated/backtested results without identifying them as simulated → VIOLATION
- Cherry-picking accounts for composite (excluding underperformers, changing composition over time) → VIOLATION
- Changing attribution methodology without notification to make results look better → VIOLATION (must report both old and new if genuinely improved)
- Inconsistently using TWR vs. MWR (whichever looks better that period) → VIOLATION
- Choosing valuation date for month-end report to show highest NAV → VIOLATION

**Standard III(E): Preservation of Confidentiality**

Core Rule: Must keep client information confidential UNLESS: (1) illegal activities by client, (2) disclosure required by law, (3) client permits disclosure.

Applies to: Current, former, AND prospective clients.

Key Decision Rules:
- Using client's charitable donation plans to solicit donation for your own charity → VIOLATION (even with good intentions)
- Client suspected of embezzlement → check with compliance/legal counsel whether securities regulations require reporting
- Government investigation of client → may have duty to disclose if law requires it
- Social media group for clients → warn that posts are publicly viewable → promptly remove accidentally posted confidential info → not a violation if reasonable precautions taken and responded promptly
- CFA Institute Professional Conduct Program investigation → providing client information to PCP is NOT a violation of III(E)

### 23.4 Standard IV: Duties to Employers — Complete Framework

**Standard IV(A): Loyalty**

Core Rule: Must act for employer's benefit; must not deprive employer of skills/abilities, divulge confidential information, or cause harm. But: client interests ALWAYS come first (III(A) trumps IV(A) when they conflict).

Leaving an Employer — Decision Framework:
PERMITTED before leaving: Making preparations (registering new business, renting office) on own time
PROHIBITED before leaving: Soliciting employer's clients, taking records/files/client lists, competing for employer's RFPs
AFTER leaving:
- Skills, experience, knowledge of client names → NOT confidential → can use at new employer
- Firm records, files, computer models, client lists → EMPLOYER PROPERTY → must return, erase from personal devices
- Contacting former clients → PERMITTED if: (a) using public information only, (b) no noncompete agreement, (c) maintained loyalty until departure, (d) did not use employer records
- Noncompete agreements → must review and understand restrictions → may limit what you can do

Whistleblowing Exception: When employer engages in illegal/unethical activity → member's duty to market integrity and client interests MAY justify actions normally violating IV(A) (contradicting employer, copying records as evidence) → BUT only if intent is to protect clients/market, not personal gain

Social Media — Key Issues:
- Firm-approved business accounts → firm property → must transfer/delete per firm policy
- Personal accounts with client connections → discuss with employer before departure → follow firm's departure notification policy
- LinkedIn update after leaving → timing matters → update AFTER employer has notified clients

Independent Practice: Must notify employer and receive consent BEFORE rendering independent services for compensation. "Practice" = any service employer currently makes available.

**Standard IV(B): Additional Compensation Arrangements**

Core Rule: Must not accept gifts, benefits, compensation, or consideration from third parties that competes with or creates a conflict with employer's interest UNLESS written consent from ALL parties.

Key Decision Rules:
- Client offers vacation to PM if portfolio returns 15%+ → must disclose in writing to employer AND get consent before accepting
- Nonmonetary compensation (gym membership, board seats with perks) → still requires disclosure → nonmonetary ≠ exempt
- Issuer invites analyst to dinner while on company visit → may accept IF: (a) got firm approval for meeting, (b) declined offered travel, (c) disclosed dinner to firm upon return

**Standard IV(C): Responsibilities of Supervisors**

Core Rule: Must make reasonable efforts to ensure anyone under your supervision complies with applicable laws, rules, regulations, firm policies, and the Code and Standards.

Supervisory Duty = PREVENTION + DETECTION:
- Must establish compliance procedures BEFORE violations occur (not reactive)
- Must take steps to DETECT violations (not just prohibit them)
- Once violation discovered → investigate immediately, increase oversight, place limits on employee → relying on assurances alone = insufficient
- If firm's compliance system is inadequate AND supervisor cannot get it fixed → must decline supervisory responsibility IN WRITING
- Delegating supervision to subordinates → must instruct them on compliance methods → ultimate responsibility remains with supervisor

Key Decision Rules:
- Supervisor distributes subordinate's research memo without reviewing → subordinate used MNPI → supervisor violated IV(C)
- VP notices unusual trading volume but takes no action because her compensation depends on trading revenue → VIOLATION (conflict of interest compounding supervisory failure)
- Social media post of buy recommendation before firm distribution → supervisor who didn't establish social media policies violated IV(C)
- Subordinate submits plagiarized report late at night; supervisor rushed → skipping review process still violates IV(C)

### 23.5 Standard V: Investment Analysis, Recommendations, and Actions — Complete Framework

**Standard V(A): Diligence and Reasonable Basis**

Core Rule: Must (1) exercise diligence, independence, and thoroughness in analysis; (2) have a reasonable and adequate basis for any recommendation or action.

Diligence Factors (what constitutes reasonable basis depends on role):
- Analyst role: Company reports, industry data, financial models, macro conditions, peer comparisons, management assessment, quantitative model limitations
- Portfolio manager role: Understanding model parameters, testing output against multiple scenarios, considering tail risks
- Manager selector role: Ethics code review, compliance procedures, published return quality, strategy adherence, operational DD
- Group researcher: May disagree with consensus but need not dissociate if process was reasonable and independent

Key Decision Rules:
- Pricing IPOs by company size alone without fundamental research → VIOLATION
- Presenting maximum production as base case → VIOLATION (must disclose range of scenarios with probabilities)
- Report based only on a few news articles and market wisdom → VIOLATION (insufficient basis)
- Third-party research: Can rely on it IF firm conducted initial due diligence AND maintains ongoing review → but if you have reason to question validity, must not rely
- Quantitative model: Must test with negative scenarios, understand assumptions, validate output → components tested individually but never as integrated whole → VIOLATION (2008 crisis parallel)
- Investment lost 60% after thorough due diligence → NOT a violation per se (reasonable basis ≠ guarantee)
- Fund-of-funds manager selecting based only on GIPS compliance and returns → VIOLATION (must do thorough operational review)
- Credit model developer applies quick fix instead of full data update → model error causes bad trades → BOTH developer AND PM who relied on model without understanding it violated V(A)
- Capacity constraint of $100M exists but fund is at $12M → must STILL disclose to current and prospective investors, even though threshold is far away

**Standard V(B): Communication with Clients and Prospective Clients**

Core Rule: Must (1) disclose nature of services and costs, (2) disclose investment process and material changes, (3) disclose significant risks and limitations, (4) identify important factors in analysis, (5) distinguish fact from opinion.

5 Disclosure Obligations (critical for practice):
1. Nature of services + costs (including third-party costs, affiliate fees, fee calculation methodology changes)
2. Investment process (how securities are analyzed, selected, portfolios constructed) + PROMPTLY disclose MATERIAL CHANGES
3. Significant risks and limitations (leverage, liquidity, capacity, counterparty, model limitations)
4. Important analytical factors (both positive and negative influences on recommendations)
5. Fact vs. opinion distinction (earnings estimates = opinions; current price = fact; geological reserve estimates = quantitative opinion not fact)

Key Decision Rules:
- PE fund charges restructuring fees to portfolio companies → must disclose to fund investors even if they benefit → cannot rely on investor sophistication as excuse not to disclose
- Fee calculation methodology changes from average daily balance to month-end value → must disclose even if it results in lower fees
- Newsletter with buy/sell recommendations but no explanation of process → VIOLATION
- Analyst's reserve estimate presented as fact rather than opinion → VIOLATION
- Small-cap manager lifts cap from $250M to $500M → must notify EXISTING clients (some hired specifically for small-cap expertise)
- Firm switches from individual analyst stock picks to committee-based selection → must disclose to all clients (material process change)
- Losing key investment personnel → must disclose to clients (material change even if not a process change)
- VaR described as "extremely effective" without disclosing it depends on model inputs → VIOLATION

**Standard V(C): Record Retention**

Core Rule: Must develop and maintain records to support investment analyses, recommendations, actions, and communications.

Records are FIRM PROPERTY (not individual's). Must return/erase when leaving employer. To recreate analysis at new firm → must use only public sources and information from covered companies directly.

Default retention: At least 7 years (CFA Institute recommendation in absence of regulatory/firm guidance).

### 23.6 Standard VI: Conflicts of Interest — Complete Framework

**Standard VI(A): Disclosure of Conflicts**

Core Rule: Must avoid or fully disclose all actual and potential conflicts of interest. Disclosures must be prominent, in plain language, and effectively communicated.

Conflict Categories:
1. To Employers: Stock ownership in recommended companies, outside board service, financial pressures that could influence judgment
2. To Clients: Firm's broker/dealer market-making activities, IB relationships with subject companies, fee arrangements with subadvisors, personal stock ownership in recommended securities, compensation structures that incentivize short-term risk-taking
3. Cross-Departmental: IB pressuring research for favorable coverage, marketing requesting analyst recommend stocks firm has in inventory
4. Stock Ownership: Must disclose ANY beneficial ownership in securities being recommended
5. Board Service: Creates 3 conflicts — (a) duty to clients vs. shareholders, (b) stock/options compensation, (c) MNPI access → should be isolated by firewalls

Key Decision Rules:
- Analyst covers company where firm's M&A department has 20-year relationship → must disclose in research report
- Firm sells 25% stake to bank, then president changes recommendation on bank from sell to buy → must disclose ownership and best practice is to discontinue coverage
- Wife inherits $3M in covered company stock → must disclose to employer AND in follow-up report → best practice: have another analyst write the report
- Compensation system creates conflicts between PM's incentives and client IPS → must disclose to clients
- Consultant accepts kickbacks from managers for pension fund allocation → VIOLATION of VI(A) and multiple other standards

**Standard VI(B): Priority of Transactions**

Core Rule: Client and employer transactions MUST have priority over personal transactions where member is beneficial owner.

Beneficial Ownership = own account + family (spouse, children) + any account with direct/indirect pecuniary interest (trusts, retirement accounts).

Key Decision Rules:
- Analyst withholds buy recommendation to purchase personally first → VIOLATION (front-running)
- PM's spouse maintains account at brokerage and gets hot issue shares before fund clients → VIOLATION
- Family member account that is regular fee-paying client → treat SAME as all other client accounts → CANNOT disadvantage simply because family → BUT must preclear if beneficial ownership
- Entry-level employee front-running buy list with personal trades → VIOLATION of VI(B) + supervisor violated IV(C) for not monitoring personal transaction forms
- Firm's trading desk acts on analyst's recommendation before client dissemination → firm violated VI(B)

Recommended Procedures: Limited IPO/private placement participation; blackout/restricted periods; reporting requirements (holdings disclosure, duplicate confirmations, preclearance); disclose personal trading policies to clients.

**Standard VI(C): Referral Fees**

Core Rule: Must disclose to employer, clients, and prospective clients any compensation received from or paid to others for referrals. Must disclose BEFORE entering into formal agreement.

Disclosure must include: Nature of consideration (flat fee, percentage, ongoing), approximate amount, estimated dollar value of noncash benefits.

Key Decision Rules:
- Broker/adviser referral arrangement where adviser gets clients and broker gets commissions → MUST disclose to ALL prospective clients referred
- Internal bank referral fee between departments → same disclosure obligation as external referral
- PM donates portion of fee from referrals to school → NOT a violation IF: disclosed to employer, clients, AND discussed with client at IPS inception
- Consultant accepting kickbacks from managers for pension allocations → VIOLATION regardless of whether recommendation was appropriate

### 23.7 Standard VII: Responsibilities as CFA Member/Candidate — Complete Framework

**Standard VII(A): Conduct as Participants in CFA Programs**

Core Rule: Must not engage in conduct that compromises the reputation/integrity of CFA Institute, the CFA designation, or the integrity/validity/security of CFA programs.

Prohibited Conduct: Cheating, violating testing policies, disclosing confidential exam information, circumventing security measures, improperly using CFA Institute association, misrepresenting Professional Conduct Statement.

Confidential Information (CANNOT share): Specific questions on exam, broad topical areas tested or NOT tested, formulas tested or not tested, question deliberations, grading information. Confidentiality extends until CFA Institute publicly releases information.

Key Decision Rules:
- Blog post after exam saying "derivatives questions were really hard, ethics had lots of questions, surprised no IPO allocation questions" → VIOLATION of VII(A) (revealed content areas tested and not tested)
- Simply saying "exam was difficult but fair" → NOT a violation
- Sharing specific questions on internet forum → VIOLATION by poster AND participants who solicit answers
- Writing after time called → VIOLATION
- GIPS Executive Committee member marketing insider knowledge to clients → VIOLATION
- Scheduling only own clients for CFA society presentations → VIOLATION (using volunteer position for personal benefit)

**Standard VII(B): Reference to CFA Institute, Designation, and Program**

Core Rule: Must not misrepresent or exaggerate meaning/implications of CFA membership, designation, or candidacy.

Proper Usage Rules:
- "CFA" is an ADJECTIVE (describing the charterholder), not a noun → "I am a CFA charterholder" or "I earned the CFA designation" → NEVER "I am a CFA" or "I am CFA-certified"
- "John Smith, CFA" is proper → "John Smith, C.F.A." is improper (no periods)
- CFA cannot be used as a noun or preceded by "a" or "the" when referring to a person
- Candidates: "I am a candidate in the CFA Program" or "I am a Level II candidate in the CFA Program" → NEVER "I am almost a CFA charterholder" or "I expect to receive my CFA charter"
- Must not imply superior performance: "expect superior returns because I have the CFA designation" → VIOLATION
- MAY highlight ethical commitment and rigor of program
- If membership lapses (failed to pay dues, failed to submit PCS) → no longer a member → cannot present as active member → CAN say "was a CFA Institute member in 2010-2020"

### 23.8 Cross-Domain Inference Chains — Ethics in Practice

1. UHNW client with concentrated stock position (Section 10) asks PM to use inside information to time hedging → PM must refuse under II(A) MNPI → must still act in client's best interest under III(A) → should propose alternative hedging strategies (Section 19: collars, prepaid forwards) using only public information → if client insists on using MNPI → must dissociate from that specific instruction while continuing relationship for other services → may need to update IPS under III(C)
Links to: Section 10 (concentrated positions), Section 19 (derivatives overlay)

2. Pension fund trustee (Section 17) pressures manager to buy company stock to prevent hostile takeover → violates III(A) loyalty to plan BENEFICIARIES (not company management) → must evaluate takeover on merits for plan participants → refusing may lead to termination but duty to beneficiaries takes precedence → also potential II(B) market manipulation if purchases are designed to inflate share price → trustee may face fiduciary liability beyond CFA sanctions
Links to: Section 17 (DB pensions), Section 21 (manager selection)

3. Analyst discovers through mosaic theory (public filings + nonmaterial industry conversations) that company will miss earnings significantly → conclusion is material but was reached through legitimate research → CAN share with clients and recommend → must document all research supporting mosaic theory conclusion → if analyst instead received material tip from insider = MNPI even if analyst did independent work → critical distinction: SOURCE of the conclusion matters, not whether the analyst could have reached it independently
Links to: Section 13 (equity analysis), Appendix E (corporate credit)

4. Family office advisor (Section 10) manages accounts for patriarch, adult children, and grandchildren → different IPS for each → hot IPO opportunity → must allocate FAIRLY across all suitable family accounts including brother's account → cannot systematically exclude family member accounts from attractive opportunities → must have written allocation procedures that treat all clients consistently → excessive trading to generate commissions on any family account = violation of both III(A) and III(B)
Links to: Section 10 (UHNW), Section 11 (portfolio construction)

5. PM's compensation structure rewards quarterly outperformance → incentivizes buying high-beta stocks contrary to conservative client IPSs → PM must disclose this conflict under VI(A) → must still follow IPS under III(C) regardless of compensation → if PM changes strategy to high-beta without IPS change = violation of III(C), VI(A), and potentially I(B) if objectivity compromised → firm's supervisors also potentially violating IV(C) if compensation structure systematically encourages violations
Links to: Section 17 (institutional), Section 18 (performance)

### 23.9 Module 2 Practice Problem Decision Rules (Q1-39)

1. No applicable local law → C&S governs (I(A)). When local law is LESS strict → follow C&S. When local law is MORE strict → follow local law. Answer: A
2. Issuer offers to pay all travel expenses for analyst visit → BEST course is to pay for ALL expenses yourself (I(B)). Accepting and disclosing is second-best. Accepting without disclosure is violation.
3. IB department asks brokerage to change sell to buy on IB client → must refuse AND best policy is FIREWALLS between IB and research (I(B)). Not: assign new analyst (doesn't address conflict), not: just change one increment.
4. Manager hosting conference offers speaker: travel + staff registrations + golf outing → LEAST LIKELY to violate I(B): accept conference expenses only (A). Golf outing and team registrations = inducements beyond speaking engagement.
5. "Bonds guaranteed by US government, default risk virtually zero" (fact) + "You will earn 10% rate of return" (guarantee) → Statement II violates I(C) Misrepresentation. Statement I is factual. Guaranteeing specific return on mutual fund = violation.
6. Average return of 26% technically true but driven by ONE client's penny stock gain; without it, return = 8% → MISREPRESENTATION (I(C)/III(D)). Technically true ≠ fair/accurate/complete.
7. PM gets better personal prices from broker, files reports only in no-trade months → violated THREE standards: III(A) loyalty, I(D) misconduct, IV(C) supervisory failure by Bates. Owning same securities as clients (B) is NOT a violation per se.
8. Mosaic theory = analyst may use MATERIAL public + NONMATERIAL nonpublic info → answer B is most precise description.
9. IB department has MNPI → best policy = physical/informational FIREWALLS (B), not permanent prohibition (A) or mere monitoring (C).
10. Interview candidate reveals pending merger (MNPI) → BEST action: place on RESTRICTED LIST (C). Not: wait a day (still MNPI), not: tell all managers (spreading MNPI).
11. CFO tells friend about exceptional quarter → friend buys ETF containing the stock → VIOLATION by Hart (A). Investing in ETF does NOT insulate from MNPI violation if purpose was to profit from inside info. Rodriguez also violated II(A) by sharing.
12. Bank president buys 4x normal volume to inflate price for merger → MARKET MANIPULATION II(B). Press release about seeking merger partner (A) and reaffirming earnings guidance (C) are legitimate disclosures.
13. Pension fund manager → duty owed to PARTICIPANTS AND BENEFICIARIES (B), not management (A) or shareholders (C).
14. Company president asks to vote profit-sharing plan shares his way; PM investigates independently and concludes management slate IS better → NOT a violation because PM reached independent conclusion through own analysis. Directed brokerage to firm providing best execution + useful research for other accounts = also not a violation.
15. Broker offers low-fee execution trades toward commission commitment; soft dollar accounts prohibit bundled research → CAN use low-fee service for those accounts because new service is EXECUTION-ONLY (A). The commission counts toward commitment but doesn't purchase research.
16. Client brokerage commissions may NOT be used for manager's OPERATING EXPENSES (C = violates). Client-directed brokerage (A) is permitted. Using commissions for client-benefit research commensurate with value (B) is correct practice.
17. Using soft dollar account to pay CFA exam fees → VIOLATION because CFA Program is personal/firm expense, not research that directly assists investment decision-making (C).
18. Fair dealing when new recommendation issued → MINIMIZE TIME between decision and dissemination (C). Telling all firm people in advance (A) increases leak risk. Distributing to institutional before individual (B) is discriminatory.
19. Analyst changes recommendation from buy to sell, mails to all clients; next day client calls with contrary buy order → must INFORM client of changed recommendation before accepting (B). Then if client still wants to proceed, may accept.
20. New client meeting, immediately recommends stock without asking about objectives/risk → violated III(C) SUITABILITY — must determine needs, objectives, risk tolerance BEFORE making recommendations (A).
21. Firm policy: add new accounts after first full month; PM adds early to capture acquisition gain → VIOLATION of III(D) (C) — deviated from firm's stated policies to inflate results.
22. Fired team member presents ENTIRE fund's performance as own without disclosing team nature → VIOLATION III(D) (B). Having employer permission to show results (C) doesn't excuse not disclosing he was one of five managers.
23. Endowment trustee's confidential financial information requested by potential donor → MUST NOT send (B) — III(E) Confidentiality. Even if disclosure might benefit fund. Must get trustees' permission first.
24. Newsletter mentioning charity fundraiser and board position → NOT a violation (A). Did not share client information with charity. Newsletter is general communication, not directed solicitation using confidential data.
25. Independent practice → written consent from employer required (B). May compete with employer IF employer consents. May make preparations before leaving.
26. Jackman (former hedge fund manager) offers $10K incentive for adviser who brings $200K+ → Member who is CURRENTLY UNEMPLOYED may take position (C). Cannot solicit current employer's clients. Incentive itself is not a C&S violation.
27. Client offers PM annual bonus based on portfolio performance → must obtain EMPLOYER PERMISSION before accepting (C) — IV(B). Not required to decline outright.
28. Analyst changes from weak hold to strong buy after overhearing rival analyst's contrary opinion at lunch → violated V(A) — did NOT have reasonable basis for changed recommendation (B). Own research supported hold; overhearing one opinion = insufficient basis.
29. Research report presents dividend increase contingent on uncertain legislation as certain fact → violated V(B) by failing to SEPARATE OPINION FROM FACT (B). Not insider trading (A); basing on uncertain info is not itself a violation (C) if disclosed as uncertain.
30. Employee saves work files to home computer; after termination uses them → VIOLATION because did not get permission from employer to keep files (A). Firm records are PROPERTY OF THE FIRM regardless of where stored.
31. Analyst recommending stock; firm holds large position → MOST LIKELY conflict requiring disclosure = firm's substantial stock position in subject company (B). Personal consumer purchases (A) = unlikely material conflict.
32. Analyst asked to write report on company where firm has 20-year IB relationship and officers serve as directors → MAY write report IF discloses all relationships (C). Not required to abstain from opinions (A). Not prohibited from writing (B).
33. Personal transactions and holdings should NOT be kept confidential from employer (C conflicts with recommended procedures). Firms SHOULD disclose policies to clients (A = correct practice). Prior approval for personal trades (B = correct practice).
34. PM wants to sell personal holdings in stock his firm just upgraded to strong buy → free to sell IF he informs firm of intentions (B). Personal needs (tuition) can justify transactions counter to firm recommendations IF three criteria met: client not disadvantaged, no personal benefit from client trades, regulatory compliance.
35. Referral fee disclosure purpose → allows client to EVALUATE PARTIALITY in service recommendations (B). Not to request fee discounts (A). Must be disclosed BEFORE formal agreement (C is wrong about timing).
36. Candidate posts about exam difficulty and mentions specific topics tested → VIOLATION VII(A) by revealing specific areas tested (C). Purchasing third-party material (A) is fine. Expressing opinion on difficulty is fine.
37. Albert's card says "CFA Level II" → VIOLATION (implies partial designation). Tye's promotional material says "participates in CFA Program, completed Levels I and II" → ALSO VIOLATION (implies partial designation by citing levels completed). Both violated (C).
38. Rule states he received "highest possible scores" on topics → VIOLATION VII(B) because CFA Institute does not issue individual topic scores, making the claim unverifiable and misleading (B). Stating three consecutive years of passing is factual (not a violation).
39. Quinn updates resume: "improved skills through CFA Program, eligible for charter upon completing work experience" → did NOT violate (C). Factual statement about skills improvement and eligibility upon meeting requirements.

### 23.10 Application Case Decision Rules (Module 3 — 7 Cases, 30+ Knowledge Checks)

**Case 1: Sovereign Investment Corporation (SIC/NI)**
- SWF CIO makes investment decision "quickly" based on political considerations → VIOLATION V(A) Diligence (no reasonable basis — political not financial analysis)
- Mitigating political bias → rigorous GOVERNANCE FRAMEWORK (B) is best answer, not government approval (which may enhance political influence) or just disclosing conflicts
- Promising maximum return to hedge fund investor when mandate is dual (socioeconomic + return) → VIOLATION III(C) Suitability (assurances inconsistent with mandate)
- Hedge fund partner invests 25% personal portfolio in NI before fund invests → VIOLATION VI(B) Priority of Transactions (front-running)
- "Subadvisers" with no financial experience paid to facilitate government access → MISREPRESENTATION I(C) (reporting bribes as "operating expenses")
- CIO lets CFA membership lapse but continues using designation → VIOLATION VII(B). Junior who KNOWS membership lapsed but stays silent → ALSO violates I(A) (must dissociate from violations)
- Soliciting specific exam questions from students at celebration → VIOLATION VII(A). Sharing general opinion on difficulty → NOT a violation
- Business card saying "CFA, Level I" + expected charter year → VIOLATION VII(B) (implies partial designation and cites expected completion date)

**Case 2: Castle Biotechnology — David Plume**
- Analyst doesn't disclose parent company owns the firm where he works → VIOLATION VI(A) even if disclosed in offering prospectus (must be prominent and in plain language IN THE RESEARCH REPORT)
- Analyst owns CB preferred shares + options → beneficial interest in STRX and APBX through CB's controlling stake → MUST DISCLOSE even though he doesn't directly own shares
- IPO bonus tied to gross proceeds → MUST DISCLOSE I(B) (links analyst compensation to IB assignments) → removes independence
- Selling APBX short while maintaining buy recommendation → VIOLATION VI(A) (conflict of interest requiring disclosure regardless of beneficial ownership claim)

**Case 3: Castle Biotech — Sandra Benning**
- Soliciting former clients via social media AFTER resignation, using personal accounts, no noncompete → NOT a violation of IV(A) (permitted if done after departure)
- $250K signing bonus based on % of former clients who moved → VIOLATION VI(A) (must disclose conflict to clients — bonus could impair objectivity in solicitation)
- Year-end bonus based on clients' IPO participation level → conflict of interest requiring disclosure under VI(A) (NOT additional compensation under IV(B) since it's from employer)
- Allocating IPO shares preferentially to institutional clients who agree to buy more on first trading day → VIOLATION of BOTH II(B) Market Manipulation (tie-in agreement) AND III(B) Fair Dealing
- Threatening to exclude HNW clients from future IPOs if they sell → VIOLATION III(B) Fair Dealing
- Altering client documents and signing on client's behalf → VIOLATION I(D) Misconduct
- Processing withdrawals at husband's request without proper authorization from account holder → VIOLATION III(A) Loyalty (must have power of attorney or co-signer status)

**Case 4: Lionsgate Limited — Tony Hill**
- Marketing material stating fund performance gross of fees → NOT a violation III(D) if clearly disclosed as "gross of fees" or "pre-fees"
- TV show "thank you gift bags" from sponsors → NOT a violation IV(B) if no conflict with employer's interests
- Martin and Hill both publicly state Hill is in charge when Martin actually makes decisions → VIOLATION V(B) by BOTH (misrepresenting investment decision-making process)
- Telling team about resignation AFTER board asked for confidentiality → VIOLATION IV(A) Loyalty (disregarded employer's confidentiality request)
- Asking team to join new firm → NOT a violation of IV(A) (departing employee may invite colleagues)
- Leasing office space and registering on weekends → NOT a violation of IV(A) (preparations for competitive business on own time)
- Telling team to solicit clients on opening day (not before) → NOT a violation of IV(A) (solicitation begins after resignation effective)

**Case 5: Lionsgate — Rob Portman, Kirk Graeme**
- Salesperson invites portfolio manager to client meetings and calls research director "assistant" → VIOLATION I(C) Misrepresentation (misleading clients about who is responsible for investment decisions)
- CIO and board members sell shares before Hill's resignation announced → VIOLATION II(A) MNPI (pending departure of key person is material nonpublic information)
- Portman sells shares after learning from public media that CIO/board sold → NOT a violation (mosaic theory — information was now publicly disseminated via Sky News)
- Adviser receives commission from issuer for new issues, only discloses when clients ask → VIOLATION VI(A) (must proactively disclose to ALL clients)
- Low risk tolerance client's account filled with new issues held < 5 months → VIOLATION III(C) Suitability (risk tolerance, not time horizon, is the binding constraint)
- Supervisor escalates but doesn't investigate → VIOLATION IV(C) (must INITIATE INVESTIGATION, not just escalate or accept assurances)
- 5-year-old IPS never updated → VIOLATION III(C) (must update at least annually and before material changes)
- Supervisor receives complaint, calls meeting where issue is resolved → NOT a violation of IV(C) (proper investigation initiated promptly)

**Ethical Decision-Making Framework (Critical — always use on exam):**
Step 1: IDENTIFY — Relevant facts, stakeholders, duties owed, ethical principles, conflicts
Step 2: CONSIDER — Situational influences, additional guidance, alternative actions
Step 3: DECIDE and ACT
Step 4: REFLECT — Was outcome as anticipated? Why or why not? (Review improves future decisions)

Limitation: The CFA Institute Ethics standards, while comprehensive, have genuine limitations for real-world application: (1) the mosaic theory defense is difficult to prove retroactively — firms should maintain contemporaneous research records but few do so systematically; (2) "materiality" is inherently subjective — what constitutes material information is judged by a "reasonable investor" standard that varies by context; (3) enforcement relies heavily on self-policing and industry cooperation — CFA Institute can sanction members but cannot impose legal penalties; (4) the standards assume binary violation/non-violation outcomes, but real situations often exist in gray areas where reasonable professionals disagree; (5) cross-border application creates genuine conflicts when local laws differ significantly from C&S, and the "follow the stricter rule" framework doesn't always resolve ambiguity; (6) social media has outpaced the standards — guidance on digital communications, algorithmic signals, and AI-generated research is still evolving.

### 23.11 Asset Manager Code of Professional Conduct (AMC) — Complete Framework

*Source: L3V5 Module 4 (pp 281-324)*

**Purpose:** Voluntary code for FIRMS (not individuals). Extends C&S from individual conduct to firm-level policies and procedures. Applies to firms managing client assets as separate accounts or pooled funds (including hedge funds, mutual funds, fund of funds). Key distinction: C&S applies to individual CFA members/candidates; AMC applies to firms that voluntarily adopt it.

**Adoption Rules:**
- Must comply with ALL provisions — NO partial compliance (cannot say "we comply with AMC except...")
- Must make compliance statement: "[Firm] claims compliance with the CFA Institute Asset Manager Code of Professional Conduct. This claim has not been verified by CFA Institute."
- Must notify CFA Institute of compliance claim (for information only — CFA Institute does NOT verify or enforce)
- Existing firm codes need not be amended if already consistent with AMC principles

**6 General Principles of Conduct — Managers Must:**
1. Act in a professional and ethical manner at all times
2. Act for the benefit of clients
3. Act with independence and objectivity
4. Act with skill, competence, and diligence
5. Communicate with clients in a timely and accurate manner
6. Uphold the applicable rules governing capital markets

**AMC Detailed Provisions (6 sections, A-F):**

**A. Loyalty to Clients (3 provisions):**
1. Place client interests before own → compensation must align financial interests of clients and managers
2. Preserve confidentiality of client information → create privacy policy → anti-money-laundering policy required
3. Refuse gifts/entertainment that could affect independence/objectivity → written policy with specific limits, prohibit cash gifts

**B. Investment Process and Actions (6 provisions):**
1. Reasonable care and prudent judgment in managing assets
2. No market manipulation (transaction-based or information-based)
3. Deal fairly and objectively with ALL clients → side-letter/sidecar arrangements permitted IF fairly allocated among similarly situated clients
4. Reasonable and adequate basis for investment decisions → passive strategy has different basis than active → may rely on third-party research IF diligent review conducted
5. When managing to specific mandate: (a) take only actions consistent with stated objectives/constraints; (b) disclose proposed changes so investors can evaluate suitability → clients should be able to redeem without penalty if material strategy change
6. When managing separate accounts: (a) evaluate investment objectives, risk tolerance, time horizon, liquidity, financial constraints, unique circumstances; (b) determine investment is suitable for client's financial situation → IPS required, reviewed at least annually

**C. Trading (5 provisions):**
1. No MNPI trading → firewalls required → mosaic theory permitted
2. Client priority over manager's own interests → no front-running → personal trading monitoring required → pre-approval for IPOs/private placements → restricted/watch lists
3. Soft commissions only for investment-related products that directly assist investment decision-making → NOT for firm management expenses
4. Best execution for all client transactions → consider transaction size, market characteristics, liquidity, security type → directed brokerage: warn client in writing it may limit best execution
5. Fair and equitable trade allocation → block trades, pro-rata, average price → IPO allocation policies must be fair → no preferential treatment

**D. Risk Management, Compliance, and Support (7 provisions):**
1. Compliance policies and procedures aligned with Code and all laws/regulations
2. Appoint compliance officer → independent from investment and operations → report to CEO or board → document breaches and disciplinary actions
3. Independent third-party confirmation/review of portfolio information
4. Record retention → at least 7 years → hard copy or electronic
5. Qualified staff and sufficient technological resources
6. Business continuity plan → off-site backup, alternative trading systems, communication plans for employees/vendors/clients during disruption
7. Firmwide risk management process → identify, measure, manage risk → stress tests, scenario analysis, backtests → risk management must be independent from portfolio management

**E. Performance and Valuation (2 provisions):**
1. Fair, accurate, relevant, timely, complete performance → no cherry-picking → hypothetical/backtested must be clearly identified → GIPS compliance recommended
2. Fair-market prices for valuation → good-faith fair value methods for illiquid securities → independent third-party valuation recommended to avoid conflict (fees based on AUM create incentive to inflate valuations)

**F. Disclosures (4 main + 12 specific items):**
1. Ongoing and timely communication
2. Truthful, accurate, complete, understandable disclosures
3. Include all material facts
4. Specifically disclose: (a) conflicts of interest, (b) regulatory/disciplinary actions, (c) investment process including lock-ups/strategies/risk factors/derivatives/leverage, (d) management fees and ALL costs with methodology, (e) soft/bundled commissions and how they benefit client, (f) performance on regular basis (quarterly minimum, within 30 days of quarter end, gross and net of fees), (g) valuation methods, (h) shareholder voting policies, (i) trade allocation policies, (j) audit/review results, (k) significant personnel/organizational changes, (l) risk management processes

### 23.12 AMC Practice Problem Decision Rules (Q1-12)

**SZR Case (Q1-6):**
1. Ronoldo broadens mid-cap strategy without disclosing to clients → VIOLATION B-5 (client disclosure of strategy changes). Must disclose BEFORE changing, allow clients to move accounts.
2. Schmidt on board of microcap company that Ronoldo may now buy (widened mandate) → VIOLATION of BOTH A-1 (client priority) and A-3 (participation in business relationships affecting independence). Board service + stock options = conflict requiring disclosure or termination.
3. Sastre directs trading to local adviser, acknowledges in writing → NOT a violation (A-3). Client has right to direct brokerage to less-than-optimal venue for discretionary (non-pension) accounts.
4. Zane routes Sastre trades first through local adviser, then commingled fund trades through national brokers → VIOLATION of BOTH best execution (C-4) AND fair/equitable trade allocation (C-5). Sastre gets first access; other clients disadvantaged.
5. Schmidt reveals Sastre hired SZR and its special fee discount to consultants → VIOLATION A-2 (confidentiality). Client relationship and fee terms are confidential.
6. Karna (brother-in-law, auto parts salesman) as compliance officer + taking home tapes as BCP → BOTH inadequate. CO must be competent/knowledgeable (D-2). BCP must include alternative systems for monitoring/trading (D-6), not just backup of trade records.

**Bornelli Case (Q7-12):**
7. CCO reports to CIO, not CEO/board → VIOLATION D-2 recommendation. CCO should be independent from investment personnel.
8. BCP lacks backup plan for monitoring/analyzing/trading investments → VIOLATION D-6. Large firm with hedge funds needs comprehensive alternative operations plan.
9. Fee disclosure doesn't include average/expected expenses for prospective clients → VIOLATION F-4d. Must disclose methods for determining fixed AND contingent fees AND average/expected costs.
10. Quarterly reporting with gross and net-of-fees returns within 30 days → CONSISTENT with AMC (F-4f). Quarterly is the minimum; AMC does not require monthly unless client requests.
11. Client requests itemized fee breakdown → MUST provide (F-4d). Disclosure should include specific management fee, incentive fee, and commissions paid.
12. Alternative assets fund reports semiannually during lock-up → VIOLATION F-4f. Clients need regular performance information to evaluate overall allocation, even during lock-up. Must report at least quarterly.

### 23.13 Module 3 Practice Problem Decision Rules (Gabby Sim + Additional Cases Q1-47)

**Gabby Sim Case (additional rules from V9 reading):**
- Destroying meeting notes at client's request → VIOLATION V(C) Record Retention. Record retention duty to firm supersedes client's desire for secrecy.
- Not disclosing bonus arrangement from client to employer → VIOLATION IV(B) Additional Compensation + I(B) Independence/Objectivity. Must disclose in writing.
- Opening account without completing suitability questionnaire → VIOLATION III(C) Suitability. Client's fact sheet is insufficient for IPS.
- Supervisor redirects subordinate's concern to board members instead of investigating → VIOLATION IV(C) Supervisory Responsibilities. Delegation doesn't absolve supervisor.
- Client says "just process transactions" but subordinate suspects money laundering → first course = contact firm's COMPLIANCE DEPARTMENT (I(A) recommended procedure). NOT regulator first.
- Client sharing confidential info when client gave permission to discuss with specific board members → NOT a violation III(E) (client permitted disclosure).
- Structured notes sold to client who doesn't understand them, can't read English brochure → VIOLATION III(C) Suitability. Must ensure client understands risks; language barrier doesn't excuse.
- Redeeming structured notes immediately without fully explaining early redemption penalty costs → VIOLATION III(A) Loyalty. Must exercise reasonable care and prudent judgment. 40% loss from penalty that could have been mitigated = failure of care.

**Locke/NGM Pension Fund (Q1-6):**
1. Pension fund → primary duty to plan BENEFICIARIES (B), not trustees or government.
2. Commodities inconsistent with equity IPS → VIOLATION = buying assets inconsistent with IPS (B), not merely lack of experience (buying unknown assets is also problematic but IPS deviation is the primary violation).
3. Fund owed interest for period when cash was removed for IPO overallocation → interest claim is legitimate (C). IPO allocation correction on a backdated basis is proper.
4. Allowing NGM to continue while evaluating → NOT a violation (A). Suspending mid-evaluation would leave assets unmanaged, potentially harming beneficiaries.
5. Trade allocation reviewed monthly against IPS + interest adjustments for errors → YES both comply (A). Monthly review is reasonable; interest adjustments protect clients from allocation errors.
6. BEST course = eliminate NGM (B). Cannot be sure manager will comply with IPS going forward; duty to beneficiaries requires decisive action.

**Nano/Markoe (Q7-12):**
7. Soliciting Patriarch clients AFTER leaving → NOT a violation (B). Was unpaid intern, no noncompete, contacted AFTER departure.
8. C&S for individuals (cannot be adopted by firms), AMC for firms (CAN be adopted) → B is correct (C&S No, AMC Yes).
9. Inadequate compliance system → decline supervisory responsibility IN WRITING (C). Not resign (A) or notify board (B).
10. Terminated accounts excluded from composite → VIOLATION III(D) (A). Must include terminated accounts.
11. Two portfolios for all clients = no individual suitability + only current portfolio holdings conflicts disclosed → Suitability NO, Conflicts NO → Answer A (both wrong).
12. Supervisor must investigate violations (not just warn) + compensation outside the firm requires disclosure → Violations NO, Compensation YES → Answer B.

**Jollie/Dean/Mahsud (Q13-18):**
13. Waited until info became public via news service → NOT a violation (A). MNPI became public when released on worldwide financial news service; no requirement to wait for slowest delivery method.
14. Thinly traded bond → legitimate market order, no intent to manipulate + Mahsud's policy consistent with Standards → Answer A (both consistent). Intent matters for manipulation.
15. Dean paid flat fee by companies (only writes buy reports) + doesn't reveal this → VIOLATION I(B) independence (doesn't disclose issuer-paid compensation structure) → Answer B.
16. Using Dean's summaries with written permission → NO violation. Citing studies found through Dean's posts but reading original and citing original → YES violation (must cite Dean as secondary source) → Answer A (summaries No, citations Yes).
17. "Illegal = unethical" is WRONG (legal actions can still be unethical). "Weak IB/research barrier = conflict source" is CORRECT → Answer B (Statement 1 wrong, Statement 2 correct).
18. Statement 3 says disclose only conflicts employee judges material → WRONG (must disclose ALL actual and potential). Statement 4 says disclose all client gifts → CORRECT (aligns with I(B)) → Answer B (Statement 3 wrong, Statement 4 correct).

**Klein/Finnegan/PlusAccount (Q19-23):**
19. PlusAccount for Vanderon (periodic investor, below-average fee funds) → saves ~$800/year vs. commissions → reasonable basis, suitable → NOT a violation (A).
20. PlusAccount for Brown (active daily trader, $300K, $9,100/year commissions) → saves ~$620/year → reasonable basis → NOT a violation (A).
21. Klein tells Vanderon "As a CFA charterholder, I am the best qualified to manage your investments" → VIOLATION VII(B) (overstates meaning of designation, implies guarantee of competence) → B.
22. Klein acknowledges referral fee from mutual fund but doesn't disclose dollar value/nature of consideration → VIOLATION VI(C) Referral Fees (must disclose whether flat/percentage/continuing + estimated monetary value) → C.
23. Finnegan delegates supervisory to assistant branch manager → delegation OK; BUT fails to ensure annual PlusAccount appropriateness reviews are enforced → VIOLATION IV(C) → B.

**Vinken/Khadri (Q24-29):**
24. Vinken shows BOTH simulated and actual returns, clearly labeled, no guarantee → NOT a violation (A). Proper disclosure makes simulated performance acceptable.
25. Khadri electronic + Vinken hard copy → BOTH consistent with V(C) Record Retention (A). Records can be either format.
26. Khadri claims "partially GIPS compliant" → VIOLATION III(D) (B). GIPS = all or nothing, firmwide only.
27. Statement 1 uses "will" (fact), Statement 2 uses "should" (opinion) → Statement 1 violates V(B), Statement 2 OK → B.
28. Returns without gross/net disclosure → VIOLATION III(D) (C). Must disclose whether performance is gross or net of fees.
29. Khadri knowingly blames computer error when it was human → LEAST LIKELY violated III(D) Performance Presentation because she corrected numbers (A). MORE LIKELY violated I(C) Misrepresentation + I(D) Misconduct.

**Omega/Brown/Chen/Smith/Garcia (Q30-35):**
30. Riskless principal trades: institutional/retail treated differently but disclosed per regulation → NOT a violation (A). Different service levels OK if disclosed and don't negatively affect clients.
31. Brown accepted programmer's report that problems were fixed without independently verifying regulatory compliance → VIOLATION IV(C) (C). Supervisor must verify.
32. System adds $0.01 markup for retail trades despite policy saying no markup → retail violates I(C) + III(B); institutional OK → Only institutional trades comply (B).
33. Morning speakerphone call near squawk box → Smith provides Garcia access to confidential info → duties to BOTH clients and employer violated (C).
34. Garcia eavesdrops on squawk box (deceitful conduct) → VIOLATION I(D) Misconduct (B). NOT II(A) because he didn't ACT on the specifically mentioned stocks. Key: POSSESSING MNPI is not a violation — ACTING on it is.
35. Garcia shorts Tefla after hearing analyst downgrade competitor → NOW acts on MNPI → VIOLATION II(A) (C).

**Riser/Swibank/Komm (Q36-41):**
36. Board position with annual payment → must get WRITTEN consent from employer before accepting (C). Verbal consent insufficient; client consent not required.
37. Road shows for subsidiary → LEAST LIKELY violated IV(B) Additional Compensation (C). Road shows are part of board duties, not separate compensation. MORE likely violated I(B) Independence + VI(A) Conflicts.
38. Recommending Komm's firm while Komm has offered Riser a job → VIOLATION VI(A) (C). Personal relationship + job offer = conflict requiring disclosure.
39. Resigning from Swibank → NOT a violation (A). No improper activities before departure date.
40. Buying Japanese equity product shares for personal account during vacation before starting at Komm → LEAST LIKELY III(C) Suitability (A). Personal account with no advisory relationship. MORE likely II(A) MNPI + VI(B) Priority of Transactions (knew he'd be placing large client orders).
41. Must disclose equity holdings (conflict) + board position (conflict) → need NOT disclose compensation from subsidiary (not a conflict requiring client disclosure) → C.

**Danko/Chau/PIA (Q42-47):**
42. "Level III candidate" AND "passed Level II" → BOTH acceptable under VII(B) (A). Can reference active candidacy and completed levels.
43. Directing trades to FTI for research access → NOT a violation IF FTI provides best execution (A). Soft dollar arrangement permitted when best execution maintained.
44. FTI provides MNPI (investment banking client earnings projections) → Danko must keep confidential and NOT use in analysis (C). Disclosing to public or terminating relationship are not the correct responses.
45. Buying "strong buy" stocks BEFORE performing fundamental analysis (research done retroactively within 5 days) → VIOLATION V(A) Diligence (B). Must have reasonable basis PRIOR to purchase.
46. Excluding wife's account from oversubscribed IPO → NOT a violation (A). When oversubscribed, beneficial-interest accounts must be filled LAST; excluding entirely is conservative but compliant.
47. Procedure 1 (5-day blackout before discretionary client trades) = MOST consistent with Standards (A). Prevents front-running. Standards require a blackout period but don't specify length.

### 23.14 Gabby Sim Extended Case — Deep Decision Framework

**Key Exam Traps from the Complete Gabby Sim Narrative:**

**Trap 1: Fee-sharing ≠ Referral fee.** Paying someone for bringing business is a reward for business development, not a VI(C) referral fee. Referral fee = compensation for recommending products/services TO others.

**Trap 2: Family conflicts require VI(A) disclosure, not I(C).** Chuong's son at Hie's firm = conflict of interest, not misrepresentation. No false statement about investment analysis was made.

**Trap 3: Record retention overrides client confidentiality.** Client requesting note destruction does NOT create a III(E) exception — V(C) duty to maintain records supporting investment recommendations supersedes.

**Trap 4: Double-violation bonus.** Client bonus creates BOTH IV(B) Additional Compensation violation AND I(B) Independence violation. Answer choices offering "only one" are wrong.

**Trap 5: Insufficient IPS even with fact sheet.** Client providing some information (occupation, net worth, risk profile) does NOT satisfy III(C) if full KYC/suitability questionnaire not completed. "I'm buying T-bills" is NOT a substitute for proper IPS.

**Trap 6: Supervisor cannot delegate to conflicted parties.** Yousoff telling Sim to check with board members who BROUGHT the client = NOT reasonable effort under IV(C). Must arrange direct investigation.

**Trap 7: Escalation sequence matters.** Supervisor fails → compliance department first (NOT regulators). C&S do not require regulatory reporting unless law mandates it.

**Trap 8: Client permission = valid confidentiality exception.** Explicit permission to discuss with named parties breaks III(E) obligation for those specific disclosures.

**Trap 9: Language barrier amplifies suitability violation.** Selling complex products to client who cannot read disclosure documents (English brochure to non-English speaker) = heightened III(C) violation.

**Trap 10: Panic ≠ authorization to skip duty of care.** Client demanding immediate action during panic does NOT relieve adviser of III(A) duty to explain costs and investigate market conditions.

## Section 24: Fixed-Income Portfolio Construction — Advanced Topics

### 24.1 Leverage in Fixed-Income Portfolios

**Leverage Mechanisms & Decision Framework:**

Rebate rate = Collateral earnings rate − Security lending rate. When securities are hard to borrow (high demand to short) → rebate rate turns negative → borrower pays fee PLUS foregoes collateral interest. Decision rule: Negative rebate = expensive borrow = crowded short.

**Leveraged Portfolio Return Formula:**
r_P = r_I + (V_B / V_E) × (r_I − r_B)
Where: r_I = return on invested funds, V_B = borrowed amount, V_E = equity, r_B = borrowing rate.

**Quantitative Anchor (from practice problem):** Aschel fund: r_I = 6.20%, V_B = $42M, V_E = $94.33M, r_B = 2.80% → r_P = 6.20% + (42/94.33)(6.20% − 2.80%) = 7.71%. Leverage amplification = 151 bps on a spread of 340 bps with leverage ratio of 0.445.

**Leverage Risk Cascade:**
Leverage → portfolio decline → equity reduced → leverage ratio INCREASES → forced liquidation risk → fire sales at depressed prices → further price declines → counterparty risk reassessment (especially during crises like 2008) → withdrawal of credit lines, repo, securities lending → FORCED deleveraging at worst possible time.

**Inference Chain:** Leverage decision → counterparty risk assessment → crisis liquidity → fire sale contagion → systemic risk → central bank intervention threshold (cross-ref Section 15: Capital Flows)

**Key Repo vs. Securities Lending Differences:**
Repo: Fixed term, specified maturity. Securities lending: Open-ended, either party can recall/return at any time. This matters for ALM: repos give duration certainty; securities lending creates uncertainty about when borrowed securities must be returned.

**Using Leverage in FI Case Study (Arturo):**
Bearish US Treasuries + bullish Brazilian rates → (1) Sell Treasury futures to reduce duration contributed by US corporate bonds (retain spread exposure, shed rate exposure), (2) Enter receive-fixed/pay-floating Brazilian rate swap (synthetic long fixed-rate Brazilian bond). BOTH positions are leveraged since only collateral is capital used. Risk: If views are wrong, losses magnified AND may force position closure at adverse time.

### 24.2 Fixed-Income Portfolio Taxation

**Tax-Exempt vs. Taxable Decision Matrix:**

| Decision | Tax-Exempt Investor | Taxable Investor |
|---|---|---|
| Which bond to liquidate | Sell overvalued bond (pure investment merit) | Consider selling bond with embedded LOSS (tax-loss harvesting) even if slightly undervalued |
| Realize capital losses | Irrelevant | Realize EARLY — offset current/future gains |
| Realize capital gains | Irrelevant | Defer as LONG as possible |
| Turnover | Optimize for return | LOWER is better — defers capital gains tax |
| Capital gains vs. income | Irrelevant | Capital gains frequently taxed at LOWER rate |

**Tax-Loss Harvesting Decision Rule:** For taxable investors, the incentive is to defer gains and realize losses early. This means: Bond with embedded capital LOSS → more attractive to sell (crystallize loss for tax offset) than bond with embedded capital GAIN → even if the loss bond is slightly undervalued.

**Practice Problem Trap (Q6):** Villash Foundation is tax-EXEMPT → sell Bond 1 (overvalued, capital gain) because tax consequences are irrelevant. For TAXABLE investor → sell Bond 2 (embedded loss) to harvest tax loss. The SAME portfolio, different liquidation decision based solely on tax status.

**Practice Problem Trap (Q9):** Dan's Statement 1 (always sell overvalued bonds first) is WRONG for taxable investors — must consider tax consequences. Statement 2 (never realize losses) is also WRONG — tax-loss harvesting creates genuine value. Answer: NEITHER statement is correct.

**Investment Vehicle Tax Treatment:**
Pass-through treatment (US): Capital gains taxed to investors in year realized, even if fund doesn't distribute cash → PM's sell decisions directly affect investor tax bills.
Non-pass-through (UK): Capital gains increase NAV → investors pay tax only when they sell fund shares → PM's trading decisions DON'T affect timing of investor tax.

**Inference Chain:** Tax status → liquidation decision → turnover optimization → vehicle structure (SMA vs. pooled) → after-tax return maximization → private wealth IPS construction (cross-ref Section 10)

### 24.3 Liability-Driven Investing (LDI) Framework

**LDI vs. ADL Decision Rule:**
LDI: Liabilities are GIVEN (e.g., DB pension obligations, insurance policies) → manage assets to match. Much more common.
ADL: Assets are GIVEN (e.g., leasing company with ST contracts) → structure liabilities to match.

**Liability Classification System (4 Types):**

| Type | Amount | Timing | Examples | Duration Measure |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| I | Known | Known | Traditional fixed-rate bonds | MacDur, ModDur, Money Dur, PVBP |
| II | Known | Uncertain | Callable/putable bonds, demand deposits, term life insurance | Effective duration (model needed) |
| III | Uncertain | Known | Floating-rate notes, inflation-protected securities | Effective duration (model needed) |
| IV | Uncertain | Uncertain | P&C insurance, contingent convertible bonds (CoCos) | Effective duration (model needed) |

**Critical Decision Rule:** Types II, III, IV ALL require effective duration calculated using a model with initial yield curve assumptions, shifting curve up/down, then re-estimating PV. Only Type I allows standard MacDur/ModDur.

**Practice Problem Application (Modern Mortgage Bank):**
Residential mortgage loans = Type IV assets (prepayment option makes BOTH amount and timing uncertain — borrower can prepay for many reasons: sale, refinancing, rates decline). Even with 80% average LTV, individual loans may default if home prices drop.
Government bonds = Type I assets (coupon and principal fixed at issuance).
Demand/time deposits = Type II liabilities (amount known, but depositor can redeem early).
Contingent convertible bonds = Type IV liabilities (conversion option makes both amount and timing uncertain).

**Inference Chain:** Liability type → duration measure → immunization strategy → structural risk from non-parallel shifts → dispersion/convexity trade-off → surplus optimization (cross-ref Section 17: Institutional Investors)

### 24.4 Managing Interest Rate Risk of Multiple Liabilities

**Cash Flow Matching:**
Build dedicated portfolio of high-quality bonds whose coupons + principal match liability payment schedule. Key constraint: cash-in-advance — funds must be available ON OR BEFORE each liability date, not after.

**Construction Algorithm:** Work BACKWARDS from last liability → buy bond maturing at that date → calculate interim coupon flows → subtract from earlier liabilities → buy bonds for remaining gaps → repeat until all liabilities matched.

**Accounting Defeasance:** When cash flow matching is used to retire debt → both dedicated asset portfolio AND debt liabilities can be removed from balance sheet. Improves credit rating. Motivation: buying back bonds in open market is costly (bonds widely held by buy-and-hold investors → illiquid → high execution cost). Cash flow matching is cheaper.

**Duration Matching (for multiple liabilities):**
Match portfolio MacDur to weighted-average MacDur of liabilities. Less precise than cash flow matching but more flexible. Structural risk: non-parallel shifts/twists can still cause mismatch → minimize by reducing cash flow DISPERSION → minimize convexity.

**Immunized Portfolio Convexity Formula:**
Convexity = (MacDur² + MacDur + Dispersion) / (1 + Cash flow yield)²

**Convexity Ranking for Same Duration:**
Barbell > Laddered > Bullet (highest to lowest convexity)
BUT for immunization: MINIMIZE convexity (= minimize dispersion = minimize structural risk from non-parallel shifts). So BULLET is best for immunization, BARBELL is worst.

**Laddered Portfolio — Private Wealth Application:**
Wang Family case (Shanghai): Concentrated in real estate + equity → recommend laddered Chinese government bond portfolio.
Three selling points for UHNW families:
1. Credit quality — diversifies away from concentrated risky assets
2. Liquidity — coupon payments available annually for household expenses, soon-to-mature bonds provide collateral or emergency cash
3. Yield curve diversification — spread across 4+ segments protects against any single-point yield spike

**Using ETFs for Laddered Portfolios:**
Fixed-maturity corporate bond ETFs (e.g., "2024 investment-grade") → replicate ladder using ~50 held-to-maturity bonds per year. Benefits: lower cost, better diversification, more liquid than individual bonds. Limitation: mutual fund may still be superior for smaller portfolios (better default diversification, easier liquidation).

### 24.5 FI Module 2 Practice Problem Decision Rules

**Cécile/Margit/Villash Vignette (Q1-6):**
1. Inflation protection for coupons = floating-coupon % + inflation-linked %. Permot (34% + 28% = 62%) > Rosaiso (17% + 21% = 38%) > Aschel (2% + 3% = 5%). Answer: B (Permot).
2. Rolling yield = Coupon income + Rolldown return. Aschel: Coupon = $3.63/$117 = 3.10%, Rolldown = ($114 − $117)/$117 = −2.56%. Rolling yield = 0.54%. Answer: B.
3. Leveraged return uses r_P formula: 6.20% + (42/94.33)(6.20% − 2.80%) = 7.71%. Answer: B.
4. Bonds with embedded options → use EFFECTIVE duration (not modified). Only Rosaiso holds significant embedded-option bonds. Answer: C.
5. Liability-based mandates: Feature 1 (match liabilities with projected cash inflows) = CORRECT. Feature 2 (minimize deficient cash inflows) = CORRECT. Both features are correct. Answer: A.
6. Villash is TAX-EXEMPT → sell overvalued Bond 1 (no tax consequence). Answer: A.

**Celia/Topanga Vignette (Q7-12):**
7. Total return mandate, +25 bps target, small deviations, <50 bps tracking error → ENHANCED INDEXING. Answer: B.
8. Strategy 2 (floating-coupon bonds) protects against inflation because reference rate adjusts. Strategy 1 (fixed-coupon) provides NO inflation protection. But Strategy 1 is better for funding liabilities (predictable cash flows) and reducing correlation with equities. Answer: A (protecting against inflation).
9. Dan says always sell overvalued bonds first (WRONG for taxable) AND never intentionally realize losses (WRONG — tax-loss harvesting adds value). NEITHER correct. Answer: C.
10. Highest liquidity premium → LEAST liquid bond. Liquidity drivers: lower credit quality, longer time since issuance, smaller issue size, smaller total issuance outstanding, longer maturity. Bond 3 (A-rated, 6 months old, $75M issue, 7yr maturity) is least liquid. Answer: C.
11. Total expected return = Rolling yield ± E(yield change) ± E(spread change) ± E(currency). = 2.46% − 0.78% − 0.67% + 0.65% = 1.66%. Answer: B.
12. Active management mandate → largest deviations from benchmark. Manager C: ModDur 6.16 vs index 5.22 (largest duration deviation), sector weights deviate most, highest turnover (290%). Answer: C.

**Expected Return Decomposition Formula:**
Total = Coupon income + Rolldown return + [−ModDur × ΔYield + ½ × Convexity × (ΔYield)²] + [−ModDur × ΔSpread + ½ × Convexity × (ΔSpread)²] + E(Currency)

**Liquidity Premium Decision Framework:**
Lower credit → higher premium. Longer time since issuance → higher. Smaller issue size → higher. Smaller total outstanding → higher. Longer maturity → higher. Combine ALL factors — don't rely on single dimension.
