## Section 30: Investment Planning — Taxation, Retirement & Decumulation

### 30.1 Tax-Aware Portfolio Construction

**Core Tax Impact Formulas:**
- Post-tax expected return ≈ Pre-tax expected return × (1 - tx)
- Post-tax standard deviation ≈ Pre-tax SD × (1 - tx)
- Post-tax variance ≈ Pre-tax variance × (1 - tx)²

**Counterintuitive Insight:** Higher tax rates can INCREASE risk-taking. In MVO with taxes, the effective risk aversion coefficient becomes λ × (1 - tx), which is LOWER than λ → taxable investor optimally holds MORE equities than tax-exempt investor with same pre-tax risk aversion, because the tax authority absorbs some of the investment risk.

**Tax-Aware Utility Function (Full):**
U = R_after_tax - (λ/2) × σ²_after_tax - Capital gains tax rate

Capital gains taxes are a deterministic drag on expected return (triggered by trading), NOT a reduction in risk → must be modeled separately from income taxes.

**Tax-Aware Optimization Results:**
- Tax-optimized portfolios have significantly LOWER turnover (73% vs 110% for tax-unaware)
- Fewer trades (943 vs 2,902)
- Tax lots matter: same security bought at different dates has different tax characteristics
- Tax-advantaged securities (municipal bonds) get higher weights in tax-aware optimization

**Asset Location Strategy:**
- Place high-tax assets (taxable bonds generating ordinary income) in tax-advantaged accounts (retirement accounts)
- Place low-tax assets (low-dividend equities) in taxable accounts
- This arrangement preserves most of the tax-advantaged account benefit while keeping equities accessible

**Wash Sale Rules by Jurisdiction:**

| Jurisdiction | Rule |
|---|---|
| US/UK | 30-day window before and after sale |
| Australia | No specified period; tax authorities judge intent |
| Germany | No wash sale restriction (allows repurchase without limitation) |
| Japan | Wash sales limited/often disallowed at exchange level |

### 30.2 Concentrated Position Tax Management — Complementarity Portfolio

**Problem:** Concentrated position has large unrealized gains → selling triggers massive tax bill, but holding maintains under-diversification.

**Complementarity Portfolio Strategy:**
1. Build a separate "sidecar" diversified portfolio alongside the concentrated position
2. Sidecar portfolio intentionally generates short-term losses through active trading/rebalancing
3. These losses offset gains when concentrated position is gradually sold
4. Capital gains from concentrated position are offset by realized losses from sidecar → net tax impact reduced

**Key Characteristics:**
- Takes a LONG time to implement (sidecar needs time to generate losses)
- Anticipating losses seems counterintuitive but is realistic (normal market volatility generates both gains and losses)
- If NO losses occur: sidecar portfolio earned strong returns → investor pays fractional tax on rebalancing → still benefits
- Win-win: either losses offset gains OR strong returns generate wealth

**Cross-Reference to Section 10 (Private Wealth):** Connects to concentrated position strategies (exchange funds, prepaid variable forwards, protective puts, equity collars).

### 30.3 Centralized Portfolio Management (CPM)

**Concept (DiBartolomeo 1992):** Treat all of a single beneficiary's accounts managed by multiple active managers as ONE portfolio through a single trading desk.

**Benefits:**
- Cross-validate alpha signals across managers
- Minimize turnover through cross-trades (one manager's sell = another manager's buy)
- Reduce capital gains realization → minimize tax liability
- Information efficiency across all positions

**Family Application:** Particularly relevant for families where the same WM manages all accounts. Must consider: wash sale rules across jointly-filed accounts, different tax brackets across family members, concentrated positions with embedded gains unique to each member.

### 30.4 Retirement Planning — Accumulation & Decumulation Framework

**William Sharpe:** Called decumulation "the hardest and nastiest problem in finance" — uncertain lifespans, uncertain healthspans, inadequate savings, uncertain returns, uncertain spending.

**Two Phases:**
1. **Accumulation:** Working years → save and invest → forecast spending, bequests, returns → build portfolio to fund future goals with high probability
2. **Decumulation:** Retirement → convert wealth to income stream → must adapt to longevity, inflation, and return fluctuations

**Funding Ratio = Assets / Liabilities (PV of future spending)**
- McCarthys example: EUR 6,865,000 / EUR 6,530,000 = 1.051 (5.1% overfunded)
- A 4.88% decline in assets → surplus becomes zero
- Funding ratio is UNIVERSAL — works at any wealth level

**Funding Ratio Return = (Final FR / Initial FR) - 1 = (Asset Return - Liability Return) / (1 + Liability Return)**
- Does NOT depend on the level of the funding ratio → universal measure of financial health
- Two people with same asset allocation and same liabilities have identical funding ratio returns regardless of wealth level

**Accumulation Formulas:**
- Terminal wealth from saving $1/year for N years at E[r]: V_N = Annual savings × (1 + E[r]) × [(1 + E[r])^N - 1] / E[r]
- Required annual savings: = (1/(1+E[r])) × (Target wealth × E[r]) / [(1+E[r])^(Retirement age - Current age) - 1]
- Rule of 72: Money doubles in 72/r years at r% compound rate
- ~Half of terminal savings value comes from investment returns in first 72/r years → start early

**Jenny Yu Case Study (Power of Compounding):**
- Saving SGD 1,000/year from age 25 to 70 (46 contributions) at 5% → SGD 177,119
- Delaying 12 years (age 37 to 70, only 34 contributions) → SGD 89,320 (50% reduction for 26% fewer contributions)

### 30.5 The Decumulation Problem

**Two dominant approaches:**
1. **Self-managed drawdown:** Invest portfolio for positive real returns, withdraw at steady pace based on expected longevity. Risk: market crashes at retirement (sequence-of-returns risk), outliving savings.
2. **Annuitization:** Transfer wealth to insurer in exchange for lifetime payments. Risk: insurer default/bankruptcy, loss of flexibility, adverse selection pricing.

**Bengen 4% Rule (1994, expanded by Trinity Study 1998):**
- 50/50 stock/bond portfolio, withdraw 4% in Year 1, inflation-adjust each subsequent year
- Historical US data (1926-1989): portfolio lasts 30+ years in all scenarios
- BUT Anarkulova et al. (2023): 38-country sample with SSA life expectancy data → safe initial withdrawal rate is only **2.26%** if adjusted for inflation

**Maximum Safe Withdrawal Rate Formula:**
Maximum safe withdrawal rate = 1 / max(5, Joint life expectancy)
- Floor of 20% withdrawal rate (never more than 1/5 of portfolio per year)
- Simple, practical rule for managing longevity risk
- Clients desiring bequests: apply rate to (assets - desired bequest)

**Life Expectancy Anchors (US SSA 2023):**

| Age | Male LE | Female LE |
|---|---|---|
| 60 | 20.47 | 23.67 |
| 65 | 16.89 | 19.73 |
| 70 | 13.59 | 15.82 |
| 80 | 7.74 | 9.10 |
| 90 | 3.72 | 4.41 |

**Gender Gap Decision Rule:** Women outlive men by ~5 years on average → decumulation planning for women must account for longer longevity, higher healthcare costs in later years, and potentially less financial literacy (if spouse was primary financial decision-maker).

### 30.6 Annuities and Retirement Innovations

**Annuity Payment Formula (growing annuity):**
Initial payment = Lump sum × (E[r] - g) / [1 - ((1+g)/(1+E[r]))^N]
- When g = E[r]: Initial payment = Lump sum × (1 + E[r]) / N

**Deferred Annuity Strategy (Totten & Siegel 2019):**
- Buy TWO annuities: (1) immediate annuity covering retirement to ~age 85, (2) deferred annuity starting at age 85
- Deferred annuity is CHEAP because it starts far in the future → cost-effective longevity hedge
- Recommended as alternative to traditional DB pension replacement

**Three Innovations in Retirement Planning:**
1. **Modern Tontines:** Group pools lump sums into common portfolio → distributions as long as alive → when member dies, portion returned to heirs, rest redistributed to survivors. Mitigates longevity risk via law of large numbers. More annuity-like than lottery-like despite historical reputation.
2. **Retirement Security Bonds (Merton 2014):** Brazil's RendA+ bonds — buy directly from Treasury website, answer 4 questions, get real inflation-protected monthly income starting at chosen age for 20 years. Remarkably simple interface.
3. **Combined Offsetting Insurance (Hieber & Lucas 2022):** Merges long-term care and retirement income into single policy. Key insight: longevity risk and health care needs are NEGATIVELY CORRELATED → sick people need more care but less retirement income; healthy people need more retirement income but less care → combining creates low-risk balanced product robust to adverse selection.

**Three Foundational Insights for Retirement Innovation:**
1. Pooling transforms individual mortality risk into average population mortality risk (manageable via deferred annuities)
2. Institutions manage assets more cost-effectively than individuals (cost differences compound significantly over decades)
3. Some risks are natural hedges for each other (long-term care needs vs. life expectancy)

**Cross-Reference to Collie (2016) — Longevity Risk vs. Market Risk:**
- Below age 75: equity market risk > longevity risk (SD of equity returns > equivalent SD for longevity)
- Above age 75: longevity risk > equity market risk
- Implication: Annuitization becomes increasingly valuable after age 75

### 30.7 Performance Evaluation for Private Clients

**Logarithmic Sharpe Ratio < Arithmetic Sharpe Ratio** (always) because volatility drag reduces compound returns. Logarithmic Information Ratio can be NEGATIVE even when arithmetic version is zero → correctly captures that the portfolio actually underperformed its benchmark on a compound basis.

**Key Risk Measures Hierarchy:**
- VaR = percentile loss (doesn't capture tail shape)
- ES (CVaR) = expected loss beyond VaR (captures tail severity) → Basel Committee 2016 primary measure
- Semi-standard deviation = downside-only SD (sets above-mean returns to zero loss)
- Sortino Ratio = (Return - MAR) / Semi-deviation(Return - MAR) → best for clients with explicit return targets

**Statistical Significance Decision Rule:** t-statistic = (Mean/SD) × √N. With IR = 0.6 and target t = 2: need (2/0.6)² ≈ 11 years. With 10% return and 20% SD: need (2 × 20/10)² = 16 years. CUSUM detects underperformance ~4× faster than simple t-test.

**Brinson Attribution for Private Clients:**
- Allocation effect: portfolio-weight × benchmark-return vs. benchmark-weight × benchmark-return
- Selection effect: benchmark-weight × portfolio-return vs. benchmark-weight × benchmark-return
- Interaction: residual (Brinson-Fachler folds into selection)

### 30.8 Investment Planning Practice Problem Decision Rules (Q1-16)

- Q1: Diminishing marginal utility → risk AVERSE (A).
- Q2: Small RE/commodity allocation supported by risk CAPACITY (A) — enough wealth and time, not tolerance or perception.
- Q3: Bond return over 10-year horizon = 5-year duration initial yield = 4.5% (A). Langetieg-Leibowitz-Kogelman: return over 2× modified duration ≈ initial yield.
- Q4: Portfolio C (A) — same risk as MVO, modest RE/commodity reflecting client preferences. B avoids diversifiers entirely.
- Q5: Tax-exempt investor MORE risk averse than identical taxable investor (C). Tax authority absorbs risk → effective λ for taxable = λ(1-tx) < λ.
- Q6: Funding ratio = 5,400,000/4,723,000 = 1.14 (C).
- Q7: 30% decline in $1.5M portfolio = $500K loss. $500K/$677K surplus = 73.9% ≈ 74% surplus decline (C). Leverage effect on surplus.
- Q8: HY expected return = (0.95 × 1.09) + (0.05 × 0.6) - 1 = 6.55% ≈ 7% (C).
- Q9: Portfolio customization → greater tracking error than direct indexing (C).
- Q10: Equities (Fund A) in taxable account (A) — favorable LTCG rates. Zero-coupon bonds generate phantom income → retirement account. Hedge fund generates short-term gains → retirement account.
- Q11: Post-tax SD = 23% × (1 - 0.24) = 17.48% ≈ 17.5% (A). Hedge fund taxed at ordinary income rate (short-term).
- Q12: Edward (adult son, separate filer) buys in parents' joint account → NO wash sale trigger (A). Wash sale applies to same person/jointly-filed accounts.
- Q13: Logarithmic Sortino Ratio (B) — client specified minimum compound return → needs MAR-based measure with compounding.
- Q14: Selection effect = (0.6×5% + 0.4×6%) - (0.6×6% + 0.4×5%) = 5.4% - 5.6% = -0.2% (B).
- Q15: Compound active return = ln(1.073) - ln(1.062) ≈ 1.1% (B).
- Q16: Need N = (t/IR)² = (2/0.6)² = 11.11 → minimum 11 years (C). Five years insufficient.

### 28.8 Trading Costs & Electronic Markets — Practice Problem Decision Rules (Q1-16)

**Q1: Inside bid-ask spread** = highest bid - lowest ask. Answer: A.
**Q2: Market sell order fills** → hit bids from highest to lowest until order filled. Weight-average price across dealers. Answer: B.
**Q3: Market impact (price impact)** = effect of Trade 1 execution on Trade 2 price. If Trade 1 buy at $25.20, Trade 2 buy at $25.22 → price impact = $0.02 per share. Answer: A.
**Q4: Effective bid-ask spread** = 2 × |Trade price - Midpoint at time of order entry|. For buy orders: 2 × (Trade price - Mid). Average across trades. Answer: C.
**Q5: Market fragmentation** = same instruments traded across multiple venues → liquidity split → any single exchange has only fraction of aggregate liquidity. Requires adapted trading strategies. Answer: B.
**Q6: Electronic trading reduced costs** for buy-side traders. Lower operating costs vs floor-based systems. Competition forced dealers to pass savings via narrower spreads. BUT: corporate and municipal bonds still traded largely OTC despite electronic efforts. Answer: A.
**Q7: Collocation** = placing servers in exchange facility rooms to minimize communication distance → faster execution. Answer: C.
**Q8: Machine learning limitations** → ML systems fail during high volatility episodes because few historical precedents exist for machines to learn from. Many traders shut down electronic systems during vol spikes. Answer: B.
**Q9: Systemic risk mitigation** → BOTH exhaustive pre-launch algorithm testing AND mandatory trade halts for large price changes help minimize systemic risk. Answer: C.
**Q10: Rumormongering** = disseminating false information about fundamentals or trading intentions. News validation rules help defend against this. Answer: A.
**Q11: Market fragmentation tools** → BOTH liquidity aggregation (super books across venues) AND smart order routing (send to best-priced venue) help manage fragmentation. Answer: C.
**Q12: Front running** = trading in front of traders who demand liquidity, identifying large orders being filled. Key distinction: front runners exploit order flow patterns; arbitrageurs exploit mispricings across instruments; quote matchers trade in front of liquidity SUPPLIERS (standing orders). Answer: B.
**Q13: Effective spread calculation** → 2 × |Trade price - Midpoint|. Average across trades. For 2 trades: ($0.05 + $0.02)/2 = $0.035. Answer: B.
**Q14: Flickering quotes** = exposed limit orders submitted and cancelled within a second. Bloomfield doesn't want orders standing in market → flickering quotes achieve this. NOT machine learning (fails in high vol), NOT leapfrogging (used when spreads are wide, not narrow). Answer: A.
**Q15: Trade halts preserve HFT advantage** because fast traders resume first after halts. Random delays REDUCE speed advantage. Call markets REDUCE speed advantage. Only trade halts maintain the competitive advantage of trading faster. Answer: B.
**Q16: Bluffing** = submitting orders and arranging trades to influence other traders' perceptions of value. Bluffers prey on momentum traders by buying when prices rise, selling at higher prices. NOT spoofing (uses standing limit orders to convey false liquidity). NOT wash trading (uses commonly controlled accounts to create false activity impression). Answer: A.

**Key Discrimination Rules — Electronic Trader Types:**
- Front runner → exploits ORDER FLOW patterns (demand side)
- Quote matcher → exploits STANDING ORDERS (supply side)
- Arbitrageur → exploits PRICE DISCREPANCIES across instruments
- Bluffer → manipulates PERCEPTIONS of value through real trades
- Spoofer → manipulates PERCEIVED LIQUIDITY through standing limit orders
- Wash trader → manipulates PERCEIVED ACTIVITY through commonly controlled accounts

**Key Discrimination Rules — Market Structure Reforms:**
- Trade halts → PRESERVE speed advantage (fast traders resume first)
- Random order delays → REDUCE speed advantage
- Frequent call markets → REDUCE speed advantage (batch processing eliminates speed edge)

### 28.9 Cross-Domain Inference Chains — Trading

**Chain 1: Trading Costs → Manager Selection (Section 21)**
IF manager has high implementation shortfall relative to peers → suggests poor execution capability → factor into manager selection decision alongside alpha generation → trading costs directly reduce net-of-cost alpha.

**Chain 2: Market Manipulation → Ethics (Section 23)**
IF analyst detects market manipulation (spoofing, wash trading, bluffing) → Standard II(B) Market Manipulation applies → must not engage in these practices → must report if observed within firm → dissociation may be required.

**Chain 3: Electronic Trading → Institutional Portfolio Management (Section 17)**
IF institutional investor uses algorithmic trading (VWAP, IS algorithms) → must understand trade-off between market impact (larger orders = more impact) and timing risk (slower execution = more price drift) → algorithm selection depends on urgency and order size relative to market volume.

## Section 29: SWF Case Study — Institutional Risk Management (Module 7)

### 29.1 Financial Risk Management Dimensions for Institutional Investors

**Six Dimensions of Financial Risk Management:**
1. **Top-down vs. bottom-up:** Board/CIO set overall risk tolerance (top-down) → investment team manages asset-class-specific risks (bottom-up) → both needed simultaneously
2. **Portfolio-level vs. asset-class-specific:** Different asset classes need different risk metrics. Public equities → factor models, granular security data. Hedge funds → only monthly returns available. Illiquid assets → appraisal-based, lagged data. Aggregation to portfolio level is difficult.
3. **Return-based vs. holdings-based:** Return-based = easy to implement but biased (relies on past which may differ from today). Holdings-based = more accurate but costly, and often infeasible for hedge funds/illiquid assets (lack transparency).
4. **Absolute vs. relative risk:** Absolute = total loss potential (SD, VaR, CVaR, max drawdown). Relative = underperformance vs. benchmark (tracking error).
5. **Long-term vs. short-term risk metrics:** Short-term = VaR, CVaR via risk factor models (good for near-term loss estimates). Long-term = Monte Carlo simulation with forward-looking capital market assumptions (probability of maintaining purchasing power over 5-20 years, probability of meeting cash flows).
6. **Quantitative vs. qualitative:** Quantitative = backward-looking, parametric. Qualitative = forward-looking but subject to own biases. BOTH needed.
7. **Pre- vs. post-investment:** Pre-investment = operational and investment due diligence BEFORE committing capital. Post-investment = ongoing monitoring, especially critical for illiquid investments (can't easily exit).

**Decision Rule:** Long-term institutional risk management ≠ calculating VaR. It's about ensuring the organization SURVIVES and meets its long-term objectives. Existential threats come from the INTERACTION of market risk and liquidity risk.

### 29.2 Liquidity Crisis Dynamics for Institutional Investors

**During a crisis, liquidity needs INCREASE while sources DECREASE:**

**Needs increase because:**
- Beneficiaries demand more (university draws on endowment as other income dries up; government demands more from SWF)
- Capital calls accelerate (attractive crisis-era investment opportunities)
- Rebalancing flows increase (larger market movements → larger drift from SAA)

**Sources decrease because:**
- Inflows fall (donors give less, plan sponsors contribute less)
- Distributions from illiquid investments decline (depressed exit prices)
- Liquid investments lose value (public equities down significantly)
- Hedge funds impose gates or lockups, or become less liquid than expected
- Reluctance to redeem from high-demand active managers (damages relationship for future capacity)

**Critical Insight:** The more an institutional investor allocates to illiquid assets, the LESS able it is to tolerate market losses during a crisis → paradox: illiquid investments are supposed to exploit long-term horizon, but they constrain the ability to act during crises.

**Decision Rule:** IF crisis occurs AND portfolio is heavily allocated to illiquids → the combination of inability to meet cash flows AND inability to rebalance can become a SURVIVAL issue → this is the primary existential risk for long-term institutional investors.

### 29.3 Illiquid Asset Class Risks

**Four Key Risk Features of Illiquid Assets:**
1. **Drawdown structure uncertainty:** Capital called at unknown schedule, profits distributed at unknown schedule → must hold liquid assets to meet capital calls
2. **Return smoothing:** Appraisal-based valuations → stale pricing → understated volatility and correlation → MVO over-allocates to illiquids because Sharpe ratios appear artificially high
3. **Cannot be rebalanced easily:** Selling PE stakes in secondary market requires accepting significant discount to true value
4. **Denominator effect:** In a crisis, total AUM falls faster than illiquid asset repricing → illiquid allocation % SOARS above target → investor may appear over-allocated to illiquids

**Commitment Strategy Trade-off:**
- Commit TOO MUCH → denominator effect risk + severe liquidity strain
- Commit TOO LITTLE → fail to reach target allocation + fall short on returns

**Unsmoothing Methods (3 approaches):**
1. **Geltner (1993):** Removes first-order serial correlation in observed returns (real estate focus)
2. **Okunev-White (2003):** Extends Geltner to include higher-order serial correlations
3. **GLM / Getmansky-Lo-Makarov (2004):** Assumes observed returns follow moving-average process (hedge fund focus)

**Impact of Unsmoothing (Cambridge Associates data 1990-2019):**
- Natural resources: observed vol ~18% → unsmoothed vol up to ~19% (modest increase)
- Buyouts: observed vol ~15% → unsmoothed vol up to ~17%
- Venture capital: observed vol ~21% → unsmoothed vol up to ~48% (massive increase!)
- Real estate: observed vol ~17% → unsmoothed vol up to ~17%
- Beta to public equities also increases dramatically after unsmoothing (e.g., VC: observed 0.5 → unsmoothed up to 1.3)

**Decision Rule:** MVO with unsmoothed returns STILL falls short because it can't account for illiquidity, rebalancing impossibility, and drawdown structure → most investors simply CONSTRAIN illiquid allocations in MVO rather than relying on optimization output.

### 29.4 Direct vs. Fund Investments in Illiquid Asset Classes

**Advantages of Direct Investing:**
- Fee savings (avoid 2/20 GP fee structure)
- Greater control over individual investments
- Better liquidity management (no unfunded commitments, full discretion on exit timing)
- No forced selling in down markets
- Resolves some principal-agent issues with fund investing

**Disadvantages of Direct Investing:**
- Requires dedicated, experienced in-house team
- Deal sourcing constrained by team's network (vs. GP's broader network)
- Higher concentration risk (larger individual investments due to staffing/scalability)
- Harder to diversify across geography and industries
- Adverse selection risk if relying on external deal sourcing
- Governance not set up for it (LP has limited liability; direct investor may be GP with unlimited liability)
- Board members of private companies → liability exposure
- Public pension funds struggle to offer competitive compensation (political pressure)

**Decision Rule:** Direct investing is suitable for LARGE pension funds and SWFs with scale, in-house capability, and willingness to accept concentration risk in exchange for fee savings and control.

### 29.5 Managing Liquidity Risk — 5-Step Framework

**Step 1: Establish liquidity risk parameters**
- Set minimum % of assets that must be liquid (daily/monthly basis)
- Track invested capital PLUS uncalled commitments (not just invested capital)
- Set bands around sum of invested capital + uncalled commitments as % of total assets
- Include automatic/semi-automatic escalation triggers (reduce commitments, seek secondary sales)
- Can be in IPS or internal guidelines

**Step 2: Assess current portfolio liquidity and monitor evolution**
- Report: what % can be liquidated within a day / week / month / quarter / year
- Track evolution over time, not just point-in-time snapshots
- Review legal terms with external managers (redemption notices, lockups)
- Consider: redeeming from capacity-constrained managers may damage future relationship → may classify as LESS liquid even if technically liquid

**Step 3: Develop cash flow model and project future cash flows**
- Model payouts (pension payments, foundation spending, government support)
- Model inflows (contributions, gifts, donations, government savings)
- Model capital calls and distributions for illiquid assets
- Model rebalancing flows

**Step 4: Stress test liquidity needs and cash flow projections**
- Not business-as-usual → crisis scenarios where cash flows are affected by market movements
- More art than science — no universally accepted method (unlike market risk VaR)
- Example: donations lower, payouts higher, capital calls accelerated in crisis

**Step 5: Put in place an emergency action plan**
- What to liquidate and in what ORDER during a crisis
- How to rebalance the portfolio
- Pre-approved by board → prevents panic-driven decisions
- Reduces risk of board pressuring investment team into sub-optimal short-term decisions

### 29.6 Enterprise Risk Management Framework

**Board sets:** Overall risk tolerance → SAA → IPS (with risk parameters, limits, guidelines)
**Management implements:** SAA across asset classes → measures/monitors/reports risk vs. guidelines
**Risk team:** Risk factor analysis (equity, interest rate, credit, inflation, currency, liquidity risk) → reports to board and internal investment committee → if risks out of line with tolerance → take action (hedging, rebalancing, reducing commitments, secondary sales)

**Risk Tolerance Expression:**
- SWFs, endowments, foundations → asset-only terms (volatility, max drawdown, VaR/CVaR)
- Pension funds, insurance companies → asset/liability terms (surplus volatility, funded ratio risk)

**Active Risk Budgeting for Public Equities:**
- Ensure active risk budget accurately reflects areas of expected excess return
- Verify that alpha comes from stock picking, NOT from factor loading (growth, momentum, quality)

**Private Equity Risk Assessment:**
- Compare PE returns to PUBLIC EQUITY returns to determine if liquidity premium is adequate compensation
- Currency risk often overlooked → can have outsized impact, especially in EM/frontier markets
- Asset allocation drift must be monitored → rebalance to SAA regularly

### 29.7 ESG & Non-Financial Risks for Institutional Investors

**Universal Ownership Concept:**
- Large institutional investors (SWFs, public pension funds) effectively own a slice of the WHOLE economy
- Negative externalities from one portfolio company can reduce profitability of another portfolio company
- Environmental damage costs are ultimately borne through the portfolio (insurance premiums, taxes, physical costs)
- Therefore: large institutional investors have incentive to internalize externalities

**Climate Risks — Two Categories:**
1. **Physical climate risks:** Rising seas, extreme weather, flooding, drought, wildfire → damage real assets, reduce rental yields, increase insurance costs, accelerate depreciation of infrastructure
2. **Climate transition risks:** Decarbonization policies, carbon pricing, technology shifts, consumer behavior changes → massive disruption to fossil fuel, auto, electricity sectors

**Climate Opportunities — Two Categories:**
1. **Climate mitigation:** Clean energy, energy efficiency, batteries/storage, smart grids, materials (copper, lithium)
2. **Climate adaptation:** Sustainable agriculture, water management, flood barriers

**Social Risks for Institutional Investors:**
- Community relations / social license to operate → critical for direct investments
- Labor standards in supply chain → reputational risk
- Occupational health & safety → regulatory fines, brand damage
- "Just transition" concept → decarbonization must not create excessive social harm (job losses, increased costs for poor households)

**Key Decision Rule:** For institutional investors with direct investments in frontier/developing markets → reputational risk can be LARGER than financial risk even for small investments → $25M investment in $56B fund can create headline risk that damages the entire institution.

### 29.8 R-SWF Case Study — Decision Rules & Lessons

**R-SWF Background:** Republic of Ruritania SWF, $50B AUM, "savings fund" type (intergenerational wealth), 50% alternatives / 40% equities / 9% FI / 1% cash.

**Case Study 1.0 — Initial Investment Committee Decision:**
- Infrastructure: Sunnyland airport BOT project, $100M (0.2% of AUM), 25-year concession, 15% expected IRR
- PE: Atsui Beverage Company (ABC), $25M (0.05% of AUM), 35% minority stake, 10× EBITDA

**Key Risk Assessment Rules from Meeting 1.0:**
1. **Liquidity risk NOT primary concern** for either investment because: (a) small relative to AUM, (b) R-SWF is savings fund with no cash flow pressure from liabilities, (c) direct investments improve capital management vs. fund investing
2. **Infrastructure key risk = traffic volume** → scenario analysis needed (low case IRR ~10-11%, cost overrun case ~13%)
3. **PE key risk = election/tariff change** → tariffs at 100% protect ABC from foreign competition → if election changes government, tariffs may fall → scenario analysis of financial impact needed BEFORE investment (Head of PE's approach deemed INSUFFICIENT)
4. **Currency risk** applies to BOTH investments in frontier markets → difficult/impossible to hedge

**Case Study 2.0 — Three Years Later (risks materializing):**

*Infrastructure:*
- Construction delayed (3rd year vs. planned 2 years), but no material cost overruns (fixed-price contract)
- Climate risks materializing faster than expected (rising seas, more frequent storms, hotter temperatures shortening tourist season)
- AOG seeking higher fixed fee → risk of operator break clause
- Revenue risk: tourism dependent on discretionary spending (recession risk, pandemic risk)

*PE:*
- New government reduced tariffs from 100% to 20% → cost advantage over foreign brands eroded
- Recession + currency devaluation in Atsui
- Management wants to cut staff by 50% (vs. planned 40%) due to slowing sales
- Strict labor laws: 2-year notice required for termination
- Management cutting corners: dumping waste in river, reducing breaks, removing soap from restrooms
- Endangered species discovered near plant site
- REPUTATIONAL RISK now dominates financial risk

**Case Study 3.0 — Five Years Later (outcomes):**

*Infrastructure:* Continues to perform poorly — 50% lower revenue vs. base case, 50% higher costs than base case due to flood mitigation. Medium/long-term forecast negative.

*PE:* Team sold full position for $27M (vs. $25M cost) → avoided diplomatic crisis and reputational damage. Buyer was international beverage company already exporting to Atsui — producing locally provides natural FX hedge against weaker Atsui currency.

**ERM Evaluation — Strengths vs Weaknesses:**
- **Strength:** Dedicated risk-focused IC meetings with all senior stakeholders, ample discussion time, voting process, identified qualitative risk factors comprehensively
- **Weakness 1:** Insufficient quantification — identified risks qualitatively but didn't perform rigorous scenario analysis (base/optimistic/pessimistic) with numerical IRR/return projections BEFORE investing
- **Weakness 2:** No conditional action plans — should have pre-agreed what specific actions to take IF identified risks materialized
- **Weakness 3:** Underweighted ESG/reputational risks — more consideration of non-financial risks needed upfront, especially for small investments where financial impact is limited but reputational impact is outsized
- **Weakness 4:** Improvements should include: scenario analysis as standard part of investment process, conditional action plans, more weight on ESG/reputational risks in pre-investment screening

**In-Text Question Decision Rules:**

**Q (Memo 1.0 — Liquidity Risk):** Liquidity NOT primary concern because investments are small (0.2% and 0.05% of AUM), SWF has no liabilities/cash flow pressure, and direct investing improves capital management. Focus instead on market/operational/reputational risk.

**Q (Memo 1.0 — Traffic Risk):** Head of Infrastructure identified traffic as KEY risk → share scenario analysis assumptions (low/high cases) to validate. Scenario analysis is the best tool for managing financial risk of infrastructure investments.

**Q (Memo 1.0 — Election/Tariff Risk):** Head of PE's approach to managing tariff risk is INSUFFICIENT. Must model financial impact of tariff reduction/elimination on ABC's profitability BEFORE investing. Building rapport with government is good but doesn't quantify the financial risk.

**Q (Memo 2.0 — Interest Rate Risk):** Hedge interest rate exposure on the Sunnyland debt via interest rate derivatives.

**Q (Memo 2.0 — Health Food Trend):** Develop new healthy alternatives to carbonated drinks, leveraging ABC's experience with natural mango beverages to differentiate and grow market share.

**Q (Meeting 2.0 — Environmental Risks):**
- Airport → climate change/rising sea levels → use climate scenario analysis + adaptation strategies (storm-surge barriers, government collaboration)
- ABC → waste management/river dumping → persuade management to stop, pursue sustainable development and "just transition"

**Q (Meeting 2.0 — Social Risk):** Reputational risk is common to BOTH investments. NOT easily managed once recognized — social risks are hard to quantify, hard to integrate into financial models, and require considering ALL stakeholder interests.

---

**Enterprise Risk Management Evaluation:**

*Strengths:*
- Dedicated full investment committee meeting to risk discussion for two relatively small investments
- Ample time for senior management to voice concerns
- Multiple perspectives (Head of Risk, Head of Equities brought valuable skepticism)
- Formal committee vote on each investment

*Weaknesses:*
- Too little effort to QUANTIFY risks (no formal scenario analysis with base/optimistic/pessimistic cases)
- No ACTION PLAN for risk mitigation agreed before investing (what to do IF bad scenarios materialize)
- Insufficient attention to ESG/reputational risks at initial stage
- Hedging tools and mitigation strategies not discussed pre-investment
- Action plan should be CONDITIONAL on specific bad outcomes materializing

**Master Decision Rule — SWF Case Study:**
IF institutional investor is considering direct investments in frontier/developing markets → must go BEYOND identifying risks qualitatively → must (1) quantify through scenario analysis, (2) agree on action plan conditional on bad outcomes, (3) explicitly assess reputational and ESG risks from the outset (not as afterthought), (4) recognize that even small investments can create outsized reputational damage.

### 29.9 Cross-Domain Inference Chains — SWF Case Study

**Chain 1: SWF Case → Ethics (Section 23)**
IF SWF invests in company engaging in environmental damage (dumping waste) or poor labor practices (removing soap, cutting breaks) → Standard I(A) Knowledge of the Law may require understanding local environmental/labor regulations → reputational risk to SWF creates conflict with Standard III(A) Duty to Clients (duty to act in best interest of ultimate beneficiaries = citizens of Ruritania) → exiting may be required.

**Chain 2: SWF Case → Alternatives (Section 25)**
IF SWF allocates to illiquid alternatives → Takahashi-Alexander model helps with commitment pacing → but the R-SWF case shows that even well-paced commitments can face unexpected risks → direct investments add control but also operational complexity and liability exposure.

**Chain 3: SWF Case → Currency Management (Section 20)**
IF SWF invests in frontier markets → currency risk is critical but hedging is costly/impossible → R-SWF infrastructure revenue partially naturally hedged (70% airport charges collected in USD via IATA) → ABC had NO natural hedge until sold to international buyer who found local production advantageous.

**Chain 4: SWF Case → Institutional Portfolio Management (Section 17)**
IF SWF is "savings fund" type → negligible liabilities → high risk tolerance → large allocation to alternatives possible → but must still manage liquidity risk especially as illiquid allocation grows → R-SWF's 50% alternatives allocation is aggressive but manageable given no cash flow pressure from liabilities.

---

## Section 31: Investment Planning — Taxation, Portfolio Optimization & Performance Evaluation (PW V1 Module 4)

### 31.1 Expected Return Estimation for Private Wealth

**Equity Return Estimation — Key Methods:**
1. **Gordon Growth Model (adjusted):** Expected Return = Forward Dividend Yield + Growth Rate of Dividends. Dilution reduces contribution from growth term by ~2% (Bernstein & Arnott 2003).
2. **Shiller CAPE (Cyclically Adjusted P/E):** Price / 10-year average real earnings. High CAPE → low expected returns and vice versa.
3. **Reverse-engineered Dividend Discount Model:** Extract implied cost of equity from current prices. Published as "Implied Cost of Capital" at market-risk-premia.com.

**Other Asset Classes:** When no data available, estimate from first principles (biological growth for timber, spot/futures relationships for commodities) or by adding/subtracting risk premia to baseline equity/bond estimates. For collectibles and art, reluctantly use historical estimates (which are biased).

**Bond Return Estimation:** Over a period equal to 2× modified duration, total return ≈ initial yield (Langetieg, Leibowitz, Kogelman theorem). For a 10-year horizon, use the 5-year bond yield.

**Decision Rule:** Equity expected return harder to estimate than bond expected return because future cash flows (dividends, buybacks) are far more uncertain than bond coupon payments.

### 31.2 Risk Estimation — Shrinkage and Illiquid Assets

**Covariance Matrix Estimation:**
- Use ~5 years of monthly returns
- Apply **shrinkage**: weighted average of sample correlation matrix and identity matrix (Ledoit & Wolf 2021)
- Use **robust statistical estimators** (R package RobStatTM) to handle outliers

**Illiquid Asset Risk Estimation:**
- Returns-based estimates grossly understate true risk (stale pricing, smoothed returns)
- Estimate from first principles when possible (e.g., PE volatility ≈ small growth stock vol × leverage)
- Example: PE fund with 2:1 leverage, 16 independent investments in firms with 40% individual vol → fund vol ≈ 40% × 2 / √16 = 20%

**Risk Aversion Coefficient (γ):**
- MVO: maximize (Expected return − γ/2 × Portfolio variance)
- γ typically between 1 and 10, with 3 being typical
- γ = 1 → maximizes geometric return and median wealth
- γ < 1 → variance so badly impacts portfolio that optimal converges to minimum-variance portfolio

### 31.3 Taxation Framework for Private Wealth

**Four Types of Taxes:** Taxes on revenues/income, taxes on wealth, taxes on consumption, taxes on gifts/inheritances/estates.

**Post-Tax Impact Formulas:**
- Post-tax expected return ≈ Pre-tax expected return × (1 − tx)
- Post-tax standard deviation ≈ Pre-tax SD × (1 − tx)
- Post-tax variance ≈ Pre-tax variance × (1 − tx)²

**Counter-Intuitive Tax Effect on Risk Aversion:**
Taxable investor's effective risk aversion = λ × (1 − tx), which is LOWER than tax-exempt investor → taxes make investors LESS risk averse → optimal portfolio tilts MORE toward equities and LESS toward bonds for taxable investors. This is because the government effectively absorbs some of the investment risk.

**Tax-Aware Utility Function:**
U = R_after_tax − (λ/2) × σ²_after_tax − Capital gains tax rate
Capital gains tax = deterministic drag on expected return (not on risk).

**Tax Loss Harvesting:** Sell securities at a loss to offset capital gains → reduce tax bill. Watch for wash sale rules (US: 30 days, UK: same, Australia: no specific rule but authorities may challenge, Germany: allowed, Japan: limited and often disallowed).

**Tax Lot Management:** Each purchase creates a separate tax lot. Tax-aware optimization differentiates lots by trade date; tax-unaware optimization groups by simple percentage weights. Tax-aware portfolios have significantly lower turnover (73% vs. 110%) and fewer trades (943 vs. 2,902).

**Asset Location Strategy:**
- Tax-advantaged accounts (retirement accounts) → place highest-taxed assets (bonds generating taxable interest, hedge funds with short-term gains)
- Taxable accounts → place tax-efficient assets (equities with qualified dividends, low-turnover index funds)

### 31.4 Concentrated Positions — Complementarity Portfolio Strategy

**Two challenges:** (1) under-diversification, (2) large unrealized capital gains make selling tax-costly.

**Complementarity Portfolio (DiBartolomeo, Horvitz, Wilcox 2006):**
- Fund a separate "sidecar" portfolio that is fully diversified, designed to INCLUDE the concentrated position
- Sidecar generates short-term losses from rebalancing → offset long-term capital gains when concentrated position is eventually liquidated
- Key characteristic: takes a long time to implement (sidecar slowly generates loss harvesting opportunities)
- Win-win: if losses occur, they offset concentrated position gains; if no losses occur, sidecar earns strong return (taxable but profitable)

### 31.5 Direct Indexing vs. Portfolio Customization

**Direct Indexing:** Index-based investing with tax-aware strategies built into benchmark-relative rebalancing. Minimizes short-term capital gains realization. Aims to surpass benchmark on after-tax basis.
- Challenge: pre-tax tracking error constraint leaves little room for truly tax-efficient trading

**Portfolio Customization:** Prioritizes ALL investor goals and circumstances over benchmark-relative performance. More tolerant of tracking error → more room for tax-efficient trades.

### 31.6 Centralized Portfolio Management (CPM)

**DiBartolomeo (1992):** Single trading desk for all managers serving one beneficiary → cross-validate alpha signals, minimize turnover via netting trades, reduce capital gains tax → empirically demonstrated benefits (Bouchey & Pritamani 2018).

**Family Tax Optimization:** For jointly filing family members → check wash sales ACROSS both accounts. For individually filing members → check wash sales within each account only. Tax lot disposition, effective tax rate, and tax drag must be incorporated per-account and at the aggregate family level.

### 31.7 Performance Evaluation for Private Wealth Clients

**Arithmetic Active Return:** R_A = R_P − R_B (portfolio return minus benchmark return)
**Logarithmic Active Return:** R̃_A = ln(1 + R_P) − ln(1 + R_B) — preferred because it accounts for compounding

**Key Insight — Return Variability Hurts Long-Run Returns:**
- Portfolio with returns +10%, −10%, +15% → cumulative = 13.85%
- Benchmark with constant 5% monthly → cumulative = 15.76%
- The arithmetic sum of returns is the same, but compounding penalizes variability
- This is the arithmetic-geometric inequality: arithmetic mean ≥ geometric mean

**Risk-Adjusted Measures:**
- **Sharpe Ratio** = Mean(Excess return) / SD(Excess return)
- **Information Ratio** = Mean(Active return) / SD(Active return)
- **Logarithmic versions** preferred (account for compounding)
- **Sortino Ratio** = (Mean return − MAR) / Semi-deviation(Return − MAR) — uses minimum acceptable return (MAR) instead of risk-free rate, denominator only counts downside deviations

**VaR vs. Expected Shortfall (ES):**
- VaR = loss not exceeded X% of the time (e.g., 95% VaR = 5th percentile loss)
- ES (CVaR) = expected loss GIVEN that VaR is exceeded → captures tail risk that VaR misses
- ES adopted as primary loss measure by Basel Committee in 2016

**Statistical Significance — t-statistic:**
t = (Sample mean / Sample SD) × √N
- Portfolio with 10% return, 20% SD → need N > 16 years for t > 2 (95% confidence)
- Manager with IR = 1 and active SD = 2% → need N ≥ 4 years for t > 2
- CUSUM (Statistical Process Control) → detects underperformance in ~3.5 years vs. 16 years for simple t-test

**Brinson-Hood-Beebower Attribution:**
- Allocation effect: A = r^A − r^B (allocation portfolio return − benchmark return)
- Selection effect: S = r^S − r^B (selection portfolio return − benchmark return)
- Interaction effect: I = r^P − r^A − r^S + r^B
- Brinson-Fachler simplification: fold interaction into selection term

**Factor-Based Attribution:** Regress active returns on factor collection (term spread, credit spread, value/growth, small/large, momentum, etc.) → decompose returns into systematic factor exposures vs. idiosyncratic alpha.

### 31.8 Module 4 Practice Problem Decision Rules (Q1-16)

**Q1:** Diminishing marginal utility of wealth → risk averse (concave utility function). Answer: A.
**Q2:** Washington's adequate wealth + 15-year recovery time + basic needs met = risk CAPACITY supports commodities/real estate allocation. Answer: A (risk capacity).
**Q3:** Bond return over 10-year horizon ≈ initial yield of bond with duration = half the horizon = 5-year yield = 4.5%. Answer: A.
**Q4:** Portfolio C best matches Washington's preferences: modest allocations to RE/commodities (unfamiliar but accepted), overweight European equities (her preference), underweight US, conservative tilt. Answer: C.
**Q5:** Tax-exempt investor (Foy in retirement account) should be MORE risk averse than his taxable brother. Effective risk aversion for taxable investor = λ(1−tx) < λ. Answer: A (less risk averse than brother = wrong; more risk averse is correct → A is correct because Foy's optimal portfolio will be MORE risk averse).
**Q6:** Funding ratio = Assets / Liabilities = 5,400,000 / 4,723,000 = 1.14. Answer: C.
**Q7:** 30% decline in investment portfolio: 1,500,000 × 0.30 = 500,000 loss. Surplus declines by 500,000/677,000 = 73.86% ≈ 74%. Answer: C.
**Q8:** Expected return on HY bonds = (1 − default rate × loss given default) × (1 + YTM) − 1 ≈ (95% × 1.09 + 5% × 0.6 × 1.09) − 1 = 6.55% ≈ 7%. Answer: C.
**Q9:** Portfolio customization = more tolerant of tracking error than direct indexing. Answer: C.
**Q10:** Equities (Fund A) in taxable joint account because equities get preferential tax treatment (qualified dividends, LTCG rates) vs. bonds (ordinary income tax). Answer: A.
**Q11:** Post-tax SD ≈ Pre-tax SD × (1 − tx). Hedge fund (short-term trading) taxed at 24% → 23% × (1 − 0.24) = 17.48%. Answer: A.
**Q12:** Wash sale applies to related tax filers or same investment account. Edward's parents file separately and use a different account → NO wash sale triggered. Answer: A.
**Q13:** Rouch specified minimum acceptable return (2% compound real return) → Logarithmic Sortino Ratio incorporates both MAR and compounding. Answer: B.
**Q14:** Selection effect = r^S − r^B. r^S = 0.6 × 5% + 0.4 × 6% = 5.4%. r^B = 0.6 × 6% + 0.4 × 5% = 5.6%. Selection = 5.4% − 5.6% = −0.2%. Answer: B.
**Q15:** Compound active return = ln(1 + R_P) − ln(1 + R_B) = ln(1.073) − ln(1.062) = 1.0% (note: NOT arithmetic difference of 7.3% − 6.2% = 1.1%). Answer: B (1.1% is closest listed answer — the logarithmic return is 1.0% but Answer B rounds to 1.1%).
**Q16:** t-statistic = IR × √N → 2 = 0.6 × √N → N = (2/0.6)² = 11.11 → need at least 11 years. Answer: C.

**Key Discrimination Rules — Performance Measures:**
- Risk-free rate increases → Sharpe Ratio DECREASES, Information Ratio UNCHANGED (risk-free rate affects excess return but not active return)
- Logarithmic Sharpe < Arithmetic Sharpe (variability penalty)
- If Logarithmic IR is negative but Arithmetic IR is zero → portfolio underperformed on compound basis despite matching on simple average basis
- Subtracting a constant (risk-free) from a random sequence → does NOT change standard deviation → SD(active return) = SD(excess return) when benchmark is constant
