## Section 32: Preserving the Wealth — Human Capital Risk, Insurance & Longevity Management

### 32.1 Risk Management Framework for Private Clients (ALM Approach)

**Five-Step Process:** Identify → Measure → Evaluate → Respond → Monitor (continuous cycle)

**Risk Response Matrix:**
- High probability + High impact → AVOID
- High probability + Low impact → MITIGATE
- Low probability + High impact → TRANSFER (insurance)
- Low probability + Low impact → ACCEPT (retain)

**Surplus-Based Decision Making:** UHNW families' assets greatly exceed liabilities → large surplus → can self-insure more risks (higher deductibles, lower premiums). Mass affluent may have liabilities approaching assets → must transfer more risks via insurance.

### 32.2 Human Capital Risk — Three Defining Characteristics

**Three factors that define human capital risk:**
1. **Earnings Volatility:** Probability of unemployment AND earnings variance when employed
2. **Earnings Correlation:** How correlated are earnings with equity markets? Investment banker = HIGH correlation (cyclical bonuses). Professor = LOW correlation (stable salary).
3. **Replaceability:** How easily can income be replaced? Nuclear physicist = LOW replaceability (specialized). HR professional = HIGH replaceability (transferable).

**Decision Rule:** Least HC risk = government HR administrator (stable, transferable, non-cyclical). Most HC risk = hedge fund manager (volatile, highly correlated, hard to replace).

**Inference Chain for Portfolio Construction:**
Equity-like HC (high vol + high correlation + low replaceability) → financial portfolio should be CONSERVATIVE (more bonds) → total portfolio risk balanced.
Bond-like HC (low vol + low correlation + high replaceability) → financial portfolio can be AGGRESSIVE (more equities) → total portfolio risk balanced.

### 32.3 Mortality, Longevity & Health Risks

**Key Distinction:** Healthier lifestyle REDUCES mortality risk but INCREASES longevity risk → they move in OPPOSITE directions.

**Sequence-of-Returns Risk:**
- Accumulation phase: return order DOESN'T matter (same ending wealth regardless)
- Decumulation phase: return order CRITICALLY matters → poor early returns + withdrawals = sell more at depressed prices → permanent capital depletion
- Higher volatility AMPLIFIES sequence risk dramatically
- Periodic withdrawals = INVERSE of dollar-cost averaging (works against retiree)

### 32.4 Probability of Ruin — Quantitative Anchors (Milevsky & Robinson 2005)

**50/50 portfolio, 5% arithmetic mean, 12% SD, 4.28% geometric mean:**
- 4% withdrawal at age 65 → ~9% ruin probability
- 4% withdrawal at age 50 → ~14% ruin
- Endowment (infinite horizon) at 4% → 49% ruin (!)
- 2% withdrawal → <2% ruin at any age

**Three Factors Driving Probability of Ruin:**
1. Withdrawal rate (most important)
2. Time horizon (longer retirement = higher risk)
3. Portfolio expected return (lower returns = higher risk)

**Safety Reserve:** Evensky et al. (2011): 2 years of spending in safe assets. Serves both financial and behavioral purposes (prevents panic selling during downturns).

**Retirement Needs Analysis Methods:**
1. **Simple Method:** Multiply desired spending by 17-29× (inverse of 3.5%-6% withdrawal rate)
2. **Mortality Table Method:** PV(Spending Need) = Σ [p(Survival_j) × Spending_j / (1+r)^j]. Discount at real risk-free rate (NOT portfolio expected return — avoid circular logic). For couples: joint survival probability = p(H survives) + p(W survives) - p(H) × p(W).
3. **Monte Carlo:** Most comprehensive — simulates cash flows, returns, inflation, taxes, mortality. Accounts for skewness, kurtosis, autocorrelation in returns. Safety reserve can be smaller (model already captures sequence risk).

**Webster Case Quantitative Anchor:** EUR 500,000/year spending growing at 3% inflation, discounted at 2% real rate, 32-year horizon → PV of retirement needs ≈ EUR 9.177M.

**Kotlikoff Rule:** Maintaining lifestyle for two costs ~1.6× one person. If one spouse dies → surviving spouse needs ~62.5% of joint spending.

### 32.5 Life Insurance for Wealth Management

**Two Methods to Calculate Insurance Need:**
1. **Human Life Value (Asset-Side):** PV(future earnings) - PV(personal expenses) + final expenses = coverage
2. **Needs Analysis (Liability-Side):** Immediate death costs + ongoing survivor expenses → PV → subtract existing assets = insurance gap

**Insurance Types:**
- Term: pure death protection, no cash value, cheapest
- Whole life: lifetime coverage, cash value builds slowly, participating (dividends) vs. non-participating
- Universal life: flexible premiums, flexible investments, cash value
- Endowment: fixed term, pays face value at maturity if alive

**Non-Forfeiture Clause (when premiums lapse):** Cash surrender / Reduced paid-up / Extended term

**UHNW Life Insurance Applications:**
- Life insurance wrappers (EU): place investment portfolio inside policy → tax-free growth
- Estate liquidity: pay inheritance/estate taxes without forced asset sales
- Legacy/charitable: fund bequests
- Key person insurance: business insures executives

### 32.6 Annuity Decision Framework

**Fixed vs. Variable:**
- Fixed annuity → bond-like income stream (stable, guaranteed)
- Variable annuity → equity-like income (fluctuating, higher potential)

**Factors increasing annuity demand:** Longer life expectancy, preference for income stability, low bequest motive, conservative investment preferences, low guaranteed income from other sources.

**Decision Rule:** Risk-tolerant households → self-insure via portfolio drawdown. Risk-averse households → buy annuities (transfer longevity risk to insurer).

### 32.7 Module 5 Practice Problem Decision Rules (Q1-6)

- Q1 (Five risk management steps): Identify → Measure → Evaluate → Respond → Monitor. Each with specific activities.
- Q2 (Mass affluent to UHNW): As wealth increases, assets eclipse liabilities (B) → focus shifts from capital insufficiency to long-term asset management.
- Q3 (Least HC risk): Government HR administrator (A) — low layoff risk, transferable skills, non-cyclical. Hedge fund manager and fast-food manager both have higher risk.
- Q4 (Healthier lifestyle): Reduces mortality risk ONLY (A). Longer life → higher longevity risk (not lower).
- Q5 (Sequence-of-returns in accumulation vs. decumulation): Greater during WITHDRAWAL phase (C). Without withdrawals, sequence doesn't matter.
- Q6 (Drawback of minimizing longevity risk): May deprive client of resources (A) — over-saving/under-spending in accumulation OR under-spending in decumulation deprives client of benefit of their own wealth.

---

## Section 32: Preserving the Wealth — Risk Management, Insurance & Longevity (PW V1 Module 5)

### 32.1 Risk Management Using Asset-Liability Management

**5-Step Risk Management Process:** Identify → Measure → Evaluate → Respond → Monitor (continuous cycle)

**Risk Response Matrix (by Probability × Impact):**
- High Probability + High Impact → **AVOID** (eliminate the risk entirely)
- High Probability + Low Impact → **MITIGATE** (loss prevention or loss reduction)
- Low Probability + High Impact → **TRANSFER** (insurance, contracts, incorporation)
- Low Probability + Low Impact → **ACCEPT/RETAIN** (self-insure, absorb the risk)

**Surplus determines risk management approach:** UHNWI/HNWI have large surplus → assets likely exceed liabilities → focus on long-term asset management. Mass affluent may have liabilities > assets → focus on capital insufficiency risk → need both asset growth AND liability management.

**Decision Rule:** Larger surplus does NOT necessarily lead to higher risk ACCEPTANCE. Risk tolerance is key: more risk-tolerant households retain more risk (higher deductibles, less insurance) regardless of surplus level.

### 32.2 Human Capital Risk — Employment Risk Deep Dive

**Three Factors Determining Human Capital Value:**
1. **Earnings Volatility:** Probability of unemployment × volatility of annual earnings when employed. Higher volatility → lower human capital value.
2. **Earnings Correlation with Markets:** Investment banker = high correlation with equity markets (cyclical bonuses, layoffs in downturns) → equity-like human capital → should hold MORE bonds in financial portfolio. Tenured professor = low correlation → bond-like human capital → CAN hold more equities.
3. **Replaceability:** How easily can earnings stream be replaced? Nuclear physicist = low replaceability (narrow skills) → higher employment risk. HR professional = high replaceability (transferable across industries) → lower risk.

**Decision Rule — Human Capital as Asset Class:**
- Equity-like HC (volatile, correlated, hard to replace) → offset with CONSERVATIVE financial portfolio (more bonds)
- Bond-like HC (stable, uncorrelated, easily replaceable) → CAN take more risk in financial portfolio (more equities)

### 32.3 Mortality Risk

**Key Mortality Risk Insights:**
- Premature death extinguishes human capital (earnings stream = 0)
- Impact highest early in career (before marketable skills established) AND when dependents are most vulnerable
- Non-wage earners also have mortality risk (primary caregiver death → household services must be replaced)
- Women have lower mortality rates than men at every age → female human capital exposed to LESS mortality risk, all else equal
- Health risks (smoking, obesity, lack of exercise) significantly affect mortality rates → wealth managers should refine standard mortality tables using client-specific health/family history

### 32.4 Longevity Risk — Retirement Spending Analysis

**Retirement Needs Using Simple Method:**
Capital needed = Annual spending / Sustainable withdrawal rate. Sustainable rate = 3.5%–6% → need 17–29× annual spending.

**Retirement Needs Using Mortality Tables:**
PV(Spending need) = Σ [p(Survival_j) × Spending_j] / (1+r)^j
- For couples: p(Either survives) = p(Husband) + p(Wife) − p(Husband) × p(Wife)
- Discount at REAL risk-free rate (NOT expected return of assets — that would create circular logic)
- Webster case study: EUR 500,000/year spending × 3% inflation → PV of retirement needs = EUR 9,176,955

**Discount Rate Decision Rule:** Use risk-free rate for spending needs because spending risk is NOT correlated with market risk factors (non-systematic mortality risk can be effectively hedged with life insurance → beta ≈ 0). Using expected portfolio return creates circular logic.

**Safety Reserve:** ~2 years of spending (Evensky, Horan, Robinson 2011). Serves as mental cushion (behavioral) + capital cushion (sequence-of-returns protection) + bequest flexibility.

**Probability of Ruin (Milevsky & Robinson 2005):**
- Depends on 3 factors: withdrawal rate, time horizon, expected portfolio return
- 65-year-old, 50/50 portfolio (5% return, 12% vol), 4% withdrawal rate → ~9% probability of ruin over median life
- Higher withdrawal rates + longer horizons + lower returns → higher probability of ruin
- 4.5% fixed withdrawal = 13.4% ruin probability over 30 years (Stout & Mitchell 2006)

**Monte Carlo vs. Mortality Table:**
- Mortality table: discounts at risk-free rate, simple, ignores market risk
- Monte Carlo: simulates portfolio returns AND cash flows, accounts for market risk + spending uncertainty + inflation
- Monte Carlo requires realistic assumptions (skewness, kurtosis, autocorrelation of returns matter — normal distribution assumption may be insufficient)

### 32.5 Sequence-of-Returns Risk

**Key Insight:** With withdrawals, the ORDER of returns matters (not just average). Without withdrawals, order is irrelevant.

**Quantitative Example (JPY 100M portfolio, 10% avg return, JPY 10M annual withdrawal):**
- Good returns first (+25%, +10%, −5%) → ending wealth JPY 101M
- Bad returns first (−5%, +10%, +25%) → ending wealth JPY 94M
- Difference: JPY 6M (6% of initial portfolio) — purely from sequence effect

**Higher volatility amplifies sequence risk:** Same 10% average return but ±35%/15% volatility instead of ±25%/5% → sequence effect doubles to JPY 10M (10% of initial)

**Decumulation ≠ Accumulation:** During accumulation (dollar-cost averaging), market volatility HELPS because you buy more shares when prices are low. During decumulation (fixed withdrawals), volatility HURTS because you sell more shares when prices are low → accelerates depletion.

**Decision Rule:** Consecutive negative returns early in retirement can PERMANENTLY reduce spending capacity → this is the single greatest financial risk in decumulation.

### 32.6 Life Insurance — Types, Methods & Uses

**Two Methods for Calculating Insurance Need:**
1. **Human Life Value Method:** PV of insured's future earnings − PV of future expenses + final expenses (funeral, etc.) = insurance need. Focuses on ASSET side of balance sheet.
2. **Needs Analysis Method:** Estimates immediate death-related costs + ongoing living expenses for survivors − existing assets = insurance need. Focuses on LIABILITY side of balance sheet.

**Term Life Insurance:**
- Coverage for specified period (e.g., 20 years), expires if insured survives
- Level-term = constant premium (higher initial, lower later vs. annually renewable)
- Annually renewable = premium increases each year as mortality risk rises
- Non-cancelable (assumed throughout this reading)

**Permanent Life Insurance:**
- **Whole Life:** Coverage for entire life. Fixed premiums. Builds cash value (investment component). Participating (share insurer profits via dividends) vs. non-participating. Non-forfeiture options: cash surrender, reduced paid-up, extended term.
- **Endowment:** Limited period (10-20 years). If alive at end → face value paid to policyholder. If dies during term → face value paid to beneficiaries.
- **Universal Life:** Flexible premiums. Broader investment options for cash value. Active as long as premiums paid or cash value covers expenses.

**Cash Value Dynamics:** Builds slowly initially → accelerates over time. Insurance value = Face value − Cash value (decreasing protection component). Cash value reverts to insurer upon death (beneficiaries receive only face value).

**Other Uses of Life Insurance Beyond Mortality Protection:**
1. **Immediate financial expenses** (funeral, legal, estate settlement)
2. **Legacy/bequests** (charitable gifts, inheritance planning)
3. **Life insurance wrappers** (EU: place portfolio inside policy → minimize tax on investment income)
4. **Liquidity** (bypasses probate, instant cash for beneficiaries when estate has illiquid assets)
5. **Tax-sheltered savings** (cash value grows tax-free until accessed)
6. **Estate planning** (fund inheritance/estate tax obligations without forced asset sales — critical for SME owners)

**Liability Risk:** For wealthy individuals, personal liability risk (lawsuits, property damage claims) is heightened because substantial assets make them targets. Umbrella insurance and incorporation can transfer this risk.

### 32.7 Responses to Longevity Risk — Annuities

**Annuities pool longevity risk** → insurance company uses law of large numbers to offer lifetime income. Public systems (Social Security, national pensions) are public annuities but often insufficient.

**DB → DC shift** transfers longevity risk from employer/government to individual → increases individual decumulation problem.

**Fixed vs. Variable Annuities:**
- Fixed annuity = set benefit for life (similar to bonds in portfolio)
- Variable annuity = benefit tied to underlying investment performance (similar to equities)
- Choice depends on: benefit volatility tolerance, flexibility needs, market expectations, fees, inflation protection

**Factors Increasing Annuity Demand:**
- Longer life expectancy
- Preference for lifetime income over lump sums
- Reduced emphasis on inheritance
- Conservative investment preferences
- Lower guaranteed income from other sources (pensions)

### 32.8 Risk Response Decision Rules (Section 2 Practice Problems)

**Q1:** 5-step process: Identify → Measure → Evaluate → Respond → Monitor.
**Q2:** Moving from mass affluent to UHNW → assets increasingly eclipse liabilities → focus shifts from capital insufficiency risk to long-term asset management. Answer: B.
**Q3:** Government HR administrator = least human capital risk (low layoff risk + transferable skills + stable earnings). Hedge fund manager = high risk (cyclical, concentrated skills). Fast food manager = moderate (cyclical but replaceable). Answer: A.
**Q4:** Healthier lifestyle → REDUCES mortality risk (live longer) but INCREASES longevity risk (more years to fund in retirement). Answer: A (mortality risk only).
**Q5:** Sequence-of-returns risk is GREATER during withdrawal (decumulation) phase. During accumulation without withdrawals, return sequence is irrelevant. Answer: C.
**Q6:** Drawback of minimizing longevity risk = may deprive client of benefit of their resources (over-saving → under-spending during working years OR under-spending in retirement). Answer: A.

---

### 32.9 Longevity Risk Management Strategies

**Volatility Control — Asset Allocation Approaches:**
- **Static allocation:** 4% rule (Bengen 1994). Little difference in sustainability between 50% and 75% equity, but too few stocks drastically shortens sustainability.
- **Dynamic allocation:** Target-date funds shift toward bonds as retirement approaches. BUT some research suggests INCREASING equity exposure during retirement can enhance sustainability (holding more cash/FI early mitigates sequence-of-returns risk, then shifting to equities later).

**Dynamic Withdrawal Programs:**
1. **Skip inflation adjustment** after years with negative returns (Guyton 2004) → increases sustainable rate from 4.0-4.3% to 5.8-6.3%
2. **Coupon/dividend spending:** Live off income (dividends + coupons) without touching principal. Less volatile than capital gains realization. Requires substantial initial portfolio.
3. **Bounded dynamic withdrawal:** Set upper/lower bounds on withdrawal rate (e.g., 4-6% of current portfolio value). If planned withdrawal falls outside bounds → clip to nearest bound. Jacobs case study: CHF1M portfolio, 5% initial, 4-6% bounds → survived 6 years of mixed returns including two consecutive negative years.

**Laddered Bonds:** Match bond maturities to specific spending needs year by year. Best combined with deferred annuity (e.g., 20-year bond ladder + ALDA starting at age 85). Zero-coupon bonds simplest implementation.

**Bucket Planning (Thaler):** Mental accounting → categorize investments by time horizon/goal. Drawbacks: (1) equity exposure rises as near-term buckets deplete (requires rebalancing), (2) behavioral discomfort from increasing equity exposure, (3) redundant positions across buckets increase transaction costs.

### 32.10 Annuity Deep Dive

**Fixed Annuity Types:**
- **SPIA (Single-Premium Immediate Annuity):** Lump sum → immediate lifetime income. One premium, payments start immediately.
- **Lifetime annuity:** Payments for life of annuitant. Highest payout per dollar invested (no residual benefits).
- **Period-certain:** Fixed number of payments regardless of death. Does NOT mitigate longevity risk.
- **Life with period certain:** Minimum guaranteed payments (e.g., 10 years) + lifetime coverage. Lower payout than pure life annuity.
- **Life with return-of-premium:** If annuitant dies before recouping investment → difference paid to beneficiary.
- **Joint life annuity:** Payments continue over lifespan of two annuitants (usually spouses). Survivor benefit = 50-100% of primary benefit. Lower payout than single life.
- **Inflation-adjusted:** Payments grow with CPI or fixed % per year. Higher cost, weaker inflation protection if fixed % ≠ actual inflation.

**Annuity Payout Rates (Exhibit 19 — representative):**
- 65-year-old male, life only: 6.75% of premium annually
- 65-year-old female, life only: 6.45% (longer life expectancy → lower payout)
- 65-year-old joint: 5.75%
- 85-year-old male, life only: 14.42% (shorter expected life → higher payout)
- Adding period certain reduces payout for same premium

**Deferred Fixed Annuity:** Purchase now, payments start later. Lower cost than immediate annuity for same eventual payout.
**ALDA (Advanced-Life Deferred Annuity):** Purchase at 65, payments start at 85. Very cost-effective longevity hedge because: (1) focuses benefits at end of life when longevity risk is greatest, (2) transfers risk most efficiently to insurance company, (3) creates defined investment horizon for wealth managers (bond ladder covers 65-85, ALDA covers 85+).

**Mortality Credits:** Annuity payment = return of premium + interest + mortality credits. Mortality credits come from deceased annuitants' funds being redistributed to survivors. Self-insuring retirees receive NO mortality credits. This makes annuities financially superior to self-insurance for those who outlive their life expectancy.

**Spending Improvement Q:** For any given year, Q = (1 − p) / p, where p = survival probability. Q is small early (low mortality) but grows dramatically at advanced ages. At age 100: $31.79 per $1 invested.

**Retirement Income Efficient Frontier (Chen & Milevsky 2003):** Trade-off between wealth at death (bequest) and shortfall risk. High annuity allocation → low shortfall risk but low wealth at death. Low annuity allocation → high wealth potential but high shortfall risk.

**Variable Annuities:**
- **Assumed Investment Return (AIR):** Typically 3.5-5%. Lower AIR → smaller initial payments but less chance of payment cuts.
- **GMWB (Guaranteed Minimum Withdrawal Benefit):** Rider on deferred variable annuity guaranteeing 4% of initial investment for life regardless of investment performance. Adds significant cost.

**Behavioral Barriers to Annuitization:** Emotional resistance to mortality discussion; perception of annuity as "gamble" (must outlive life expectancy to "win"); desire to bequeath assets; illiquidity of annuities (irreversible).

### 32.11 Inflation — Types, Measurement & Impact

**Three Types of Inflation:**
1. **Cost-push:** Rising production costs (wages, commodities) → businesses raise prices. Human capital = natural partial hedge (wages tend to rise with cost-push inflation).
2. **Demand-pull:** Excessive demand outstrips supply → prices rise → workers demand higher wages → wage-price spiral.
3. **Unanchored expectations:** Households/firms believe inflation will persist → self-fulfilling behavior. Arnott & Shakernia (2023): reverting to 3% from 4% is easy; from 6% hard; from 8% takes 6-20 years.

**Inflation Persistence:** Positive autocorrelation → inflation exhibits momentum and clustering. Extended calm periods can be followed by rapid flare-ups.

**Inflation Measurement:**
- CPI varies by country (US: CPI-U, China: CPI, India: CPI-UNME, Germany: HICP)
- CPI-E (Consumer Price Index for the Elderly): doubles healthcare weighting (5.6% → 11%), reduces education weighting. CPI-E has risen faster than CPI-W over 40 years.
- Baumol cost disease: education and healthcare inflate faster than CPI (low productivity growth sectors).
- Lower-income households face HIGHER effective inflation than higher-income households.
- Forbes Cost of Living Extremely Well Index: exceeded CPI by 2.4% annually over 34 years.

### 32.12 Inflation Hedging Framework

**Inflation Factor Beta:** β_Inf,Goal = ρ × (σ_Goal / σ_Inf)
- ρ = correlation between investment goal return and inflation
- σ_Goal = volatility of investment goal return
- σ_Inf = volatility of inflation

**Inflation Hedge Ratio:** H_Inf = −(β_Inf,Goal / β_Inf,Hedge) × (Goal / Hedge)
- To eliminate inflation exposure: set β_Target = 0
- Hedging more effective when: correlation is high, relative volatility of goal is high, R² of regression is high

**Asset Class Inflation Correlations (DMS 1900-2022, 35 countries):**
- Bills: −0.80 (worst hedge)
- Bonds: −0.59
- 60/40 portfolio: −0.42
- ILBs (inflation-protected bonds): −0.37 (surprisingly poor — price risk from interest rate fluctuations)
- Housing: −0.31
- Commercial RE: −0.25
- Equities: −0.25 (negative! Equities EXCEED inflation long-term but are NOT good short-term hedges)
- Spot commodities: +0.10 (modest positive)
- Commodity futures: +0.21 (better than spot; 5-year correlation = +0.45)
- Gold: +0.34 (highest, but only post-1972; full 1900-2022 correlation = −0.04)

**Key Decision Rules — Inflation Hedging:**
- Most assets are NEGATIVELY correlated with inflation → most assets are unsuitable inflation hedges
- Equities SURPASS inflation over decades but do NOT HEDGE against it (distinction between surpassing and hedging)
- Only commodities have positive inflation correlations → best direct hedge
- Commodity futures by inflation type: energy → cost-push; industrial metals → demand-pull; precious metals → monetary/banking crisis
- Gold: excellent crisis hedge but poor long-term inflation hedge (real price extremely volatile 1975-2012)
- Real estate: better inflation hedge long-term (rents adjust) than short-term (leases are fixed)
- Borrowing (short FI position) = highest inflation-correlated asset → effectively hedges inflation but introduces leverage risk
- No single investment tool effectively hedges inflation → diversification across multiple partial hedges is best approach

**Inflation × Taxes Interaction:**
FVIF_cgb = (1 + r)^n × (1 − t_cg) + t_cg × B
- B = cost basis as proportion of current market value
- As inflation increases → nominal returns increase → tax on nominal gains increases → real after-tax return DECREASES
- Effect is multiplicative: high inflation × high tax rate × long horizon = devastating compound effect
- Example: HUF120M portfolio, 20% nominal return, 12% inflation, 40% CG tax → nominal after-tax value = HUF112M → real after-tax value = HUF100M (ZERO real gain despite 20% nominal return)

### 32.13 Exchange Rate Risk for Multi-Jurisdictional Families

**Reasons for Cross-Border Asset Location:**
1. Favorable taxation (Singapore: no CG tax, BVI: no income/CG tax)
2. Protection from currency devaluation (Turkish lira: −90% in 10 years)
3. Legal protection from third-party claims (BVI common law jurisdiction)
4. Protection from government confiscation (Venezuela, Mexico oil nationalization)

**Risk: Strategies intended to reduce one risk may introduce others.** Example: Cyprus investors (2013) sought tax advantages → banking crisis seized deposits > EUR100,000. Strategy introduced confiscation risk while reducing tax risk.

**Hemmingworth Family Case Study — Multi-Currency Balance Sheet:**
Family extended balance sheet must be decomposed by currency denomination:
- EUR-denominated assets: HC (EUR7M), property (EUR500K), investments (EUR1M), cash (EUR100K)
- USD-denominated assets: investments (USD1.5M), cash (USD50K)
- EUR-denominated liabilities: living expenses PV (EUR800K), mortgage (EUR200K)
- USD-denominated liabilities: education (USD900K), retirement (USD5M)
- Currency surplus/deficit reveals FX risk: if EUR weakens, EUR-denominated assets shrink in USD terms → surplus at risk

**Decision Rule:** The family's REFERENCE CURRENCY is determined by their long-term spending plans, not their current income currency. Hemmingworth family earns EUR but plans to retire in US → reference currency = USD → EUR-denominated assets carry currency risk.

---

## Section 33: Entrepreneurs, Business Owners & Concentrated Position Management (PW V1 Module 6)

### 33.1 Concentrated Position Exit Strategies

**Strategic Buyer vs. Recapitalization — Decision Framework:**
- **Strategic buyer (outright sale):** Frees owner from daily operations, enables more family time, likely yields HIGHEST immediate proceeds, avoids upcoming tax hikes. BUT: owner loses CEO role and business identity.
- **Recapitalization (PE firm):** Owner KEEPS CEO role, retains equity stake (typically 20%), PE provides debt financing for growth, potential for second exit (IPO or strategic sale) if growth expectations realized. BUT: owner retains concentrated risk in business, PE firm has exit timeline.

**After-Tax Monetization Calculation:**
Amount monetized = Firm valuation × Percent of equity sold × (1 − Capital gains tax rate)
- Garcia example: USD45M × 80% × (1 − 0.15) = USD30.6M after-tax
- Remaining stake value = USD45M × 20% = USD9M (before discounts for lack of control/marketability)

**Risk Bucket Reallocation Post-Monetization:**
After-tax proceeds allocated to personal risk bucket (cash, ST FI, TIPS) and market risk bucket (LT FI, equities, alternatives). Garcia: USD10M → personal, USD20.3M → market. Primary capital = personal + market risk buckets. Net worth declined 10.8% (USD50M → USD44.6M) due to CG taxes + portfolio realignment. Risk allocation shifted: 27% personal, 53% market, 20% aspirational.

**Decision Rule:** IF impending CG tax increase → favor selling/monetizing BEFORE increase to lock in lower rate. BUT weigh against present value of step-up in basis at death (if jurisdiction allows) — for young owners with 30+ year horizons, PV of step-up is LOW → diversification benefits dominate.

### 33.2 Mitigating Concentrated Risk — Completion Portfolio

**Completion Portfolio:** Index-based portfolio designed via quantitative optimization to balance the concentrated asset against diversified holdings. The optimization process:
1. Account for ALL explicit and implicit assets on extended balance sheet
2. Treat family business as distinct asset with its own volatility and correlations
3. Limit illiquid portfolio weight to equity retained in family business
4. Adjust remaining liquid assets to form efficient frontier portfolio

**Key Result:** Completion portfolio OFFSETS high equity exposure from illiquid business by adopting conservative stance for liquid portfolio — favoring assets uncorrelated or negatively correlated with the family business. If permitted, may use inverse ETFs for short risk exposure through long positions.

**Tax-Optimized Completion Portfolio:** Initially funded by partially selling concentrated position, then rebalanced using quantitative model that minimizes active risk vs benchmark while maximizing after-tax returns — realizing more capital losses than gains, using losses to offset gains from concentrated stock sales. Portfolio managed on AGGREGATE basis to match broad index pre-tax and OUTPERFORM after taxes.

**Mancuso Family Case Study:**
- IT business = USD25M (cost basis zero), liquid portfolio = USD15M, total extended BS = USD42M
- Current equity allocation = 83.3% (business + equities + alternatives treated as equity-like = USD35M/USD42M)
- Completion portfolio: reduce liquid equities, increase less-risky assets, EXCLUDE IT sector investments (high correlation with business), if permitted allocate to negatively correlated assets (inverse IT ETFs)

### 33.3 Managing Concentrated Real Estate Positions

**Unique Real Estate Risks:** Concentration in RE sector, specific property risk, illiquidity (lengthy transactions, private negotiations), low cost basis making outright sale tax-costly.

**Monetization Without Selling — Debt/Equity Financing:**
- Mortgage financing: use property as collateral, monetize built-up equity via loan proceeds
- LTV ratio determines leverage: 60% LTV on NOK100M property = NOK60M tax-free monetization
- Non-recourse loans preferred (downside protection — lender takes property if default, no personal liability)
- Interest-only loans with balloon payments = another financing option (rental income covers interest, retains upside)

**Decision Rule:** Debt-based monetization strategies work best in: stable financial environments, consistent interest rates, long-term upward property trends. In volatile periods: collateral value at risk, potential forced liquidation, floating rate borrowings add element of risk.

**Gustafsson Case Study:** SEK250M net worth, 90% in grocery business + RE. Strategies: (1) personal line of credit against company shares as collateral, (2) real estate loan on store properties (non-recourse preferred). Cash proceeds → diversified portfolio while retaining business ownership. Alternative: acquire complementary or uncorrelated business (logistics, food distribution for vertical integration; or completely different sector like renewable energy as hedge).

### 33.4 Psychological Barriers to Diversification

**Emotional Biases (harder to correct — drawing attention may cause defensiveness):**
- Overconfidence/familiarity (illusion of knowledge about the business)
- Status quo bias (preference for no change)
- Naive extrapolation of past returns (recency bias)
- Endowment effect (asking price >> buying price for same asset)
- Loyalty effects (to company, deceased spouse's wishes, family identity)

**Cognitive Biases (easier to correct — investor more receptive to evidence):**
- Conservatism (reluctance to update beliefs)
- Confirmation bias (seeking confirming evidence only)
- Illusion of control (overestimating control over outcomes)
- Anchoring (adjusting insufficiently from initial reference point)
- Availability heuristic (probability influenced by ease of recall)

**Adviser Strategy:** For emotional biases → ask hypothetical: "If you received this amount in cash today, would you invest it all in this one stock?" For inherited positions → explore donor's true intent (was it specific investment conviction, or simply to benefit heirs?). For cognitive biases → present contradicting evidence directly (investor more likely to accept correction).

### 33.5 Bailer/CCC Case Study — Constraints on Concentrated Positions

**Profile:** Pierce Bailer, 55, retired CEO of CCC (public company). 3M shares worth USD60M = 80% of USD75M portfolio. Cost basis ≈ zero. 15% CG tax rate → USD9M tax liability on full sale. CG rate expected to rise to 23% next year. Step-up in basis available at death (jurisdiction allows). Life expectancy: 87 years (32 years remaining).

**Primary Objectives (ordered):**
1. Significantly reduce concentration risk
2. Monetize to satisfy spending needs
3. Achieve objectives in most tax-efficient manner

**Constraints Analysis:**
- CG tax is primary constraint (USD9M at 15%, rising to USD13.8M at 23% — USD4.8M increase)
- Impending tax increase → SELL THIS YEAR to lock in 15% rate
- Step-up in basis has LOW present value over 32-year horizon → diversification benefits outweigh waiting for step-up
- Former insider restrictions NOW ELIMINATED (left board of directors)
- Previous: employment contract, company policy, securities regulations all restricted sales → constraints removed

**Behavioral Biases Identified:**
- Mr. Bailer: loyalty, overconfidence/familiarity, confirmation bias
- Mrs. Bailer: status quo bias, naive extrapolation, anchoring/adjustment

### 33.6 Family Governance

**Three-Generation Wealth Decline:** Founder creates wealth → second generation maintains → third generation depletes. Universal phenomenon (70% of family businesses fail before Gen 2 takeover, only 10% survive to Gen 3).

**Causes:** Wealth dilution among more descendants, declining interest in family business, lack of education/planning, divergent values/work ethic across generations.

**Family Governance Framework:** Regular communication, collective decision-making, family constitution. Voting vs non-voting shares for control management. Transfer ownership to trust/vehicle BEFORE sale to remove future appreciation from estate (discount for lack of control/marketability reduces gift/estate tax). Sale proceeds → family foundation or philanthropic entity to maintain family unity post-business.

### 33.7 Cameron Case Study — Business Exit Strategies

**Profile:** Blaine Cameron, 70, founder of tool & dye company. Daughter Sophia = COO (groomed for leadership). Son Drake = uninvolved but family identifies with business.

**IPO/Strategic Sale Assessment:** INAPPROPRIATE because: (1) family identity tied to company, (2) Sophia plays active role, (3) mid-market company in non-growth industry → IPO premature. Explore alternative exits.

**Alternative Exit Options:**
- **MBO (Management Buyout):** Sophia has potential, but likely lacks financial resources → Cameron may need to provide seller financing. Family retains control. Limited initial cash but steady debt repayments.
- **Leveraged Recapitalization:** Greater initial monetization. Can structure equity stake for Sophia as incentive. Better than MBO for Cameron's liquidity needs.
- **ESOP (Employee Stock Ownership Plan):** Provides initial liquidity but unlikely to offer control features Cameron desires.
- **Borrowing against shares:** Puttable debt for price protection, or non-recourse loan (tender shares if value falls below loan — cedes control).

**Post-Sale Issues:** Family conflicts when wealth allocated among members (Sophia active, Drake not). Family governance system (constitution) essential. Plan for CG tax liability and ensure adequate liquidity.

## Section 34: Managing Concentrated Positions for Professionals & Executives (PW V1 Module 5 Section 5)

### 34.1 Executive Wealth Sources vs. Traditional Earners

**Executive compensation components:** Salary (larger HC than traditional), bonuses (variable, complicates cash flow planning), stock options/equity awards (increase concentration), deferred compensation (adds risk if equity-based), retirement plans (typically generous), perquisites.

**Spending Pattern Implications:** Variable income → cautious spending in low-bonus years. Wealth accumulation prioritized through stock options. High fixed costs (lifestyle maintenance) → vulnerability in downturns.

### 34.2 Public vs. Private Company Concentrated Positions

**Public company advantages for managing concentration:**
- Liquid derivatives available as underlying → better hedging transparency, pricing, lower costs
- Publicly available price discovery → easier valuation
- Eliminates marketability discounts
- OTC derivatives reference liquid public equity → dealers can hedge more efficiently → better pricing

**Public company constraints (NOT faced by private owners):**
- Deferred compensation contracts restricting sales
- Disclosure requirements (public filing of intended trades)
- Insider trading restrictions (MNPI rules, blackout periods)
- Lockup agreements post-IPO
- Company policies discouraging executive share sales
- Right of first refusal requirements

### 34.3 OTC vs. Exchange-Traded Derivatives

**OTC Derivatives:**
- Single counterparty credit risk (vs clearinghouse for exchange-traded)
- Early termination possible but costly (counterparty extracts concession)
- Priced through single dealer negotiation → obtain competing bids from multiple dealers
- Fees less transparent (embedded in instrument pricing, especially prepaid variable forwards)
- Minimum size typically ~USD3M
- Maximum flexibility on terms and conditions

**Exchange-Traded Derivatives:**
- Clearinghouse as counterparty → lower credit risk
- Robust price discovery
- Standardized contracts → less flexibility
- Smaller minimum sizes
- Transparent fees (commissions on trade tickets)
- Can close out by offsetting with any market participant

**Prepaid Variable Forward:** Owner receives immediate cash WITHOUT triggering sale. Provides liquidity + downside hedge. No immediate tax consequences. Contract specifies variable future delivery of shares (flexibility on number/value). Beneficial for investors wanting to cash in on significant equity holdings without immediate tax impact.

### 34.4 Hedging Strategies for Public Company Concentrated Positions

**Strategy 1 — Short Against the Box:**
- Short sell same number of shares as long position → creates riskless position (long + short cancel out)
- Earns money market return on USD equivalent of position
- Can borrow up to ~99% of stock value (high LTV) → proceeds into diversified portfolio
- Net borrowing cost low (interest income offsets margin interest)
- PROBLEM: Typically triggers taxable event (realized CG) → best used when cost basis is HIGH (unrealized loss) or to offset other gains

**Strategy 2 — Protective Put:**
- Buy put option on concentrated stock → establishes minimum selling price (strike price)
- Retain unlimited upside, dividend rights, voting rights
- Put strike typically at/just below current market price (like insurance deductible)
- Cost depends on: volatility, strike price, risk-free rate, time to maturity
- Must be rolled over at expiration → rollover risk (absorb losses down to strike on each renewal)
- Tax implication: put profits often taxed as capital gains
- Perception risk: market may view CEO buying puts negatively (signals lack of confidence)

**Strategy 3 — Zero-Cost Collar:**
- Simultaneously sell OTM call + buy OTM put → call premium finances put premium (zero net cost)
- Creates bounded payoff: protected below put strike, capped above call strike
- LeMesurier case: 100K shares × USD40 = USD4M position. Buy USD36 puts at USD3, sell USD48 calls at USD3 → zero cost. Stock closes at USD42 → both expire worthless.
- **Tax trap with exchange-traded collar:** Call premium = immediate short-term CG. Put premium added to cost basis (NOT deductible until shares sold). Asymmetric tax timing.
- **OTC collar advantage:** Documented as single instrument → no separate tax events until collar settlement. More tax-efficient but sacrifices exchange-traded benefits (transparency, counterparty safety, price discovery).

**Decision Rule — OTC vs Exchange-Traded Collar:** IF tax efficiency is priority AND willing to accept single-dealer counterparty risk → use OTC collar. IF transparency, lower counterparty risk, and price discovery are priorities → use exchange-traded options (accept tax asymmetry).

**Strategy 4 — Covered Call:**
- Sell call options on concentrated stock position → collect premium income
- NOT a true hedge: only offsets losses by amount of premium collected. Maximum loss = stock price − premium received.
- Caps upside at call strike price. More accurately described as "yield enhancement" strategy.
- Useful as: (1) income generation when stock expected to trade in range, (2) structured selling program (mentally prepares owner for eventual sale), (3) alternative to staged diversification.

**Banner Case Study — Strategy Comparison (300K shares × EUR76):**

| Strategy | Max Profit | Max Loss | Net Cost |
|----------|-----------|----------|----------|
| Protective Put (K=70) | Unlimited | EUR3.3M | EUR1.5M premium |
| Zero-Cost Collar (70/80) | EUR1.2M | EUR1.8M | Zero |
| Covered Call (K=80) | EUR2.7M | EUR21.3M | Zero (receives EUR1.5M) |

**Decision Rule:** Protective put = best downside protection + unlimited upside but costly. Zero-cost collar = free downside protection but caps upside + allows monetization via borrowing. Covered call = highest max profit potential among free strategies but NO downside protection → significant risk.

### 34.5 Tax Considerations for Hedging Strategies

**Constructive Sale / Deemed Disposition Risk:** Tax authorities may view narrow-band collars as constructive sales → triggering immediate CG tax. Wide band collars (significant spread between put and call strikes) reduce this risk.

**Tax Treatment of Option Premiums:**
- **Long positions (puts bought):** If expires worthless → taxable loss (ST or LT). If exercised → premium increases cost basis of underlying (reduces future CG). If closed before expiry → net difference is taxable income/loss.
- **Short positions (calls sold):** If expires worthless → premium is taxable ordinary income or ST CG. If exercised against → premium decreases cost basis (increases realized gain on stock). If closed before expiry → net difference is taxable.
- **In-the-money call options near expiry:** May cause dividends to be taxed at ordinary income rate instead of qualified dividend rate.

**Tax-Efficient Hedging Principles:**
1. Recognize taxable income/CG at relatively LOW tax rates
2. Recognize tax-deductible expenses/losses at relatively HIGH tax rates
3. Defer tax-associated income/CG (hold pretax cash flow constant)
4. Accelerate deductions/losses as preferred (hold pretax cash flow constant)

**Tax-Inefficient Scenarios:** (1) Covered call premiums taxed as ordinary income when expire worthless, (2) put premium added to cost basis (deferred, not deductible now), (3) CG from closing positions taxed at higher ST rate than LT stock rate.

### 34.6 Collar Variations to Enhance Upside

- **Wider collar:** Lower put strike → lower put premium → higher call strike achievable → more upside retained. BUT: more downside risk below put strike.
- **Put spread collar:** Replace standard put with bear put spread (buy higher-K put, sell lower-K put) → net put cost reduced → can raise call strike. Exposes investor below short put strike BUT increases upside cap.
- **Debit collar:** Pay some out-of-pocket for put premium (supplement with call premium) → allows higher call strike than zero-cost collar. Trade-off: some cash outlay for more upside.

### 34.7 Equity Monetization WITHOUT Sale

**Five Strategies — All Avoid Immediate Tax Event:**

**1. Personal Line of Credit:** Borrow against shares as collateral. No tax event (loan, not sale). Interest may be tax-deductible. Proceeds → diversified portfolio. Private company: lower LTV (30-60%) due to illiquidity. Non-recourse loan (Lombard loan) = synthetic protective put (owner can walk away if collateral < loan). Monetization tactic, NOT risk-reduction strategy (leverage introduces systematic risk).

**2. Total Return Swap:** Investor pays counterparty share appreciation + dividends; counterparty pays reference rate + any depreciation. Position fully hedged → resembles short against the box but with slightly lower return (dealer's spread). High LTV monetization feasible.

**3. Equity Forward Contract:** Commit to sell shares at predetermined forward price on future date. If market > forward → investor receives only forward price. Risk-free combined position → money market return factored into forward price. High LTV achievable.

**4. Synthetic Equity Forward:** Use options to replicate forward: buy put + sell call at same strike and expiry on same number of shares. Combined with stock → locks in sale price = strike. Risk-free position → money market return. Identical economics to equity forward.

**All four strategies achieve:** Risk-free position via direct/synthetic short, money market return on full position value, high LTV borrowing feasible, borrowing cost offset by hedged income.

**5. Tax-Free Exchange (Exchange Fund):** Contribute low-basis concentrated stock to partnership pool of diversified low-basis stocks. Non-taxable event. Tax basis unchanged. Minimum holding period (7 years in US). Distributions in diversified shares (not own stock). At least 20% must be "qualified assets" (typically RE). Fund manager has discretion over share acceptance and composition. Higher final liquidation value than sell-and-diversify (tax deferral benefit) BUT management fees offset some advantage.

### 34.8 Choosing Best Hedging Strategy

**Key Factor:** Tax attributes of shares determine optimal strategy. Employee stock options/restricted stock taxed differently (often as ordinary income) vs. long-held investment shares (LT CG rate). The tax character of the instrument being hedged influences which hedging tool is optimal and how it should be structured/documented.

### 34.9 Lopes Case Study — Public vs. Private Concentrated Positions

**T-Moto (public, post-IPO):** Broader hedging options (short against box, protective put, zero-cost collar, covered call, synthetic forward, exchange fund, TRS). BUT: 1-year lockup restriction. Liquid market → OTC dealers can price and hedge efficiently. Tax treatment: shares have zero cost basis → full CG on any sale. If shares were executive compensation → may be taxed as ordinary income (not CG).

**MotoElétrica (private):** Fewer options — no liquid derivatives, no exchange fund, no synthetic forward. Must find strategic/financial buyer or use MBO/leveraged recap/ESOP. Monetization via loans possible (puttable or non-recourse) but lower LTV. No market price → OTC dealers cannot price hedging instruments competitively.

**Decision Rule:** Public shares → wider array of hedging AND monetization tools available. Private shares → limited to sale strategies, lending strategies, or business restructuring. The ability to observe market price is CRUCIAL for derivative pricing and dealer willingness to hedge.

### 34.10 Athletes and Actors — Special Considerations

**Key Characteristics:** Short, high-income earning windows (athletes: avg 8-11 years, retire age 31-35; actors: window 5-7 years for women, longer for men). Extended retirement phase relative to earning phase.

**Recommended Savings Rate:** 50-75% of annual income (vs. 10-15% for traditional professionals). Dual purpose: (1) emergency cushion for career-ending injuries/disruptions, (2) long-term retirement funding.

**Challenges:** Sudden wealth → extravagant lifestyle → rapid depletion post-career. Financial illiteracy common. Susceptible to exploitation by associates seeking loans/investments. Monte Carlo simulations needed for discontinuous income streams.

### 34.11 Practice Problem Decision Rules (Modules 5-6)

**Gonzalez Case (Q1-4):**
- Q1: Born in US to Spanish parents → citizenship path = C (naturalization, not birth — born in US not Spain)
- Q2: Dual citizenship advantage = A (avoidance of double taxation via credit method treaty)
- Q3: US exit tax = deemed disposition of ALL assets. Tax = (USD3M − USD1M) × 10% + (USD1.5M − USD750K) × 10% = USD200K + USD75K = USD275K → closest to B (USD400K — need to verify exact calculation with home inclusion)
- Q4: Social Security effective tax rate: US taxes at 24%, Spain taxes at 35%, credit for US tax → effective = 35% (higher rate applies under credit method). Answer: B.

**Montalban Case (Q5-8):**
- Q5: Community property regime, no prenuptial → Ivette entitled to half of assets acquired DURING marriage. Art collection = Fernando's pre-marriage inheritance → NOT community property. Vacation property = inherited during marriage → depends on jurisdiction (typically separate property). Answer: B (vacation property only, inherited during marriage from parents).
- Q6: First USD20K gift tax-free. RV_gift = [1 + r(1-t)]^n / [(1 + r(1-t))^n × (1-t_e) + t_e] for tax-free portion. For tax-free USD20K: RV = 1 / (1-0.25) = 1.33. Answer: B.
- Q7: Additional USD100K taxed at 20% gift tax → donor gives USD120K for recipient to net USD100K. RV changes because gift tax reduces amount transferred. Answer closer to A (1.07).
- Q8: If law changes to tax gifts at donor (not recipient) → gift tax paid from donor's estate → reduces amount available → REDUCES relative value. Answer: B.

**Pineda Case (Q9-11):**
- Q9: If one dies → surviving spouse inherits. Spousal bequests NOT taxed. Optimal = inherit enough to use estate exclusion. Each has USD10M exclusion. Answer: B (USD15 million — covers spending + surplus, uses exclusion efficiently).
- Q10: Gift to Michael: lower tax bracket (10% vs 20%). RV_gift vs bequest calculation with differential tax rates. Answer: B (1.55).
- Q11: Sara's gift must: (1) be placed in trust to prevent husband accessing in divorce, (2) Sara lives abroad → consider jurisdiction/domicile for estate planning.

**Given Case (Q12-15):**
- Q12: Primary capital = home + cash + tax-deferred portfolio = USD1.5M + USD1M + USD2M = USD4.5M. Needs = mortgage + spending = USD0.5M + USD6M = USD6.5M. Shortfall = USD2M. Answer: C.
- Q13: Given wants independence/keep company → constraint AGAINST sale. Control = constraint. Answer: A.
- Q14: Company repurchases 10% of shares + starts dividends → partial monetization while retaining control. This = recapitalization. Answer: B.
- Q15: ESOP tax savings: selling USD1M/year via ESOP avoids CG tax that immediate full sale would trigger. At 15% rate on gains above basis: need to calculate incremental savings from spreading over time. Answer: A (USD1.25M).

**Restrepo Case (Q16-19):**
- Q16: Short trading history → dealers reluctant to hedge via exchange (need established history). OTC dealer (bank) more willing. Answer: C (trading history supports dealer over exchange).
- Q17: Short against the box triggers taxable event → fails TAX objective. Answer: A.
- Q18: Protective put locks in gains (minimum sale price = strike). Answer: A (gains).
- Q19: Increase upside without increasing downside → wider collar (lower put strike funds higher call strike, but this INCREASES downside). Put spread replaces standard put with spread → funds higher call strike. BUT question says "without increasing downside" → debit collar (pay for part of put, get higher call strike, same put protection). Answer: C (wider collar increases upside cap by lowering put strike → increases downside).

**CORRECTED from Solutions:**
- Q1: A (birth — born to Spanish parents → jus sanguinis citizenship by birth, NOT naturalization)
- Q2: C (access to healthcare systems of both countries — NOT avoidance of double taxation; dual citizenship actually increases administrative burden and double taxation risk)
- Q3: C (USD550,000 — deemed disposition of ALL US assets: unrealized CG on investments = (USD3M − USD1M) × 20% = USD400K; unrealized CG on home = (USD1.5M − USD750K) × 20% = USD150K; total = USD550K)
- Q4: B (35% — credit method: Spain taxes at 35%, credit for US 24% → effective = Spanish rate of 35%)
- Q5: B (vacation property only — pre-marital inheritances are separate property even in community property regimes)
- Q6: B (1.33 — RV = 1/(1 − T_e) = 1/(1 − 0.25) = 1.333 when pretax returns and tax rates are same for donor and donee)
- Q7: A (1.07 — taxable gift RV = (1 − T_g)/(1 − T_e) = (1 − 0.20)/(1 − 0.25) = 0.80/0.75 = 1.067)
- Q8: C (increases value — when donor pays gift tax, it reduces estate → acts as partial gift tax credit = T_g × T_e, effectively making gifts MORE valuable relative to bequests)
- Q9: B (USD15M — use first spouse's exclusion to transfer USD10M to children tax-free, leave USD15M to surviving spouse tax-free via spousal exemption)
- Q10: C (1.70 — RV formula with differential tax rates: [1 + 0.08(1−0.1)]^20 × [1 − 0.2 + 0.2×0.4] / {[1 + 0.08(1−0.2)]^20 × (1−0.4)} = 1.704)
- Q11: Two considerations: (A) foreign jurisdiction tax treatment of gift and returns may differ from Michael's jurisdiction → could change relative value; (B) whether foreign jurisdiction is separate or community property regime → affects husband's access in divorce → may need postnuptial agreement or trust
- Q12: C (primary capital = house + cash/bonds + tax-deferred = USD1.5M + USD1M + USD2M = USD4.5M; needs = mortgage + spending = USD0.5M + USD6M = USD6.5M; shortfall = USD2M)
- Q13: B (liquidity — sale provides immediate liquidity; control and time horizon argue AGAINST sale)
- Q14: B (recapitalization — company repurchases shares + starts paying dividends = issuing/selling shares and increasing dividends)
- Q15: A (USD1.25M — ESOP sells USD1M/year; first USD1M taxed at 15%, remainder at 20%. Immediate sale: USD25M gain × 5% additional rate = USD1.25M saved by phased ESOP approach)
- Q16: A (liquidity — stock is liquid → dealers can adjust hedge by borrowing/shorting shares easily. Short trading history is actually a DISADVANTAGE for exchange-traded derivatives, making dealer/OTC MORE likely)
- Q17: A (taxes — short against the box triggers taxable event, failing tax objective)
- Q18: A (gains — protective put establishes minimum selling price, locking in gains)
- Q19: B (debit collar — pay part of put premium out of pocket → can sell higher-strike call → more upside without additional downside. Wider collar and put spread BOTH increase downside risk)
