## Section 35: Transferring the Wealth — Estate Planning & Gifting (PW V1 Module 7)

### 35.1 Estate Planning Overview

**Core Challenge:** Sustainable spending rate = 3.5-6% of initial portfolio value (inflation-adjusted). At 4.3% spending, 6.5% growth, 10% SD, 2.5% inflation → 99% probability of portfolio exhaustion at 30-year horizon. Family members grow exponentially (doubling per generation) while portfolio may not keep pace.

**Estate Planning Goals:** Maximize benefits to beneficiaries, minimize transfer taxes, minimize family conflict. Starting point: determine spending needs of first generation → surplus determines giftable amount.

### 35.2 Transfer Tax Framework

**Two primary forms:** Gift tax (lifetime transfers) and estate/inheritance tax (transfers at death).
- **Gift tax:** May be on donor (US, UK) or donee (Germany, Netherlands, Japan, India, South Korea)
- **Estate tax:** Levied on estate of deceased (US, UK). **Inheritance tax:** Levied on recipients (many European/Asian countries)
- Purpose of gift tax: prevent circumventing estate/death taxes via lifetime gifting
- Progressive rates common; statutory allowances/deductions may apply
- Spousal transfers often exempt; rates may depend on relationship

**Gift & Estate Tax Rates by Jurisdiction:**
- Japan: up to 55% (spousal exemption + JPY10M per heir)
- Belgium: up to 40% (lower for spouses/children)
- Netherlands: up to 20% (partner exemption EUR723K)
- UK: up to 40% (unlimited spousal exemption; tax paid by DONOR)
- South Korea: up to 50% (spousal exemption + KRW50M per child of deceased)
- US: up to 40% (first USD12.92M exempt; unlimited spousal exemption; tax paid by DONOR)

**Tax-Free Annual Allowances:** South Africa ZAR100K/year, UK GBP3K/year, Germany EUR400K per child per 10 years, US USD17K per donee per year (USD34K from couple).

### 35.3 Annual Gifting Program Value

**Example:** USD17K/year × 30 years at 6% nominal, 25% CG tax, 2.5% inflation:
- Real after-tax return = 6% × (1 − 0.25) − 2.5% = 2.0%
- FV of annuity due = USD17K × (1.02) × [(1.02^30 − 1)/0.02] = **USD703,450** real after-tax
- If tax-EFFICIENT strategy (defer CG to end): real pre-tax return = 3.5%, FV of annuity = USD908,301. CG = USD398,301, after-tax CG = USD298,726. Total = USD510K + USD298K = **USD808,726**
- Tax efficiency adds ~USD105K (15% improvement)

**Decision Rule:** Start gifting programs EARLY. Even small annual tax-free gifts compound to significant wealth transfers over decades. Tax-efficient investment strategies (deferring CG) enhance transfer value by ~15%.

### 35.4 Valuation Discounts

**Illiquidity Discount:** 20-25% for privately held company shares (inversely related to company size and profit margin). Reflects inability to sell quickly at fair value.

**Lack of Control Discount:** 25-40% for minority interests. Reflects inability to influence management, dividends, or liquidation decisions.

**Combined Discount = MULTIPLICATIVE (not additive):**
Combined = 1 − [(1 − illiquidity discount) × (1 − control discount)]
- Example: 20% illiquidity + 35% lack of control → 1 − (0.80 × 0.65) = 1 − 0.48 = **48% combined** (not 55%)

**Rivera Case Study:** Herbert's 10% of 50% stake in USD75M company:
- Fair market value = USD75M × 0.5 × 0.1 = USD3.75M
- With 25% illiquidity discount: USD3.75M × 0.75 = USD2,812,500
- With 25% illiquidity + 40% control discount: USD3.75M × (1 − 0.55) = USD1,687,500

**Family Limited Partnership (FLP):** Pool family assets into partnership → gift minority LP interests to children. LP interests qualify for both illiquidity AND lack of control discounts even for liquid assets (because LP structure restricts access). FLP with cash/securities gets LESS discount than operating company. Tax authorities scrutinize heavily. Additional benefit: pooled assets access institutional minimums (PE, HF, VC).

**Public securities gift valuation:** Always at CURRENT FAIR MARKET VALUE (not cost basis). No lack of control discount applies to publicly traded securities (market provides liquidity). Laurie case: USD10M stock bought at USD100K → gift value = USD10M, NOT USD100K. But if gifted via FLP → FLP interests qualify for discounts.

### 35.5 Trusts

**Revocable vs. Irrevocable:**

| Feature | Revocable | Irrevocable |
|---------|-----------|-------------|
| Can be rescinded | Yes | No |
| Tax ownership | Settlor/grantor | Trustee |
| Creditor protection | No (assets reachable) | Yes (not owned by settlor) |

**Fixed vs. Discretionary:**

| Feature | Fixed | Discretionary |
|---------|-------|---------------|
| Distributions | Amount/timing specified | Trustee determines |
| Beneficiary legal right | Yes | No |
| Creditor access to trust | Yes | No (stronger protection) |

**Why Use a Trust:** (1) Control — make resources available without giving full control (minors, disabled, spendthrifts), (2) Asset protection — irrevocable trust protects from settlor's creditors and beneficiary's creditors (discretionary), avoids probate, protects from community property dilution, (3) Tax management — shift income to lower-tax beneficiaries, create trust in low-tax jurisdiction.

### 35.6 Trust Duration & Generation Skipping

**Rule Against Perpetuities:** Common law rule requiring trusts to terminate within 21 years after death of last living person alive when trust was created. Many US states have abolished this → **dynasty trusts** can last in perpetuity, sheltering wealth from transfer taxes across multiple generations.

**Generation Skipping:** Transfer excess capital directly to grandchildren (skip children's generation) → taxed ONCE instead of twice. Relative value of skipping = **1/(1 − T1)** where T1 = transfer tax rate.
- Kawaguchi case: JPY200M, 50% tax rate, 5% real return, 35 years
  - Via children then grandchildren (taxed twice): JPY200M × (1.05^10)(1−0.50)(1.05^25)(1−0.50) = **JPY275.8M**
  - Direct to grandchildren (taxed once): JPY200M × (1.05^35)(1−0.50) = **JPY551.6M** — exactly DOUBLE

**US Generation-Skipping Transfer Tax (GST):** Additional tax on transfers to grandchildren or younger, designed to replicate tax effect of passing through each generation. USD12.96M exemption (2023) can be sheltered in dynasty trust. Non-US persons NOT subject to GST.

### 35.7 Gifts to Trusts & Trust Taxation

**Inter Vivos Trust:** Irrevocable trust created and funded during grantor's lifetime → gift considered complete for transfer tax purposes.
**Testamentary Trust:** Created under will/estate plan of deceased individual.

**Intentionally Defective Grantor Trust (IDGT):** Irrevocable trust where grantor continues to pay income tax on trust assets. Benefit: removes three amounts from taxable estate — (1) original corpus, (2) future investment returns, (3) taxes paid by grantor on those returns. Trust assets appreciate free of income tax erosion.

**Offshore Trust:** Established in foreign jurisdiction (Caribbean, Channel Islands) for: tax advantages, confidentiality, asset protection, access to investments not available domestically. Must comply with BOTH home country and trust jurisdiction regulations. US has stringent reporting requirements with severe penalties for non-compliance.

### 35.8 Non-Trust Jurisdictions

**Civil law countries** generally don't recognize trusts. Alternatives:
- **Stiftung (foundation):** Institution for personal, non-commercial purposes (Liechtenstein). Similar to trust but may not have certainty of treatment in common law jurisdictions.
- **Anstalt:** Hybrid between company and foundation (Liechtenstein).
- **Hague Trust Convention:** 14 countries recognize validity of trusts regardless of whether their domestic law includes trust concept → enables civil law country residents to create trusts in common law jurisdictions.
- **Forced inheritance rules:** Many civil law jurisdictions require portions of estate go to specific family members → trusts in other jurisdictions sometimes used to circumvent these rules.
- Founders of civil law structures often convert to common law trusts for beneficiaries in common law jurisdictions (tax certainty).

### 35.9 Investment of Trusts — Prudent Investor Rule

Trustees must invest according to the **prudent investor rule** (codification of modern portfolio theory). Requires considering: general economic conditions, inflation/deflation, tax consequences, role of each investment within overall trust portfolio, expected total return, other resources of beneficiaries, liquidity needs, special relationship of asset to trust/beneficiary. Trustees are legally accountable to beneficiaries and should document decisions.

### 35.10 Harper Family Case Study — Estate Planning Objectives

**Profile:** John Harper (56), founder/CEO of USD300M supermarket chain. Wife Breda (54). Three children: James (35, helping in business, wants own venture), Nick (30, artist, no financial knowledge), Ann (27, pharmacist, daughter needs medical care).

**Seven Estate Planning Objectives Identified:**
1. **Income/liquidity** — support lifestyle, medical costs (Ann's daughter), Nick's living expenses, tax obligations
2. **Business succession** — who runs company after John retires? Family member, external manager, or sell?
3. **Control over assets** — John uncomfortable with Nick managing money → need discretionary trust with institutional trustee
4. **Tax-aware transfer** — use allowances, spousal exemptions, generation-skipping strategies
5. **Asset protection** — James taking loans → protect from creditors via irrevocable trust; discretionary trust protects from beneficiaries' creditors
6. **Family wealth preservation** — family governance to align different goals across generations
7. **Charitable goals** — John wants active involvement + family participation → private foundation (not direct gifts)

**Key Decision:** Discretionary trust best for Nick (trustee controls distributions, not beneficiary). Institutional trustee (trust company) avoids family conflicts as successor trustee.

### 35.11 Bequests and Inheritances

**Key Definitions:**
- **Bequest:** Directive in a will for transferring property to a person upon death
- **Testator:** Person making the will
- **Inheritance:** Assets received by beneficiary after someone's death
- **Intestacy:** Dying without a valid will → courts decide distribution according to domicile law

**Legal Frameworks for Testamentary Plans:**
- **Common law (US, UK):** Generally allows testamentary freedom — testator can dispose of property by will as they see fit
- **Civil law (EU, Japan, Brazil, Switzerland):** Forced heirship rules — legal obligation to leave portion of estate to certain family members (children, spouse). Children have RIGHT to fixed share regardless of will. Forced heirship supersedes will. Circumvention: move assets to offshore trust in common law jurisdiction.
- **Shari'a law:** Similar to civil law in restricting testamentary freedom; inheritance guided by Islamic principles.

**Forced Heirship — Berelli Case Study:**
Philippe: EUR800K estate, EUR200K separate property. Community property regime + forced heirship.
- Helena (spouse) entitled to GREATER of: community property share (50% of EUR600K = EUR300K) or forced heirship share (1/3 of EUR800K = EUR266.7K) → Helena receives EUR300K
- Children collectively entitled to 1/3 of EUR800K = EUR266.7K (EUR133.3K each)
- Philippe's freely disposable amount = EUR800K − EUR300K − EUR266.7K = EUR233.3K → cannot bequeath EUR300K to mother

### 35.12 Community vs. Separate Property & Inheritances

**Inheritance Classification Matrix:**

| Timing | Community Property Regime | Separate Property Regime |
|--------|------------------------|----------------------|
| Before marriage | Separate property | Separate property |
| During marriage | Community (especially if commingled) | Separate (if not commingled, no value increase, no transmutation) |

**Factors that transform separate → community property:**
1. **Commingling** — mixing inherited funds with joint accounts/expenses
2. **Increase in value** — if inheritance grows and non-owning spouse contributed to growth
3. **Transmutation** — non-owning spouse's efforts significantly contribute to asset growth/improvement
4. **Legal variation** — jurisdiction-specific exceptions

**Decision Rule:** To protect inheritance as separate property → keep in separate account, never commingle with marital funds, never use for joint expenses. California example: inheritance during marriage stays separate IF not commingled with community property funds.

### 35.13 Estate Planning Components

**Estate composition:** Financial assets (bank accounts, securities, business interests), tangible personal assets (art, vehicles, collectibles), immovable property (real estate, timber rights), intellectual property (royalties).

**Probate:** Legal process to confirm validity of will. Problems: sizable court/attorney fees, delays in asset transfer, assets frozen during proceedings, will can be challenged, public record (privacy concern), multi-jurisdiction complications.

**Probate Avoidance Tools:** Joint ownership (JTWROS — automatic transfer to surviving owner), trusts (assets pass per trust terms), life insurance (proceeds to named beneficiary), retirement plans (named beneficiary).

**JTWROS Features:** Automatic transfer (bypasses probate), equal shares, undivided interest in entire property, can be terminated if one owner sells/transfers their interest.

### 35.14 Estate & Succession Planning

**Testamentary Gratuitous Transfer:** Transfer at death (bequest from testator's perspective, inheritance from donee's). Tax treatment mirrors lifetime gifts — depends on jurisdiction, relationship, residency, asset type/location.

**Deemed Dispositions:** Some countries (Australia, Canada, Colombia) treat death as deemed sale of all assets → trigger unrealized CG tax. Tax is on GAINS only (not principal value). Contrast with estate tax on total value.

**Estate Tax Exemptions & Structuring:**
- **Lifetime exemption** (US: USD12.92M, UK: GBP325K threshold)
- **Marital deduction/exemption** (US: unlimited for citizen spouses)
- **Portability** (US: surviving spouse can use deceased spouse's unused exemption)

**Juarez Family Case Study — Trust-Based vs. Initial Plan:**
- Initial plan (all to Julia via marital deduction): No tax at Carlo's death. Julia's estate at death = USD83.4M. Tax = 40% × (USD83.4M − USD24.9M combined exemptions) = USD23.4M. After-tax = **USD59.99M**.
- Trust-based plan (exemption trust + marital deduction trust): Exemption trust = USD12.92M grows to USD15.4M (tax-free forever). Marital trust = USD32.08M grows to USD38.2M. Julia's own = USD29.8M. Julia's total taxable = USD55.98M. Tax = USD22.4M. After-tax = **USD60.98M**.
- **Tax savings: ~USD987K** — because exemption trust removes appreciation on USD12.92M from Julia's taxable estate.

**Decision Rule:** Use exemption amount at FIRST spouse's death (via trust or direct bequest to children) rather than relying solely on portability. In appreciating markets, earlier use of exemption removes future appreciation from taxable estate. BUT: if assets in exemption trust DEPRECIATE, result is worse (depreciation + estate tax deduction removed from estate).

### 35.15 Charitable Gratuitous Transfers

**Tax Advantages of Charitable Giving:**
1. Charitable donations exempt from gift/estate tax
2. Income tax deductions for donations
3. Charity pays no tax on investment returns → tax-free compounding

**Relative Value of Charitable Gift vs. Taxable Bequest:**
RV = [(1 + r_g)^n + T_oi × [1 + r_e(1 − t_ie)]^n × (1 − T_e)] / {[1 + r_e(1 − t_ie)]^n × (1 − T_e)}

Where: r_g = pretax return to charity, r_e = pretax return if kept in estate, t_ie = effective tax on investment returns in estate, T_oi = income tax rate (deduction benefit), T_e = estate tax rate, n = years until death.

**Zachary Case Study:** 60% estate tax, 48% income tax rate, 6% real return, 20 years.
RV = [(1.06)^20 + 0.48 × (1 + 0.06(1−0.48))^20 × (1−0.60)] / [(1 + 0.06(1−0.48))^20 × (1−0.60)] = **4.82**
Charitable gift worth almost 5× a taxable bequest because: (a) escapes estate tax, (b) tax-free compounding inside charity, (c) income tax deduction for donor.

### 35.16 Wealth Transfer Strategy & Conflict Minimization

**Combining Lifetime Gifts + Testamentary Transfers:**
1. Determine descendants' needs → gift amounts in trust during lifetime
2. Use marital deductions, lifetime exemptions, trusts, valuation discounts, FLPs
3. Retain sufficient assets for own lifetime needs
4. At death: fund children's trusts → spouse receives marital deduction trust → remaining to charity

**GRAT (Grantor Retained Annuity Trust):** Grantor gifts assets to irrevocable trust, retains annuity payments. If annuity = fair market value of gift → taxable gift value = ZERO. If trust assets appreciate faster than IRS assumed rate → excess appreciation passes to children TAX-FREE.

**Minimizing Conflict:** Transparent communication with all family members about estate plan, no contest clauses (disinherit anyone who challenges will — check local enforceability), clear treatment of blended family issues (stepchildren, surviving stepparent as trustee with remainder beneficiaries being children from prior marriage creates conflict → use independent trustee).

**Blended Family Conflict:** Surviving stepparent as income beneficiary vs. children as remainder beneficiaries → conflicting investment objectives (stepparent wants income/conservative, children want growth/aggressive). Solution: use independent institutional trustee, separate trusts for different beneficiary classes.

### 35.17 Juarez Family Case Study — Exemption Trust vs. Marital Deduction

**Setup:** Carlo (USD45M) and Julia (USD25M) Juarez, US citizens in Florida. Two children. Carlo dies first. Estate tax exemption = USD12,920,000. 40% estate tax rate. Assets appreciate 6%/year. Julia dies 3 years later when exemption reduced to USD12M.

**Plan A — Leave Everything to Julia (Marital Deduction Only):**
- Carlo's death: No estate tax (unlimited marital deduction)
- Julia's death: Combined estate = USD83,371,120 (= USD70M × 1.06^3). Julia's total exemption with portability = USD24,920,000 (own USD12M + Carlo's ported USD12,920,000)
- Estate tax = 40% × (USD83,371,120 − USD24,920,000) = **USD23,380,448**. After-tax estate = **USD59,990,672**

**Plan B — Two-Trust Strategy (Exemption Trust + Marital Deduction Trust):**
- At Carlo's death: Exemption Trust = USD12,920,000 (excluded from estate tax). Marital Deduction Trust = USD32,080,000
- 3 years later: Exemption Trust = USD15,387,927 (tax-free). Marital Trust = USD38,207,793. Julia's own = USD29,775,400
- Julia adds USD12M to exemption trust → total = USD27,387,927. Taxable = USD55,983,193. Tax = **USD22,393,277**. After-tax = **USD60,977,843**

**Tax Savings = USD987,171** — removing exemption at Carlo's death removes USD2,467,927 of future appreciation from Julia's taxable estate (USD2,467,927 × 40% = USD987,171).

**Decision Rule:** USE exemption trust at first spouse's death. In appreciating markets, earlier exemption use removes future appreciation from taxable estate. If trust assets depreciate → result is worse. Can achieve same result via direct bequest to children/foundation, but trust provides control + creditor protection.

### 35.18 Deemed Dispositions

Countries using deemed disposition regime (Australia, Canada, Colombia): treat bequests as if property was sold → tax is on **unrealized capital gains only**, not total estate value. Cost basis matters enormously.

## Section 36: Charities & Philanthropy (PW V1 Module 7 Section 4)

### 36.1 Philanthropy Overview

**The Giving Pledge:** 241 signatories from 29 countries (as of 2023), committing majority of wealth to charity during lifetime or at death. Started 2010 by Warren Buffett, Bill & Melinda Gates.

**Discovery Process:** Adviser helps client crystallize charitable mission through: (1) motivations for giving, (2) desired outcomes, (3) immediate vs long-term goals, (4) risk appetite for philanthropic ventures, (5) legacy intentions. Create overarching mission statement + specific focus statements per issue area.

**Mission-Related Investing:** Some clients direct investment assets toward projects promoting societal/environmental change, aligning portfolio with philanthropic mission.

### 36.2 Charitable Giving Vehicles

**Outright Gifts to Charity:**
- Cash gifts → income tax deduction
- Appreciated securities → deduction at FULL fair market value, avoid realizing CG, serves portfolio management function (tax-efficient rebalancing)
- **Qualified Charitable Distribution (QCD):** US: up to USD100K from retirement plan RMD directly to charity → escapes income tax entirely

**Donor-Advised Fund (DAF):**
- Administered by sponsoring organization (community foundation, financial institution's charitable arm)
- Donor contributes → immediate tax deduction → advises on grants to charities over time
- Sponsoring organization retains legal control (but typically follows donor's recommendations)
- Successor advisors possible (children inherit advisory role)
- No annual distribution requirement → strategic timing flexibility
- Lower cost and administrative burden than private foundation
- Good option when donation size doesn't justify full private foundation

**Klein DAF Case Study:** USD60M business sale, USD40M CG taxed at 20% = USD8M tax. USD5M DAF contribution → income tax deduction reduces CG tax from USD8M to USD7M (saves USD1M). DAF assets grow tax-free outside estate. Klein advises grants to university, museum, hospital over many years.

**Private Grant-Making Foundation:**
- Independent legal entity for charitable purposes. Board of directors (3+ including family/associates). Donor receives tax deduction for gift TO foundation (not for subsequent grants FROM foundation).
- US: must distribute at least 5% of assets annually for charitable activities or pay penalty
- Can exist in perpetuity (or limited-life by choice)
- Public disclosure requirements (annual tax filing of grants, investments, expenses)
- Full control over investment decisions and grantmaking within legal boundaries
- Family involvement: founding donors and families play active management/governance roles

**Private Operating Foundation:** Operates charitable programs directly (e.g., runs a museum) rather than making grants to other organizations.

**Limited Liability Company (LLC) for Charity:**
- No annual distribution requirement, no public filing requirement
- Donor does NOT get tax deduction for funding LLC — only when LLC makes charitable contribution
- Full operational control + flexibility (can pursue for-profit AND charitable activities)
- Maximum privacy (no public disclosure)
- Example: Chan Zuckerberg Initiative (Meta founder, 2015)

### 36.3 DAF vs. Private Foundation vs. LLC Comparison

| Dimension | DAF | Private Foundation | LLC |
|-----------|-----|-------------------|-----|
| Tax deduction | Immediate on contribution | On contribution to foundation | Only when LLC gives to charity |
| Distribution requirement | None | 5% of assets annually | None |
| Grantmaking control | Advisory (not legal) | High (board controls) | Full (owners decide) |
| Investment decisions | Within sponsor's options | Complete discretion | Full control |
| Involvement/control | Advisory + succession | Active family governance | Direct management |
| Flexibility | Broad (various charities) | Can operate programs + grant | Maximum (charitable + for-profit) |
| Legacy | Multi-generational advisory | Enduring family vehicle | Supports philanthropy but not inherently designed for legacy |
| Privacy | Anonymous giving possible | Public disclosure required | Significant privacy |
| Administrative cost | Low | High | Medium to High |

### 36.4 Planned Giving — Charitable Remainder Trust (CRT)

**Structure:** Irrevocable trust → donor/beneficiaries receive income stream (≥5% of assets annually) for lifetime or up to 20 years → remainder to charity.
- **CRAT (Charitable Remainder Annuity Trust):** Fixed annual payment amount
- **CRUT (Charitable Remainder Unitrust):** Variable annual payment (fixed % of trust value, recalculated annually)

**Tax Benefits:**
- Charitable income tax deduction in year established (based on actuarial value of remainder — must be ≥10% of initial FMV)
- Trust can sell appreciated assets with NO immediate CG tax (tax-exempt trust)
- Distributions to beneficiaries carry CG tax consequences (spreading the gain over multiple years)
- Assets removed from estate → estate tax savings

**Cons:** Irrevocable (cannot reclaim assets), inflation risk (fixed annuity), investment risk, administrative costs.

**Klein CRT Case Study:** Transfers USD60M business to CRT before sale. Business sold inside CRT → USD40M CG NOT taxed (CRT is tax-exempt). 5% lifetime annuity = USD3M/year to Klein. Income tax deduction = USD23.4M. Annual distributions taxable (include CG component). At death → remainder to children's hospital. Can pair with irrevocable life insurance trust (ILIT) to replace wealth for children.

### 36.5 Planned Giving — Charitable Lead Trust (CLT)

**Structure:** REVERSES CRT. Donor contributes to irrevocable trust → charity receives income stream for set term → remainder passes to donor's heirs TAX-FREE (if structured correctly).
- **CLAT (Charitable Lead Annuity Trust):** Fixed payment to charity
- **CLUT (Charitable Lead Unitrust):** Variable payment to charity

**Zeroed-Out CLAT:** Annuity payments sized so actuarial value of remainder = zero → no gift/estate tax on transfer. IF trust assets appreciate faster than IRS assumed rate → excess appreciation passes to heirs TAX-FREE.

**Clemente Case Study:** USD25M company contributed to 3-year zeroed-out CLAT. Annual payment to charity = USD9.249M × 3 = USD27.747M. If company goes public at USD75M → remainder = USD75M − USD27.747M = USD47.253M passes to family trust TAX-FREE.

**CLT Tax Benefits:** (1) Estate tax only on actuarial value of remainder to heirs, (2) charitable income tax deductions for CLT's annual distributions (non-grantor CLT), (3) effective generational wealth transfer tool.

**Cons:** Complex/expensive to establish, donor's assets tied up for trust term, if investments underperform → heirs receive less than expected, non-grantor CLT provides no income tax deduction to DONOR.

### 36.6 Cross-Border Philanthropy

**Key Challenges:** Risk of money laundering/terrorist financing → US PATRIOT Act compliance required. Accountability complex across multiple entities and jurisdictions. Cross-cultural competency essential (avoiding unintended harm).

**US Cross-Border Grantmaking Rules:**
1. **Equivalency determination:** Foreign charity must be equivalent to US public charity
2. **Expenditure responsibility:** Written grant agreement specifying uses + reporting mechanism + filing with IRS

### 36.7 Charitable Giving Vehicles by Jurisdiction

**Common Law Jurisdictions (UK, Hong Kong, Singapore):** Charitable trusts (similar to US), foundations. Tax exemptions on income/CG, donor deductions.

**Civil Law Jurisdictions:**
- **Stiftung (Germany, Switzerland, Austria, Liechtenstein):** Foundation for charitable/scientific/cultural/educational purposes. Tax-exempt. Designed for longevity. Cons: inflexible (hard to change purpose), regulatory scrutiny, requires significant initial capital.
- **Fondation (France, Belgium, Luxembourg):** Independent legal entity for philanthropy. No shareholders/owners. Pros: tax deductions, public recognition, founder can set objectives. Cons: expensive, inflexible, government oversight.
- **Fideicomiso (Latin America):** Trust-like entity. Settlor transfers assets to fiduciary for third-party benefit. Assets removed from taxable estate, income can be tax-exempt. Cons: less widely recognized outside Latin America, legal complexity.
- **Charitable Incorporated Organization (Japan):** Government-approved entities for non-profit purposes. Tax-exempt, donations deductible. Pros: government support, wide range of activities. Cons: government approval required, bureaucratic oversight.

**Cross-Jurisdiction Themes:** More regulated → more structured but less flexible. Tax incentives vary widely. Flexibility vs control trade-off. Cultural norms influence preferred vehicle. Advisers must navigate complexities across jurisdictions.

### 36.8 Module 7 Practice Problem Decision Rules

**McDaniel Case (Q1-4):**
- Q1: B (USD4.4M) — Maximum gift per heir = USD36K/year (USD18K from each parent). 5 heirs × USD36K = USD180K/year. Real after-tax return = 7% × (1−0.1) − 2.5% = 3.8%. FV annuity due over 17 years = USD180K × 1.038 × [(1.038^17 − 1)/0.038] = USD4,352,337. Answer B closest.
- Q2: Generation-skipping RV = 1/(1 − T) = 1/(1 − 0.40) = 1.67. Gifts directly to grandchildren are 67% more valuable than gifts to children that would be taxed again on transfer to grandchildren.
- Q3: C (irrevocable and discretionary) — Irrevocable protects from creditors. Discretionary allows trustee to control whether money is used appropriately (university, home) vs frivolous purchases. Fixed trust would let beneficiary spend on anything.
- Q4: FLP benefits: (1) USD24K of underlying assets gifted as FLP shares with 25% combined discount → qualifies for USD18K annual gift exclusion (USD24K × 0.75 = USD18K). (2) Provides heirs access to alternative investments (PE, HF, VC, timber) with high minimums they couldn't access individually.

**Claret Case (Q5-8):**
- Q5: B (transmutation) — Lauren inherited RE before/during marriage, retained as separate property. BUT Vincente helped manage and significantly increased value → transmutation converts separate → community property.
- Q6: Three strategies to avoid leaving business to Guillaume: (1) Move business to offshore trust in jurisdiction without forced heirship, (2) JTWROS with Marko and Elise on business shares, (3) Gift maximum to Marko and Elise during lifetime to reduce final estate subject to forced heirship.
- Q7: Two elements: (A) Plan gifting + testamentary transfers to give business to Marko/Elise while leaving fair inheritance to Guillaume, (B) Communicate plan clearly to all children + consider no-contest clauses given blended family tensions.
- Q8: C (non-grantor CLT) — Charities receive immediate steady income stream during trust term → meets environmental/wildlife goals. Children receive remainder at trust termination → meets wealth transfer goal. CRT (A) would give income to children FIRST, charity gets remainder (backwards). Exemption trust (B) protects from estate tax but doesn't benefit charities.

**Sousa Case (Q9-12):**
- Q9: LLC is most appropriate. Two reasons PF is less suitable: (i) 5% annual distribution requirement constrains timing flexibility, (ii) less privacy (public disclosure required). Two reasons DAF is less suitable: (i) only advisory control, not legal control over assets/distributions, (ii) ultimate legal control rests with sponsoring organization.
- Q10: B (charitable remainder unitrust/CRUT) — Variable payout based on % of trust value → meets income objective for Sousas and children. CLAT (A) would pay charity first, not family. Dynasty trust (C) is for multi-generational family wealth, not income generation + charitable giving.
- Q11: Cross-cultural competency requirement — intentional set of principles and ethics to guide work across different cultures. Without it, gifts like modern farming equipment may go to waste if local residents cannot operate, maintain, or repair it.
- Q12: C (both equivalency determination AND expenditure responsibility) — US cross-border grant to non-US charity requires BOTH: (1) equivalency determination (foreign charity equivalent to US public charity), (2) expenditure responsibility (written grant agreement + reporting + IRS filing).
