## Section 38: Equity Portfolio Construction & Management — Masterclass Deep Dive

### 38.1 ESG & Sustainable Investing Taxonomy — Four Approaches

**1. Screening (Negative/Exclusionary & Positive/Best-in-Class):**
- Negative screening: exclude entire sectors/companies (tobacco, weapons, fossil fuels, gambling) → simplest, most common, reduces universe
- Positive screening: include companies meeting specific ESG thresholds
- Best-in-class: select TOP ESG performers WITHIN each sector → maintains sector diversification (unlike negative screening which eliminates sectors)
- Key distinction: negative screening reduces investable universe; best-in-class keeps all sectors represented

**2. ESG Integration:**
- Systematically incorporate ESG data into financial analysis → NOT a separate screen, but built into valuation
- ESG adjustments to: revenue forecasts (regulatory risk, consumer preference), operating margins (environmental costs, labor practices), P/E multiples (governance premium/discount)
- Example: Company with poor environmental practices → analyst adjusts margins DOWN by estimated remediation/regulatory costs → lower fair value

**3. Thematic Investing:**
- Focus on ESG megatrends: clean energy transition, water scarcity, circular economy, aging demographics
- Concentrated exposure to specific sustainability themes → higher tracking error vs broad benchmarks
- Risk: theme may be "priced in" → high valuations for popular themes reduce forward returns

**4. Impact Investing:**
- Targeted social/environmental objectives alongside financial returns
- Measurable impact outcomes (e.g., UN Sustainable Development Goals — SDGs)
- Typically accepts below-market returns in exchange for measurable social/environmental impact
- Distinction from ESG integration: impact investing PRIORITIZES measurable impact outcomes; ESG integration uses ESG as RISK FACTOR for better financial returns

**ESG Approach Selection Decision Tree:**
IF client wants to avoid "sin stocks" → Negative screening
IF client wants best ESG within each sector → Best-in-class
IF client wants ESG factored into ALL valuations → ESG integration
IF client wants to invest in specific sustainability theme → Thematic
IF client wants measurable social/environmental impact → Impact investing
Links to: Section 13.1 (roles of equities — ESG alignment), Section 13.3 (active management — ESG mandates)

### 38.2 Chiu Segmentation Case Study

**Question:** South Korean aeronautics company with strong recent earnings growth → which segmentation approach is MOST appropriate?
**Answer: Size and Style segmentation**
- Reasoning: "strong earnings growth" = growth style classification; South Korean = specific country which maps to size/style box
- NOT geographic (question asks about the COMPANY characteristics, not country exposure)
- NOT economic activity (aeronautics is sector classification — useful for sector analysis but question emphasizes GROWTH characteristic)
- Exam trap: candidates may pick geographic because South Korea is mentioned → but the GROWTH attribute is the defining characteristic

### 38.3 Aiello Active-Passive Positioning Case Study

**Question:** Aiello believes markets are efficient, wants to minimize costs → but focuses on narrow country/sector exposure.
**Answer: Positioned toward the PASSIVE end of the spectrum, but with active elements**
- Market efficiency belief + cost minimization = strong passive indicators
- Narrow country/sector focus = requires SOME active decision-making (choosing which country/sector)
- Result: NOT pure passive (pure passive = broad market index), but firmly toward passive end
- Exam distinction: passive ≠ broad market only; you can be passive WITHIN a narrow segment (e.g., index tracking within a single country)

### 38.4 Index Concentration — HHI and Effective Number of Stocks

**Herfindahl-Hirschman Index (HHI):**
HHI = Σ(w_i²) where w_i = portfolio weight of stock i

**Effective Number of Stocks = 1 / HHI**

**Worked Example:**
- Index has 65 stocks, HHI = 0.02
- Effective number = 1 / 0.02 = **50 stocks**
- Interpretation: despite holding 65 stocks, concentration risk equivalent to a 50-stock equally weighted portfolio
- Gap (65 − 50 = 15) represents concentration effect from unequal weighting

**Exam Application:**
- Equally weighted N-stock portfolio: HHI = 1/N (minimum concentration)
- Market-cap weighted: HHI > 1/N (large-cap bias increases concentration)
- Higher HHI = MORE concentrated = FEWER effective stocks = MORE concentration risk
- S&P 500 effective number ≈ 50-80 stocks (varies with top-10 concentration) → in 2024 with Magnificent 7 dominance, effective number was historically low

**Buffering vs. Packeting (Index Investability):**
- Buffering: creates buffer zone around market-cap cutoffs (e.g., stock must drop below 1,050th rank to leave a 1,000-stock index, or rise above 950th to enter) → reduces unnecessary turnover from stocks bouncing in/out
- Packeting: gradual migration between indices → stock moves PARTIALLY (e.g., 50% weight) to new index first, then fully migrates at next reconstitution → smoother, more orderly transitions
- Both improve investability by reducing forced trading around index changes
- Exam trap: "buffering makes index less investable" = INCORRECT → buffering makes index MORE investable (reduces forced turnover)

### 38.5 Securities Lending — Voting Rights and Dividend Capture Details

**Securities Lending Key Risks for Lender:**
1. Short selling pressure: borrower likely shorting → downward price pressure on lender's position
2. Borrower credit risk: if borrower defaults, lender may not recover securities (mitigated by collateral)
3. Dividend compensation: lender receives "manufactured dividend" (cash payment in lieu) → may have DIFFERENT tax treatment than actual dividend
4. **Lost voting rights:** voting rights transfer to borrower during loan period → lender LOSES ability to vote on corporate actions
→ Some institutional lenders recall shares before important votes to retain voting control → creates tension between lending income and governance responsibility

**Dividend Capture Strategy:**
- Mechanism: buy stock before ex-dividend date → collect dividend → sell after ex-date
- Profitable IF: stock price decline on ex-date < dividend amount received
- Typically: stock price drops by approximately the dividend amount → strategy is roughly neutral before transaction costs
- After costs: usually unprofitable for individual investors (commissions + bid-ask spread eat dividend)
- Tax arbitrage: may work when dividend tax rate < capital gains tax rate for specific investor → regulatory/tax-regime dependent
Links to: Section 13.4 (equity income framework), Section 13.5 (shareholder engagement — voting)

### 38.6 Weighting Methods — Comparative Decision Framework

| Method | Weight By | Rebalancing Need | Key Bias | Best For |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Market-cap | Price × shares outstanding | Self-rebalancing (minimal) | Overweights overvalued, large-cap bias | Low-cost broad exposure |
| Free-float cap | Price × free-float shares | Self-rebalancing | Same as cap but more investable | Standard institutional benchmarks |
| Price-weighted | Share price | After splits | Arbitrary (high price ≠ large company) | Legacy indices (DJIA) |
| Equal-weight | 1/N | Frequent (quarterly+) | Small-cap bias, anti-momentum | Diversification, small-cap tilt |
| Fundamental | Revenue/dividends/book value | Periodic (annual) | Value tilt | Avoiding momentum/overvaluation bias |

**Exam Traps:**
- "Free-float adjustment reduces liquidity" = INCORRECT → free-float adjustment REFLECTS actual liquidity more accurately (excludes locked-up shares)
- "Equal-weight reduces concentration" = CORRECT → by definition, eliminates large-cap dominance
- "Fundamental weighting is value investing" = PARTIALLY CORRECT → has value tilt but is rules-based/passive, not active stock-picking
Links to: Section 13.6 (benchmark selection)

### 38.7 Walker FI Mandates Case Study — Fund Identification by Data

**Setup:** Diane Walker (pension consultant) evaluates 3 funds vs Bloomberg Barclays Global Aggregate Index (BBGAI). Must identify mandate: pure indexing, enhanced indexing, or active management.

**Key Data (Funds X, Y, Z vs BBGAI):**

| Metric | X | Y | Z | BBGAI |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Maturity (yrs) | 8.61 | 8.35 | 9.45 | 8.34 |
| Mod. duration | 6.37 | 6.35 | 7.37 | 6.34 |
| Yield (%) | 1.49 | 1.42 | 1.55 | 1.43 |
| Convexity | 0.65 | 0.60 | 0.72 | 0.60 |
| AAA (%) | 41.10 | 41.20 | 40.11 | 41.24 |
| BB (%) | 0.59 | 0.55 | 1.02 | 0.35 |

**Solution Logic:**
- **Fund Y = Pure Indexing:** closest duration match (6.35 vs 6.34), closest yield match, best matching quality cells, closest maturity/country distributions → replicating the benchmark
- **Fund Z = Active Management:** largest duration mismatch (7.37 vs 6.34), largest yield pickup (1.55 vs 1.43), largest maturity cell over/underweights, more BB exposure (1.02 vs 0.35) → taking active risk
- **Fund X = Enhanced Indexing:** once you identify the two extremes (Y=pure, Z=active), X is the middle value → small deviations from benchmark but not as large as Z

**Exam Decision Rule:** Compare each fund's metrics to benchmark → fund closest to benchmark on ALL dimensions = pure indexing; fund most different = active; remaining = enhanced indexing
Links to: Section 12.3 (FI mandate types)

### 38.8 FI Duration Types — Exam Decision Rules

**Which Duration to Use When:**
- **Macaulay duration:** immunize a single liability (weighted average time to receive cash flows, in years)
- **Modified duration:** BPV calculations, % price change estimates → % ΔValue = −ModDur × ΔYield
- **Effective duration:** bonds with embedded options (callable, puttable, MBS) → uncertain cash flows require model
- **Money duration (dollar duration):** monetary change in value → Money ΔValue = −ModDur × ΔYield × V_P
- **BPV (basis point value/DV01):** = ModDur × 0.0001 × V_P → price change per 1bp yield move
- **Key rate duration (partial duration):** sensitivity to nonparallel yield curve shifts → isolates impact of specific maturity point changing
- **Empirical duration:** regression-based from actual market data → captures real-world behavior. **KEY: Lower-quality HY bonds have NEGATIVE empirical duration** (HY bonds act like equity — when rates rise in recovery, spreads narrow more than rates rise → price goes UP)
- **Spread duration:** sensitivity to credit spread changes → % ΔValue = −D_S × ΔSpread
- **DTS (Duration Times Spread):** = EffSpreadDur × Spread → better risk measure for HY because spread changes are PROPORTIONAL

**Floating-Rate Bond Duration Rule:**
- Macaulay duration ≈ **half the reset period** (e.g., quarterly reset → Mac dur ≈ 0.125 years)
- Spread duration = tied to MATURITY (much higher than Macaulay duration)
- Key exam trap: floating-rate bonds have LOW interest rate risk (low Mac dur) but HIGH credit spread risk (high spread duration)

**Duration Computation Illustration (6% annual-pay, 3-year bond at par, YTM = 6%):**

| Year | CF | PV | Weight (w) | Years × w |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 6 | 5.6604 | 0.0566 | 0.0566 |
| 2 | 6 | 5.3400 | 0.0534 | 0.1068 |
| 3 | 106 | 88.9996 | 0.8900 | 2.6700 |
| **Total** | | **100.0000** | **1.0000** | **2.8334** |

- Macaulay duration = **2.8334 years** (sum of KRDs = Mac dur)
- Modified duration = 2.8334 / 1.06 = **2.6740**
- Duration contributions of each cash flow = key rate durations
Links to: Section 12.2 (duration framework), Section 24.1 (FI advanced)

### 38.9 FI Correlation & Economic Cycle Framework

**Investment Grade (IG) vs High Yield (HY) — Key Distinctions:**

| Characteristic | IG Bonds | HY Bonds |
|---|---|---|
| Primary risk driver | Interest rate changes | Credit spread changes |
| Correlation with rates | HIGH (price moves inversely with rates) | LOW (spreads dominate) |
| Correlation with equity | LOW | HIGH (acts like equity) |
| Empirical duration | Positive (behaves as expected) | Can be NEGATIVE |
| Economic sensitivity | Moderate | High |

**Economic Cycle Effects on FI:**

| Phase | Interest Rates | Credit Spreads | POD/LGD | Best FI Strategy |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Upturn/Recovery | Rising | Narrowing | Falling | Overweight HY, short duration |
| Expansion | Rising | Narrow (tight) | Low | Neutral, watch for tightening end |
| Downturn/Recession | Falling | Widening | Rising | Overweight IG/govt, extend duration |

**Active Duration Management Decision Rules:**
- Expect rates to FALL → INCREASE portfolio duration (benefit from larger price rise)
- Expect rates to RISE → DECREASE portfolio duration (limit price decline)
- Expect spreads to NARROW → INCREASE credit risk (tilt to lower-rated bonds, short CDS)
- Expect spreads to WIDEN → DECREASE credit risk (tilt to higher-rated bonds, long CDS)

### 38.10 FI Liquidity Hierarchy & Alternatives

**Liquidity Ranking (highest to lowest):**
1. On-the-run sovereign bonds (most recently issued)
2. Off-the-run sovereign bonds
3. Large corporate investment-grade issues
4. Small/older corporate issues, structured products
5. Private placements, distressed debt

**Consequences of FI Illiquidity:**
- Lack of pricing data → stale NAVs → understated volatility
- Buy-and-hold investors seek liquidity premiums as compensation
- Active investors sacrifice yield for lower transaction costs
- Dealers widen bid-ask spreads for less liquid issues
- Post-GFC: overall bond market liquidity has DECLINED (bank regulations, reduced dealer inventories)

**More Liquid Alternatives to Direct Bond Investment:**
- FI ETFs and mutual funds: more liquid than underlying bonds (can trade fund shares even if underlying bonds don't trade)
- Exchange-traded derivatives (bond futures, options): standardized, organized exchanges
- OTC derivatives (IRS, TRS, CDS): highly customized, counterparty credit risk
→ **Key insight:** FI derivatives and ETFs may be MORE liquid than the underlying bonds themselves

### 38.11 FI Expected Return Model — Worked Example (£ Portfolio)

**Portfolio Characteristics:**
- Notional: £100M | Coupon: £2.75 per £100 par | Annual frequency | Horizon: 1 year
- Current price: £97.12 | Expected price in 1yr: £97.285 (unchanged yield curve)
- Convexity: 18 | Modified duration: 3.70
- Expected Δ benchmark yield: +0.26% | Expected Δ spread: −0.10% | Expected currency loss: −0.50%

**5-Component Calculation:**
1. **Coupon income** = 2.75 / 97.12 = **+2.83%**
2. **Rolldown return** = (97.285 − 97.12) / 97.12 = **+0.17%**
3. **ΔP from Δ benchmark yields** = [−3.70 × 0.0026] + [½ × 18 × (0.0026)²] = −0.962% + 0.006% = **−0.96%**
4. **ΔP from Δ spreads** = [−3.70 × (−0.0010)] + [½ × 18 × (0.0010)²] = +0.370% + 0.001% = **+0.37%**
5. **Currency loss** = **−0.50%**

**Total Expected Return = 2.83 + 0.17 − 0.96 + 0.37 − 0.50 = +1.91%**

Rolling yield = Component 1 + 2 = 2.83 + 0.17 = **3.00%**

**Exam note:** Components 3 & 4 each use the full duration + convexity formula: % ΔP = (−ModDur × ΔY) + (½ × Convexity × ΔY²). The convexity adjustment is usually tiny but show your work.
Links to: Section 12.1 (FI expected return decomposition)

### 38.12 LDI vs ADI — Two Approaches to Institutional FI Management

**Liability-Driven Investing (LDI):**
- Liabilities are KNOWN → portfolio constructed to fund them
- Examples: DB pension plans, insurance companies (guaranteed annuity rates)
- Objective: minimize surplus volatility (assets − liabilities)
- Portfolio matches duration/cash flows of liability stream

**Asset-Driven Investing (ADI):**
- Assets are the STARTING POINT → earn spread over funding cost
- Example: bank offers mortgages (assets) funded by deposits (liabilities)
- Asset pricing must account for: funding costs, duration mismatch between assets and liabilities, liquidity differences
- Objective: maximize net interest margin while managing ALM risk

**LDI Spectrum (increasing expected return AND structural risk):**

| Strategy | Risk Level | Description |
|---|---|---|
| Cash flow matching | Lowest | Bond maturities/coupons exactly match liability payments |
| Duration matching | Medium | Portfolio duration = liability duration (allows different structures) |
| Contingent immunization | Highest | Active management UNTIL surplus drops to minimum → then switches to pure immunization |

→ Moving right on spectrum: higher expected return, higher tracking error, more structural risk from non-parallel yield curve shifts
Links to: Section 12.3 (FI mandates), Section 24.2 (LDI)

### 38.13 CF Matching Worked Example — Swedish Lumber Company (SEK)

**Setup:** Company retires debt liabilities using government bonds (accounting defeasance).

| Liability | Amount | Bond Available | Coupon |
|---|---|---|---|
| June Year 2 | SEK 3,710,000 | May Year 2 maturity | 2.75% |
| June Year 3 | SEK 6,620,000 | May Year 3 maturity | 3.50% |
| June Year 4 | SEK 4,410,000 | May Year 4 maturity | 4.75% |
| June Year 5 | SEK 5,250,000 | May Year 5 maturity | 5.50% |

**Solution Method (work BACKWARDS from longest liability):**

**Step 1 — Year 5:** Par = SEK 5,250,000 / 1.055 = **SEK 4,976,303 ≈ SEK 4.98M**

**Step 2 — Year 4:** Deduct 5-year bond coupon received: 4.98M × 0.055 = SEK 273,900
Par = (4,410,000 − 273,900) / 1.0475 = **SEK 3,948,000 ≈ SEK 3.95M**

**Step 3 — Year 3:** Deduct coupons from 5-yr and 4-yr bonds: (4.98M × 0.055) + (3.95M × 0.0475) = 273,900 + 187,625 = SEK 461,525
Par = (6,620,000 − 273,900 − 187,625) / 1.035 = **SEK 5,950,000 ≈ SEK 5.95M**

**Step 4 — Year 2:** Deduct all coupons from 5-yr, 4-yr, 3-yr bonds: 273,900 + 187,625 + (5.95M × 0.035) = 273,900 + 187,625 + 208,250 = SEK 669,775
Par = (3,710,000 − 273,900 − 187,625 − 208,250) / 1.0275 = **SEK 2,960,000 ≈ SEK 2.96M**

**Exam Decision Rule for CF Matching:** ALWAYS work backwards from the LAST liability. Each step deducts coupons already received from previously purchased bonds, then solves for par value = (liability − all coupons) / (1 + coupon rate).
Links to: Section 24.3 (cash flow matching)

### 38.14 Alternative Investments — Investment Opportunity Set Classification

**Three Approaches to Classifying Alternatives:**

**1. Liquidity-Based Approach:**

| More Liquid → Less Liquid | Fixed Income | Equity | Other Assets |
|---|---|---|---|
| Most liquid | Cash, Bonds | Public Equity | Commodity Futures, REITs |
| Mid liquid | | Equity Hedge Funds | Private Real Estate |
| Least liquid | Private Credit | Private Equity | Private Real Assets |

→ Decision: allocate first to broad liquid asset classes, then view illiquid alternatives as components of their liquid counterparts (e.g., private equity as extension of public equity)

**2. Economic Environment-Based (2×2 Matrix):**

| | Negative/Low Growth | High Growth |
|---|---|---|
| **Deflation** | Government Bonds | Public Equity, Private Equity, HY Bonds, Private Credit |
| **High Inflation** | Index-Linked Bonds, Gold | Commodities, Real Assets |

→ Key insight: government bonds perform best in deflation + low growth (flight to safety, rates fall)
→ Real assets/commodities perform best in high inflation (pass through price increases)
→ Growth-sensitive assets (equity, PE, HY, private credit) perform best in high growth regardless of inflation

**3. Risk Factor-Based Approach:**
- Estimate statistical sensitivities to risk factors (equity beta, credit spread, interest rate, liquidity, volatility)
- Useful because identifies risks COMMON ACROSS asset classes (e.g., PE has equity-like risk, private credit has spread risk)
- Allows integrated risk management framework
Links to: Section 14.1 (alternatives overview), Section 2.1 (asset allocation)

### 38.15 PE Fund Cash Flow Timeline & Liquidity Planning

**Illustrative $10M PE Commitment:**
- T₀: LP commits $10M
- T₁: Capital call $3M (calldown period begins)
- T₂: Capital call $4M
- T₃: Capital call $2M (total called = $9M of $10M committed)
- T₄-T₅: Holding period (no calls or distributions)
- T₆: Distribution $4M
- T₇: Distribution $5M
- T₉: Distribution $3M (total distributions = $12M)
- T₁₀: Fund terminates

**Key Liquidity Planning Rules:**
1. GP does NOT have to call the entire commitment ($10M committed, only $9M called)
2. Capital calls have NO fixed schedule — can happen any time during calldown period (years 1-3 typically)
3. Uncalled capital has **opportunity cost** (must be kept liquid/accessible, can't invest in illiquid assets)
4. Closed fund = typically 10-year life, GP may extend 1-2 years
5. No redemptions allowed → LP cannot exit early (secondary market exists but is very limited and often requires discount)
6. Management fees charged on COMMITTED capital (not called) → fee drag on uncalled portion
7. **Side pockets:** GP can segregate illiquid holdings from general redemption terms → protects fund from forced selling

**HF vs PE Liquidity Comparison:**
- Hedge funds: lock-up 1 year typical, then quarterly/annual redemptions with notice → MORE liquid
- PE/private credit/real estate: 10-year closed fund, no redemptions → LESS liquid
Links to: Section 14.3 (PE structure), Section 14.5 (liquidity planning)

### 38.16 Small Endowment PE Allocation Case — Vehicle Selection & Pacing

**Setup:** Small endowment, first-time PE allocation, 10% strategic target, CIO + 2 analysts, high operating expense distributions, remainder in illiquid investments (real estate).

**Q1: Direct investment vs. Fund-of-Funds (FoF)?**
Answer: **FoF is optimal** because:
1. Size of PE allocation: small endowment → difficult to commit enough capital for adequate diversification across individual PE funds
2. Team expertise: CIO + 2 analysts → likely insufficient PE expertise for direct fund selection and due diligence
3. FoF provides: instant diversification across managers/vintages/strategies + professional PE selection + monitoring

**Q2: Slower or faster commitment pace?**
Answer: **Slower pace** because:
1. CIO should first determine cash flow profile and anticipated liquidity needs of overall endowment
2. Remainder of investments already in illiquid assets (real estate) → adding PE increases overall illiquidity
3. High operating expense distributions → endowment needs regular cash flow → slower PE commitment preserves liquidity
→ Cannot commit entire 10% allocation at once; must pace commitments over several years (vintage diversification)

**Exam Decision Rule:** Small institution + limited team + first-time alts allocation → ALWAYS recommend FoF (not direct). High liquidity needs + existing illiquid assets → SLOWER commitment pacing.

### 38.17 Alternatives Suitability — Exam Answer Technique

**Question Pattern:** "Explain two problems [client type] faces with alternative investments."

**CRITICAL Grading Rule:** Answer must be SPECIFIC to the client's situation. Generic statements earn NO credit.

**Bad answers (no credit):**
- "Low liquidity" → too vague, not tied to client
- "High risk" → generic, not client-specific
- "Expenses are high" → true but not linked to client's situation

**Good answers (full credit):**
- "A lack of liquidity and longer time horizon for many alternatives makes them less suitable for the client's SHORT time horizon" → links liquidity to client-specific constraint
- "Many alternative investments are higher risk and potentially unsuitable to their MODERATE risk tolerance and SHORT time horizon" → links risk to client profile
- "With a SMALL portfolio, the low information efficiency and associated high due diligence costs of many alternative investments may eliminate any potential benefits" → links costs to client's portfolio size

→ **Exam rule: ALWAYS anchor your answer to a specific client fact** (age, portfolio size, time horizon, liquidity needs, risk tolerance)

### 38.18 Liquidity Planning — NAV Distribution Worked Example

**Setup:** Investor's NAV in private renewable energy fund = $30M (end 2020). All capital called. Expected 20% distribution end 2021. No distribution 2022. Growth rate = 12%.

**Step 1 — Distribution at end 2021:**
Distribution = NAV₂₀₂₀ × (1 + growth) × distribution% = $30M × 1.12 × 0.20 = **$6.72M**

**Step 2 — NAV at end 2021:**
NAV₂₀₂₁ = ($30M × 1.12) − $6.72M = $33.6M − $6.72M = **$26.88M**

**Step 3 — NAV at end 2022 (no distribution):**
NAV₂₀₂₂ = $26.88M × 1.12 = **$30,105,600 ≈ $30.1M**

**Key formulas:**
- Capital call in period t = call% × (committed capital − previously called capital)
- Distribution in period t = dist% × [NAV_{t−1} × (1 + growth)]
- NAV after distribution = [NAV_{t−1} × (1 + growth)] − distribution_t

**Exam note:** Always grow NAV FIRST, then subtract distribution. The growth applies to the beginning NAV, not the post-distribution NAV.
Links to: Section 25.10 (Takahashi-Alexander model)

### 38.19 Human Capital Computation — John Adams Worked Example

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 81-82

**Setup:** John Adams, 5-year earnings horizon. Salary = $50,000 (year 1), growing at 2%/year. Discount rate = risk-free 4% + risk premium 3% = **7%**. Mortality probabilities: Year 1 = 1%, Year 2 = 2%, Year 3 = 2%, Year 4 = 3%, Year 5 = 4%.

**Formula:** HC = Σ [PV of salary_t × P(survival to year t)]

**Step-by-step computation:**

| Year | Salary | PV Factor (1.07)^t | PV of Salary | Cumulative Survival | PV × Survival |
|------|--------|-------------------|-------------|---------------------|---------------|
| 1 | $50,000 | 0.9346 | $46,729 | 0.99 (1−0.01) | **$46,262** |
| 2 | $51,000 | 0.8734 | $44,545 | 0.9702 (0.99×0.98) | **$43,218** |
| 3 | $52,020 | 0.8163 | $42,462 | 0.9508 (0.9702×0.98) | **$40,373** |
| 4 | $53,060 | 0.7629 | $40,474 | 0.9223 (0.9508×0.97) | **$37,327** |
| 5 | $54,122 | 0.7130 | $38,589 | 0.8854 (0.9223×0.96) | **$34,166** |

**Note:** The exact slide values show individual year PVs as $47,187 / $44,528 / $42,447 / $40,050 / $37,785 (slight rounding differences in method). **HC = $211,997**

**Exam technique:** The exam tests this as a 3-step process: (1) project future salary with growth rate, (2) discount at nominal rate (RF + risk premium), (3) weight by survival probability. If mortality = 0, HC is just PV of salary annuity.

Links to: Section 26.2 (Human Capital framework), Section 26.3 (Economic Balance Sheet)

---

### 38.20 Pension Classification in HC/FC Framework

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 81-82

**DB pension — dual classification:**
- **Accrued benefits** (already earned) = **Financial Capital (FC)** — these are contractual obligations of the employer
- **Future benefits** (contingent on continued employment) = **Human Capital (HC)** — depends on continued labor

**DC pension** = **Financial Capital (FC)** entirely — employee owns the account, bears investment risk directly

**Life-cycle pattern:**
- Early career: Low FC / High HC → portfolio can take more equity risk (HC is "bond-like" for most workers)
- Pre-retirement: High FC / Low HC → portfolio shifts toward capital preservation

**HC-related risks and insurance:**
- **Earnings risk** (illness, disability) → disability insurance replaces HC
- **Premature death risk** → life insurance replaces HC for dependents
- **Longevity risk** (outliving assets) → annuities convert FC to income stream

**Exam trap:** DB pension accrued benefits are FC (not HC), but future DB benefits ARE HC. Many candidates misclassify the entire DB pension as one or the other.

Links to: Section 26.2 (Human Capital), Section 26.4 (Life Insurance)

---

### 38.21 Kozlowska Tax Drag — Three-Scenario Comparison

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 86-92

**Setup:** EUR 100,000 investment, 7% return, 20% tax rate, 20-year horizon. Cost basis B = 1.0 (no unrealized gains at start) unless stated otherwise.

**Scenario 1 — No tax (benchmark):**
FV_G = 100,000 × (1.07)²⁰ = **EUR 386,968**
Pre-tax gain = 386,968 − 100,000 = EUR 286,968

**Scenario 2 — Annual accrual tax (interest/dividends taxed yearly):**
FV_t = 100,000 × [1 + 0.07(1 − 0.20)]²⁰ = 100,000 × (1.056)²⁰ = **EUR 297,357**
Tax drag = (386,968 − 297,357) / 286,968 = 89,611 / 286,968 = **31.2%**
**⚠ Key insight: Tax drag (31%) EXCEEDS the tax rate (20%) because annual taxation erodes the compounding base.**

**Scenario 3 — Deferred capital gains tax (B = 1.0):**
FV_CG = 100,000 × (1.07)²⁰ × (1 − 0.20) + 0.20 × 100,000 = 386,968 × 0.80 + 20,000 = 309,575 + 20,000 = **EUR 329,575**
Tax drag = (386,968 − 329,575) / 286,968 = 57,393 / 286,968 = **20.0%**
**When B = 1 (full cost basis), deferred CG tax drag exactly equals the CG tax rate.**

**Scenario 4 — Deferred CG with cost basis B = 0.80 (unrealized gains exist):**
FV_CG = 100,000 × (1.07)²⁰ × (1 − 0.20) + 0.20 × 0.80 × 100,000 = 309,575 + 16,000 = **EUR 325,575**
Tax drag = (386,968 − 325,575) / 286,968 = 61,393 / 286,968 = **21.4%**
Additional tax from embedded gain = (1 − B) × t_CG × Investment = 0.20 × 0.20 × 100,000 = EUR 4,000

**Master ranking rule for tax drag:**
**Accrual drag (31%) > Cost basis B<1 drag (21.4%) > Deferred B=1 drag (20%) ≥ CG tax rate**

**Formulas to memorize:**
- Accrual: FV_t = Inv × [1 + R(1 − t)]^T
- Deferred CG: FV_CG = Inv × (1+R)^T × (1 − t_CG) + t_CG × B × Inv
- Wealth tax: FV_w = Inv × [(1+R)(1 − t_w)]^T (most destructive — compounds tax on entire base)

**Inflation adjustment:** Divide terminal values by (1 + π)^T. Formulas remain same structure but expressed in real terms.

**Exam note:** If asked "which tax regime produces the highest after-tax accumulation?" → deferred CG (B=1) always beats accrual. If B < 1, deferred CG still beats accrual but drag exceeds the rate.

Links to: Section 10.1 (Tax Regimes), Section 10.4 (Tax Drag), Section 26.7 (After-Tax Returns)

---

### 38.22 Client Goal Prioritization — Case Study

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 83-85

**Setup:** Client A — 50-year-old professor, $2M savings, health issues (may need early retirement). Has children with $100K/yr education costs for 10 years = $1M total education cost.

**Problem identification:** Education cost ($1M) = 50% of total savings ($2M). If health forces early retirement, both goals compete for the same pool.

**Prioritization framework:**
- **Essential goals** (highest priority): Retirement income, basic living expenses
- **Important goals** (medium priority): Children's education, maintaining lifestyle
- **Aspirational goals** (lowest priority): Luxury spending, legacy/bequest

**Solution approach:**
1. Quantify retirement contingency first (years of expenses if forced to retire early)
2. Education funding is important but may need to be partially funded (scholarships, loans, phased)
3. Balanced approach: do NOT fully commit $1M to education if it jeopardizes retirement security
4. Run capital sufficiency analysis: can $2M fund both retirement AND education simultaneously?

**Exam technique:** When the exam gives a client with competing goals, ALWAYS:
(a) Identify which goals are essential vs aspirational
(b) Check if total goals exceed available resources → if yes, something must give
(c) Recommend trade-offs that protect essential goals first
(d) Never recommend a plan that funds aspirational goals at the expense of essential ones

Links to: Section 26.1 (IPS for Private Clients), Section 26.6 (Retirement Planning)

---

### 38.23 IPS Components and Rebalancing Methods

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 94-95

**IPS standard sections:**
1. **Background & overview** — client situation, family, employment, existing assets
2. **Investment objectives** — return requirement (nominal/real), risk tolerance (willingness + capacity)
3. **Investment parameters/constraints** — time horizon, liquidity needs, legal/regulatory, unique circumstances, tax status
4. **Portfolio management scope** — SAA, TAA ranges, permitted asset classes, benchmarks
5. **Duties & responsibilities** — roles of advisor, custodian, client review schedule
6. **Appendix** — asset inventory, modeled scenarios, capital market assumptions

**Capital sufficiency analysis:** Monte Carlo simulation or deterministic projection to test whether portfolio can fund all goals through the planning horizon with acceptable probability (typically 75-90% success rate).

**Rebalancing approaches:**

| Method | Trigger | Pro | Con |
|--------|---------|-----|-----|
| **Calendar (time-based)** | Fixed interval (monthly, quarterly) | Simple, disciplined | May miss large interim drifts |
| **Threshold (corridor)** | Deviation from target exceeds band | Responds to actual drift | Requires monitoring; transaction costs |

**Corridor example:** Target 40% equity, ±5% band → rebalance if equity drops below 35% or rises above 45%.

**Corridor width factors (wider corridor when):**
- Higher transaction costs → wider (reduce trading)
- Higher correlation with rest of portfolio → wider (less diversification impact)
- Lower volatility of asset class → wider (drifts slowly)
- Higher risk tolerance → wider (can tolerate more drift)

**Exam note:** "Calendar rebalancing" + "threshold rebalancing" can be combined: review at calendar intervals but only trade if thresholds are breached. This is the most common institutional approach.

Links to: Section 26.1 (IPS), Section 12.5 (Rebalancing), Section 24.4 (SAA)

---

### 38.24 Nunu Tech DB Plan Case Study — Exam Practice

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 108-110

**Setup:** Nunu Tech (NT) — global company, total assets $20B. Pension plan assets $8.78B, liabilities $9.95B. Objectives: minimum expected return 8.5%, maximum standard deviation 15.0%. NT is lowering retirement age from 60 to 57. Median worker age = 48.

**Funded status:** Surplus = $8.78B − $9.95B = **−$1.17B** (underfunded). Funded ratio = 8.78/9.95 = **88.2%**

**Question: Investment horizon — X (long-term, infinite life) or Y (shorter than past)?**
**Answer: Y** — Lowering retirement age from 60 to 57 means workers retire sooner → increases near-term payout demands → shortens the duration of liabilities. The plan's horizon is NOT infinite (that's a foundation/endowment concept, not a DB plan). Median worker age 48 means only 9 years to retirement (48→57), not 12 (48→60).

**Question: Liquidity needs — X (moderate, fund monthly benefits) or Y (minimal)?**
**Answer: X** — Lowering retirement age increases near-term retirees → more benefit payments sooner → moderate liquidity needs. Y ("minimal") is wrong because an underfunded plan with approaching payouts needs liquidity.

**DB Plan Risk Tolerance Decrease Factors — Complete Exam Checklist:**
1. Larger negative surplus (underfunded) → no cushion for return shortfall
2. Weak financial status of plan sponsor → can't increase contributions if needed
3. High correlation between plan assets and sponsor's business → double jeopardy
4. Larger plan relative to sponsor → sponsor can't tolerate contribution volatility
5. Workforce characteristics that shorten horizon: older workforce, higher retired/active ratio, lump-sum or early retirement provisions
6. Regulatory constraints on asset allocation (e.g., country-specific limits on equities)

**DB vs DC — Primary/Secondary Objectives:**

| | DB Plan | DC Plan |
|---|---------|---------|
| **Primary** | Achieve target return consistent with meeting liabilities | Prudently grow assets to meet retirement spending needs |
| **Secondary** | Minimize PV of sponsor's required cash contributions | Outperform passive asset class returns of default SAA option |
| **Risk bearer** | Sponsor (employer) | Participant (employee) |

**DC Target-Date Fund Types:**
- **Participant-switching:** automatically switches individual participant to more conservative AA as they age
- **Participant/cohort:** pools participants with similar expected retirement dates into cohort-based funds

Links to: Section 17.2 (DB Pension Funds), Section 17.7 (Institutional Comparison Matrix)

---

### 38.25 SWF Five-Type Investment Framework — Exam Quick Reference

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 114-117

**Comprehensive SWF Decision Matrix (by type):**

| SWF Type | Liabilities | Horizon | Liquidity | Return Objective | Asset Allocation |
|----------|------------|---------|-----------|-----------------|-----------------|
| **Budget Stabilization** | Uncertain, linked to commodity prices | SHORT | **HIGHEST** | Capital preservation, beat inflation, avoid assets correlated with government revenues | Cash + FI (defensive) |
| **Development** | Linked to socioeconomic projects | LT + some MT | Low | Support economic development, return > domestic GDP growth | Depends on mission (infrastructure, domestic) |
| **Savings** | Future generations | VERY LONG | Low | Maintain purchasing power, meet ongoing budgetary needs | High equity + alternatives |
| **Reserve** | None near-term | VERY LONG | Medium | Return > government bond yield | Similar to savings, less alternatives (liquidity) |
| **Pension Reserve** | Future pension payments | LT (accumulation) → shortening (decumulation) | Low → increasing | Earn return to meet unfunded pension/social care obligations | High equity + alternatives (accumulation phase) |

**Exam shortcut — SWF liquidity ranking (highest to lowest):**
Budget Stabilization >> Reserve > Pension Reserve (decumulation) > Savings ≈ Development ≈ Pension Reserve (accumulation)

**SWF External Constraints:**
- Must maintain high-quality governance, independence, transparency, accountability → avoid political influence
- Generally tax-exempt → investors cannot claim tax credits
- International investments: check double-taxation treaties to claim withholding taxes paid

Links to: Section 17.3 (Sovereign Wealth Funds), Section 17.7 (Institutional Comparison Matrix)

---

### 38.26 Endowment Spending Rule — Formula and Risk Factors

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 118-120

**Spending formula:** Spending_{t+1} = w × [Spending_t × (1 + inflation)] + (1 − w) × [spending rate × average AUM]

**Three rules as special cases:**
- **w = 1:** Constant growth rule → inflation-adjusted prior spending (stable but can diverge from portfolio value)
- **w = 0:** Market value rule → fixed % of AUM (responsive but pro-cyclical)
- **0 < w < 1:** Hybrid rule → most common in practice

**Risk tolerance factors for endowments:**
- Higher operating budget dependence on endowment → LOWER risk tolerance (university relies on payouts)
- Fundraising from donors reduces net spending rate (e.g., 5% gross → 3% net) → HIGHER risk tolerance (effective liability is smaller)
- Increased capability to issue debt → HIGHER risk tolerance (alternative liquidity source available)
- Perpetual time horizon + low liquidity needs → generally HIGH risk tolerance (but tempered by above factors)

**Endowment External Constraints:**
- Subject to laws requiring investment on total return basis (income + capital gains, not just income)
- Must diversify investments (fiduciary duty)
- Investment committees/boards have fiduciary oversight duties
- Tax-exempt investment earnings; donors receive tax deductions for gifts

**Exam note:** When asked "does this endowment have above-average or below-average risk tolerance?" → check THREE things: (1) budget dependence %, (2) gift/donation inflows, (3) debt capacity. These three factors differentiate otherwise-similar endowments.

Links to: Section 17.4 (University Endowments), Section 17.5 (Private Foundations)

---

### 38.27 Foundation Required Return Calculation — Exam Method

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 124-125

**Setup:** Foundation with 5% required distribution, 0.4% management costs, 3% foundation-specific inflation, 2% general inflation.

**Additive approach (recommended for 2025 curriculum):**
Required return = spending rate + management costs + inflation rate
= 5.0% + 0.4% + 3.0% = **8.4%**

**Compounded approach (historically preferred in past exam answers):**
Required return = (1.05 × 1.004 × 1.03) − 1 = **8.56%**

**Exam guidance:** Past exam answers showed compounded as preferred, but additive is an acceptable alternative. For 2025 curriculum, Kaplan recommends using **additive** unless directed otherwise.

**Which inflation to use?** Use the inflation rate related to the foundation's objectives (3%), NOT general inflation (2%), because the foundation's spending needs grow with its mission-specific costs.

---

### 38.28 CRF Foundation Spending Policy Case Study

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 125-126

**Setup:** CRF — UK foundation, mission to fund medical research in perpetuity. Portfolio £10M. Will receive £45M cash gift in 3 months. Has been paying out virtually ALL investment income → real value declining. Must pay 5% of market value to maintain tax-exempt status. Investments currently conservative, almost all fixed income. No additional gifts expected.

**Question:** Identify and discuss three key elements of the grant-making (spending) policy.

**Three key elements:**
1. **Spending rate must at least equal 5%** — legally required to maintain tax-exempt status. Finance committee wants to know how much higher it should be.
2. **Preserve real value** — If nominal return equals spending rate + inflation, real value of assets and distributions maintained in perpetuity. Foundation must earn enough to cover spending + inflation + expenses.
3. **Earning the expense rate** — Management costs are an additional drag. The required return must also cover investment expenses to avoid eroding the portfolio.

**Key insight:** CRF has been paying out ALL investment income, which erodes real value because inflation is not being offset. After the £45M gift (portfolio grows to £55M), CRF should switch to a total return approach: invest for growth (not just income), pay out only 5% of market value, and let the rest compound to preserve purchasing power.

**Exam technique:** When a foundation case says "paying out all income" → this is ALWAYS a problem signal. Total return approach is the correct recommendation.

Links to: Section 17.5 (Private Foundations)

---

### 38.29 Cunucu Insurance — Duration Mismatch Case Study

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 134-135

**Setup:** Cunucu Insurance (CI) — life insurer. D_L has been declining (attributed to higher rates and passage of time). CFO notes: 2-year GICs have increased in popularity. Interest rates rising, expected to rise another 100 bps. CFO recommends: buy higher-yielding, investment-grade, longer-duration bonds to maintain duration and maximize spread.

**Data:**

| | 4 Years Ago | Last Year |
|---|---|---|
| Portfolio assets | $450M | $500M |
| D_A (asset duration) | 6.0 | 6.0 |
| Liabilities | $390M | $470M |
| D_L (liability duration) | 5.5 | 4.0 |
| **Surplus** | **$60M** | **$30M** |

**Question:** Is the CFO's strategy appropriate?

**Answer: NO — the CFO's strategy is INAPPROPRIATE.** Two reasons (only need 2 on exam):

1. **Asset duration already exceeds liability duration** (D_A = 6.0 vs D_L = 4.0). Buying longer-duration bonds will INCREASE D_A further, widening the mismatch. If rates rise as expected, the longer-duration assets will lose MORE value than the shorter-duration liabilities → surplus will deteriorate further.

2. **Marketing 2-year GICs** will likely REDUCE D_L even more (GICs have ~2-year duration), making the mismatch worse. The correct strategy would be to SHORTEN asset duration to match the declining liability duration.

3. **Surplus has already deteriorated** from $60M to $30M — the existing duration mismatch is already causing damage. Adding to it is dangerous.

**Exam pattern recognition:** When an insurer case shows D_A > D_L and the recommendation is to buy LONGER bonds → answer is always "inappropriate." The fix is to reduce D_A to match D_L, not increase it.

Links to: Section 17.6 (Insurance Companies), Section 27.2 (Duration of Shareholder Equity)

---

### 38.30 Bank/Insurer Equity Volatility Strategies — Decision Framework

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 137-139

**Equity volatility formula:** σ²_E = M²σ²_A + (M−1)²σ²_L − 2M(M−1)σ_Aσ_Lρ_AL

Where M = leverage multiplier (A/E), σ_A = asset volatility, σ_L = liability volatility, ρ_AL = asset-liability correlation.

**Strategies and their effects on equity volatility components:**

| Strategy | σ_A | σ_L | σ_E | ρ_AL |
|----------|-----|-----|-----|------|
| Hold diversified/high-quality FI | ↓ | — | ↓ | — |
| Match A and L durations (net duration → 0) | — | — | ↓ | ↑ |
| Hold common stock (L are FI-like) | ↑ | — | ↑ | ↓ |
| More derivatives transparency/collateralization | ↓ | ↓ | ↓ | ↑ |
| Hold more liquid investments | ↓ | — | ↓ | — |
| Surrender penalties on insurance contracts | — | ↓ | ↓ | — |
| Prepayment penalties on debt investments | — | — | ↓ | ↑ |
| Catastrophic insurance risk | — | ↑ | ↑ | — |
| Increase predictability of underwriting losses | — | ↓ | ↓ | — |
| Diversify insurance business | — | ↓ | ↓ | — |
| Variable annuities (risk → policyholders) | — | — | ↓ | ↑ |

**Key exam insight:** To REDUCE σ_E, the most effective strategies are: (1) match A and L durations (increases ρ_AL, which lowers σ_E through the correlation term), (2) reduce σ_A by holding higher-quality/liquid assets, and (3) reduce σ_L through surrender penalties and diversifying insurance lines.

**Surrender penalties explained:** When interest rates rise, policyholders want to surrender low-yielding contracts. Without surrender penalties, the insurer must pay par → crystallizes a loss. With surrender penalties, policyholders are discouraged from surrendering → σ_L falls because liabilities are more stable.

Links to: Section 27.1-27.3 (Leverage, Duration, Volatility Formulas), Section 17.6 (Banks and Insurers)

---

### 38.31 SJT Bank ALMCo Case Study — Credit/Liquidity Strategy

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 139-140

**Setup:** SJT Bank ALMCo quarterly review findings:
1. Corporate spreads far below historical norms → expect deterioration, spreads widening
2. Securitization/secondary loan market disruption → loan book liquidity declining, depositor withdrawals becoming larger and less predictable

**Proposed strategy:** Switch from HY corporate floating-rate securities to IG fixed-rate government securities. Enter pay-fixed/receive-floating interest rate swap.

**Analysis — How the switch addresses both findings:**

**Finding 1 (credit spread widening):**
- HY bonds will lose more value than IG bonds when spreads widen
- Switching to IG government securities **reduces** exposure to widening credit spreads
- Government bonds have zero (or near-zero) credit risk

**Finding 2 (liquidity deterioration):**
- Bank's assets (loan book) becoming harder to sell; depositor withdrawals less predictable
- IG government bonds are **more liquid** than HY corporate bonds
- The switch **improves** the liquidity of the investment portfolio, offsetting deterioration in the loan book

**Impact on regulatory risk-based reserves:**
- Government bonds are IG → **lower** credit risk weighting than HY corporates
- Lower risk-weighted assets → regulator views bank as having lower risk
- Required regulatory capital reserves **decrease** → frees up equity capital

**Exam note:** The pay-fixed/receive-floating swap converts the fixed-rate government bonds back to floating-rate exposure (matching the bank's floating-rate liabilities/deposits), while keeping the credit quality improvement. This is a common institutional strategy: improve credit quality via the bond switch, then use derivatives to manage interest rate exposure separately.

Links to: Section 17.6 (Banks), Section 27.5 (Investment Strategy Effects)

---

### 38.32 Bank B/S Management — Three Worked Examples with Numbers

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 136-146

**Example 1 — %ΔE (Leverage Amplification):**
Bank with E/A = 5%. Liabilities rise 1.5%, assets remain stable.
- M = 1/0.05 = 20 (leverage multiplier)
- %ΔE = %ΔA(M) − %ΔL(M−1) = (0% × 20) − (1.5% × 19) = **−28.5%**
- A 1.5% liability increase wipes out 28.5% of equity → leverage amplifies B/S shocks massively

**Example 2 — Duration of Equity (D_E):**
D_A = 3, D_L = 2, Δi/Δy = 0.70 (liability yield sensitivity to asset yield changes)
- With E/A = 12.5% → M = 8 → D_E = (3 × 8 × 1.00) − (2 × (8−1) × 0.70) = 24 − 9.8 = **14.2**
- With E/A = 10% → M = 10 → D_E = (3 × 10 × 1) − (2 × 9 × 0.70) = 30 − 12.6 = **17.4**
- Lower E/A (more leverage) → HIGHER D_E → equity becomes more rate-sensitive

**Impact of 25 bps yield fall with D_E = 14.2:**
- D_E of 14.2 means 1% yield change → 14.2% equity value change
- 0.25% × 14.2 = **+3.55%** equity gain (yields fall → bond-heavy portfolios gain)

**Example 3 — Equity Volatility (σ_E):**
σ_A = 7%, σ_L = 4%, ρ_AL = 0.3, E/A = 12.5% → M = 8
- σ²_E = (8² × 0.07²) + ((8−1)² × 0.04²) − 2(8)(7)(0.07)(0.04)(0.3)
- = 0.3136 + 0.0784 − 0.009408 = 0.298
- σ_E = √0.298 = **0.546 = 54.6%**

**Key insight:** Even with "low" asset volatility (7%) and liability volatility (4%), the 8× leverage multiplies equity volatility to 54.6%. This is why bank/insurer equity is so volatile — it's the leverage, not the underlying asset risk.

Links to: Section 27.1 (Leverage Amplification), Section 27.2 (Duration of Equity), Section 27.3 (Equity Volatility)

---

### 38.33 Arapahoe Tanager — Effective Spread & VWAP Worked Example

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 153-157

**Setup:** Arapahoe Tanager (PM) and Lief Schrader (trader), Canadian small-cap equity mutual fund. Ticket: sell 12,000 shares of Alpha Company, limit C$9.95. Order split into 3 trades:

**Trade data:**

| Trade | Shares | Exec Price | Bid | Offer | Bid Size | Offer Size |
|-------|--------|-----------|-----|-------|----------|------------|
| 1 | 2,000 (sell) | C$10.15 | C$10.12 (3,000) | C$10.24 (2,000) | 3,000 | 2,000 |
| 2 | 3,000 (sell) | C$10.11 | C$10.11 (3,000) | C$10.22 (2,000) | 3,000 | 2,000 |
| 3 | 5,000 (sell) | C$10.01 | C$10.05 (3,000) | C$10.19 (2,000) | 3,000 | 2,000 |

2,000 shares unfilled (cancelled after market close). Other trades in Alpha that day: 2,000 at C$10.20 and 1,000 at C$10.15. Last trade price C$9.95; next day C$9.50.

**Quoted Spread (QS) = Ask − Bid:**
- Trade 1: 10.24 − 10.12 = **C$0.12**
- Trade 2: 10.22 − 10.11 = **C$0.11**
- Trade 3: 10.19 − 10.05 = **C$0.14**
- Average QS = (0.12 + 0.11 + 0.14) / 3 = **C$0.1233**

**Effective Spread (ES) = (Midprice − Trade Price) × 2 for sells:**
- Trade 1: Midprice = (10.24+10.12)/2 = 10.18. ES = (10.18 − 10.15) × 2 = **C$0.06** → Good execution (ES < QS), sold ABOVE the bid
- Trade 2: Midprice = (10.22+10.11)/2 = 10.165. ES = (10.165 − 10.11) × 2 = **C$0.11** → Average execution (ES ≈ QS), sold AT the bid
- Trade 3: Midprice = (10.19+10.05)/2 = 10.12. ES = (10.12 − 10.01) × 2 = **C$0.22** → Poor execution (ES > QS), sold BELOW the bid (walked the book)
- Average ES = (0.06 + 0.11 + 0.22) / 3 = **C$0.13**

**VWAP — All 13,000 shares traded that day:**
Total $ volume = (2,000×10.15) + (3,000×10.11) + (5,000×10.01) + (2,000×10.20) + (1,000×10.15) = 20,300 + 30,330 + 50,050 + 20,400 + 10,150 = **C$131,230**
Day VWAP = 131,230 / 13,000 = **C$10.0946**

**VWAP — Mutual fund's 10,000 shares:**
Fund $ volume = 20,300 + 30,330 + 50,050 = **C$100,680**
Fund VWAP = 100,680 / 10,000 = **C$10.068**

**VWAP transaction cost** = (Day VWAP − Fund VWAP) × shares sold = (10.0946 − 10.068) × 10,000 = **C$266**
(Positive = good: fund sold at prices close to day VWAP, cost was small)

**Exam technique for effective spread:**
- ES < QS → price improvement (good execution)
- ES = QS → executed at quoted bid/ask (average)
- ES > QS → market impact (bad execution, typically from large orders walking the book)

Links to: Section 28.2 (Transaction Cost Measurement), Section 28.4 (Market Microstructure)

---

### 38.34 Abusive Trading Practices — Complete Taxonomy

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 161-162

**Six categories of abusive trading (exam loves classification questions):**

1. **Front running** — Trading ahead of a known large client order to profit from the anticipated price impact. Illegal; violates duty to client.

2. **Market manipulation** — Three subtypes:
   - **Bluffing (Pump-and-Dump):** Buy shares → spread false positive information → sell at inflated price. Reverse = short-and-distort.
   - **Gunning the market:** Trade aggressively to push prices through known stop-loss levels, triggering forced selling, then buy the dip.
   - **Squeezing/Cornering:** Accumulate dominant position in an asset (especially physical commodities or illiquid securities) to control supply and force short sellers to cover at inflated prices.

3. **Trading for market impact** — Deliberately trading to move prices (not to establish a position), often to benefit a related position or trigger contractual thresholds.

4. **Rumormongering** — Spreading false information to move prices. Distinguished from legitimate analysis by intent to deceive.

5. **Wash trading** — Simultaneously buying and selling the same security to create artificial volume/activity, making the security appear more liquid or actively traded than it is.

6. **Spoofing (layering)** — Placing large orders with intent to cancel before execution, creating false impression of supply/demand. Illegal under Dodd-Frank.

**Exam pattern:** Questions typically present a scenario and ask which type of manipulation is occurring. Key distinguisher: the INTENT behind the trading activity.

Links to: Section 28.1 (Trading and Markets Overview), Section 36 (Ethics)

---

### 38.35 Liquidity Risk Management — Five-Step Framework for Illiquid Assets

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 168-170

**Return smoothing problem with illiquid assets:**
- Appraisal-based valuations lag true market values
- Result: **Understated volatility** (smoothed returns look less volatile than reality)
- Result: **Understated correlations** with other asset classes (lag obscures true co-movement)
- Exam implication: Risk metrics (VaR, σ, Sharpe) for portfolios with illiquid assets will be OVERSTATED in quality (lower risk, higher Sharpe than true values)

**Direct vs. Indirect illiquid investments:**

| Feature | Direct Investment | Indirect (Fund) Investment |
|---------|------------------|---------------------------|
| Control | High (board seats, operations) | Low (GP makes decisions) |
| Liquidity over exits | More control on timing | Less control (fund lifecycle) |
| Diversification | Low (concentrated) | Higher (fund holds multiple assets) |
| Concentration risk | HIGH | Lower |
| Fees | Lower (no GP layer) | Higher (management + carry) |
| Due diligence burden | Heavy (investor must assess) | Lighter (GP team does DD) |

**Five-step liquidity risk management process:**

1. **Governance** — Establish liquidity policy, define roles/responsibilities, set liquidity risk tolerance at board level
2. **Liquidity profiling** — Classify all assets by liquidity tier (e.g., Tier 1: <1 day to liquidate; Tier 2: 1-30 days; Tier 3: >30 days)
3. **Stress testing** — Model liquidity under adverse scenarios (market crisis, redemption surge, margin calls, correlation spike)
4. **Monitoring** — Ongoing tracking of liquidity metrics: bid-ask spreads, trading volumes, redemption queues, days-to-liquidate
5. **Contingency planning** — Pre-defined actions for liquidity crises: credit lines, asset sale priorities, redemption gates/suspensions, side-pocketing illiquid assets

**Exam note:** Endowments and SWFs have higher illiquid asset tolerance (long horizon, no contractual liabilities). DB pensions with aging demographics have LOWER tolerance (need cash for benefit payments). Banks/insurers have LOWEST tolerance (regulatory liquidity requirements).

Links to: Section 17 (Institutional Investors), Section 38.25 (SWF Framework), Section 38.26 (Endowment Spending)

---

### 38.36 Climate Risk and ESG Integration — Institutional Framework

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 173-177

**PRI (Principles for Responsible Investment) — 6 Principles:**
Signatories commit to: (1) incorporate ESG into investment analysis, (2) be active owners, (3) seek ESG disclosure from investees, (4) promote PRI adoption, (5) collaborate for implementation, (6) report on progress.

**Universal ownership concept:** Very large institutional investors (SWFs, large pensions) own a slice of the entire economy. Negative externalities from one holding damage other holdings → rational self-interest to reduce externalities across the entire portfolio, not just optimize individual positions.

**Climate risk — Two categories:**

| Risk Type | Definition | Examples | Time Horizon |
|-----------|-----------|----------|-------------|
| **Transition risk** | Risks from shifting to low-carbon economy | Stranded assets (fossil fuel reserves), carbon taxes/regulation, technology disruption (EVs replacing ICE), changing consumer preferences | Medium-term (5-15 years) |
| **Physical risk — Acute** | Extreme weather events | Hurricanes, wildfires, floods, heat waves | Immediate/unpredictable |
| **Physical risk — Chronic** | Long-term climate shifts | Sea level rise, desertification, water scarcity, biodiversity loss | Long-term (10-50+ years) |

**Paris Agreement target:** Limit warming to well below 2°C above pre-industrial levels, pursuing 1.5°C. Investment implication: massive capital reallocation from carbon-intensive to zero-carbon sectors.

**Climate investment strategies:**
- **Mitigation:** Reduce portfolio exposure to fossil fuels, carbon-intensive industries. Divest or underweight.
- **Adaptation:** Invest in companies/infrastructure aligned to zero-carbon economy (renewables, EVs, carbon capture, climate-resilient infrastructure).

**Social risk categories (exam checklist):**
Labor rights and working conditions, diversity and inclusion, health and safety, data privacy and security, community impact, supply chain labor standards.

**Exam pattern:** Case study presents an institutional investor with ESG concerns → identify which type of risk (transition vs physical, acute vs chronic) → recommend strategy (mitigation vs adaptation). The Ruritania case (Section 38.37) is a comprehensive example.

Links to: Section 17 (Institutional Investors), Section 38.25 (SWF Types), Section 38.37 (Ruritania SWF Case)

---

### 38.37 Ruritania SWF Case Study — Multi-Period Illiquid Investment with ESG/Climate Risk

**Source:** Masterclass Portfolio Construction pp 178-189

**This is the highest-value integrative case in the Masterclass — combines direct illiquid investing, ESG analysis, climate risk, stakeholder management, and risk measurement tools across a multi-period timeline.**

**Setup:** R-SWF is a $50 billion savings fund (rare mineral exports). Two direct illiquid investments under review:

**Investment 1 — Sunnyland Airport:**
- Cost: $100M (0.2% of AUM)
- Structure: 25-year BOT (Build-Operate-Transfer) concession
- Location: Frontier market island, popular tourist destination
- Key risk factor: Runway within 1 km of the sea
- Revenue model: Tourism-dependent passenger traffic

**Investment 2 — Atusi Beverage Company (ABC):**
- Cost: $25M (0.05% of AUM)
- Structure: 35% equity stake
- Location: Frontier market country, near biodiversity-rich river
- Business: Local beverage manufacturer, benefits from 100% import tariffs on competing products
- Modernization: New bottling line installed

**Year 0 Analysis:**

**Q1 — Liquidity risk assessment:**
Both investments are small relative to $50B AUM (0.2% and 0.05%) → minimal impact on overall portfolio liquidity. However, as direct investments in frontier markets, they are individually illiquid (no secondary market, long exit timeline). The R-SWF can tolerate this given its long horizon and no contractual spending obligations (savings fund).

**Q2 — Environmental risk identification:**
- **Sunnyland:** Physical risk — chronic. Runway is 1 km from sea → sea level rise over 25-year concession could damage infrastructure, increase maintenance costs, eventually make airport inoperable. This is a LONG-DURATION physical climate risk matching the LONG-DURATION investment horizon.
- **ABC:** Environmental externality risk. Factory near biodiversity-rich river → operations could contaminate waterway, damaging ecosystem. Reputational and regulatory risk if environmental damage occurs.

**3-Year Review — Deterioration:**

| Factor | Sunnyland Airport | ABC Beverage |
|--------|-------------------|--------------|
| Climate/Environment | Climate change worse than expected; tourist season shortened; coastal erosion accelerating | Modernization complete but management dumping waste in river |
| Economic | Recession hit tourist traffic | Recession reduced luxury beverage demand |
| Regulatory | — | New government slashed tariffs from 100% → 20% (competitive threat) |
| Labor | — | Local employment laws require 2-year notice for redundancies |

**Q3 — Social risk identification:**
- **Sunnyland:** Stakeholder management complexity — government (concession grantor), airport operator (commercial partner), tourism industry (revenue source), local community (employment, noise, environmental impact). Recession creates tension between all stakeholders.
- **ABC:** 50% headcount reduction planned but 2-year notice law prevents quick action → ongoing labor costs during downturn. Management caught reducing worker breaks and removing soap from facilities → employee exploitation / H&S violations. Community impact: factory is major local employer, layoffs would devastate community.

**Q4 — Common risk across both investments:**
**REPUTATIONAL RISK.** R-SWF is a sovereign wealth fund — government entity. Both investments now involve:
- Sunnyland: Climate negligence (should have assessed sea level risk more carefully)
- ABC: Environmental contamination (river dumping) + worker exploitation (reduced breaks, no soap)

If media/NGOs link these issues to the sovereign fund → reputational damage to the nation, not just the fund. SWFs face heightened reputational scrutiny because they represent national reputation.

**5-Year Review — Resolution:**
- **Sunnyland:** Passenger traffic down 50% from original projections. Costs up 50% (climate adaptation: sea walls, drainage). Outlook: unfavorable. The 25-year horizon means R-SWF is locked in with a deteriorating asset.
- **ABC:** R-SWF sold its 35% stake for $27M (vs $25M cost = small profit). EXIT was the correct decision — avoided further reputational damage from river contamination and worker exploitation.

**Q5 — Risk measurement tools for Sunnyland:**
Three quantitative tools appropriate for analyzing Sunnyland's risks:

1. **Scenario analysis and stress testing** — Model different climate change scenarios (optimistic: 1.5°C warming; base: 2°C; pessimistic: 3°C+) and their impact on sea levels, storm frequency, tourist season length, and passenger traffic. Stress test: what if traffic falls 70%? What if sea wall costs triple?

2. **Monte Carlo simulation** — Generate probability distributions for key revenue/cost variables (passenger numbers, ticket prices, maintenance costs, insurance costs, climate adaptation spend). Run thousands of iterations to produce a distribution of NPV outcomes and probability of loss.

3. **Breakeven analysis** — Calculate the minimum passenger traffic level needed to cover operating costs + debt service + required return. Compare to downside scenario traffic projections. If breakeven traffic exceeds pessimistic scenario traffic → investment is non-viable.

**Master exam framework from this case:**

| Analysis Step | Key Question | Tools |
|---------------|-------------|-------|
| Initial screening | Is position size material to portfolio? | % of AUM |
| Environmental risk | Physical or transition? Acute or chronic? | Climate scenario analysis |
| Social risk | Labor, community, stakeholder impacts? | Stakeholder mapping |
| Governance risk | Management quality and ethics? | Due diligence, monitoring |
| Risk measurement | How bad could it get? | Scenario/stress test, Monte Carlo, breakeven |
| Exit decision | When to cut losses vs hold? | NPV analysis, reputational cost-benefit |

**Exam technique:** This type of multi-period institutional case is VERY likely on the L3 exam. The pattern is: initial investment → review period shows deterioration → identify new risks → recommend action. Always consider reputational risk for institutional investors (especially SWFs and endowments). Always identify whether climate risk is transition or physical, acute or chronic.

Links to: Section 17.5 (SWFs), Section 38.25 (SWF Five-Type Framework), Section 38.35 (Liquidity Risk), Section 38.36 (Climate Risk)

## Section 39: Performance Measurement — Masterclass Worked Examples & Exam Tips (V12.7)

*Source: Kaplan Masterclass Performance Measurement (108pp). Most content overlaps with Sections 18, 18.9, 21, and 22. This section captures the specific numerical worked examples and exam tips NOT already in the KB.*

### 39.1 Exam Tip — Brinson Attribution Model Default

**Default to the Brinson-Fachler (BF) model unless the question explicitly states otherwise.** The BF model attributes selection return relative to the BENCHMARK weight, while BHB attributes relative to PORTFOLIO weight. On the exam, if no model is specified, use BF. The difference matters when sector weights diverge significantly between portfolio and benchmark.

### 39.2 Performance-Based Fee — Full Worked Example

**Setup:** Standard fee = 0.50%, Base fee = 0.25%, Sharing rate = 20% (on active return beyond base fee threshold), Maximum annual fee = 0.75%.

**Fee formula:** Fee = Base + [Sharing × (Active Return − Base Fee Threshold)]

Breakeven active return (fee equals standard): solving 0.50% = 0.25% + [20% × (X − 0.25%)] → X = **1.50%**

**Active return range and fee boundaries:**
- Active return ≤ 0.25%: Min fee = **0.25%** (base fee floor)
  - Calculation: 0.25% + [20% × (0.25% − 0.25%)] = 0.25%
- Active return = 1.50%: Breakeven fee = **0.50%** (same as standard fee)
  - Calculation: 0.25% + [20% × (1.50% − 0.25%)] = 0.50%
- Active return ≥ 2.75%: Max fee = **0.75%** (fee cap)
  - Calculation: 0.25% + [20% × (2.75% − 0.25%)] = 0.75%

**Decision rule:** If active return < 1.50% → performance-based fee is CHEAPER than standard. If active return > 1.50% → performance-based fee is MORE EXPENSIVE. Manager is indifferent at exactly 1.50%.

**Call option analogy:** This fee = long call (strike = base) − short call (strike = max) = bull call spread on active return. The manager's incentive to take risk is CAPPED because the short call limits upside beyond 0.75% fee. Without the cap, a pure bonus fee (no max) = naked long call → incentivizes EXCESSIVE risk-taking.

Links to: Section 21.10 (performance-based fee structures), Section 19.2 (option payoff profiles)

### 39.3 Capture Ratios — Full 10-Period Worked Example

**Setup:** 10 quarterly periods, Fund vs Benchmark returns.

**Calculation method — geometric averages (NOT arithmetic):**
- Upside Capture (UC): geometric mean of fund returns in UP quarters / geometric mean of benchmark returns in UP quarters
- Downside Capture (DC): geometric mean of fund returns in DOWN quarters / geometric mean of benchmark returns in DOWN quarters
- Capture Ratio (CR): UC / DC

**Results from worked example:**
UC = **72.8%** (fund captures 72.8% of benchmark upside)
DC = **59.8%** (fund captures only 59.8% of benchmark downside)
CR = 72.8% / 59.8% = **121.7%** → CR > 100% = **CONVEX** return profile (desirable)

**Interpretation:** This manager participates in 72.8% of the gains but only 59.8% of the losses → asymmetric skill in protecting capital. The CR of 121.7% indicates the manager adds approximately 21.7% more value in down markets (relative to participation) than in up markets.

**Exam trap:** ALWAYS use geometric averages for capture ratios, not arithmetic. Arithmetic averages overstate both UC and DC, and the resulting CR can be misleading. The geometric method correctly accounts for compounding.

Links to: Section 18.9 (capture ratio 4-profile framework), Section 5.2 (risk-adjusted measures)

### 39.4 Drawdown Analysis — Full 14-Month Worked Example

**Setup:** Monthly fund returns over 14-month period showing peak-to-trough-to-recovery cycle.

**Results:**
- Maximum drawdown = **−16.26%** (cumulative peak-to-trough loss)
- Drawdown duration = **10 months** (from peak to full recovery)
  - Breakdown: decline phase + recovery phase = 10 months total
- Key insight: the DURATION matters as much as the DEPTH — a −16% drawdown lasting 10 months is MORE concerning for a client with near-term liquidity needs than a −25% drawdown lasting 3 months

**Asymmetry check:** After −16.26% loss, need +19.42% gain to recover [(1/0.8374) − 1]. This recovery requirement grows EXPONENTIALLY: −50% needs +100%, −75% needs +300%.

Links to: Section 18.9 (drawdown analysis), Section 5.2 (maximum drawdown)

### 39.5 JAM vs S&P Appraisal — Treynor/Sharpe Contradiction

**Setup:** Fund JAM evaluated against S&P 500.

**Results:**
- JAM Treynor ratio > S&P Treynor → JAM OUTPERFORMS on systematic-risk-adjusted basis
- JAM Sharpe ratio < S&P Sharpe → JAM UNDERPERFORMS on total-risk-adjusted basis

**Interpretation of the contradiction:**
When Treynor outperforms but Sharpe underperforms, the manager has **significant DIVERSIFIABLE (unsystematic) risk** that is NOT being compensated.
- Treynor uses only beta (systematic risk) in denominator → ignores stock-specific risk
- Sharpe uses total standard deviation → captures BOTH systematic AND unsystematic risk
- The gap between the two signals reveals the magnitude of uncompensated residual risk

**Decision rule:** If Treynor > benchmark but Sharpe < benchmark → manager's portfolio has too much unsystematic risk → either (a) the portfolio is too concentrated, (b) sector bets are too aggressive, or (c) the manager is taking idiosyncratic positions that aren't paying off.

**Exam application:** When asked "which appraisal measure is more appropriate," answer depends on context:
- If fund will be the investor's ONLY holding → use **Sharpe** (total risk matters)
- If fund is ONE COMPONENT of a diversified portfolio → use **Treynor** (only systematic risk matters, unsystematic diversifies away)

Links to: Section 18.9 (appraisal measures), Section 5.2 (Sharpe vs Treynor decision)

### 39.6 Risk Attribution — 3×2 Classification Matrix

**Risk attribution classifies approaches along two dimensions:**

| | Bottom-Up | Top-Down | Factor-Based |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Relative** (vs benchmark) | Security-level contributions to tracking error | Sector/asset class allocation risk vs benchmark | Factor exposure deviations from benchmark (active factor bets) |
| **Absolute** | Security-level contributions to total portfolio risk | Sector/asset class contributions to total risk | Factor contributions to total portfolio variance |

**Exam decision framework:**
- **Relative + Factor-based** = most common for equity managers with factor mandates → decompose tracking error into factor bets (size, value, momentum, quality) vs residual
- **Absolute + Bottom-up** = most common for concentrated portfolios → identify which positions drive total risk
- **Relative + Top-down** = most common for multi-asset or TAA mandates → which allocation tilts contribute to deviation from policy benchmark

Links to: Section 18 (performance attribution), Section 11.4 (factor-based allocation)

### 39.7 Benchmark Misspecification — Numerical Example

**Setup:** Manager benchmarked against Investor's Benchmark (broad market).

**P = M + S + A decomposition:**
- True active return (P − Normal Portfolio) = **−2%** (manager UNDERPERFORMS their true style benchmark)
- Misfit active return (Normal Portfolio − Investor Benchmark) = **+3%** (style effect — manager's style outperformed the broad market)
- Measured active return (P − Investor Benchmark) = −2% + 3% = **+1%** (appears positive!)

**Critical insight:** The investor sees +1% measured alpha and thinks the manager is adding value. In reality, the manager is DESTROYING 2% of value through poor security selection, but this is MASKED by a +3% style tailwind. If style reverses, the measured alpha will become deeply negative.

**Exam trap:** ALWAYS decompose into true active + misfit active. A positive measured alpha does NOT confirm skill — it may be entirely driven by style mismatch between the manager's normal portfolio and the investor's chosen benchmark.

Links to: Section 18 (P = M + S + A framework), Section 21.5 (benchmark quality criteria)
