## Section 40: Review Workshop — Supplemental Decision Rules & New Content

*Source: CFA 2026 L3 Review Workshop Questions (396pp, ALL pages read). The vast majority of RWQ content overlaps with existing KB Sections 1-39. This section captures ONLY genuinely new material not covered elsewhere.*

### 40.1 Wealth Management Industry Ethics — Retrocession, PFOF & Churning

**Retrocession:** Payment from a product provider (fund manager, structured product issuer) back to the wealth manager/distributor for recommending their product. Creates a direct conflict of interest: the WM is financially incentivized to recommend products that pay higher retrocessions, NOT products that best serve the client.

**Decision Rule:** Retrocessions are a CONFLICT OF INTEREST requiring disclosure under Standard VI(A). In some jurisdictions (EU under MiFID II), retrocessions are banned entirely for independent advisers. When evaluating whether a WM recommendation is conflicted, check: (1) does the WM receive any payment from the product issuer? (2) was the product selected BECAUSE of that payment? (3) was the retrocession disclosed to the client?

**Payment for Order Flow (PFOF):** Practice where a broker receives compensation from a market maker for routing client orders to that market maker, rather than seeking best execution. The market maker profits from the bid-ask spread; the broker gets paid; the client potentially gets worse execution.

**Exam Application:** PFOF creates tension with Standard III(A) (duty of loyalty — best execution). If a broker routes orders to maximize PFOF rather than minimize client execution costs → violation. The key question: does the PFOF arrangement compromise best execution?

**Churning Warning Signs:**
- Excessive trading frequency relative to account objectives
- High portfolio turnover with no clear investment rationale
- Revenue generation for the WM disproportionate to account size
- Pattern of buying and quickly selling same or similar securities
- Client account underperforms despite high activity

**Decision Rule:** Churning = Standard III(A) violation (loyalty/prudence/care) + Standard III(C) violation (suitability — trades inconsistent with objectives). Distinguishing churning from active management: active management has a documented investment thesis for each trade; churning generates commissions without serving client goals.

**Execution-Only Transactions:** Trades where the WM acts solely as an order-taker with NO advice component. Client directs the specific trade; WM simply executes. Lower duty of care (no suitability obligation for the specific trade), but Standard III(A) (loyalty) still applies. WM must still seek best execution even for client-directed trades.

Links to: Section 23 (Standards I-VII), Section 28 (Trading Costs & Electronic Markets)

### 40.2 AML Case Studies — Expanded Reference

**Historical AML/Financial Crime Cases (exam context):**

| Case | Year | Key Facts | Lesson |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Riggs Bank** | 2004 | Failed to report suspicious transactions from Augusto Pinochet and Equatorial Guinea's president. $25M fine, forced sale. | Banks must apply enhanced due diligence to politically exposed persons (PEPs). Correspondent banking relationships require scrutiny. |
| **1MDB (1Malaysia Development Berhad)** | 2015-2020 | $4.5B embezzled from Malaysian sovereign wealth fund. Funds laundered through shell companies, luxury real estate, art, film financing (Wolf of Wall Street). Goldman Sachs paid $2.9B settlement. | SWFs require independent governance and oversight. Complex multi-jurisdictional structures enable layering. Financial institutions must scrutinize source of funds even from sovereign entities. |
| **Danske Bank** | 2017-18 | €200B in suspicious transactions flowed through Estonian branch. Non-resident accounts used for Russian money laundering. | Branches in higher-risk jurisdictions need proportionate controls. Volume of transactions should trigger automated monitoring. |
| **Panama Papers** | 2016 | 11.5M leaked documents from Mossack Fonseca. Exposed offshore holdings of politicians, celebrities, athletes globally. | Offshore structures are not inherently illegal but require proper disclosure and tax reporting. Shell companies used to obscure beneficial ownership. |
| **Paradise Papers** | 2017 | 13.4M documents from Appleby (Bermuda law firm). Exposed offshore investments of corporations and individuals. | Even legitimate tax planning structures can carry reputational risk when exposed. |
| **Pandora Papers** | 2021 | 11.9M documents exposing hidden wealth and tax avoidance by world leaders and billionaires. Largest leak in history. | Beneficial ownership registries increasingly mandated globally. Transparency trend is accelerating. |

**Decision Rule for Ethics Exam:** When a case describes suspicious transaction patterns → identify which AML obligation is being violated → link to Standard I(A) (Knowledge of the Law — must comply with AML regulations) and Standard I(D) (Misconduct — facilitating illegal activity). A WM who ignores red flags is NOT just passively failing — they are actively enabling financial crime through willful blindness.

Links to: Section 23.1 (Standard I — Professionalism), Section 16 (Ethical Decision Frameworks)

### 40.3 Golden Visa Programs & Citizenship-by-Investment

**Golden Visa:** Residence permit (and eventual citizenship path) granted in exchange for substantial investment in the host country. Common programs: Portugal (€500K+ real estate or fund investment), Spain (€500K+ real estate), Greece (€250K+ real estate), Malta (€150K+ combined with government bond and donation), Caribbean nations (USD100K-250K donation or real estate).

**Wealth Planning Implications:**
1. **Tax residency shift:** Moving tax domicile can change applicable tax regime (from worldwide taxation to territorial, or vice versa)
2. **Estate planning:** New jurisdiction's inheritance laws may apply (forced heirship, community property, different exemptions)
3. **Double taxation:** Must evaluate bilateral tax treaties between old and new residence
4. **Citizenship types involved:** Investment route = one of the 5 methods (cross-ref Section 37, line on citizenship types: birth, extended descent, naturalization, marriage, investment)
5. **Substance requirements:** Many programs now require minimum physical presence days to maintain tax benefits

**Decision Rule:** Golden Visa planning intersects with: (a) tax regime analysis (Section 26.4), (b) cross-border estate planning (Section 37.33-37.36), (c) residency vs domicile distinction (Section 10), (d) double taxation treaties (credit/deduction/exemption methods). A client considering Golden Visa needs comprehensive analysis across ALL these dimensions simultaneously.

Links to: Section 26.4 (taxation formulas), Section 37.33 (cross-border estate planning), Section 10 (Private Wealth)

### 40.4 Earnout Clauses in Business Sales

**Earnout:** A contractual provision in a business sale where part of the purchase price is contingent on the business achieving specified financial targets post-sale. Bridges the valuation gap between buyer's and seller's expectations.

**Structure:**
- **Fixed portion:** Paid at closing (provides immediate liquidity)
- **Contingent portion:** Paid over 1-5 years if performance milestones are met (revenue, EBITDA, customer retention, etc.)
- **Typical split:** 60-80% at close, 20-40% contingent

**Wealth Planning Decision Rules:**
1. **Liquidity planning:** Only the FIXED portion is guaranteed → financial plan should be conservative, based on fixed portion alone
2. **Tax timing:** Earnout payments may be taxed as capital gains OR ordinary income depending on jurisdiction and structure
3. **Reinvestment risk:** If earnout is large % of total consideration → seller has significant exposure to buyer's management of the business post-sale
4. **Negotiation leverage:** Sellers prefer all cash at close; buyers prefer higher earnout percentage → earnout percentage signals buyer's confidence in the business

**Cross-Reference:** Leveraged recapitalization (Section 37, line 8312) + management buyout often include earnout provisions to align incentives between departing owner and incoming management/PE sponsor.

Links to: Section 37 (Private Wealth Masterclass — concentrated positions), Section 10 (Private Wealth Deep Dive)

### 40.5 RWQ Cross-PDF Decision Rule Reinforcements

The Review Workshop Questions (396 pages across 6 sections: Asset Allocation, Portfolio Construction, Portfolio Management, Derivatives, Ethics, Private Wealth) confirmed and reinforced the following HIGH-FREQUENCY exam patterns:

**Pattern 1 — Brinson Attribution Always Decomposes Three Ways:**
- Allocation effect = (w_fund − w_bench) × R_bench_sector
- Selection effect = w_bench × (R_fund_sector − R_bench_sector)
- Interaction effect = (w_fund − w_bench) × (R_fund_sector − R_bench_sector)
- **Exam trap:** Students forget the interaction term or confuse which weight applies to which effect

**Pattern 2 — Double Taxation Credit vs Deduction vs Exemption:**
- **Credit method:** Tax = max(home rate, source rate) × income. Most common. Effective rate = higher of the two rates.
- **Deduction method:** Home tax = home rate × (income − source tax paid). Source tax treated as expense. Total tax > credit method. Additional tax from deduction = (1 − source rate) × home rate.
- **Exemption method:** Home country gives up taxing rights on foreign-source income. Only source country taxes. Effective rate = source rate only.
- **Exam decision rule:** Credit method ALWAYS produces effective rate = max(home, source). Deduction method ALWAYS produces higher total tax than credit method. Exemption method ALWAYS produces effective rate = source rate.

**Pattern 3 — RV Gift vs Bequest (Receiver-Pays vs Donor-Pays Gift Tax):**
- **Receiver-pays:** RV = [1 + r_g(1 − t_ig)]^n × (1 − T_g) / {[1 + r_e(1 − t_ie)]^n × (1 − T_e)}
- If RV > 1 → gift is better than bequest
- If RV < 1 → bequest is better than gift
- **Donor-pays:** Same formula but gift tax reduces amount available for investment: effective gift = amount × (1 − T_g) invested for n years
- **Exam trap:** Students confuse who pays the gift tax. If RECEIVER pays → tax reduces terminal value. If DONOR pays → tax reduces initial investment.

**Pattern 4 — Charitable Gift RV Formula:**
- RV = [(1 + r_g)^n + T_oi × (1 + r_e(1 − t_ie))^n × (1 − T_e)] / [(1 + r_e(1 − t_ie))^n × (1 − T_e)]
- Where T_oi = tax savings from charitable deduction (ordinary income tax rate × deduction percentage)
- If RV > 1 → charitable gift NOW is better than charitable bequest
- **Key insight:** The tax deduction benefit of giving now (T_oi term) is what often makes lifetime giving superior

**Pattern 5 — Variance Swap Mid-Life Valuation (Worked Example Reinforcement):**
- VarSwap_t = VarNotional × PV_t(T) × {(t/T) × [RealVol(0,t)]² + [(T−t)/T] × [ImpliedVol(t,T)]² − Strike²}
- The "blended" term is TIME-WEIGHTED: realized vol applies to elapsed fraction, implied vol applies to remaining fraction
- If blended variance > strike² → long position has positive value
- If blended variance < strike² → long position has negative value (short position profits)
- **Exam trap:** Do NOT square the strike after blending — the strike is ALREADY expressed as variance (strike²) in the formula. The comparison is blended VARIANCE vs strike VARIANCE, not blended vol vs strike vol.

Links to: Section 19 (Derivatives — variance swaps), Section 37 (Private Wealth — taxation, estate planning), Section 18 (Performance Attribution — Brinson)

## SECTION 41: PRACTICAL SKILLS MODULES — ANALYST FRAMEWORKS & APPLIED METHODOLOGY

### Purpose
CFA Institute Practical Skills Modules (PSMs) provide applied, practitioner-oriented skills complementing the curriculum. This section synthesizes frameworks from L2 Analyst Skills (Jim Valentine / Analyst Solutions), L3 Managing Private Wealth Clients (MPWC), and L1 Python Fundamentals. The L2 content introduces 11+ proprietary frameworks for equity research that are entirely distinct from the main CFA curriculum. The L3 MPWC content applies private wealth concepts from Section 10/37 in a hands-on IPS construction and portfolio management context. L1 Python covers financial data retrieval and portfolio analytics in code.

### 41.1 GAMMA PI™ — The Seven Pillars of Analyst Excellence

The GAMMA PI framework (Source: Jim Valentine, CFA) defines the complete skill set of a successful equity research analyst. It serves as the overarching architecture within which all other PSM frameworks operate:

**G — Generate informed insights:** Identify and validate the 1-4 critical factors most likely to move a stock using HELP and EPIC frameworks. This is the foundation — without differentiated insights, all downstream work (forecasting, recommendations, communication) is worthless.

**A — Accurately forecast:** Translate insights into financial forecasts more accurate than consensus. Use reference class forecasting (outside view first, then adjust with inside view). The "forecasting sweet spot" is the zone where enough information exists to form a view but consensus hasn't yet fully incorporated it.

**M — Make accurate stock recommendations:** Apply the TIER system to convert forecasts into actionable, risk-adjusted stock calls with realistic price targets, identified catalysts, and defined entry/exit points.

**M — Motivate others to act:** Use ENTER quality framework and ADViCE delivery framework to communicate recommendations so that portfolio managers, clients, or investment committees actually take action.

**A — Acquire buy-side votes (sell-side specific):** For sell-side analysts, the ultimate measure of success is client votes in broker reviews, which determine compensation and firm revenue.

**P — Productivity:** Maximize time spent on alpha-generating research activities vs. reactive, low-value tasks. Play offense (proactive research scheduling) rather than defense (responding to inbox).

**I — Individual characteristics:** Personal traits that enable all of the above — intellectual curiosity, ethical grounding, resilience, adaptability, and continuous improvement mindset.

**Decision Rule:** When allocating research time, always ask: "Which of the seven GAMMA PI areas will this activity improve?" If the answer is unclear, the activity is likely low-value.

Links to: Section 3 (Valuation Framework Hierarchy), Section 4 (Behavioral Finance), Section 13 (Equity Portfolio Management)

### 41.2 EPIC™ — Critical Factor Identification Funnel

EPIC is the primary screening framework for determining whether a factor qualifies as a "critical factor" — one that can drive an out-of-consensus stock call. A factor must pass ALL FOUR criteria to be classified as critical:

**E — Exceeds materiality threshold:** The factor's impact on EPS/CFPS must exceed the analyst's materiality threshold (typically 3-5% of stock price). Use the Magic Number computation (see 41.3) to quantify this.

**P — Probably going to occur:** There must be a high probability the factor will materialize during the analyst's investment time horizon. Speculative or distant-future factors fail this test even if material.

**I — I'm good at forecasting this factor:** The analyst must have genuine skill and informational advantage in forecasting this specific factor and its catalyst. Self-awareness about forecasting limitations is essential.

**C — Consensus is poor at forecasting this factor:** The consensus must be likely to get this factor wrong — either mispricing the magnitude, timing, or direction. If consensus already accurately forecasts the factor, there is no alpha opportunity regardless of how material it is.

**EPIC Scoring Matrix:** Rate each potential factor 1-5 on all four criteria. Sum the scores. Factors scoring 15+ (out of 20) are likely critical factors worth deep research. Factors scoring below 10 should receive minimal research time.

**Non-Critical Factors (cannot typically base stock calls on):**
- Changes in most macro-economic factors (GDP, unemployment, Fed policy)
- Changes in most commodity prices
- Most market-moving events (elections, geopolitical shocks)
- Exception: If you are well-versed on why a factor is "not critical," you may exploit short-term trades when the market overreacts by treating it as critical

**Information Flow → Critical Factor Funnel:** Information flow → many non-factors → few potential factors → (EPIC filter) → 1-4 critical factors + potential future factors → compare "My View" vs "Consensus View" → if divergent = alpha opportunity

Links to: Section 3 (Valuation), Section 41.3 (Magic Number), Section 41.4 (HELP)

### 41.3 Magic Number™ — Materiality Threshold Computation

The Magic Number quantifies the minimum earnings impact (in currency) required for a factor to be considered material to a stock's price. This is the quantitative backbone of the "E" in EPIC.

**Formula:**
Magic Number = (EPS × Materiality Threshold%) × Shares Outstanding / (1 − Tax Rate)

Where:
- EPS = current or NTM consensus earnings per share
- Materiality Threshold% = analyst-defined stock price move threshold (typically 3-5%)
- Shares Outstanding = diluted share count
- Tax Rate = effective corporate tax rate

**Impact Zone Thresholds** — Once you have the Magic Number, determine how large a change in key business drivers is required to reach it:
- **Volume Impact Zone** = Magic Number / EBIT Margin (how much incremental volume is needed)
- **Pricing Impact Zone** = Magic Number / Revenue (how much pricing change is needed)
- **Expense Impact Zone** = Magic Number (direct — a cost change flows through dollar-for-dollar pre-tax)

**Decision Rule:** If a factor's estimated impact on pre-tax income is less than the Magic Number, it fails the "E" criterion of EPIC and should NOT be a primary driver of a stock call, regardless of how interesting or novel it appears.

Links to: Section 3 (Valuation Framework), Section 41.2 (EPIC)

### 41.4 HELP™ — Four-Step Factor Discovery Process

HELP is the systematic process for identifying potential critical factors. It structures the research workflow from initial data gathering through prioritization:

**H — Research Historical data & documents:**
- Identify periods when the stock substantially out- or under-performed its peers and the sector out- or under-performed a broad index (go back 10-30 years if data available)
- Identify the cause/catalyst using numerical data — company operating/financial data, industry/competitor data, macro data
- Data requirements: stock closing price (split-adjusted, NOT dividend-reinvested), rolling NTM consensus EPS/sales/CFPS, key company-specific metrics (quarterly sales, margins, volume, pricing)
- Sector-level: sector stock index, sector demand/supply data, broad index for comparison
- Macro-level: display rate-of-change rather than raw numbers; validate cyclical trends over multiple cycles not just the most recent
- Clean the data: smooth artificial spikes (spin-offs, special items), use E/P ratio when earnings near zero, use real (inflation-adjusted) data for macro comparisons

**E — Explore Emerging data & documents:**
- Use "read-only" sources: conference call transcripts (Q&A sections best), regulatory filings (MD&A sections best), notes from colleagues previously covering the stock, sell-side reports (upgrades/downgrades are most useful), news stories by specialized journalists, industry trade blogs/journals
- Primary goals: (1) Find any newly emerging factors, (2) Assess all potential factors against EPIC criteria
- Seek anomalies: current valuation vs. historical levels, anomalies within the full range of consensus estimates

**L — Validate/refute assumptions with Live sources (use ASPIRE framework):**
- Best live sources for confirming/refuting historical factors: company's competitor (privately-held is best), consultant/expert/company retiree, customer or supplier
- Best live sources for assessing new/emerging factors: company's competitor, sell-side analyst (top 3 in sector), industry journalist/blogger, buy-side PMs/analysts/traders
- Best live sources for assessing market psychology: buy-side analyst or PM, sell-side salesperson/trader

**P — Prioritize factors using EPIC framework:**
- Apply the four EPIC criteria to all potential factors
- Narrow to 1-4 critical factors per stock
- Document and continuously monitor

Links to: Section 41.2 (EPIC), Section 41.5 (ASPIRE), Section 41.8 (Information Sources)

### 41.5 ASPIRE™ — Generating Sustainable Sources of Insight

ASPIRE is a six-step process for extracting differentiated insights from live information sources. It is the detailed methodology for executing the "L" step of HELP:

**A — Assumptions:** Define the critical factor assumptions you need to validate or refute before approaching any source. Know exactly what you're seeking before you make contact.

**S — Sources of insight:** Identify the best sources for the specific information you need (see Information Sources Pros/Cons matrix in 41.8). Prioritize sources that are close to the issue, willing to speak, and unlikely to have already shared insights with your competitors.

**P — Prepare to approach & interview:** Research the source's background (LinkedIn, Google, Bloomberg, social media). Determine "What's in it for them?" (WIIFT). Prepare questions using the ICE framework (see 41.6). Self-assess: Will you be credible? Are your communication skills adequate? Are you empathetic and adaptable?

**I — Introduce & interview:** Build rapport first — let them do 95% of the talking in the first conversation. Show reverence, mention referrals, listen attentively without interrupting. Use the PRACTICE framework (see 41.7) to maximize influence and information extraction.

**R — Respond with follow-up:** Satisfy direct requests promptly (reciprocity principle). Offer to connect them with industry contacts. Periodically reach out to offer help, not just when you need information.

**E — Evaluate benefit:** Assess whether the source provided actionable insight. Determine if the relationship should be cultivated for ongoing insight generation. High-value sources are those who consistently provide forward-looking, non-consensus information.

Links to: Section 41.6 (ICE), Section 41.7 (PRACTICE), Section 41.4 (HELP)

### 41.6 ICE™ — Questioning Framework for Obtaining Insights

ICE structures how to ask questions during interviews with information sources:

**I — Identify parameters:** Use open-ended questions to map the landscape. "What are the key factors driving [X]?" "How has [Y] changed over the past 12 months?" Avoid yes/no questions early — they limit the information flow.

**C — Calm their concerns:** Address any reasons the source might be hesitant to share information. Assure confidentiality. Explain how the information will be used. Demonstrate that you already have substantial knowledge (people share more with informed questioners than uninformed ones).

**E — Entice thorough response:** Use techniques that encourage detailed answers: silence (let them fill the void), reflective questions ("So what you're saying is..."), hypothetical framing ("If [X] were to happen, how would that affect..."), and progressive specificity (broad → narrow questioning).

Links to: Section 41.5 (ASPIRE), Section 41.7 (PRACTICE)

### 41.7 PRACTICE™ — Framework for Influencing Others

PRACTICE structures how to influence information sources, clients, portfolio managers, and investment committees. It applies across multiple contexts — extracting insights, winning buy-side votes, and getting recommendations acted upon:

**P — Prepare to influence:** Research WIIFT (What's In It For Them?). Search the person's background. Determine how to conform to "their ways." Self-assess your credibility, communication skills, empathy, and adaptability.

**R — Rapport building:** Be the first to say hello. Ask genuine questions about them. Show reverence by explaining why you reached out. Listen attentively — at all costs, do not interrupt. Establish common ground (agree with points they make). Be confident but not arrogant. Offer to provide something to meet their needs (reciprocity).

**A — Ask about needs (WIIFT):** If you haven't already learned WIIFT during preparation, start the conversation by understanding their motivations and what they need from you.

**C — Conform:** Avoid passing judgment if the source says something you disagree with. Use appropriate jargon (ROIC, PEG ratio) to show competence. Match their style (dress, communication pace, formality). Show interest in their profession, background, and regional context.

**T — Trustworthy (build credibility):** Share your insights in advance of meetings when permitted. Follow through on all commitments. Take notes and review afterward. Proactively explain confidentiality practices. Treat everyone with respect (including assistants and support staff).

**I — Ignore distractions:** Be fully present — turn off phones, avoid email. For in-person meetings, choose conducive locations. Ignore internal distractions: inattention, self-absorption, mental preparation for your next question, misinterpretation, and personal biases.

**C — Communicate persuasively:** Use non-threatening tone, timing, pace, and volume. Maintain open body language (eye contact, lean forward, no crossed arms, no scanning phone). Leverage Cialdini's six weapons of influence: reciprocation, commitment/consistency, social proof, liking, authority, and scarcity.

**E — Ensure needs are met:** Satisfy reasonable requests promptly. Offer proprietary event invitations (conference calls with experts). Share your research when permitted. Contact the source periodically to offer help, not only when you need information.

Links to: Section 41.5 (ASPIRE), Section 41.6 (ICE), Section 4 (Behavioral Finance)

### 41.8 Information Sources — Pros, Cons, and Best Use Cases

**Individual Conversation Sources (ranked by best use case):**

| Source | Best For | Key Limitation |
|--------|----------|----------------|
| Buy-side analyst/PM | Assessing market psychology, gauging investor expectations | May not represent broader investor base |
| Company's competitor (privately-held best) | Confirming/refuting historical factors, assessing emerging factors | May not know other company's specific factors |
| Company executives / IR | Forthcoming about opportunistic factors | Usually downplay or ignore negative factors |
| Conference speaker / academic | Historical factor validation, industry context | May not understand company/stock implications |
| Consultant / expert / retiree | Very close to the issue, deep domain knowledge | Can be difficult to find |
| Customer or supplier | First-hand knowledge of value proposition | May not represent larger base |
| Government officials | Closest to regulatory/legislative changes | Often won't speak or provide forecast |
| Industry association | Complex industry issues understanding | May be biased toward sector, limited stock-level insight |
| Industry journalist/blogger | Direction to other sources, complex issue understanding | May not understand stock implications |
| Sell-side analyst (top 3 in sector) | Only place to gauge consensus, proprietary research | Subjectivity, potential low-quality output |
| Sell-side salesperson (with stock knowledge) | Close to many buy-side investors | Generalists may lack deep single-stock understanding |
| Trader (with stock knowledge) | Short-term psychology of specific stock | May not understand long-term company issues |

**Read-Only Sources:**
Company-released information (free but positively biased), data services (granular but expensive/not proprietary), economic data (reliable/objective but not company-specific), financial media (low cost/somewhat objective but limited stock implications), forecasting services (best at trend forecasting but expensive/not always timely), industry journals (low cost/somewhat objective but potentially sector-biased), proprietary surveys (proprietary output but expensive/time-consuming), sell-side reports (easy access but potentially rating-biased), third-party research firms (answers specific questions and you own data but expensive/time-consuming)

Links to: Section 41.5 (ASPIRE), Section 41.7 (PRACTICE)

### 41.9 TIER™ — Stock Recommendation System

TIER is the comprehensive system for making, monitoring, and managing stock recommendations:

**Step 1 — Target Realistic Prices (uses SHARE sub-framework):**
- **S — Select valuation method:** Identify methods currently used for the stock/sector. Consider whether catalysts could cause a method change. Use the Valuation Method Selection Flowchart (see 41.10).
- **H — Historical and current valuation:** Review current valuation vs. its past; compare with similar stocks. Investigate anomalies — understand why current market psychology may differ from history.
- **A — Adjust multiple for the future:** Forecast a future valuation multiple by assessing macro/company-specific direction and comparing with similar-prospect stocks.
- **R — Range of multiples and price targets:** Apply multiples to financial forecast scenarios (upside/downside/base) to derive a range of price targets. Set exit thresholds.
- **E — Evaluate as circumstances change:** Update price target when: new forward forecast period rolls in, justifiable cause to revise the multiple (use sparingly), or justifiable cause to revise the method (extremely rare).

**Philosophical guardrails for Step 1:**
- Don't waste time developing precision where it's not required (e.g., forecasting immaterial factors, computing risk-free rate to tenth decimal)
- Avoid unproven or overly simplistic valuation methods
- Avoid "incrementalism" — raising price targets in small increments rather than making substantial, well-justified changes

**Step 2 — Identify and Forecast Catalysts:**
- Identify potential catalysts that would cause the market to accept your out-of-consensus thesis
- Specifically identify WHERE the catalyst will appear in financial statements (pricing, volume, cost, margins, FCF, returns, EPS growth)
- Narrow to catalysts meeting ALL criteria: pertains to critical factor, material enough to move stock, likely within investment time horizon, not currently appreciated by market, forecastable with some certainty
- Proactively calendar expected catalyst dates: earnings releases, analyst meetings, annual pricing/volume guidance, regulatory deadlines, competitor announcements, new product launches, interim sales data, non-deal investor roadshows

**Step 3 — Ensure Ideal Entry Point:**
- Avoid costly psychological shortcuts (see 41.11 Bias Framework)
- Ensure call is differentiated using FaVeS (see 41.13)
- Avoid Pollyannaish/hopeful thinking biases
- Weigh risk as well as return: predictability of earnings/CF forecasts, conviction in catalyst, reliability of management, timing of catalyst
- Ensure no imminent danger: current quarter consensus vs your long-term view, investor class rotation risk, economic cycle peak risk, management lock-up expiration
- Document thesis and stop-loss price BEFORE making recommendation
- Sleep on it — avoid rash emotion-driven changes
- Monitor trading data: investor type changes, short interest, insider buying/selling, stock vs CDS movement
- Know consensus quality: how many analysts, estimate dispersion, stale estimates, "whisper" numbers

**Philosophical guardrails for Step 3:**
- Don't force a rating where one doesn't exist — not every stock in your universe must be a strong call
- Avoid overly complex recommendations
- Don't be contrarian just to be contrarian
- Be willing to reverse your rating if the thesis breaks
- Avoid recommendation changes primarily based on recent management meetings (especially if company-initiated)
- Increase the urge to sell when everyone loves a sector; buy when no one wants to own it
- When bad news no longer makes stocks go down (or good news stops making them go up), market psychology may be shifting
- When you're long a stock, resist panic from short-sellers' negative news flow
- Don't wait for "greater clarity" when a stock drops — the lack of clarity creates the opportunity
- Research management's skin in the game; be leery of teams more focused on stock price than business

**Step 4 — Review Performance and Thesis:**
- Continuously monitor whether your thesis is playing out
- Reverse if thesis breaks — "If I switched firms tomorrow, would my initial stock recommendations match my current ones? If not, understand why."

Links to: Section 3 (Valuation Framework), Section 41.2 (EPIC), Section 41.10 (Valuation Selection), Section 41.11 (Psychological Biases), Section 41.13 (FaVeS)

### 41.10 Valuation Method Selection Flowchart & Comparison

**Decision Flowchart (from Analyst Solutions):**
1. Are you evaluated for generating RELATIVE or ABSOLUTE returns?
   - If Absolute → Can the company's assets and debt be reliably valued using public market pricing (e.g., financial/resource companies)? → Yes: **Price-to-Book** → No: Is the company likely to generate after-tax earnings? → Yes: **P/E** → No: Can capex and payout ratio be accurately forecast for multiple periods? → Yes: **DCF** → No: STOP (red)
   - If Relative → Do the company's peers have similar growth, betas, and capital structures? → Yes: proceed below → No: Search for companies with similar growth, beta, and capital structures in other sectors
2. Is the company likely to generate EBITDA during the forecast period? → No: **EV/Sales** → Yes: **EV/EBITDA**
3. Does the company pay a dividend consistently? → Yes: Can the company's EPS growth be accurately forecast over multiple periods? → Yes: **PEG** → No: Can maintenance capex be accurately forecast? → Yes: **P/FCF** → No: STOP (red) → No: **Dividend Yield**

**Benefits and Limitations Comparison (9 Methods):**

| Method | Key Benefit | Key Limitation |
|--------|------------|----------------|
| P/E | Most widely understood; can analyze historical relative valuation | Management can manipulate earnings; doesn't capture cash flow |
| PEG | Incorporates multi-period growth; cross-sector comparison possible | No standard growth computation method; unreliable long-term growth estimates |
| P/FCF | Incorporates free cash flow (best value measure) | Methodology varies (maintenance vs. growth capex); single-period only |
| EV/Sales | Useful when no earnings or cash flow exist | Sales ≠ free cash flow; not a true measure of value |
| P/B | Good proxy for asset-heavy industries (financial, resource) | Book value rarely equals market value; subjective accounting |
| EV/EBITDA | Compares companies with different capital structures; useful with no pre-tax income | EBITDA ≠ free cash flow or earnings |
| Dividend Yield | Measures floor when stocks collapse | Dividends ≠ FCF; hard to forecast dividend cuts |
| DCF | Best measure of intrinsic value; focuses on ROIC and FCF over life of enterprise | Highly sensitive to minor input changes; time-consuming; complex models prone to errors |
| Residual Income | Same DCF benefits; better for identifying overheated/oversold markets | Same DCF limitations plus complexity |

**Weighted comparison (by ability to measure long-term FCF on regular basis):**
- P/FCF and DCF score highest overall
- P/E and PEG score highest for simplicity and speed
- EV/EBITDA scores highest for cross-capital-structure comparison
- P/B and EV/Sales are niche methods for specific situations

Links to: Section 3 (Valuation Framework Hierarchy), Section 41.9 (TIER Step 1)

### 41.11 Psychological Biases in Equity Research — Identification & Countermeasures

**Category 1 — Fear of Failure:**

| Bias | Manifestation | Countermeasure |
|------|---------------|----------------|
| Sunk Cost Fallacy | Recommending names in a sector because of time invested, when only 1-2 will outperform | Periodically ask "What will I do if I discover nothing new?" to cap time on any effort. Ask "If I switched firms, would my current recs match?" |
| Loss Aversion | Finding new rationales to keep recommending an underperforming stock | Document thesis + price target specifics BEFORE recommending. If thesis fails, strongly consider reversing. |
| Anxiety | Changing views weekly/monthly based on stock price movement rather than fundamentals | Don't let stock price alter your fundamental view. Conduct scenario analysis around worst-case. Think about recommendation changes overnight. |
| Snakebite Effect | Dismissing a stock due to its bad past performance | Research whether problems were within/outside management control. The company's weakness may be the upside catalyst if you can identify the change agent. |

**Category 2 — Costly Psychological Shortcuts:**

| Bias | Manifestation | Countermeasure |
|------|---------------|----------------|
| Familiarity / Availability | Preferring familiar stocks over better alternatives | Ensure no "forgotten" stocks in assigned universe. Set aside time for even the most complicated names. |
| Recency Bias | Over-weighting recent information (e.g., bullish after recent management meeting) | Don't ignore past critical factors — they often recur. Avoid recs primarily stimulated by recent company-initiated contact. |
| Rules of Thumb / Heuristics | "Buy below 12x P/E, sell above 15x" without validation | Validate every heuristic with historical analysis. If a simple rule always worked, computers would have arbitraged it. |

**Category 3 — Following the Herd (Social Psychology):**

| Bias | Manifestation | Countermeasure |
|------|---------------|----------------|
| Overreaction | Downgrading on bad news when research suggests concern is overblown | Document thesis and exit thresholds in advance. Update scenario analysis. Resist changing rec mid-day or after earnings. "Sleep on it." |
| Momentum Bias | Raising targets to justify rallied stock using unrealistic multiples/forecasts | Research historical growth rates and multiples. Ask "Who's on the other side of this trade?" Consider where you are on the greed-vs-fear spectrum. |

**Category 4 — Pollyannaish or Hopeful Thinking:**

| Bias | Manifestation | Countermeasure |
|------|---------------|----------------|
| Confirmation Bias | Seeking supportive info while ignoring contradictory evidence | Wait until all research is done before deciding the rating. Build upside AND downside scenarios simultaneously. Know the opposing view. |
| Over-confidence | Letting down guard on risk assessment after a few wins | No professional investor is right 100% of the time. Understand the other side before recommending. Have a trusted colleague pressure-test your thesis. |
| Self-Attribution | Taking credit for wins, blaming others for losses | Review original thesis documents after big wins — did your thesis actually play out or was it another factor? Ask "Did anyone else see this coming?" |
| Optimism Bias | Modeling 12% EPS CAGR when historical rate is 8% | Research historical growth rates. If your target relies on above-historical trends, have a sound reason. Spend as much time on risk catalysts as upside catalysts. |
| Falling in Love with a Stock | Unable to objectively analyze a franchise name you're attached to | Periodically review universe objectively: "Am I less likely to change this recommendation due to relationship concerns?" Hide company names in comp tables — do the numbers alone support the view? |

Links to: Section 4 (Behavioral Finance Framework), Section 41.9 (TIER Step 3)

### 41.12 ENTER™ — Content Quality Framework

ENTER ensures that research output has genuine value before communicating it. Every stock call or research report should pass all five criteria:

**E — Expectational:** Does your forecast of financial results differ from consensus? If not, your content may be informative but will not generate alpha.

**N — Novel:** Is your insight new information or a new interpretation of existing information? Restating what's already known doesn't add value.

**T — Thorough:** Have you done comprehensive research — historical analysis, emerging data review, live source validation? A superficial insight that hasn't been pressure-tested risks embarrassment and loss of credibility.

**E — Examinable:** Can your thesis be tested against observable outcomes? "The stock will go up because management is good" is not examinable. "EPS will beat by $0.15 due to pricing power from new contract wins, visible in Q3 revenue" is examinable.

**R — Revealing:** Does your content reveal something the market hasn't fully appreciated? This is the ultimate test — even expectational, novel, thorough, and examinable content fails if the market already implicitly knows the insight.

**Decision Rule:** If your research doesn't meet ALL FIVE criteria, it is not ready for communication. Go back and fill the gaps before publishing.

Links to: Section 41.13 (ADViCE), Section 41.9 (TIER)

### 41.13 ADViCE™ — Message Delivery Impact Framework + FaVeS™ Out-of-Consensus Check

**ADViCE structures HOW you communicate (after ENTER validates WHAT you communicate):**

**A — Aware:** Demonstrate awareness of the counter-arguments and risks. Acknowledge where you could be wrong. Quantify upside/downside/base scenarios around your 1-4 critical factors. Explain why the market doesn't currently hold your view and what catalyst will change this.

**D — Differentiated:** Show exactly how your view differs from consensus. This means differentiating on at least one element of FaVeS (see below). If your view isn't differentiated, ask why your communication will help others generate alpha.

**V — Validated:** Demonstrate that you've confirmed your thesis with more than one independent, informed source (not relying solely on company management). Multi-source validation dramatically increases credibility.

**C — Conclusion-oriented:** Lead with the conclusion — upgrade/downgrade, becoming more/less positive, or returning with support for current recommendation. If none of the above, ask how your communication will generate alpha.

**E — Easy to consume:** Be concise. Most PMs won't read past page 2 unless it's an in-depth report on a very important topic. Anything past page 2 should be considered appendix. Use exhibits that tell the story (one good exhibit replaces 3-4 paragraphs). Don't overuse jargon.

**FaVeS™ — Out-of-Consensus Check (used within TIER Step 3):**
Your stock recommendation must be differentiated from consensus in at least ONE of these three dimensions:
- **Fa — Forecast:** Your forecast of financial results (EPS, cash flow) differs from consensus
- **Ve — Valuation:** Your valuation multiple or methodology differs from the market's current approach
- **S — Sentiment:** Your reading of market sentiment toward the stock differs (e.g., you believe sentiment is about to shift due to an impending catalyst that will change the consensus multiple/method)

If you are not out-of-consensus on at least one FaVeS dimension, you do NOT have a differentiated stock call.

Links to: Section 41.12 (ENTER), Section 41.9 (TIER)

### 41.14 10 Questions Before Communicating a Stock Call

This checklist integrates ENTER and ADViCE into a pre-communication quality check:

1. Does your communication begin with an upgrade/downgrade, becoming more/less positive, or returning with support? If not, how will it generate alpha? (Conclusion-oriented)
2. How much does your price target differ from current stock price? If immaterial, what alpha value does this communication provide? (Conclusion-oriented)
3. How do the two elements of your price target differ from consensus — superior forecast vs. consensus? AND/OR superior future valuation multiple/method vs. current? (Differentiated)
4. If you have a superior forecast, which critical factor(s) is consensus wrong about? (Differentiated)
5. If you have a superior valuation multiple/method, why is the market likely to change its view? (Differentiated)
6. How have you validated your superior forecast and/or multiple with more than one independent, informed source? (Validated)
7. Why does the market NOT currently hold your view? What catalyst will cause the market to adopt it, and when? (Aware)
8. Have you quantified upside, downside, and base-case scenarios for assumptions where you differ from consensus, along with your conviction level? (Aware)
9. Have you identified where you could be wrong? (Aware)
10. Is your communication easy to digest — concise, not jargon-heavy, conveying the above points quickly? (Easy-to-consume)

Links to: Section 41.12 (ENTER), Section 41.13 (ADViCE)

### 41.15 Research Report & Communication Best Practices

**Writing Research Reports:**
- Showcase key points in the first two pages — most PMs stop reading there
- Write titles that include your conclusion and the company name/ticker
- Use page 1 bullet points to keep the message succinct
- Include at least one supporting fact per paragraph (if no data, ask "What purpose does it serve?")
- Cite all sources critical to the thesis (keep proprietary sources anonymous)
- Use exhibits to tell the story — always include: (1) how your estimate has changed vs consensus, (2) upside/downside/base scenarios with assumptions and implied price targets, (3) historical valuation trends if the thesis involves a method/multiple change
- Don't include confusing raw data tables unless key numbers are highlighted
- Include a financial model forecasting at least 2 years out (preferably 3-4)
- Use "Exhibit" as a unified label for all tables, charts, and graphs (not "Table" or "Graph" separately)
- Never attack people in reports — it's permanent and damages credibility

**Comp Table Best Practices (11 Guidelines):**
1. Automate data collection as much as possible (formulas pulling from data providers + your models)
2. Display BOTH your expectations AND consensus multiples
3. Include both current year and next year forecast columns (possibly year after if critical)
4. Minimum columns per company: financial forecast data (analyst AND consensus), difference between analyst and consensus, forward valuation multiples (analyst and consensus), historical average multiples (adjusted for anomalies), sector-specific financial metrics, rating/view, price target (% upside/downside), market capitalization
5. Hidden columns for internal use: analyst conviction level, exit thresholds
6. Set formulas to automatically roll to next forward period (avoid arbitrary FY cutover causing abrupt valuation jumps)
7. "Other financial metrics" section should auto-update: ROIC, 5-year EPS growth, consensus 2-year forward growth
8. Group comparable companies by similar sector/sub-sector AND growth/risk characteristics
9. Calculate mean and median for each group (separate large-cap from small-cap if needed)
10. Use conditional formatting to highlight: most upside/downside stocks, analyst-vs-consensus divergence >5%, current multiple vs. historical average divergence >5%, group median vs mean divergence >5%
11. Enable sorting by risk-adjusted upside/downside to price target

Links to: Section 41.13 (ADViCE), Section 13 (Equity Portfolio Management)

### 41.16 Time & Productivity Management for Analysts

**Core Principle — Play Offense, Not Defense:**
- Schedule and protect research time blocks (proactive) rather than reacting to your inbox
- The inbox is someone else's agenda — don't let it manage your day

**Email Productivity:**
- Convert emails into tasks, calendar entries, or notes — then archive. Never leave actionable items sitting in the inbox.
- Batch email processing (e.g., check 3 times/day for 20 min each) rather than continuous monitoring
- Shut off email/chat notifications during deep research time

**Scheduling Best Practices:**
- Weekly planning routine: review upcoming catalysts, schedule research blocks, identify highest-priority activities
- Use a digital to-do system and calendar religiously
- Set time limits for activities — when the limit is reached, stop and evaluate whether continuing will yield additional alpha
- Eliminate low-value activities that don't map to any GAMMA PI pillar

**Preventing and Minimizing Disruptions:**
- Turn off all notifications during research time
- Use "do not disturb" mode
- For in-person work, signal unavailability (closed door, headphones)
- Batch similar tasks (all calls together, all modeling together)

Links to: Section 41.1 (GAMMA PI — the P pillar)

### 41.17 Information Asymmetry and Alpha Generation

**Core Insight:** An analyst's goal is to "identify mispriced securities" — which means identifying where consensus is wrong. The probability distribution of price targets based on proprietary research should be shifted from the consensus distribution. The magnitude of this shift, times your conviction, determines the expected alpha.

**Fund Collects Fees vs Fund Generates Alpha (balance):**
- A fund that only consumes public information and speaks with company management (same as everyone else) merely collects fees — it provides no information asymmetry
- A fund that ALSO identifies proprietary information (through the ASPIRE/PRACTICE frameworks applied to differentiated sources) generates genuine alpha by shifting the probability distribution

**Forecasting Resources (recommended):**
- *Superforecasting: The Art and Science of Prediction* by Tetlock & Gardner — methods for improving forecast accuracy
- *Structured Analytic Techniques for Intelligence Analysis* by Pherson & Heuer Jr. — methods to reduce intuitive errors and identify where biases creep in
- Goodjudgment.com — calibration and forecasting practice platform

**Reference Class Forecasting:** Always start with the "outside view" (base rates from historical data across similar situations), then adjust with the "inside view" (company-specific factors). This corrects for systematic optimism bias.

Links to: Section 41.1 (GAMMA PI), Section 41.2 (EPIC), Section 4 (Behavioral Finance)

### 41.18 Managing Private Wealth Clients — Applied IPS Construction (L3 PSM)

This subsection integrates the L3 MPWC Practical Skills Module, which applies private wealth concepts from Sections 10 and 37 in a hands-on, case-study-driven format. The MPWC PSM covers four units: (1) Developing a Goals-Based IPS, (2) Core Portfolio Construction, (3) Investment Proposal Meeting, (4) Performance Meeting. The completed case study features Frederick Otti (USD 28M portfolio) with three sub-portfolios (Liquidity, Trust, Growth) and base/optimistic/pessimistic cashflow projections.

**Goals-Based IPS Template Structure (5 Sections):**

**Section 1 — Client Background and Objectives:**
- Personal/financial information: age, family status, property, liabilities, income sources, expenses breakdown (discretionary vs non-discretionary)
- Goals categorized by time horizon: short-term (1-2 years), medium-term (3-7 years), long-term (8+ years)
- Distinguish between REQUIRED goals (must fund) and ASPIRATIONAL goals (fund if wealth permits)

**Section 2 — Risk Tolerance and Return Expectations:**
- Ability to take risk (net worth, income stability, liability profile) vs willingness to take risk (questionnaire results, behavioral observations, stated preferences)
- Total expected portfolio risk target (e.g., 16% standard deviation)
- Expected return range (e.g., 7-9% annualized) justified by goals and risk tolerance
- **Cashflow projection model** with three scenarios (base/optimistic/pessimistic) projecting portfolio value, growth, inflows, outflows, net cashflow, and year-end totals over minimum 5 years. This is the quantitative backbone of the IPS.

**Section 3 — Asset Allocation via Sub-Portfolios (Goals-Based):**
- **Liquidity (Reserve) Sub-Portfolio:** Cash-equivalents to cover immediate needs — renovations, liability provisions, mortgage repayment, 1-2 years of net non-discretionary spending. Prioritizes capital preservation and availability.
- **Intermediate Goals Sub-Portfolio (e.g., Trust):** Low-risk fixed income to fund medium-term goals with known funding dates. Present value of future obligation invested in duration-matched bonds.
- **Growth Sub-Portfolio:** Diversified portfolio (global equities, inflation-linked assets, private equity, real estate) targeting the growth needed for long-term goals (philanthropic endowment, legacy wealth transfer). Reflects the client's target risk tolerance.
- Execution plan: specific dollar allocations to each sub-portfolio, transition instructions, annual rebalancing triggers.

**Section 4 — Constraints and Behavioral Considerations:**
- Time horizons mapped to each goal and sub-portfolio
- Identified behavioral biases with mitigation strategies (overconfidence → comparative performance data; anchoring → opportunity cost illustrations)
- Legal considerations: estate planning, tax optimization, charitable giving structures
- Tax considerations: implications of court liabilities, ongoing charitable deductions

**Section 5 — Monitoring and Review:**
- Benchmark each sub-portfolio: liquidity → Treasury Bill index, intermediate → aggregate bond index, growth → blended benchmark matching strategic allocation
- Quarterly performance reviews against benchmarks
- Annual assumption updates (inflation, return expectations, liability timing)
- Regular scenario analysis to demonstrate impact of market changes and address behavioral biases

**Unit 2 — Core Portfolio Construction (Applied Excel):**
- Progressive complexity: 2-asset → 3-asset → multi-asset → optimized → remodeled portfolios
- Mean-variance optimization in Excel with Solver to maximize Sharpe ratio
- Inputs: expected returns, standard deviations, correlation matrix for each asset class
- Capital Market Assumptions (CMAs): historical-based AND forward-looking, with comparison of results
- Efficient frontier generation and optimal portfolio identification
- Constraint handling: minimum/maximum allocation bounds, asset class exclusions per client preference

**Unit 3 — Investment Proposal Meeting:**
- Data visualization best practices for client presentations (chart type selection, clarity, simplicity)
- Excel chart construction for portfolio proposals
- Behavioral bias coaching during proposal meetings (recognizing and addressing client biases in real-time)
- Professional skills for proposal delivery (confidence, empathy, adaptability)
- Creating a polished investment proposal presentation (PowerPoint)

**Unit 4 — Performance Meeting:**
- Portfolio evaluation methodology: absolute return, risk-adjusted return, benchmark-relative return
- Performance evaluation metrics: Sharpe ratio, information ratio, tracking error, max drawdown
- Performance report creation (PowerPoint) with appropriate visualization of results vs benchmarks
- Client communication of performance — framing underperformance constructively, reinforcing long-term thesis

Links to: Section 10 (Private Wealth Deep Dive), Section 37 (Private Wealth Applied), Section 2 (Asset Allocation Decision Framework), Section 11 (Portfolio Construction)

### 41.19 Python Fundamentals for Financial Analysis (L1 PSM)

The L1 Python Fundamentals PSM introduces programming skills applied to financial data. While primarily a coding skills module, it covers finance-relevant applications:

**Financial Data Retrieval:** Using yfinance (Yahoo Finance API wrapper) to obtain stock prices, beta, EPS, free cash flow, P/E ratios, balance sheets, income statements, cash flow statements, dividend history, analyst recommendations, and price history for any ticker symbol.

**Key Financial Metrics Accessible via Code:**
- Beta (stock volatility relative to market)
- Free Cash Flow (cash after operating and capital expenditures)
- P/E Ratio (trailing and forward)
- Complete financial statements (balance sheet, income statement, cash flow)
- Analyst recommendations aggregation
- Historical price data with custom date ranges

**Data Analysis Workflow:**
- Import data with Pandas DataFrames → clean/filter → compute derived metrics → visualize
- DataFrame operations: filtering, sorting, merging, handling missing data, concatenation
- Statistical analysis: returns computation, volatility calculation, correlation matrices

**Portfolio Analytics:**
- Single-stock data visualization (price history, returns distribution, moving averages)
- Multi-stock comparison and relative performance
- Portfolio optimization using mean-variance framework (Python implementation of concepts from Unit 2 of L3 MPWC)
- Monte Carlo simulation for portfolio scenario analysis

**Decision Rule:** Python enables automation of many GAMMA PI tasks — automated data collection for comp tables (41.15), automated Magic Number computation (41.3), automated HELP historical analysis (41.4), and portfolio optimization (41.18). Analysts who can code have a significant productivity advantage (the "P" in GAMMA PI).

Links to: Section 41.1 (GAMMA PI — Productivity), Section 41.15 (Comp Table automation), Section 41.18 (Portfolio Construction)

## APPENDIX F: L1 FOUNDATIONS — CORPORATE ISSUERS, CAPITAL STRUCTURE & WORKING CAPITAL (V14 — NEW)

*Source: CFA L1 Kaplan Study Notes — Corporate Issuers (7 readings), FSA Analysis Techniques. Integrated where content provides foundational detail not covered by L3-derived sections.*

### F.1 Business Structures & Organizational Forms

**Sole Proprietorship:** Single owner, unlimited personal liability, no separate legal entity. Simple to form but owner bears all risk. Business income taxed as personal income.

**General Partnership (GP):** Two or more owners, all with unlimited liability. Each partner is jointly and severally liable for all partnership obligations. Income flows through to partners.

**Limited Partnership (LP):** At least one general partner (unlimited liability, manages) + limited partners (liability limited to investment, no management role). Common structure for PE/VC/hedge funds.

**Limited Liability Partnership (LLP):** Partners have limited liability for other partners' actions but may still have unlimited liability for own actions. Common for professional services (law, accounting).

**Limited Liability Company (LLC):** Hybrid — liability protection of a corporation + tax treatment of a partnership. Members not personally liable for company debts. Flexible management structure.

**Corporation — Five Key Features:**
1. Separate legal entity from owners
2. Limited liability for shareholders (maximum loss = investment amount)
3. Perpetual existence (survives beyond founders)
4. Transferable ownership (shares can be bought/sold)
5. Centralized management (board of directors + executive officers)

**Public vs. Private Corporations:**
- Public: shares traded on exchange, subject to securities regulation, mandatory disclosures, broader investor base, higher liquidity
- Private: shares not publicly traded, fewer regulatory requirements, concentrated ownership, less liquidity, lower disclosure burden
- Benefits of going public: access to capital, liquidity for existing shareholders, currency for acquisitions, enhanced visibility
- Benefits of staying private: no disclosure requirements, no short-term earnings pressure, lower compliance costs, flexibility in strategy

**Methods of Going Public:**
- IPO (Initial Public Offering): traditional underwritten offering, investment bank markets shares, roadshow, book-building
- Direct Listing: company lists existing shares directly on exchange without underwriter, no new capital raised, no lockup period
- SPAC (Special Purpose Acquisition Company): "blank check" company raises capital via IPO, then merges with private target → target becomes public without traditional IPO process. Risk: misaligned incentives between SPAC sponsors and investors.
- LBO (Leveraged Buyout): taking a public company private using significant debt financing → concentrate ownership → restructure → eventually re-IPO or sell

Links to: Appendix E (Credit Analysis — capital structure), Section 14.6 (PE/VC structures use LP form)

### F.2 Business Models Framework

**Business Model Definition:** How a company creates, delivers, and captures value. Analyzed through four dimensions: Who (target customers), What (value proposition), Where (channels & markets), How Much (pricing & revenue model).

**Value Proposition vs. Value Chain vs. Supply Chain:**
- Value proposition: the benefit a company offers to its customers that differentiates it from competitors
- Value chain: the internal activities (inbound logistics → operations → outbound logistics → marketing → service) that create value
- Supply chain: the external network of suppliers, manufacturers, distributors connecting raw materials to end customers

**Pricing Models Taxonomy:**
- Tiered pricing: different price levels for different product tiers/quantities (e.g., software plans)
- Dynamic pricing: prices change in real-time based on demand, time, customer segment (e.g., airlines, ride-sharing)
- Value-based pricing: price set based on perceived customer value rather than cost (e.g., luxury goods, pharmaceuticals)
- Auction-based pricing: price determined by competitive bidding (e.g., art, government bonds, online auctions)

**Multi-Product Pricing Strategies:**
- Bundling: combining multiple products at a discount vs. individual prices (e.g., cable TV packages, software suites)
- Razor-razorblade: sell the base product cheaply, profit from recurring consumable sales (e.g., printers/ink, razors/blades, gaming consoles/games)
- Add-on pricing: low base price + premium add-ons (e.g., airline tickets + baggage/seat selection fees)

**Other Revenue Models:**
- Penetration pricing: initially low price to gain market share, then raise prices once established
- Freemium: basic product free, charge for premium features (e.g., Spotify, LinkedIn)
- Hidden revenue / advertising: service free to users, revenue from advertising or data (e.g., Google, social media platforms)
- Subscription: recurring payments for ongoing access (e.g., Netflix, SaaS)
- Leasing / licensing / franchising: monetize assets or IP without outright sale

**Network Effects:**
- One-sided: value increases as more users join the same side of the platform (e.g., social media — more users = more content = more valuable to each user)
- Two-sided: value increases as more users join BOTH sides (e.g., Uber — more drivers attract more riders, and vice versa; credit cards — more merchants attract more cardholders)
- Network effects create competitive moats but can also create winner-take-all dynamics

**Crowdsourcing:** Obtaining ideas, content, or services from a large group of people (typically online) rather than traditional employees/suppliers. Examples: Wikipedia, open-source software, crowdfunding platforms.

Links to: Section 3.1 (valuation — understanding business model is prerequisite), Section 13.8 (margin sustainability depends on business model)

### F.3 Capital Structure — Modigliani-Miller Framework

**WACC (Weighted Average Cost of Capital):**
WACC = (E/V × r_e) + (D/V × r_d × (1 - t))
Where: E = market value of equity, D = market value of debt, V = E + D, r_e = cost of equity, r_d = cost of debt, t = corporate tax rate.
→ WACC is the minimum required return on invested capital for value creation. Projects with return > WACC create shareholder value.

**Cost of Debt (r_d):** The yield at which the company can issue new debt. Always lower than cost of equity because debt holders have priority in bankruptcy + interest is tax-deductible. After-tax cost of debt = r_d × (1 - t).

**Cost of Equity (r_e):** Higher than cost of debt because equity holders are residual claimants. Commonly estimated via CAPM: r_e = r_f + β × (E(R_m) - r_f). As leverage increases, equity becomes riskier → β increases → r_e increases.

**Corporate Life Cycle & Capital Structure:**
- Startup: high risk, no debt capacity, funded by founders/angels/VC → equity only
- Growth: increasing revenue, some debt capacity, but still primarily equity-funded → low D/E ratio
- Mature: stable cash flows, strong debt capacity, optimal time for leverage → moderate to high D/E ratio
- Decline: shrinking revenue, debt becomes risky again → companies may deleverage or face distress

**MM Proposition I (Without Taxes):** V_L = V_U
→ In a perfect market (no taxes, no bankruptcy costs, no agency costs), capital structure is IRRELEVANT — the value of a levered firm equals the value of an unlevered firm. The total pie doesn't change; leverage just reslices it between debt and equity holders.

**MM Proposition II (Without Taxes):** r_e = r_0 + (r_0 - r_d)(D/E)
→ The cost of equity rises linearly with leverage. As D/E increases, equity holders demand higher returns to compensate for increased financial risk. But WACC remains constant because the cheaper debt is exactly offset by more expensive equity.

**MM Proposition I (With Taxes):** V_L = V_U + tD
→ With corporate taxes, leverage DOES add value because interest payments are tax-deductible. The value of the tax shield = tax rate × debt amount. This creates an incentive to use debt (all else equal, more debt = more value).

**MM Proposition II (With Taxes):** r_e = r_0 + (r_0 - r_d)(1 - t)(D/E)
→ Cost of equity still rises with leverage, but more slowly than without taxes (the (1-t) term dampens the increase). WACC decreases as leverage increases because of the tax shield.

**Static Trade-Off Theory:** V_L = V_U + tD - PV(costs of financial distress)
→ Optimal capital structure balances the tax benefit of debt against the expected costs of financial distress (bankruptcy costs, loss of customers/suppliers, loss of key employees, fire-sale asset prices). The optimal D/E is where the marginal tax benefit equals the marginal increase in expected distress costs.

**Pecking Order Theory:** Firms prefer financing in this order: (1) internal funds (retained earnings) → (2) private debt → (3) public debt → (4) equity. Rationale: information asymmetry — managers know more than investors → equity issuance signals overvaluation → stock price drops 2-3% on announcement.

**Free Cash Flow Hypothesis (Jensen):** When firms have excess free cash flow and no profitable projects, managers may waste it on empire-building, perks, or value-destroying acquisitions. Debt forces discipline → required interest payments reduce available free cash flow → constrains management waste. → Implication: high FCF + low growth opportunities = debt is beneficial as a governance mechanism.

**Agency Costs of Equity vs. Debt:**
- Agency costs of equity: managers spending on perks, empire-building, risk avoidance → solved by debt discipline
- Agency costs of debt: asset substitution (risky projects), underinvestment (passing up +NPV projects if benefits go to debtholders) → solved by covenants

**Target Capital Structure:** Most companies target a range rather than a precise ratio. Actual leverage fluctuates around the target due to market movements, investment needs, and financing opportunities. Companies periodically rebalance toward the target through debt issuance/retirement, equity buybacks, or retained earnings accumulation.

Links to: Appendix E.2 (Capital Structure Framework), Section 12.7 (leverage in FI portfolios), Section 27.1 (leverage amplification)

### F.4 Capital Investments & Allocation

**Project Types:**
- Going-concern projects: maintenance of existing operations (replacing equipment, maintaining facilities)
- Regulatory/compliance: required by law or regulation, no choice but to invest
- Expansion of existing business: increasing capacity for existing products/markets
- New product/market: entering new business lines or geographies (highest risk)

**Capital Allocation Process (4 Steps):**
1. Idea generation → identify potential projects from all levels of the organization
2. Analyzing project proposals → estimate cash flows, assess risk, calculate NPV/IRR
3. Create firm-wide capital budget → rank projects, allocate limited capital to highest-value projects
4. Monitoring decisions and conducting post-audit → track actual vs. projected performance, learn from mistakes

**NPV (Net Present Value):** Sum of all discounted future cash flows minus initial investment. NPV > 0 → project creates value → accept. NPV is the GOLD STANDARD for capital allocation decisions because it directly measures value creation in dollar terms.

**IRR (Internal Rate of Return):** The discount rate that makes NPV = 0. Accept if IRR > required rate of return (WACC). Limitation: IRR can give misleading results for mutually exclusive projects (different scales) or non-conventional cash flows (multiple sign changes → multiple IRRs).

**ROIC (Return on Invested Capital):** ROIC = After-tax operating profit / Average invested capital. Measures how efficiently a company uses its capital. ROIC > WACC → value creation. ROIC < WACC → value destruction. Decomposition: ROIC = Operating profit margin × Capital turnover × (1 - t).

**Real Options in Capital Investment:**
1. Timing option: delay investment until more information is available (value increases with uncertainty)
2. Sizing option: expand or contract the scale of a project based on realized demand
3. Flexibility option: change inputs, outputs, or production methods after investment (e.g., dual-fuel power plant)
4. Fundamental option: option to abandon a project if it underperforms → limits downside loss
→ Real options increase project value beyond static NPV. Projects in highly uncertain environments have higher real option value.

**Capital Allocation Cognitive Errors:**
1. Forecasting errors: overly optimistic revenue/cost projections → inflate NPV
2. Ignoring internal financing costs: treating retained earnings as "free" → underestimate true cost of capital
3. Inflation mismatches: mixing nominal cash flows with real discount rates (or vice versa) → systematic NPV errors

**Capital Allocation Behavioral Biases:**
1. Anchoring: fixating on initial project estimates despite new contradictory information
2. Accounting reliance: overreliance on accounting metrics (EPS impact) rather than economic value (NPV)
3. Pet projects: emotional attachment to specific investments → continue funding despite poor performance (escalation of commitment / sunk cost fallacy)
4. Insufficient alternative evaluation: not considering enough alternatives → accept the first adequate option rather than optimizing

Links to: Section 3.1 (valuation hierarchy), Appendix E.2 (capital structure), Section 41.3 (GAMMA PI — project evaluation)

### F.5 Working Capital & Liquidity Management

**Working Capital Definitions:**
- Total working capital = current assets − current liabilities
- Net working capital = (current assets excluding cash and marketable securities) − (current liabilities excluding short-term debt and current portion of long-term debt)
- Net working capital is usually expressed as a percentage of sales
- A short cash conversion cycle typically means a low ratio of working capital to sales
- In general, companies should minimize working capital to free capital for higher-return uses or return to investors

**Cash Conversion Cycle (CCC):**
CCC = Days of Inventory on Hand (DOH) + Days Sales Outstanding (DSO) − Days Payable Outstanding (DPO)
→ Measures the time between paying suppliers and receiving cash from customers. Lower CCC = more efficient working capital management = less capital tied up in operations.

**Shortening the Cash Conversion Cycle:**
- Reduce DOH: JIT inventory, cancel slow-moving products, improve demand forecasting
- Reduce DSO: offer early payment discounts, encourage electronic payments, tighten credit standards, charge late fees
- Increase DPO: negotiate longer payment terms with suppliers (depends on bargaining power)

**EAR of Supplier Financing (Cost of Trade Credit):**
EAR = [(1 + discount% / (100% − discount%))^(365 / (payment period − discount period))] − 1
→ Example: "2/10 net 30" means 2% discount if paid within 10 days, otherwise full amount due in 30 days. Not taking the discount means effectively borrowing for 20 extra days at an annualized cost that can exceed 30%.

**Primary Sources of Liquidity:**
1. Cash and marketable securities on hand
2. Borrowings (bank lines of credit, trade credit from suppliers)
3. Cash flow from the business (operating cash flow — the most important long-term liquidity source)

**Secondary Sources of Liquidity (more drastic, signal distress):**
1. Suspending or discontinuing dividends
2. Deferring or lowering capital spending
3. Issuing equity
4. Renegotiating contract terms
5. Selling assets
6. Filing for bankruptcy protection

**Drags on Liquidity (lagging cash inflows):** uncollected receivables, obsolete inventory, borrowing constraints.

**Pulls on Liquidity (accelerating cash outflows):** making payments early, lowered credit limits, limits on short-term lines of credit, weak liquidity positions.

**Liquidity Ratios:**
- Current ratio = current assets / current liabilities (general guideline: > 1)
- Quick ratio = (cash + marketable securities + receivables) / current liabilities (excludes inventory)
- Cash ratio = (cash + marketable securities) / current liabilities (most conservative)

Links to: Section 5.1 (liquidity risk), Appendix E (credit analysis — liquidity assessment), Section 29.2 (institutional liquidity risk)

### F.6 Financial Statement Analysis — Ratio Taxonomy & DuPont Decomposition

**Activity Ratios (Efficiency):**
- Inventory turnover = COGS / average inventory → DOH = 365 / inventory turnover
- Receivables turnover = revenue / average receivables → DSO = 365 / receivables turnover
- Payables turnover = purchases / average payables → DPO = 365 / payables turnover
- Total asset turnover = revenue / average total assets
- Fixed asset turnover = revenue / average net fixed assets

**Liquidity Ratios:** Current ratio, quick ratio, cash ratio (see F.5), plus:
- Defensive interval = (cash + marketable securities + receivables) / daily operating expenses
→ Number of days a company can operate from liquid assets without additional revenue

**Solvency Ratios:**
- Debt-to-assets = total debt / total assets
- Debt-to-equity = total debt / total equity
- Debt-to-capital = total debt / (total debt + total equity)
- Financial leverage = average total assets / average total equity
- Interest coverage = EBIT / interest expense (>6x = IG territory; <1.5x = distress)
- Fixed charge coverage = (EBIT + lease payments) / (interest expense + lease payments)

**Profitability Ratios:**
- Gross profit margin = gross profit / revenue
- Operating profit margin = EBIT / revenue
- Pretax margin = EBT / revenue
- Net profit margin = net income / revenue
- ROA = net income / average total assets
- Operating ROA = EBIT / average total assets (removes financing effects)
- ROE = net income / average total equity
- ROIC = EBIT(1-t) / average invested capital (see F.4)

**DuPont 3-Stage Decomposition:**
ROE = Net profit margin × Asset turnover × Financial leverage
ROE = (Net income / Revenue) × (Revenue / Average assets) × (Average assets / Average equity)
→ Separates profitability, efficiency, and leverage drivers of equity returns.

**DuPont 5-Stage Decomposition:**
ROE = Tax burden × Interest burden × EBIT margin × Asset turnover × Financial leverage
ROE = (Net income / EBT) × (EBT / EBIT) × (EBIT / Revenue) × (Revenue / Avg assets) × (Avg assets / Avg equity)
→ Further separates the impact of taxes, interest costs, and operating profitability. The tax burden and interest burden ratios are both ≤ 1; lower values mean higher tax/interest impact on ROE.

Links to: Section 13.8 (earnings quality & margin sustainability), Section 3.2 (Grinold-Kroner — earnings growth), Appendix E.1 (credit ratios)
